# swg3js: what this is and how we work on it

Read this first. It is the handoff from the sessions that built the game so far, written so a new session can carry on without the old chat.

## What we are building

A just-for-fun recreation of Star Wars Galaxies (the 2003 MMO) that runs in a browser: Three.js for rendering, Rapier for physics, Vite and TypeScript, no framework. The owner has a legal SWG install (the SWG Legends client, which contains the original SOE archives) and Jedi Academy; everything the game shows is converted from those installs on the owner's own machine into `assets-private/`, which is git-ignored. The game code is public; the assets never are.

The owner plays it on Windows, in Chrome, and tests by hand. They report what they see in words ("the cockpit is a metre too high"), paste console output, and run converter commands you give them. They cannot see your tool calls, so every reply has to stand on its own and say exactly what to run and what to look for.

## Hard rules

- Never commit, copy into the repo, or publish anything from `assets-private/`, any `.tre` archive, or anything derived from the game's files, with one exception the owner made on 2026-10-18: `tools/swg/manifests/final-names.json`, the names list taken from SOE's own final index (each file's path, archive, offset, lengths, compressor and checksum, and SOE's deletions, for archives every NGE install already holds; no content), so every player's converter reads SOE's late archives from their own install with nothing extra. Nothing else derived from SOE's indexes, the development tree or the NGE copy is committed or quoted. Diagnostic output pasted into chat is fine; files are not.
- The converter must work with `--retail-only`, which mounts only the SOE-origin archives, and the whole game must run from what that converts. Emulator and mod content stays out, with one exception the owner made on 2026-10-18: SWG Legends' own additions (its new armour, clothes, creatures, places and planets) may be converted where a player's install has them and they can be read as they stand, never by breaking an archive's encryption. Whatever comes from them is optional and marked as Legends in the game, nothing may depend on a Legends file, and a player without them simply does not see them. Like everything converted, nothing of theirs is ever committed. See `docs/ASSETS.md`.
- The interface is entirely our own and none of the client's own interface art is used: no `ui_*` atlas, icon, bar, reticle, frame or colour scheme of the game's is converted, read or drawn, by the owner's decision. Everything on the screen is drawn by this game from one table of eighteen named colours (`src/core/palette.ts` and the `palette` block in `src/style.css`), the shapes in `src/ui/hudCanvas.ts` and `src/ui/hud.css`, and the marks we drew by hand in `src/ui/hud.svg`. The client's own map pictures (`texture/ui_map_<planet>.dds`) and zone-map icons are the map window's and are not the interface's art.
- Never put a model name or model identifier in a commit message, PR text, or code comment.
- Commit messages: a one-line summary in plain words (what changed for the player or the converter), then a short paragraph of why, no bullet lists, no file lists. Attribution trailers are whatever the current tool adds; do not invent them.
- Do not create pull requests unless asked.
- Development happens on `claude/gun-combat`. After every commit, push the branch and fast-forward `main`:
  `git push -u origin claude/gun-combat && git checkout main && git merge --ff-only claude/gun-combat && git push origin main && git checkout claude/gun-combat`
- Before pushing: `npx tsc --noEmit -p .` and `npx vite build` must be clean, and `npm run test:all` must pass (it runs the converter, terrain, effects, weather, first-person head, close combat, inventory, audio, display and network suites; several other suites are still separate scripts). `node --check` any converter or server module you touched. The tests are plain node scripts (`npm run test:converter`, `npm run test:net`, …), not vitest suites.

## Setup on a new machine

Node 22.18 or newer, `npm install`. Copy `.env.example` to `.env` and fill in:

```
SWG=<folder holding the client's .tre archives>
JKA=<Jedi Academy's GameData folder>
```

The converter replaces `@SWG` and `@JKA` on its command line with those. Then follow "Converting your own SWG install" in `README.md` (the whole list, in order, every command with `--retail-only`), and `npm run dev`. `npm run swg -- status assets-private` says what is in place and prints the command for anything missing.

`.claude/launch.json` starts the Vite dev server for the Claude Code extension.

## How the pieces fit

- `tools/swg/cli.mjs` is the converter, one `case` per command; `tools/swg/README.md` documents every command. It reads the client's IFF-based formats (`iff.mjs`, `msh.mjs`, `appearance.mjs`, `pob.mjs`, `sht.mjs`, `dds.mjs`, `objtemplate.mjs`, `datatable.mjs`, `shipdata.mjs`), writes GLB (`glb.mjs`) plus a `manifest.json` per pack. Diagnostics that exist and are worth knowing: `list`, `dump --strings` (prints every readable string per chunk, the way to learn a file layout), `template` (an object template's parameter chain), `materials --ship=<id>` (every shader a hull uses with what the converter made of it, and its hardpoints), `shader`, `pob`.
- `src/main.ts` is the app: the game loop, input dispatch, mounting and boarding, the camera choice, the `__debug` object on `window` (every helper is documented in the README's Debugging section).
- `src/world/` streams the planet: terrain from the game's own terrain rules, placed objects, portal buildings with cells drawn through a stencil portal renderer (`portalRender.ts`), a compile queue that pre-compiles shaders so nothing stalls on first draw, cascaded shadow maps.
- `src/player/` is the character: a parts-based skinned rig, the Jedi Academy movement and saber port, the blaster carries, and the "aboard" mode (see ships).
- `src/vehicles/` is the garage (`garage.ts` reads the manifests and spawns), the vehicle physics (`vehicle.ts`), ship interiors (`interior.ts`), engine trails (`trail.ts`).
- `src/net/` and `server/relay.mjs`: a dependency-free WebSocket server, grown in place from the relay that came before it, which passes player states around and holds the few things nobody can hold alone — one clock, who each player is, and which browser keeps each of the world's creatures (`ownership.mjs`, the browser's half `src/net/owned.ts` and `npcNet.ts`). Its sibling modules (`hash.mjs`, `clock.mjs`, `rooms.mjs`, `wire.mjs`, `store.mjs`, `identity.mjs`, `ownership.mjs`, `npcWire.mjs`) are dependency-free and shared with the node tests exactly as `shipWire.mjs` is; the browser's half is `src/net/session.ts` and `src/net/hash.ts`, and `src/world/sharedClock.ts` is what the day and the weather read. Other players are drawn as their own characters. With no server address set none of it runs and the game is what it was.
- `src/story/` is the story's rule book, pure and imported by the server as it stands: the book and its changes (`book.ts`), the sets read from `story-private/` and the test set (`set.ts`, `talkSet.ts`, `doc.ts`, `standing.ts`'s ladders), the step machine (`quests.ts`), conversations (`talkRules.ts`), documents (`docRules.ts`), the journal and the ISB's file, Standing and the named people (`standing.ts`, `people.ts`), the checker (`check.ts`), and the one host loop both hosts are (`hostCore.ts`): `localHost.ts` in the browser with no server, `server/stories.mjs` with one, `remoteHost.ts` the browser's side of that. What the world does with it (detectors, the tracker, the windows, the stood cast, the companion) is `src/main.ts`, `src/world/` and `src/ui/`.

## Things that took a long time to learn (do not relearn them)

- GLTFLoader strips `:` from node names and keeps the original in `userData.name`. The converter names hardpoints `hp:<name>` and cells `cell:<index>:<name>`; read them through `hardpointName()` in `garage.ts` and `cellIndexOf()` in `interior.ts`, never by matching `o.name` for a colon.
- A ship's hardpoints live in its `.lod` appearance, not its mesh, and the appearance chain (`.apt`, `.lod`, `.cmp`) carries them through `resolveParts` onto the first part. A single-mesh ship must keep its chain when the chain has hardpoints.
- Rapier scene queries see nothing until the world has stepped once. A ray cast into a freshly built world misses everything.
- Rapier's character controller with "apply impulses to dynamic bodies" panics on a trimesh collider. The push is off (`Player.pushBodies`), and every trimesh goes through `cleanTrimesh()` with `TRIMESH_FLAGS`.
- Changing the number of lights in the scene recompiles every shader (a second each on the owner's machine). Lights are pooled and never added or removed at runtime: `effects.flash` is a pool of point lights that everything borrows, including a ship's room lights.
- Materials with `transparent` true and any opacity still cast full shadows; glass meshes get `castShadow = false`. The converter marks glass-named shaders with `userData.glass`; the game never blends them (the client did not either), it swaps in a clear copy of the material while someone is in the ship.
- The game's rooms in a portal building are often larger than the hull around them and are only meant to be seen through portals. The window openings are "invisible collidable" meshes: colliders only, never drawn, never casting.
- A cockpit frame (`cockpit/<ship>.iff`, FORM CPIT > 0000: FRAM the appearance, ZOOM the camera distances, FRST the first step out, 3OFF the chase offset, 1OFF the first-person offset) is authored in the ship's own space: hang it on the model at its origin and it lands in the canopy. Every frame carries one hardpoint, `camera`, pointing down the nose: that point plus 1OFF (X mirrored), or a frame's share of it (`COCKPIT_OFFSET_SHARE`: the B-wing takes half, the owner's split between its full offset, better to fight by, and none, which looked more real), is the first-person eye hovering and flying (`Vehicle.cockpitEye`, `cockpitShare`). The game never drew a pilot. The body hangs with the seated clip's eyes exactly on that eye, in first and third person alike (`SEAT_RULE` 'eyes', the owner's call: the head lines up with the view): where the seated clip's first frame puts the eye joints (`rig.seatedPoints`; space_sitting: eyes 0.708 m over the pelvis and 0.109 ahead; skeletons without eye joints, the rodian, mon calamari and ithorian, use the head plus 0.07 up and 0.09 ahead), scaled by the figure's height. The cushion one downward ray through the frame's triangles finds is still measured (`seatDropBelow`, `Vehicle.cushionDrop`, `__debug.cockpit().seatGap`), and the older rule that moved the body onto it (the pelvis 0.10 m over it, at most 0.10 m up in first person, `bodyLift`) is `__debug.cockpit({ cushion: true })`. `Player.syncMount` hangs the body from `cockpitEye()` less `cockpitOffset` plus the file's own 1OFF, which is the view eye only while `cockpitOffset` is the whole 1OFF: a share goes in `cockpitShare`, never into `cockpitOffset`. The eyes are measured from the clip's first frame, never the live bones: the idle's fidget moves them 0.04 m.
- A hardpoint the gun patterns take is never a seat or an eye (`isSeatHardpoint`, `isEyeHardpoint` in `cockpitSeat.ts`): the gunboats' `pilotmuzzle1` is a gun. The ships without a frame are all room ships flown from their bridge, whose first-person eye is the pilot's own; seated in one (no pilot spot), the rider is not drawn. Only the ships pack's ships sit by the eye (`Vehicle.eyeSeat`); anything spawned "as a ship" keeps its own seat.
- Seated in a ship and zoomed all the way in, the view is locked to the hull hovering as in flight (`ThirdPersonCamera.cockpit`, then `chase`, both at `pos + q * eye`, so lift-off never moves it). Hovering, the mouse's sideways movement moves a heading target at most 0.6 rad ahead of the hull (`cockpitYawStep`), which also steers a bridge pilot hovering: aboard, the camera's yaw is in the hull's frame and must never be handed to `drive.heading`. Alt looks round from the eye itself (`update`'s `eyeAhead` 0), starting at the nose. A rider's spine takes no torso twist while mounted, and the on-foot twist is cleared on mount.
- Ship attachments are templates under `object/tangible/ship/attachment/`; the stock ones come from the per-hull `datatables/space/ship_chassis_<chassis>.iff` (each slot's stock component's look, `pickStock`, which on every retail hull is the column's most common `attachment:hardpoint` cell; a `modification_*` slot, whose one filled row is a reward look, has no stock part), never by name (the Jedi starfighter's parts are `jedifighter_*`, and a name search hung every X-wing gun twice). A client data file's version form also carries chunks beside its forms: HOBJ (appearance, hardpoint), IHOB (appearance, hardpoint, three floats that are non-zero only on turret barrels), CHL2 (an object template, then 17 floats, the first three its place in the parent) and CHLD (the same with an appearance): the Black Sun wing structures, the Hutt wings, turret bodies and barrels, radar dishes. A hardpoint a part's client data names is looked for on that part, then on what already hangs under it (a turret's barrels are on its body), then on the whole ship (an engine's ONOF names the hull's `engine_on1`). `yt1300_engine_none` is the empty engine slot's stand-in, the same mesh as the engine: written with `standInFor` and not hung while a component fills the slot. The ships command writes the tree (`tools/swg/shipparts.mjs`: `parent`, `hardpoint`, `place`, `turn`; `owner` on what a stock part's own client data produced; `chassis` on every ship, which is how status tells an old one; manifest `assembly: 2`); a def with no `parent` is hung by name anywhere on the ship, as before. `expandPart` (one part's own subtree, for a part swapped in) writes by name what the part does not carry itself (the Y-wing engine's "on" appearance on the hull's `engine1`, the V-wing engine's glow on the hull's `wing1`), so a part's `children` must be hung after the ship's other parts, over the whole ship, not under the part alone.
- A player hull's components are three tables: `datatables/space/ship_chassis.iff`, a row per chassis (`player_<ship>`) whose slot cells are comma lists of compatibility classes (an empty cell: no such slot); `ship_components.iff`, each component's templates, type and one class (seventeen names end in `.iff`); and `ship_chassis_<chassis>.iff`, a row per component that shows anything on that hull, each slot column's cell `attachment:hardpoint` pairs (an empty hardpoint is the hull's origin). The per-hull table is looser than the chassis both ways: a slot offers only what its classes accept, and a looks column the chassis row gives no class is a fixed part (the Star Destroyer's 92 turret bases); `buildSlots` in `shipfit.mjs` applies both. The chassis name is the template's file name without `shared_` (`chassisNameFor`: a decorated YT-1300 uses `player_yt1300`, the Corellian corvette `player_corvette`). The stock part is the modal look's best-ranked component (`pickStock`), and a `modification_*` slot has no stock part: it starts empty (`STOCK_LEFT_OUT`). The modal look over the components a slot accepts is the column's most common cell on every retail hull, so the stock parts hung and their order (the looks table's columns, `stockPairs`) did not change when the stock rule did.
- A component's name is `space/space_item:<name>_n` for most, the shared template's `objectName` for about two hundred more (the crafted ones are in `space_crafting_n`, and an unset string id must not stop the search up the template chain: `setStringId`), and nothing at all for about fifty (the collection rewards, the capital ships' turrets), which get one made from the key (`fallbackLabel`).
- An NPC ship template (`object/ship/shared_<type>_tierN.iff`) resolves to the same appearance and client data as the player ship of that family, so an NPC type is drawn with the garage hull whose template has its appearance (170 tiered types in 34 families did on the mount before SOE's final names list, all at tiers 1 to 5; with it, SOE's own families run to tier 10 and 295 types in 47 families do, the droid fighter's among them now that a player hull shares its appearance, and the twelve families that exist only from tier 6 (the Dunelizard, Ixiyen, Kihraxz, Kimogila, Krayt, Rihkxyrk and Scyk hulls) match no side's pattern and list as neutral; the gunboats, shuttles, freighters, merchant cruisers, transports, the pods, the Kessel mine turret, the smuggler warlord's ship and the `_npc` YT-1300, YKL-37R, Y-8 and Decimator have NPC-only appearances and are listed in `combat.json`'s `skipped`; `nova_orion_*`, a quest line's with tiers up to 10, is still not the tier ladder and is dropped). Tiers 6 to 10 fly, fit and take damage as tier 5 (`src/space/tierLadder.ts`, a ladder of ours moved through `__debug.npcShips({ ladder })`: the skill reaches a pilot in the air at once, since the brain reads it at every think, while a ship's fit and strength are made when it is stood and reach the next one), and the rosters still stop at 5, so a patrol is what it was. Its chassis row in `ship_chassis.iff` drops the style (`blacksun_light_s01_tier1` reads `blacksun_light_tier1`, so the Black Sun styles share one tier fit). The per-hull tier tables (`ship_chassis_<chassis>.iff`) are look tables, not loadouts: they are the same at every tier (one family differs in a few look cells) and list every component that shows a model, from mk1 to heavy ion cannons. Which components an NPC carried was the server's choice and did not ship; the game picks them by tier (`loadout.ts`, invented) from the tier chassis's fit, which the ships command writes into `combat.json` with `buildSlots` and the same per-look conversion as a player hull's (`looksToParts` in `cli.mjs`), with no paint.
- The formation tables (`datatables/space/formation/{arrow,claw,wall}.iff`) are 20 slots of X, Y, Z metres in the leader's frame (row 0 the leader), then X2D, Z2D for the same slots on one plane. The arrow's row 1 is ahead of the leader (+25): the leader flies second. Taunt tables (`string/en/space/taunts/*.stf`) are `entercombat`, `gothit`, `hityou`, `death` 1 to 5; `%TU` and `%NU` both name the player, and the `_low` tables name nobody. `ship_target_appearance.iff` names `target_acquired_appearance` twice, and `parseDatatable` keeps only the second (a sound). The name table (`space_mobile_type.stf`) misses a few tiers (four of the Imperial Guard interceptor's five), which take another tier's name.
- The flight computers are `object/intangible/ship/shared_navicomputer_1..6.iff` ("v1 Flight Computer"); the astromechs are the pet templates in `datatables/space_command/programmable_droids.iff`, the crafted and plain ones sharing an appearance, drawn as the mobiles pack's `astromech_rN.glb` (R2-D2 has no model there and is left out). The eight hulls with `hp:astromech` take a droid there; `ship_droid_appearance_override.iff` (ship, ground appearance, space appearance) swaps in the N-1's head, which the ships command converts. A droid stands sunk in its socket so only the dome and the top of the body show (every socket's `hp:astromech` is at the hull's top surface, which covers the socket): its posed box, measured once per file in `Garage.fitPart`, puts its point `DROID_SHOWN` (0.35, invented) of its height down from the top on the hardpoint, with a share per hull where the socket lies otherwise (`DROID_SHOWN_BY_HULL`: the V-wing's hardpoint is 0.21 m under its hull's top, the Jedi starfighter's in a wing 0.67 m thick); the N-1's head is the game's own and never sunk; `__debug.droid({ shown, hull })`. Status asks for the ships again when the astromechs arrive after them.
- Ship paint is FORM CSHD: TXTR's TX1D operations pick a pattern's MAIN and HUEB textures together (`index_texture_1`), and TFAC's PAL operations set MAIN's and HUEB's colour factors from 64-colour palettes (`index_color_1`, `index_color_2`); all three are non-private, so one value per ship covers the hull, the wings, the carriers (the Black Sun wing structures, the Hutt wings) and the parts. The colours are passes two and three of the effect, so a first-pass check (`shaderNeedsBake`) misses them: the ships command bakes every pass at the shader's defaults (`paintedMain` in `cli.mjs`, through `surfaceTexture`'s `mainImage`, whose colour replaces the main's before the glow split, so the glow is split from the painted colour; its alpha, the gloss and a MAIN glow's mask, stays the chosen pattern's, since the bake's alpha is the first pass's and opaque: `withPatternAlpha`), and names the image `<pattern>#paint:<shader>`, since two paint shaders on one pattern differ by their colours. A hologram palette is not paint. A recipe's `glow` is written only when the converted material glows: seven emismap paint shaders have masks too faint to split, and a glow put on a material with no emissive map would need a new program.
- A WING form's DATA is the wing template, a float open angle in degrees, a float seconds to open, then the sound; then either PSOR (x, y, z and three angles in degrees) or HARD (a hardpoint of the part whose client data lists it). Either is where the wing object stands and its hinge: its mesh is modelled about that frame and turns about its own Z. The X-wing's PSOR is all zeros, which is why reading it as a hinge in the hull's frame looked right there and nowhere else; the ARC-170's and Jedi starfighter's PSOR places are exactly the hulls' wing1..4 hardpoints. An earlier try at placing wings failed only because it did not mirror X. The middle angle is never used and no wing uses two, so their order cannot matter; which axis each turns about is taken as yaw (Y), pitch, roll (Z): the V-wing is the only yaw, the B-wing the only roll (its body is a collar round the pod, which only a turn about Z keeps on it), each one constant in `shipAssembly.ts`. A wing's own client data can hold wings: the B-wing body's two foils hang on its `wing_l1`/`wing_r1` with HARD.
- Ship bolts come from `datatables/projectile/projectile.iff` (index to the bolt's `.prt`, its fire `.cef` and its hit `.cef` per surface; a `.cef` names a `.prt` and a `.snd`) and `datatables/space/ship_weapon_components.iff` (weapon name to projectile index, speed, range). Space bolts are 600 m/s, 512 m, drawn 19 m long ahead of the projectile point in local-space emitters, which `particles.ts` honours; passing effects are placed `transient`.
- A bolt is a thing in flight with a velocity: it carries the shooter's velocity (`inherit`) and its ray leads by its visual reach, so a fast ship's bolts never lag it and a long streak does not poke through what it hits.
- A ship in flight is flown by a cursor the mouse moves under pointer lock (`space/mouseFlight.ts`, `App.flightCursor`), which stays where it is left: inside the aim circle it aims the guns (every gun's bolt crosses under the cursor at the lead's range; within `snapDeg` of the lead the bolts take it exactly) and the ship holds its course; outside it the stick points at the cursor, full at the ring, and `flyShip` holds a given `stickX/stickY` for as long as it is given. A stick that sinks back to the middle when the mouse rests turns a fighter 36 degrees per flick, which is why the owner could never come round onto an NPC. The cursor is measured about the hull's nose from the pilot's eye (`hullRay`), never from the camera: the chase view lags a turn by up to a quarter of a second and looks 0.1 rad under the hull, and measured from it the guns could not reach what the ship was turning after (none of 24 tier-1 TIEs killed in the sim) and bolts left up to 125 degrees off the nose. The HUD draws the circle where the boresight lands on the camera's screen at the guns' range, so in the chase view it slides off the middle during a turn. Every mouse-flight shot is then kept within `SHIP_GUN_CONE` of the nose (`coneClamp`). An NPC's hand (`skillStick`, `PILOT_SKILL` in `pilot.ts`) caps the length of its stick (both sticks were clamped per axis, the player's and the NPCs', so a diagonal was 1.41 of the hull's rate; the cursor's stick is round too) and limits how fast the stick moves; an easing lag there instead sat inside the steering loop and left tier-1 pilots weaving 15 degrees either side of a heading for good. Pulling away from an obstacle, another ship or the ground takes the whole stick at once. Every number of both is invented and live through `__debug.flight`.
- Ships fight in `World.ships` (`ShipContacts`, `src/space/contacts.ts`), a list beside `World.targets()`, not in it: every member is a `Living` (key, side, damage with a source) so blame and retaliation work, but the ground brains and the Force powers never see a hull. The player in any form (on foot, or the ship they fly) is remembered as `PLAYER_KEY`, and `find(PLAYER_KEY)` answers with the ship the player flies now, else the player on foot. A ship's condition is its `ShipCombat` (shields and armour front and back, parts, chassis, from `src/space/shipStats.ts`, whose numbers are all invented), mirrored into `vehicle.hp` so a destroyed ship is what it always was to the rest of the game; its handling starts from the vehicle's own spec, kept from before the combat first wrote it, times the class (a hull over 18 m is big; a smaller one the manifest calls a bomber, except the TIE Advanced, flies at 0.82 of its speed and 0.77 of its turn) and the family's trait (`FAMILY_TRAITS`), so only a stock fighter or big hull of a family with no trait flies exactly as before (the Y-wing is slower, the TIE interceptor and A-wing faster, all invented), and a refit restats from the undamaged numbers, not the spec a downed engine left. A bolt reaches it whole through `Hittable.takeBolt`, where the game's per-layer hit effects (`ship_hit_effects.iff`) are placed at the true hit point in the hull's frame, so they ride the ship; when the combat file has none for the layer struck (or there is no combat file) it answers `'taken'` and the bolt's own burst and hit effect play as before. A combat file that arrives after a ship was adopted is given to it then (`ShipCombat.setEffects`, then a restat). A collision skips the shield (`applyCollision`) and costs the same share of the chassis it cost of `hp` before ships had a fight, the armour taking the first of it (a choice, not the client's), and one collision with another ship takes at most `COLLISION_CAP_SHARE` of the front armour and chassis.
- `DriveInput.stickX`/`stickY` hold flyShip's stick where the player's mouse cursor, an NPC pilot's hand or an autopilot puts it, for as long as it is given (x > 0 turns right, y > 0 noses down, and `steer` > 0 rolls right, the mouse's own senses; `pilot.ts` derives its stick from them) and `DriveInput.cruise` eases the speed toward a wanted one at the spec's accel and brake. `Vehicle.resumeFlight` clears `held` (the held branch of `update` already drops the collision check's memory every held frame, so the first free step is not taken for a hit).
- Every ship attachment hangs under the node it names (its parent's hardpoint, or its parent's origin at its place), a wing under a pivot turned about its own Z, so what rides a wing follows it with nothing copied: guns keep their muzzle node and are read at the shot (`Vehicle.muzzle`), glows are sprites under their hardpoints (made, with their trails, before the ship is prepared, so they are compiled with it) and trails follow the sprites. Each wing opens on its own clock (one shared counter opened four wings four times too fast). The hull colliders under a pivot are moved with it (`setTranslationWrtParent`, `setRotationWrtParent`) while it moves, and the mass sits on a piece that never moves. Hardpoints on parts never set the seat or the cockpit eye (a turret body's `turretcamera1` would). A ship is centred across on the meshes that do not ride a wing and along on the whole box (`frameExtents`): framed on its closed box, the B-wing turned about a point 7 m out along its flat wing, and centred along on its hull alone, the X-wing's turn point moved 0.77 m forward (its foils' engines reach behind the fuselage). A vehicle is prepared (`World.vehiclePrepare`: material schemes, textures, then a compile a drawable at a time) before `new Vehicle`; `World.breath` is the yield between steps (a MessageChannel when the tab is hidden), and a hidden tab compiles with `compile`, not `compileAsync`, whose link poll is a chained timer a hidden tab holds to one a minute. A travel during that wait is caught by the Terrain changing (`spawnVehicle`), and the vehicle is disposed rather than left in the next world.
- A ship converted with its fit (`fit` in its manifest entry, resolved against `components.json` by `shipFit.ts`; a pack without `components.json` is built as its stock tree) hangs its components from the fit, never from `attachments`: the garage's `assemble` drops every `kind: 'component'` def, every def with `owner` (what a stock component's own client data produced) and the stand-in of a slot the fit fills, and a def whose parent was dropped is hung by name after the fit's parts (`fitTree` in `shipMounts.ts`, tested on the X-wing's and the YT-1300's trees). The fit's parts go through `hangParts` (`shipMounts.ts`): by hardpoint name, the hull's own first, then anywhere on the ship, in repeated passes, never at the origin; each part's `children` whose chain ends at the part are hung on it with `hangAttachments`, and the ones with no `parent` (the Y-wing engine's "on" appearance on the hull's `engine1`) are pending parts marked `byName`, which `hangParts` hangs over the whole ship in a second round once every fitted part is on, never with `hangAttachments(part, …)`, which only looks on the part. `ShipBuild.fitParts` keeps each slot's top nodes (`''` for the ship's own by-name nodes), so a refit takes a slot down and re-hangs whatever rode it; `ShipBuild.standIns` keeps each empty-slot stand-in (the YT-1300's `yt1300_engine_none`), which every swap decides again (`settleStandIns`: down when a part of its slot hangs, up again when none does).
- Ship paint renders take 50 to 800 ms each, so they run in a worker of their own (`renderPaint` in `paintRender.ts`, a renderer of `src/player/recipeWorker.ts`, whose worker is `recipeWorker.worker.ts`) through the customizer's `renderOff` hook, never on the main thread. A ship wears the GLB's own materials until it is first given custom paint; then it gets its own copies (`ShipPaint`), prepared on stand-in meshes and compiled (the program is found, not built) before they go on, and a repaint swaps `map`, and on a glowing paint shader `emissiveMap` as well, between textures on the same channel (the glow is split from the painted colour in the worker, `glowSplit.ts`, byte for byte the converter's split, with the mask from the texture the pattern chose; a glow map is never added to a copy that has none). The copies leave the world's material sets through the paint's `forget` (`World.forgetMaterials`) when the ship goes or returns to stock. A render that lands after the paint changed course is dropped by a generation check made in the same step the texture would go on (`Customizer.accept`).
- A refit (`Garage.refit`, `World.refitVehicle`) stages the new parts, with spare glow sprites and trails for any engine spots the new look can bring (a part's, or the hull's thrusters when a slot is emptied) and any stand-in the slots may need, in a detached group, prepares them in their own materials (what a part wears whenever the paint is or goes back to stock: prepared only in the copies, a part would compile on a live frame when the paint went back), then lets the paint track them (which puts the ship's copies on while it is custom) and prepares again if that changed any material, and waits for their paint (`stageRefit`). One vehicle's refits run one after another (`inTurn` in `World.refitVehicle`), each staged from the fit the last one left, since two staged from the same fit leave the model and `v.fit` disagreeing. Then in one synchronous step (`refitSwap`) it moves every glow sprite to the vehicle's group, takes the old parts down and re-hangs what rode them, hangs the new ones, measures the guns and engine spots again with the wings closed (`collectMounts`, in the group's frame) and re-hangs the glows (`rebindGlows`: surplus sprites parked, hidden, under the group, their trails aged out; `v.engines` and `v.trails` rebuilt in place, so the heat shimmer follows). Colliders, bounds, seat and cockpit are not rebuilt. A fitted ship's gun fires its own slot's component (`gunWeapon`); a gun whose slot fires nothing is left out. Vehicles are prepared with `World.prepareVehicle`, not `prepareActor`, which would make glass, panes and cockpit frames cast shadows. `__debug.refit` and `__debug.paint` report `compiledOnSwap`, which must be 0.
- The ship edit page's preview (`shipPreview.ts`) has a WebGL context of its own, made the first time the page opens, stopped while it is shut, and made again on a fresh canvas after three ships (only a lost context frees the textures it uploaded; a texture's `dispose()` would free the world renderer's copy too, so it never disposes a geometry or a garage material). It compiles a drawable at a time with a breath between, visible drawables only, synchronously in a hidden tab, as `World.compileReady` does. Compiling a garage material the world set up for its cascades in a second context would take CSM's one shader record per material from the world's program (the cascades' uniforms would then move only in the preview's), so each compile is wrapped in `World.keepShadowRecords`. `__debug.refit` and `__debug.paint` count as `compiledOnSwap` only programs made for the ship's own materials, since a gun change also warms its new bolt's effects on the same frames.
- The map window has one WebGL context for both of its picture tabs: the single canvas is moved between the space view's body and the galaxy's as the tabs change (a canvas keeps its context across a move), each tab sizes the renderer to its own holder on the frame it draws (its two list tabs, Waypoints and Group, never touch it: see the lesson on the map's tabs), and the galaxy takes the mouse through a transparent layer over the canvas so the space view's own listeners never see a galaxy drag. Where a system hangs in the galaxy is not in the archives at all: there is a picture of the galaxy (`texture/galaxy_map.dds`) and a kiosk hologram, but nothing that places a world on either, and `system_locations` is five placeholder rows. So the squares in `src/data/galaxy.ts` are canon, the layout round them is ours, and the only game data the galaxy draws is the shuttle routes, read from the pack's `galaxy.json` and never typed into the code. A jump into a system picks that world's STATION before any other kind of place: a zone lists its hyperspace points before its stations and the game names some points after a neighbouring world (Corellia's fifth point carries Talus's name), so the first place that merely names the world is the wrong one by kilometres.
- Wings open whenever a ship flies with room under them, and a chassis's `wing_open_speed_factor` (0.95 on the X-wing, advanced X-wing, B-wing and V-wing, 1 elsewhere) is taken as its top speed's share while they are open (with W up too: a cruise between the open top and the closed one eases down at the brake; only a boost's coast stays above it, as it always did); the client's own use of it is not in the archives, and the other reading (open only under that share of top speed) is `WING_RULE` in `wings.ts`, flipped live by `__debug.wings('threshold')`. In this game that reading would keep the wings shut in ordinary flight: holding W pins the cruise at top and it stays there. The wings key (the `wings` action, U by default; `__debug.wings('toggle')`) puts the pilot's own choice over the rule (`WingSet.pilot`, `pilotWings`): closed is closed, open is open at any speed and on the ground too, except that a wing swinging below the belly on a planet still waits for the room and folds on landing. A press flips the choice itself, not where the wings are (`toggle`), so an open still waiting for room can be taken back, and the prompt shows the choice (`WingSet.chosen`). The choice holds until the key is pressed again or the pilot leaves the seat (`dropPilotChoices`, every frame, over every ship not flown), and it crosses the relay as the wings' target does (`veh.w`). On a planet a wing that swings below the belly waits for that much air plus half a metre, with a metre's band (the B-wing's 15 m), and the ground easing and the slow-flight hold measure the wing as it hangs (`wingBelow`) while the crash stays on the belly. **Two more hands are on the wings, and one pure function holds the order of all of them** (`wingWant` in `wings.ts`, which `updateWings` and `launch` both ask and nothing else weighs): the console's `force`, then `dockHold` (shut), then the pilot's choice, then an NPC pilot's `brain`, both of those through `pilotWings` so a low wing on a planet still waits for room, then the flight rule. A second copy of that order anywhere is the flutter bug again. `brain` is written by the NPC brain on every thought (`NpcWingHand`: shut on patrol, open on the think that takes a target, shut `WING_AUTO.closeAfter` seconds after its last fight, ours at 3 where the game used nought, which stops a fighter between two targets flapping), set shut before the spawn's `launch` snaps the wings so a patrol arrives with them shut, never cleared by `dropPilotChoices` (which runs over every ship nobody flies, every frame, and so could never have carried an NPC's choice in `pilot`), and never sent. `dockHold` is written every frame over every hull from the dock's own state (`Docking.holdsWings`: the dock's ship in any phase but idle, and a hull carried in a clamp), so a launch, a break-off, a lost hull or a travel cannot leave it stuck, and letting go hands the wings back to the pilot's choice or the rule, which is the game's "reopen only if they were open" in our terms. A held hull never stepped its wings (`holdOn` returns before `updateWings`), so a hull parked straight at a dock or eased onto a carrier kept them open; it steps them now while a dock holds it, and under no other hold. A ship a dock stands at its dock outright (`Docking.park`: a crew back from going aboard what it was docked at, or the console's park) has them snapped shut there (`Vehicle.snapWings`), since the launch that brought the hull out across the loading screen snapped them open by the flight rule and the hold would otherwise fold them in front of the player as the screen lifts; and a snap is never heard, because the wings' sound skips a turn of `target` that finds the set already standing at it (`stepWings`, through `progress`). The game also refused fire with the wings shut, which the owner chose not to take. `__debug.wings({ auto: { npc: 'flight' } })` puts the NPCs back on the flight rule, open the whole time in space.
- The converter writes normal maps exactly as the game has them and the game reads the green as it stands, `normalScale (1, 1)`. It was `(1, -1)` for years, on the reasoning that the game's maps are Direct3D's (green down) and the glTF loader flips a map without tangents; by eye on a wall's edges that is the wrong way up, and the owner's call is that it is not the flip that is wrong but the whole chain of reasoning about where the flip happens. `__debug.normals(1, -1)` puts the old way back live to compare.
- **A surface is its colour texture times a detail texture, at a coordinate set of its own.** All twenty-six of the client's detail programs are the same line -- `result.rgb = diffuseColor * detailColor * light`, or with a specular map `diffuseColor * allDiffuseLight * detailColor + allSpecularLight` -- so it multiplies the **diffuse** term and never the specular, which is what multiplying the base colour does in three. It reads **coordinate set 1**: every detail vertex program writes `#define textureCoordinateSetDETA textureCoordinateSet1`, and the effect file says so in its own bytes (the first byte of each pass's PTXM chunk is the set index, 0 on MAIN, 1 on DETA, 2 on SPEC). That set is **not** the main one scaled -- measured over the 12,632 retail vertex arrays belonging to a detail shader, only 60.7% fit `u1 = a*u0 + b` and the rest miss by up to 137 texture units -- so it is carried (`msh.mjs` keeps `uvs2`, `glb.mjs` writes `TEXCOORD_1`, GLTFLoader names it `uv1`) and a per-material tiling factor would have been wrong on four surfaces in ten. 1,369 shaders over 281 images; Tatooine alone is 297 surfaces on 36% of its placed objects. Getting `uv1` into a three material is the awkward part: three decides `USE_UV1` in `getParameters`, **before** `onBeforeCompile` runs, so an injection cannot ask for it -- but `customDefines` is emitted at the top of the vertex prefix and the `#ifdef USE_UV1 / attribute vec2 uv1;` block a hundred and fifty lines below it, so `material.defines.USE_UV1 = ''` is enough and no decoy ao map is needed. The injection is `src/world/detailMap.ts`, shaped like `injectWetness` and wrapped in the same pass; a material naming a detail map with **no** second set under it is written nothing at all, because an unset attribute reads as zero and would sample one texel over the whole surface.
- **How much of a surface is a mirror is a second mask, and it is not always in the colour texture's alpha.** The client's line is `result.rgb = lerp(diffuseLitSurface, envColor, envMask) + allSpecularLight`, so `envMask` is metalness in three's terms with the scene's environment behind it. Measured over the 1,400 retail shaders with a reflection cube (before SOE's final names list, which brings 1,407): **981 keep the mask in MAIN's alpha, 378 in the MASK slot's alpha, 25 in SPEC's, and 16 cannot be read**. On the whole two-tone customizable family (`h_color2_envmask_*`) MAIN's alpha is the **hue** mask -- a shape mask with nothing to do with reflection -- so reading it there made an armour piece mirror-bright wherever its second colour fell, and on 600 more the *gloss map* was standing in for it, which makes a hull shiny instead of chrome. `envMaskOf` (`surface.mjs`) reads it off the pixel program the way `emissiveOf` reads the glow's, in the four shapes the archives use: the plain `envMask = tex2D(x, uv).a`, the one that reuses a local name (the hue programs declare `sample` twice), the assembly `lrp r0, t0.a, t1, r0` whose t1 is the cube, and that same lerp with the mask taken through a register (`mov r0, t0` first). Roughness and metalness are now two textures sampled into one image. And **IRID is a cube under another name**: the fourteen iridescent shaders (the chitin armour) name theirs IRID and are drawn by the very same envmask program with that slot on its `envMap` sampler, so every test for a reflection has to accept both names or that family is the one reflective thing in the game with no reflection at all.
- **A normal map is decoded by the slot it sits in, and a surface's shine is the mask its own program multiplies its specular by** (material format 6: `surfaceReaders`, `normalReadOf`, `specMaskOf` and `materialOf` in `tools/swg/surface.mjs`, the decoder in `src/swg/normalDecode.ts`). Over all 22,322 retail shaders (measured before SOE's final names list, which brings 22,902) every program that reads a CNRM slot decodes the compressed layout (x in the alpha, y in the green, z rebuilt) and every one that reads NRML plain signed RGB, so the slot alone decides; the converter guessed from the picture instead (an alpha that varied more than the red was taken for x), and an uncompressed map keeps the height it was made from in its alpha, so 1,157 used shaders -- 97 of Tatooine's 114 bumped ones -- were lit about 33 degrees off, and the recolour path (`recipeNormal`) repeated the guess over every recoloured worn piece until it too decoded by the tag it already had. The mask is the texture and channel the program names (SPEC's **alpha**, not its luminance; the normal map's alpha on the two-tone cloth, whose colour alpha is the hue mask; MASK's red where a float is set from `.rgb`), written into the metal-rough image's **red**, which three never reads; green stays the roughness from our own 0.85 slope because reflections and the rain's wet look read it; a mask of one everywhere writes no image. Five things cost time. A pixel program is an IFF and its text is the PSRC chunk, whose first line (`//hlsl`) is the only thing that says it is HLSL (`programSource`); read raw, the binary header hides it. In assembly the specular is `v1` and the specular constants followed through the registers, and a lookup (a `texm3x2tex` result, an LKUP table) is part of the specular and never its mask, or the aniso tables read as masks. A program that multiplies the mask in twice (`allSpecularLight * specularMask` after the raw term already has it) is `squared`, and a ps 1.1 one that applies it once where the specular is added is not. A shader whose program reads a SPEC slot it names no file for is drawn with a mask of one (what an unbound sampler gives) and logged. And the metal-rough image is named for its masks (`glbImages`), since two shaders on one colour texture whose programs read their shine from two places were one image in a GLB. What the highlight is goes on every textured material as `extras.swgSpec` (mode `phong`, `aniso` where the program calls the client's band function, or `none`; the MATL's fourth group as the colour and its seventeenth float as the power) and `extras.swgCube`, **never under `extras.swg`**: a transparent material with `userData.swg` is drawn after the water, and the surfaces plugin walks every material of a model once one carries `extras.swg`. A material with no cube has `metallicFactor` 0. A normal map the shader's TCSS puts on set 1 is drawn on `uv1` (on 139 of the 324 vertex arrays that draw such a shader set 1 is not set 0 scaled), with the plain copy for a primitive without the set; a mask or normal map on a set the models do not carry (NRML 2, MASK 2 and 3, SPEC 1) is drawn on the main's and counted, as is a program that cannot be read (the 17 used shaders with none keep the old rule). `player`, `parts` and `species` finally stamp `materialFormat` (and `creatures`, `space`, `sandbox`, `spawns` and `travel` with them), and `status` also reads the first textured model each of the three lists for `extras.swgSpec` out of its JSON chunk (`glbSpecStamped`, `glbsCurrent`): a stamp a later command copied forward must not pass for a new model. The mobiles carry the format in their models' and wearables' signatures (`m`) and not in `MOBILES_FORMAT`, which would rebake every animation pack for nothing; and since a catalogue planned before holds the old signatures, which its units' records still match, `status` reads the catalogue's own `materialFormat` to know a run would find them stale. Four more were found in review. **Every pass of the implementation is read, each through its own samplers** (`passState`'s `passes`, `firstPassThat`): a blended surface draws its colour in one pass and adds its highlight in a second, so read off the first pass alone 76 used shaders -- most of the long hair, the glass, the lenses, the bacta gels -- came out drawing no highlight at all, and one membrane lost its normal map; one light-pass program reads its mask from SPEC under one effect and from MASK under another, which is why the samplers are per pass (the mirror and the glow are never only in a later pass, measured, and stay the first pass's). **A skinned shader's normal map takes its slot from the plain path and its file from the run's own values** (`skinnedTexture`): the plain path describes a customizable shader by its base shader's files, not its choices, and the human male's face's base names the fourth wrinkle map while its default age chooses the first. **A command that keeps a model because its file is there must ask what the record it wrote last time stood for** (`materialStale`; travel's `travelModelsToConvert`, the spawns' `nestModels`), or the run a stamp asked for writes the stamp over the very models it was asked to redo and nothing asks again; the spawns stamp only from a run that made its models. And in `status` nothing may sit between an `if` and its `else if`: the snapshot's surface counts were tallied in an `if` of their own there and took the detail levels' `else` for themselves. Every test of it decodes pixels out of a GLB the converter's own readers wrote (`normals.test.ts`, `specMask.test.ts`, run on any pack folder through `SWG3JS_PACKS`). Measured after the pass's one conversion: every model a pack names was written again except five on Tatooine that the snapshot did not rewrite (the cantina's far level, Watto's junk shop and its two pieces, the Lucky Despot's engine debris and two house pieces, last written 2026-09-07). **What skipped them is the manifest, not a model**: the snapshot read the manifest it found and kept every category in it, so the old `pack` command's `structures`, `rocks`, `debris` and `vaporators` rode along on Tatooine under a `materialFormat` the run never made true of them, and those five (seven counting the far levels) are theirs; nothing with a layout draws them (the scatter loads rocks and debris and draws them only on a world with no flora). The snapshot now keeps only the categories it makes (`snapshotCategories` in `tools/swg/worldManifest.mjs`: `layout` and `flora`) and says which it dropped, `status` asks for the snapshot while a world's manifest carries any other, and `worldStamps.test.ts` holds every textured model a world's layout and flora name, and their lower levels, to `glbSpecStamped` (7,691 over the nineteen worlds once SOE's final files were converted, 7,656 before, every one stamped) and names a world still carrying a stray category, which on an install converted before the fix is Tatooine until its snapshot runs again; a pack folder also holds files no manifest names any more (793 in one wardrobe folder), which the tests step over by the stamp rather than read as a writer's fault.
- **A surface's highlight is the client's own line, put in place of three's GGX, and three's lights are worked out in functions that cannot see `main()`** (`src/world/specular.ts`, its arithmetic mirrored line for line in `src/world/specMath.ts`). The client drew the specular colour times the mask times (N·H) to the power, with no Fresnel term and no N·L factor; on its fake-anisotropic programs a band of N·H instead (one less the distance of 1.25 N·H from one, to the power of 0.15 p, less 0.15, never below nought), times N·L, which peaks at N·H 0.8 at 0.85 of the colour: a ring 37 degrees off the mirror direction, which no GGX roughness can make. Its highlight was measured against a white surface facing the light, which is the light over pi in three, so the term is divided by pi and a colour of 0.5 is half a sunlit white wall. The injection expands `#include <lights_physical_pars_fragment>` and replaces three 0.185's one line in `RE_Direct_Physical` (`irradiance * BRDF_GGX_Multiscatter(...)`), or nothing at all if three no longer has it. **The mask and the wet amounts are read in `main()` into globals**, because `RE_Direct_Physical` is defined above `main()` and three's `texelRoughness` (whose red is the client's mask, which three never reads) and the wet chunk's `wetSurface`/`wetPuddle` are `main()`'s locals; the wet chunk now says `#define SWG_WET` with its declarations so the highlight can ask. A wet or snowed surface keeps three's GGX by the largest of how wet it is, its puddle and the snow on it (`film`, `weatherShare`), since the rain's glint and the snow's matt both ride on roughness, which the client's term never reads: with the rain alone in it, every phong roof under settled snow kept a highlight brighter than the snow beside it. The mode, colour and power are per-material uniforms kept in a `WeakMap` so a recompile reuses them, and the knobs are shared by reference (`SPEC_UNIFORMS`), so every converted material shares one program text under the key prefix `spec`, put on last of the four in `World.adoptMaterials` (cascades, wet, detail, highlight) and only on a standard material carrying `userData.swgSpec`: a pack from before format 6 draws three's GGX exactly as before, and mode `none` (every leaf, every shader whose program has no specular) draws no highlight rather than three's. Every base is held above nought before `pow` (N·H below nought, a power of nought, a light behind the eye), since a NaN pixel is a black box once the bloom spreads it. Ours: the cap at a white surface facing the light (the client clamped every pixel to white and an HDR frame does not; glass's colour of 10 would bloom), `gate`, `film` and the live switch back to GGX (`__debug.spec({ use: 'three' })`, a uniform); `localStorage['swg.spec'] = 'three'` skips the wrap at load, because a program that fails to compile draws nothing. **A surface whose shader has no reflection cube takes none of the environment** (`userData.swgCube === false`: `envMapIntensity` times `REFLECTIONS.plain`, nought): in three 0.185 an environment map also lights the diffuse of the material that carries it, so a registered plain wall wore a faint mirror and a sky-coloured fill its matt neighbours lacked, neither of which the client drew. It stays registered with its map, so its program does not change and `__debug.reflections({ plain: 1 })` compares live; **a copy of a registered material is registered with its original's own strength** (`registerReflective(copy, original)`, the interior cells' copies), because once an environment is in force the original's field holds what the registry wrote into it, nought on a plain wall, so read off the copy every room's plain walls stayed at nought under `plain: 1` and every mirroring copy took the scale twice. **A preview wraps what the world has not adopted** (`specWrapTree` in `CharacterPreview.refresh`, `specWrap` in `ShipPreview`'s prepare): the select screen and the creator's dark stage never reach the world's scan, so their figure wore three's GGX on a fresh session and the client's once the rig had been played; the world builds nothing more for it, since the cascades replace the hook and the scan wraps it again under the same key. `__debug.specSelfTest()` draws a lit sphere through the injected program against `specMath.ts`, the switch to GGX against an unwrapped material (building no program), and the weather's film through a stand-in for the wet chunk, and builds four programs of its own, so it runs after `shaders().builtInPlay` is read, never before.
- **What is worn, held, flown and ridden, and the creatures, reflect the world's sky only where their shader has a cube, and they join the registry as the world adopts them, never as they load** (`joinModelReflections` in `src/world/envmap.ts`, called at the first sight of a material in `World.adoptMaterials`, which reaches everything else the world loads outside a planet's own pack as well: a door, a shuttle, a camp's tents). Nothing outside `AssetPack` registered before, so the worn-side shaders with a cube (wardrobe 150, mobiles 77, characters 9, creatures 5), 100 weapons' and 45 ships' drew their chrome dark. The environment is in three's program key, and adoption is the one step every path that loads something in play takes before it compiles (`prepareActor`, `prepareVehicle`, `prepareExtras`, `compileReady`, the streamer, the sweeps), so a model is built with its reflection once, behind whatever prepares it; `modelReflections.test.ts` reads every one of those paths and the hooks the game hands its loaders. A plain shader and a pack from before format 6 are left out (neither reflected before), and a planet's own material is never made a model's (its pack registered it). **A copy takes its original's strength wherever it is made** (a ship's paint, a clear pane), because `registerReflective` keeps the strength on the material's userData, which `clone()` and a spread both carry: read off the copy's own field it took whatever the registry had last written into the original, nought while the models were switched off. **The previews draw the same materials in WebGL contexts of their own, and swap a studio of their own onto them around each of their own draws and compiles** (`EnvironmentSwap`, `src/ui/previewStudio.ts`: the doll, the select screen, the creator, the ship edit page), putting the world's back in a `finally` and setting nothing that asks three for a program again: a texture the world's renderer filtered is nothing in another context, and a swap left standing across an await would hand the world's renderer the preview's. **A swap takes each material once, however many meshes wear it** (`take` steps over one already carrying its studio, which nothing but the swap ever puts on a material): a material the world never registered -- a figure on the select screen, a hull never spawned this session, a preview's own paint copies -- has no strength but its own field, which the first meeting writes, so taken at every meeting four guns cloned from one part file drew at a sixteenth of their share where the swap meant half, and the same armour previewed dimmer before it had been played than after. The doll makes its studio through `StudioChoice` (asked once a clone, made once). The studio is filtered from a 128 px cube like every environment the world hands out, so a program keyed with either is keyed alike. The ship edit page gives its room to a format-6 hull's plain parts at `plain` (nought), as the world gives them its sky, where it used to light every material with it as the scene's environment; an older pack's still takes it that way. **Every registered material carries a black stand-in while the world has none** (`setEnvironmentStandIn`, the water's own, handed over at boot): with none, a world going took the environment out of every registered program, which cost nothing for a planet's own materials, unloaded with it, and built a program on a live frame for anything carried across -- the hull a jump carries to another system, and its crew's armour, are drawn all through the unload, inside the tunnel. Black reflects and fills nothing, so it draws exactly what no environment drew, and the next sky is a texture swapped in. `__debug.reflections({ models: false })` writes the models at nought live (a uniform), and `localStorage['swg.reflections.models'] = '0'` keeps them out of the registry at load, which is exactly how they were drawn before.
- **A leaf card is drawn from its front, casts from both sides and is never glass, and the rule is keyed by the shader the card wears, not by the model it is on** (`src/world/cardRule.ts`, `cardAlpha.ts`, `cardBacks.ts`; the converter's `tools/swg/cardBleed.mjs` and the flora command's card table). The client drew a card from one side (the owner saw a lone card vanish from behind), so a card material is `FrontSide` with `shadowSide` `DoubleSide` (a lone card facing the sun must still throw a shadow), cut out at a half and never blended (three sorts a blended crown per object and draws far over near), with no vertex colours. Its class is the client's own pass, written by the converter on the material as `extras.swgCard` (`blend` where the pass blends and writes no depth, and the MATL's emissive) and into the flora manifest as `cards`, which is how a placed model, a guest pack's or a baked place wearing the same shader is found and ruled the same (`CARD_TUNE.scope` 'shaders'; a baked place is ruled after its world's pack has loaded, `World.preparePlace`, since the table comes with the pack). **The table is every world's at once** (`flora-cards.json` beside the packs, which the flora command merges each world's rows into, read once a session before the first pack and the world's own `cards` over it, `registerSharedCards`): a world's placed trees can wear a shader only another world plants (all eight card-wearing placed models of Kashyyyk's southern dungeons wear one the main zone plants), and known by each world's own table alone they were ruled only once a world that plants it had been visited that session; `cardRule.test` holds every world's placed card models known at its own load in a fresh session. **A model a world both plants and places loads as its flora** (`AssetPack.defFor`; `isFlora` by id): 103 ids are filed under both, the placed entry first, and loaded by that entry every planting of them -- Kashyyyk's wroshyrs and trillium palms among them -- drew the snapshot's GLB, unbled and unstamped, and stood on a guessed cylinder, since the client's collision shapes are merged onto the flora's defs; the two entries are one appearance at the same bounds and levels. A pack converted before the table guesses the class from the name (`_aa<n>` blended): right on 327 of 329 shaders, wrong on the Dathomir leaf `leaf_dath_rinor_aa7` (cut out) and Kashyyyk's cone flower `kashyyyk_floor_cone_flower_as8` (blended). A blended card's alpha is raised by its texture's level (`mipScale` 0.1, the floor inside the `log2`, or the alpha is a NaN `blackBox` cannot see), so a far crown stays full, and rain darkens a card and never glosses it. `canopy` came out of the converter's glass words: it had made Naboo's and Rori's tree canopies and a Corellian crystal tree glass that cast no shadow, which is the difference between the forest floor in shade and in sun. **The client lit a two-sided card with one normal and three lights each face by its own**, so a coplanar pair of sheets sharing normals, drawn from the front, came out lit on one face and black on the other: the back sheet is copied with its normal reflected through each vertex's own sheet plane (`mirrorCardGeometry`; the plane rides as `cardPlane`, Int8 normalised, w 127 where mirrored, so the stored normal is one reflection away for `backs: 'stored'` and `sunSide: 'stored'`), 0.26 MB and 22 ms on the busiest world. A geometry another material also draws, or one with morphs or no normals, is left as it is with a plane of nought, and every card geometry carries the attribute, because `defaultAttributeValues` guards only a program's first draw. **The converter bleeds every cut-out card picture's clear texels with the leaf round them** (pull-push, alpha-weighted; alpha and every kept texel byte for byte), because every planted leaf is a leaf on black and the browser's box mips pulled the black into every edge. The bled picture goes into the GLB under a name of its own (`#bled`): a GLB embeds one image per name and a flora model's bark often shares its leaves' texture, so under the texture's own name whichever material came first decided the other's picture (four card pictures went unbled and 72 barks drew a bled one). Measured on a pilot conversion of all seventeen flora packs, 328 pictures, in linear light: the kept texels' brightness at mip 3 rose from a median 0.892 of mip 0's to 0.923 (the tenth darkest 0.658 to 0.739), and their error against the leaf under them fell from 0.127 to 0.082 (tenth worst 0.343 to 0.199). "No picture's mip-3 brightness falls" is not true and is not what is tested: 97 pictures were lighter at mip 3 than at mip 0 because their clear texels held a light fill, and the bleed takes that halo away as it takes the black one; what is held is that the edge error grows on no picture by more than a tenth (worst 0.073). `status` asks for the flora again while a flora GLB's card material lacks `extras.swgCard`, read out of the GLB and never off a stamp, and while the table beside the packs lacks a shader any world's own names. `localStorage['swg.cards'] = 'three'` skips the rule at load and `swg.cardBacks = '0'` the mirror.
- **A leaf card is lit by the client's own sum, carried back through three's tone curve so that it shows the client's bytes, and no tree shades its own leaves** (`src/world/cardLight.ts`, its mirror `cardLightMath.ts`, the lookups `leafShadow.ts`). The client lit a card by a ramp about the sun's axis -- the front, tangent and back rows of the area's environment, read as bytes -- plus the ambient, the card's emissive and two plain parallel lights, the sum held to one, and multiplied the texel by it on the screen. The game puts three's own sun and fill out on a card (`swgCardSunOff` before each directional `RE_Direct`), takes the ambient alone for its indirect light (no hemisphere), and adds that colour back in scene light through the inverse of three's ACES fit (`curve: 'client'`, held under the bloom's threshold by `bloomCap` 0.84), so after tone mapping a card shows the client's bytes: the carry is exact to a thousandth of a byte, the browser draws it within a tenth of a level (`cardLightSelfTest`), and through each world's own colour grade a card's brightness lands within 4.3 bytes of the client's (the grade's mood moves a hue by up to 14, which is the grade's business). The cascades' `lights_fragment_begin` makes the directional lookup at five sites (the fade lookup, no fade, the shadows-off single light, the unshadowed loop, no cascades) and every one is rewritten or none is (`cardLightsBegin`); the no-fade line must be replaced before the fade lookup, whose text is a suffix of it, or one `replace` eats the other. The shadow the front row is lowered by is captured with the cascades' own fade weights (`swgSunShadow`). A card's shadow offset is pushed toward the light rather than along its normal (minus row 2 of the cascade's matrix), because a mirrored back's normal faces away. Under a point light a card turns `pointFlat` (0.5) of its normal toward the face the screen sees, or the torch lit one face of a pair and not the other. **A card standing in a building's rooms takes the rooms' light**: a potted plant wears its model's own materials wherever the model stands, so the define cannot tell it from the same plant out of doors; each rooms pass puts the card light out for its own draws and back after (`PortalRenderer.roomPass`, `setCardRoomPass`), a shared uniform three uploads anew at every pass's first draw of a material, so nothing compiles and nothing is per object (a card of a building's own cells has no define at all). **The leaf skip** (`shadow: 'self'`): a card's lookup has its bias lowered by how far its ray toward the sun stays inside its own crown's box (`crownExit`, metres over the cascade's range) and by a floor of twice the bias times `acne`, so its own tree never shades it and a wall 30 m beyond still does; the box is over the card vertices of every level (277 lower-level sprite meshes stood outside the finest crown) and grows with the sway. `'none'` takes receiving off the card meshes of trees that cast (a per-object uniform, nothing compiles), decided by the batch's finest-level cast rule, never the live `castShadow`. `swg.cardLight = '0'` and `swg.leafShadow = 'all'` are the hatches at load.
- **Flora sway is one field over every primitive of a model, from its own canopy base, and its shadow sways with it** (`src/world/floraSway.ts`; the chunk, its mirror and the laws in `swayMath.ts`; all ours). Deciding by material would tear a leaf off its branch, so every vertex of a swaying model bends by ((h - c)/(1 - c))² from c, the 5th percentile of its card heights over its height (nought for a model of cards alone, which bends from the ground): the visible trunk below the crown stays still, and a flutter weighted off the trunk's axis and radius moves the leaves. Which models sway is decided at the finest level -- a card there or a soft name, and no rigid one, in the words the measurement used (`grss`, `log` only as a word of its own, `crystl`) -- 269 of the 455 flora models; two Tatooine succulents whose lowest level is a card are left out. Card positions are welded to a millimetre when measured (the mirror's copies stand on their originals) and opaque ones counted as the model has them, which puts the canopy base, axis and trunk radius on the study's own to 0.03, 2 mm and 2%. Nothing in the chunk reads `cameraPosition` or `viewMatrix`, which in a shadow pass are the cascade's: the fade reads `uSwayEye`. The clock wraps at an hour and every frequency is a whole number of cycles in it. **The planting's phase hash must be well conditioned**: the common form multiplies two numbers near 70 and 140 and takes the fraction of ten thousand, where a float keeps three decimals, so a GPU's fused multiply-add moved a storm 3 mm off its mirror in the browser self-test; the fraction is now taken before the last product. **Every swaying mesh casts with a `MeshDepthMaterial` of its own** (`customDepthMaterial`, one per source material, the same chunk and uniform objects, one program key; three uses a custom depth material as it stands and copies the side, the cut and the picture onto it at each shadow draw), and three things about those programs were learned in the browser and are the reason `World.compileShadowDepth` draws rather than compiles. Three draws the shadow maps *before* it sets up the lights of the pass that asked for them (`WebGLRenderer.render`, `shadowMap.render` then `setupLights`), so a depth program is keyed on the light set of the last pass drawn with that scene -- the world's outdoors, a room's wherever a building's rooms are drawn after it, which is in and beside every town -- with the cascades still casting; compiled through the shadow camera they came out keyed on every layer's lights at once, a set no frame draws with. A depth material is unlit, so three gives it its program at its first draw and never changes it with the lights; the frames drawn between the loading screen's batches have already drawn the models' own. So the warm-up sets up each pass's lights by a compile of nothing under its layers and draws one stand-in per combination (instancing, the side the shadow is drawn from, the cut, the picture and its set, the second sets) into the shadow maps through the portal renderer's own shadow-only draw (`drawShadowsOnly`, its strays dropped), each light set through depth materials of its own kept for the session (`depthTwin`), and puts the world pass's lights back after, or the next frame keys every depth material it draws first on the probe's. The combinations drawn are the scene's own and also every one a swaying material can cast with (`swayDepthCombos`: instanced or not, the shadow from both sides or the back, plain, pictured or cut out), since a placed tier streamed in after the loading screen could bring one nothing at the arrival had; three keys a depth program by the picture's coordinate set (`mapUv`) and never by whether the geometry carries a second set, and the converted packs' swaying meshes come to two combinations in all, which `floraSway.test` holds inside the fixed twelve. A plant standing in a room stands in no wind: each rooms pass turns the sway's switch off for its own draws (`setSwayRoomPass`). Measured over the same flow with the rules on and off -- Kashyyyk's placed forest, a travel to Naboo, its forest and its town -- the programs built in play were 0, 43, 59 and 63 against 0, 45, 59 and 63, none of them a card's or the sway's (the rest are the people's looks and three's own depth materials, which were already so); frame time 4.19 against 4.20 ms with the sway's amplitude at nought; the ground's dapples changed over 21% of a top-down view as the sway clock moved with the sun held, against 0.01% with it frozen. A geometry's bounding sphere is grown once by the model's largest excursion as it loads, and the leaf box with it. The sway wrap is the chain's last and lands before the card's leaf capture at the same anchor, so the leaf skip reads the swayed place, as does the rain's insert after `project_vertex`. It ships on; `swg.sway = '0'` skips it at load and `__debug.sway({ on: false })` holds it still live.

- **The terrain's own grass is its radial flora, read and planted the client's way and drawn by one program of ours, and the far hills' cards are the same file's far ring** (`RadialGroup` in `src/swg/terrain/flora.ts`, `AffectorFloraDynamic` in `generator.ts`, the placing in `src/world/grassMath.ts`, the drawing in `src/world/grass.ts`; the `radial` pass in `tools/swg/radial.mjs`, its file in `src/swg/terrain/radialFile.ts`). The radial group is `RGRP` beside the static flora's group (version `0004` on every retail world, `0000` to `0003` read too): an `RFAM` a family, each child a shader with its weight, a distance, width and height bounds, whether it keeps its aspect, its sway, displacement and period, whether it aligns to the ground, and whether it is drawn as two quads crossed (`createPlus`, an int32 like the rest, read and drawn: five retail children carry it, Corellia's and Naboo's starflower and Endor's and Yavin 4's blue fern). It is planted by `AFDN` (near) and `AFDF` (far), whose versions are numbered differently from the static `AFSC`/`AFSN` (0001 an operation and remove-all, 0002 a density override) and whose arithmetic is theirs exactly (the pole's coordinate hash seeding the engine's fast generator, its first float under amount times density, an add setting family and choice, a replace removing one family), into two maps of their own beside the static flora's, which the worker hands back with the heights. **Roads and rivers clear all four flora maps** (`clearFlora`, within a road's flat half-width less its feather share and a river's bed), the client's own rule, behind `TERRAIN_GROUND_TUNE.roadsClearFlora`, which the workers take at init: on the blocks round the roads and rivers of seven worlds our trees where the client's own baked flora map has none fall from 135 to 78, exactly the research's count, and not one of the client's own is lost (`roadFlora.test.ts`). A tuft is placed one a near tile (the tiling is the file's own: 1 m tiles on ten of the sixteen worlds that plant, 1.25 m on Dathomir and five of Kashyyyk's zones, 2 m on Lok, reaching 20 to 32 m), never one a pole: the tiling's border read as how far a tuft may wander off its tile's middle (ten of the sixteen carry one, from 0.2 m on Talus to 0.48 on Tatooine and Corellia; the six that carry none, Dantooine, Endor, Rori, Yavin 4 and Kashyyyk's two dungeon zones, would stand every tuft on a grid, so there `jitterMin` of the tile is ours), each tile rolling its own place, child, turn, size and keep in that order from a hash of the tile, its family the near family of the pole under it read out of a cached block only, and dropped where there is no family, where an `AEXC` exclusion holds the pole (an inference: the static flora obeys it), inside a placed object's live circle (`LayoutStreamer.liveExclusionsFor`, so a building put down in play clears it and a removal gives it back, which the trees cannot do), where its picture is missing, or under the drawn water unless its shader is meant for water (`MEANT_FOR_WATER`: the underwater, cattail, reed and lily names). Measured with the real generator over 200 chunks a world, the share of candidates that find a family is the same chunks' own poles' share to a tenth of a point, and on every world within three standard errors of the study's own script run over 2,000 random blocks (Lok 55 against 53: the 49 first written down for it was the study's first 120 blocks, a sample too small for a world whose blocks run from bare to full); `radialAffect.test.ts` holds the maps to the pole to the engine's rule written out again, over every real world's own boundaries, feathers and densities, and `floraCircles.test.ts` holds a house put down and taken up to the grass it takes and gives back. **One `ShaderMaterial` of the session draws every tuft and every far card** (`lights: false`, fog on, `unlit` and `dry` so no scan changes its key, a sampler2DArray of every world's pictures resampled to 256 px), lit per vertex by the leaf cards' own client sum through the very uniform objects `updateCardLight` writes and carried back through the inverse of three's tone curve (`CARD_CURVE_PARS`, cut out of the card chunk so both carry the same lines), tinted by the colour map, and swayed by the trees' own `swgSwayOffset` with a model's two uniforms declared as plain globals written per tuft (`SWAY_SHARED_PARS`, `SWAY_MODEL_PARS`, `SWAY_FUNCTIONS`, which joined are the trees' chunk to the byte). Three still keys a program on the pass's light counts with `lights` off, so the grass hangs only where the world pass draws it (under each chunk's own group on layer 0, so it hides with the ground) and a stand-in compiled in `compileEverything` builds the one program behind the first loading screen: traced through every program link of a driven session, it is linked once there and never again over Tatooine, Naboo and back. The client's radial flora cast no shadow and took none; ours takes the sun's shadow and can cast (wave 8, the lesson after this one). **A far card is a far tile's own sample** (the far grid's 16 m samples, `radialFar` mirrored as the heights are), standing `FAR_TILE_DROP` under the far grid's height like the far tile itself, from where our own trees stop to the far tiling's end, and hidden by a flag per card while a near chunk stands under it with its trees shown (`chunksChanged`), so the two never stand together. Every buffer is made once a chunk and written in place, a steady frame writes nothing (200,000 frames: 2.5 KB and no scavenge), and a chunk builds in about 1.4 ms, one a frame, nearest first. Measured on the RTX 5080 at 1600x900: Naboo's plains 4.07 ms a frame with neither and 4.09 to 4.14 with both, Yavin 4's jungle floor 4.31 against 4.33 to 4.38. The pictures are a light pass of their own (`radial`, seconds, 100 MB over sixteen worlds; Mustafar, the world of copies and Kashyyyk's pob dungeons plant none) whose JSON carries its terrain file's length and SHA-1, so `status` asks again when the terrain moves. `localStorage['swg.grass'] = '0'` and `swg.farCards = '0'` skip them at load; `__debug.grass` and `__debug.farCards` move every number live.
- **Grass casts through one depth material of the session and takes the shadow by reading the cascades itself; a flora shadow switch is a `castShadow` and nothing else; and a console knob with a setting writes the setting in memory and never saves it** (wave 8: `grass.ts`, `grassShadowMath.ts`, `shadowCasters.ts`, `floraSettings.ts`, the Graphics page's Flora section). **Casting** (off: the client's grass cast nothing): `grassDepthMaterial` is the grass's own vertex text under `SWG_GRASS_DEPTH`, drawn once behind the first loading screen on the grass stand-in, under the world's light set alone (grass is drawn in the world pass only), which fixes its unlit program for the session. It measures from `uSwayEye` through `SWG_GRASS_EYE`, never `cameraPosition`, which in a shadow pass is the cascade's camera 150 m toward the sun, so every tuft measured out of reach cast nothing. The colour material carries `shadowSide = DoubleSide` from creation, since three hands a depth material its source's `shadowSide`, else its side turned over, and side is in the depth program's key.
