Every mesh a chunk or tile builds hangs the depth material and sets `castShadow` at build (`tuftMesh`; a toggle alone would be lost at the next build), a near chunk casts only while its square is within the reach (`GrassField.applyCast`, a frame), and only into the cascades whose near edge the reach reaches (`grassCastCascades`): a cascade past the count draws it with its geometry's `instanceCount` set to nought for that one draw in `onBeforeShadow` and put back in `onAfterShadow`, the cascade read off the hooks' **fourth** argument, `shadowCamera` (the third is the portal renderer's probe), and never a uniform, which three uploads only when the material or program changes from the last draw, so one depth material drawn into cascade after cascade carried the first one's value. **Receiving** (on): one lookup a fragment at the point the vertex stage lifted, by the ambient occlusion's own arithmetic -- `cascadesUsable` is the one readiness test `fillCascades` now calls, the pick and the far fade are the AO's (`cascadeShadowKeep` imported into the mirror, not restated), its bounds guard -- with the cascades' own `shadow.matrix` objects by reference, not `fillCascades`' copies, since the shadow pass refreshes them inside the very render call whose world pass reads them; every shadow sampler holds a compare `DepthTexture` stand-in where there is no map (`GRASS_NO_SHADOW`, never null), maps and matrices checked by identity a frame (`World.syncGrassShadows`); the lookup is lifted toward the sun by a share of the tuft's larger side, so a tuft and its neighbours never shade it, and the shade lowers the card sum's front term toward the tangent as a leaf's does. **Never make a shadow compare (`sampler2DShadow`) in a vertex stage**: it links, and on the owner's card under ANGLE's D3D11 it lost the WebGL context outright, the game's and a bare self-test page's alike, after which Chrome refused WebGL to the page for a while; moved to the fragment stage it draws (`__debug.grassShadowSelfTest()` holds it to the mirror on sixteen cases, each cascade in turn the only one able to shade so no slot can be read for another's, and in a real shadow pass, and checks for NaN). **Casters**: what casts is one rule every path reads live (`floraCasts`, `placedCasts`): flora by its own 1.2 m line and the page's Trees and Small plants switches (a small plant at its finest level only), a placed piece that is flora by the same -- every piece of a model its pack files as flora, and otherwise **a piece that is itself a leaf card, decided by the piece and never the model**: 188 of the packs' 6,821 placed models no pack files as flora wear a card somewhere (6,796 before SOE's final files) (Corellia's 55 m geyser rock and 47 m crystal arch, the Ewok village tree with its platforms), and ruled whole they lost rock and platforms to "Trees cast shadows" off -- everything else placed by the page's Smallest shadow caster, stamped on each mesh as it is made (`casterSize`, `casterRadius`, `casterFlora`, `casterLevel`) so `LayoutStreamer.applyCasters` and `World.applyFloraCasters` can apply a moved switch to every tier and chunk standing, what players put down included -- the old slider walked the scene once when it moved and missed every tier loaded after. A caster that never cast before casts through three's own depth material or a clone of it per source material, whose program is fixed at its first draw under the lights of the moment, so the warm-up draws one plain stand-in per way three's depth material can be drawn (`plainDepthCombos`: side, picture, cut, alpha picture; instanced), under the world's set and the rooms' each through sources of its own; `shadowCasters.test` holds them against the packs' casters. **Three chooses a material's program again only when its version or the draw's instancing (or skinning, morphs, clipping, fog, tone mapping) moves, never when `map`, `side` or `alphaMap` do**, and its shadow pass writes exactly those three onto its one shared depth material for each caster with no version step, so fifteen instanced stand-ins drawn back to back on it chose one program between them; each stand-in's `onBeforeShadow` now steps the version of the depth material three hands it (`chooseDepthProgram`, the hook's sixth argument), and the test plays three's own rule over them. The leaves' `'none'` stays a rule about size, never the live cast. **The far cards** are hidden where a chunk's trees are shown, not wherever a chunk stands, and shown lifted onto the coarse height (`TUFT_LIFT`, the far ground's drop) over a chunk whose trees a short Tree distance hides; the plant sweep reports which chunks' trees it flipped and the world hands them over once a sweep. **The settings**: the console writes a setting the page shows into `this.settings` in memory and applies it, never saving (`App.setSettingFromConsole`); past the page's range it applies live and leaves the setting, which the next slider moved or a reload puts back; a reach typed in metres or a held window stands over its setting and says so on the message line when the slider under it moves. `applyFlora` runs in the constructor before `attachCamera`, so the first world is planted by the settings.
- **Slots deliberately left unread, each with the measurement that decided it.** LKUP, 2,653 shaders: `a_specmap_bump_ps20.psh` declares `diffuseMap` and `normalMap` and nothing else -- LKUP is the fixed-function path's lighting table and the modern program never samples it. MNSM, 29: a half-resolution copy of MAIN, on sky bodies only. The `2blend_dirt` family (DIRT, DTLA, DTLB, 162 apiece): four textures on four coordinate sets with no MAIN in the program at all (`lerp(detailB, detailA, mask) * dirt`), reaching 1.0% to 4.9% of a world's placed objects, and already drawing a sensible single texture. DCAL 40, REP0 16, MFFP 25 (the water effect's second pass), and the half-dozen singletons. **A fountain's or a pool's basin water is drawn by the water system** (the owner's call, 2026-09-26): each outdoor copy's level water surface becomes a lake body (`World.basinWaterBody`, through `LayoutStreamer.waterSurface`), so it reflects through the water pass, ripples and takes rain rings, and never swells; the model's own piece is then not drawn for that copy. Which shaders count is an exact list (`BASIN_WATER_SHADERS` in `surfaces.ts`), each measured level over the whole of its surface: the Corellian, Theed and statue fountains, the garden fountains, the swimming pool and the Tusken fort's pool. Falling water, foam, geyser bowls, tanks and canisters stay as the model draws them, and a basin standing in a room stays the model's, since the room pass does not draw world water bodies. A basin wears a look of its own (`BASIN_WATER_TUNE` in `basinWater.ts`, a clear green-grey well under the planet's lake opacity: the lake colour read as a swimming pool in a stone bowl), is left out of the model's colliders (every triangle of a placed model is one, so the surface was a floor you stood on and nothing you did rippled it), and its footprint -- the level triangles flattened onto the ground, never the box, whose corners reach the paving round a round fountain -- answers `footSurfaces.waterTop`, the splashes' height and the ripple field's level near the player: the field is one plane, so beside a basin it takes the basin's. A fountain's own spray is drawn at `FOUNTAIN_SPRAY_TUNE.alpha` of its alpha (`particleDraw.ts`, every `pt_fountain_*` but the one brazier that shares the name): the files are right, a white texture averaging alpha 0.23, but a hundred-odd quads in the air stack to a sheet. `__debug.fountains` moves all of it.
- **An IFF tag is always four bytes, so a three-letter tag carries a trailing space, and `childrenOf` matches it exactly.** This has now cost the project twice in the same file. `VAL ` below is the first; the second was `DIR `, the animation tables' direction selector, read as `'DIR'` fourteen lines above the comment explaining the trap. Every direction selector therefore returned an empty list, and so did every time-scale and probability selector standing over one: 358 of the game's own animation names were silently deleted across 23 tables, and on the body table every playable species shares that was exactly the 34 aimed blaster stances, standing and kneeling, and every whole-body blaster shot. Nobody in the game could aim because the poses were never converted, while `player.ts` and `npcs.ts` asked for them by pattern and fell back on the last frame of a transition -- a freeze, not a missing-animation error, which is why it never looked like a converter fault. Worse, `status` had been *blaming the archives in print*, telling the owner the retail table was smaller and to convert without `--retail-only`; the four duplicate names plus these 34 are exactly the difference it was reporting. Direction codes are a bitmask (4 front, 8 back, 1 right, 2 left) and are never 0; reading 4 as "front" is an inference from the file names, which end `_front` there, and from the sub-selectors, which sit on that branch and nowhere else, and it lives in one function so it can be overturned in one place. **Before trusting any tag comparison in a reader, count the letters.**
- An animation table's string selector (SSAT) keeps its value list in a chunk tagged `VAL ` (three letters and a space), not `VALS`: an i16 count, then each value's name and the i16 index of the branch it picks; several values share a branch. The converter names branches for their first value (`loop_riding:vehicle_speeder_bike`, `skill_action_5:dance_18`), the default branch plain, and writes every branch's values to the manifest as `variants`; `rig.variant(base, value)` resolves them in the game.
- An animation file (`.ans`, `FORM CKAT > 0001` or `FORM KFAT > 0003`) may carry a `FORM MSGS` saying when things happen inside the clip: an `INFO` count, then one `MESG` per message, each an int16 frame count, a name and that many int16 frame numbers. 3,399 of the 8,587 retail animations carry one (3,308 of 8,351 before SOE's final names list) and every one parses to the byte, which is checked on every file of every run rather than once by hand. A clip's frames are numbered 0 to one less than its count, and 1,524 marks sit exactly on the count: one past the last keyframe, which is the clip's last instant and a looping clip's wrap, so a reader must expect it rather than call the file corrupt. The shot marks (`fire1..fire11`), the hardpoint effects, the cinematic camera cuts and the marks that put a weapon in a hand and take it away are the code's already and are left out, which leaves the footsteps, the voices and the blows landing. A mark's time is `frame / fps` from the clip's own rate, not the fraction times a glTF clip's duration, which is exact only for a looping clip. Jedi Academy's own marks are in `models/players/_humanoid/animevents.cfg`, in an `UPPEREVENTS` and a `LOWEREVENTS` block, and their frames count from each animation's own first frame and not from the shared `.gla`; both of its humanoid config files must be read from the archives the game ships and from nothing else, because any `.pk3` dropped into the folder overrides them and a mod that doubles a walk's frame count puts a footstep a quarter of a cycle out. The other direction is caught by checking every clip's frame count against the clips already converted for the player, so it shows in `status` instead of in the feet. A name written with `%d` is drawn from every index range the event lines give it, and the extension a sound is named with is not always the one in the archives -- it slips both ways -- so every lookup tries the other before calling a file missing, as Jedi Academy's own loader does. A mark's chance is Jedi Academy's own convention and not a fraction: it is out of a hundred and nought means always, so 516 of the 519 marks in the pack carry nought and reading the field as a fraction dropped every footstep, voice and effect it has, leaving only the three lines that carry 30.
- A character's moods are the animation table's `mood` selector, and it reaches the idle and nothing else: on the body table all twenty playable species share, the selector sits under the standing loop's still branch, so the walk and the run have none of its forty values. SOE's final table (with SOE's final names list) adds one mood under the walk and the run as well, `undead`, which the species packs carry as `idle:undead`, `walk:undead` and `run:undead` and nothing wears yet (the people's business, not a menu's). Of its 40 values 10 pick the very animation the default branch plays (`conversation` and `threaten` are the plain breathing loop under another name, as are the scene NPCs' terminal, consoling, accusing and imperial poses) and six more share a branch or a file with an earlier value, so the 40 come to 24 clips, about 5 MB of a 218 MB pack. They are baked as `idle:<label>` with every value that reaches one in the manifest's `variants`, which `rig.variant('idle', mood)` resolves; a mood with no branch falls back to `idle` and the body is unchanged, which is what a chat-only mood means. The shipped flattener cannot read them: it spreads each selector's variable and values over what it has already built as it unwinds, so the outermost wins and every mood branch comes out carrying the *gender's* values and no mood value at all — `tools/swg/moods.mjs` reads the table with the path-keeping flattener the mobiles command wrote, which is also why the two paths' spellings of an animation are compared normalised (the flatteners lowercase and strip a leading slash differently, and one uppercase letter would empty the join and convert nothing). That none of the forty touches the walk is read off the table and not off a failed lookup. Three of the 24 are single poses, so the player and parts bake pads every one-frame clip to two keys a frame apart as the mobiles packs always have: a zero-length action finishes on its first update and a repeating one poses at NaN. The chat's moods are the other table's 187 names, of which exactly 7 are in both, so the body takes a branch when there is one and the words take the mood always; the performances and the scene NPCs' death and sitting poses the same conversion writes can never be worn by a walking player.
- A mobile's animations are shared: every appearance with the same skeletons and animation table plays one pack (`mobiles/anims/<pack>.glb`), so a clip is baked once for the hundreds of templates that use it, and the pack's JSON names the clip for each role rather than the game guessing by name. The tables nest their selectors (a mount's branch outside a mood's), and the shipped flattener loses the inner one, so `mobiles` has its own path-keeping flattener and picks the wild, calm, unridden branch: a bantha walks at 0.86 and runs at 3.47 m/s, not the ridden 6.93. Packs are compacted on the way out (no track for a joint that never leaves its bind pose, two keys for one that holds still), which is about half the bytes, and a one-frame pose is written twice a frame apart because three finishes a zero-length clip on its first update and poses a repeating one at NaN.
- How a rider sits comes from three mount tables (`datatables/mount/`): logical_saddle_name_map (a `.sat`, listed as its `_hue` variant, to a logical saddle), saddle_appearance_map (to the saddle's appearance), rider_pose_map (appearance and seat to the pose). The pose is the rider_pose selector's value; the hover chair is not in the tables, so unknown vehicles are tried as `vehicle_<id>`. A rideable vehicle's `pv_*.sat` is a placeholder whose body is the static appearance of the same name; the pose still keys on the `.sat`.
- A skinned mesh's hardpoints are `SKMG > <version form, 0004> > FORM HPTS > STAT` (and `DYN `, with a trailing space): an i16 count, then per hardpoint its name, its parent joint's name, a quaternion (w, x, y, z) and a position, in that joint's frame; every one in the retail archives parses to the byte this way. The `creatures` command writes them as `hp:<name>` nodes under their joints, mirrored exactly as the joints are (q to (w, x, -y, -z), x to -x). The mounts the game really let players ride (`datatables/mount/valid_scale_range.iff`) are exactly those whose mesh carries a `saddle` hardpoint; for the bantha, kaadu, bol and rancor only the `_hue` appearance the mount tables list carries it (the plain one has identical geometry and none), so it is read from there when both share a skeleton. The other creatures in the saddle map have no hardpoint and get a saddle on the back as measured posed.
- Every retail saddle (`appearance/mnt_saddle_*.apt`) has one hardpoint, `player`, at (0.0002, 0.164, -0.060) but the armoured varactyl's, which SOE's final names list brings and which stands it 0.018 m higher and 0.077 m further back (the seat hangs on each saddle's own point, so it rides as it should), and the saddle poses' clip root (`loop_riding:saddle_*` and the plain `loop_riding`) is (0, 0.165, -0.057): the game stands the rider at the saddle's origin and the clip lifts the pelvis to `player`, as with the speeder bike. The seat hangs on the saddle's `player` node with `seatPelvis`, so the pelvis lands there whatever riding clip plays, and the seat is `add`ed (not `attach`ed) under the creature's `hp:saddle`, so the saddle and rider ride the joint in its own frame and the rest pose never matters (the bantha's saddle moves 1.5 m between its rest pose and its idle). Nothing in `Garage.finishSpawn` may be awaited after `new Vehicle`: the body is live, falling and unsprung until the world adds it, so the saddle model is loaded beside the creature's before it.
- A rideable vehicle's `pv_*.sat` is a placeholder only when its animation table is the shared `monstrosity.lat`; the walkers (AT-ST, AT-XT) and the basilisk carry their own table and mesh and walk with them. The game's own rider poses are what they are: the AT-ST seats its pilot as the hover chair does, and the AT-XT, by SOE's final table (with SOE's final names list), seats two in the bird saddle's pose, where the table before it seated one as a pod racer.
- Three applies tone mapping only to the default framebuffer: with a render target bound the materials come out linear, so post-processing must end in an OutputPass, and toggling it recompiles every program (the tone mapping is in the program key). World warm-up binds `world.compileTarget()` (the effects' scene target) around every compile, so the programs built behind the loading screen are the ones drawn; switching Effects compiles the other variants in the background (`reconcileEffects`) and changes over when they are linked. Three's own output pass works its tone-mapping defines out on its first draw, so it is given one throw-away draw before the warm-up compiles it, or the program built there is one no frame ever uses. Every effect's settings, knobs, pass order, products and budgets live in `src/core/fxRegistry.ts`; `npm run test:fx` checks them.
- The game seats a rider at the vehicle's authored origin and the riding clip's root offset puts the pelvis in the seat: a speeder bike's `player` hardpoint is exactly its clip's root (0, 1.16, -0.04), so the rider must not be placed at the hardpoint as well. The garage recentres a model on its box, so the authored origin is `model.position` afterwards. A seat on a saddle's `player` point or on a creature's own `player` hardpoint names the pelvis instead and takes the clip's root off (`seatPelvis`); a pod racer's seat names the pelvis too, and hangs under its cockpit's joint (`src/vehicles/podSeat.ts`, the lesson below); a ship's pilot is placed by the eye instead (`Vehicle.eyeSeat`, the cockpit-frame lesson), and a wild creature's bare back names neither; `__debug.seat(dx, dy, dz)` nudges a seat live and prints the pose's root.
- **A pod's seat is the owner's table, else the game's own `player` hardpoint, else the cockpit joint, and it hangs under that joint so the pilot rides the pod's clips** (`src/vehicles/podSeat.ts`). Every skinned pod shares one skeleton (root > lft_engine > lft_leg > left_foot > body), its cockpit is skinned to `body`, and on the three pods with clips (gasgano's pod_racer_one, the light-bending one on the same pose, mawhonical's pod_racer_two) `body` stands 2.6 m under its rest place in the idle and 1.4 m over it in the run: a seat on the vehicle's group left the pilot behind as the cockpit rose four metres. Every pod's mesh carries the game's own `player` point on `body` (`SKMG > HPTS`), and the owner's hand-placed seats land within 0.12 to 0.21 m of it, so it names the pelvis, as a creature's own player point does; **the gallery threw it away**, because it called `convertSat` without `hardpoints: true` (`gallerySatOptions` now keeps every skeletal model's, and `status` asks for the gallery again while no pod lists `player` in its manifest entry). Until that reconversion a pod sits on `body`'s own origin, about half a metre from the point. The guess from the mesh that came before picked the wrong end of the pod on all three pods that used it (z +4.47, +4.06, +5.60 against cockpits at -4.88, -4.70, -6.20), and the rider override that applied it read the spawn-time seat, so `__debug.seat` did nothing on those three; it is gone, and the guess is only the last rule for a pod with no joint at all. The seat is added under the joint with its place worked out through the joint's place in the vehicle at the idle's first frame (`Vehicle.seatBind`), which is also what a nudge goes through, since a nudge in the joint's own frame goes sideways wherever the joint is turned. The idle and run change over with a band (`POD_GAIT`, ours: run past 0.6 m/s, idle again under 0.25) or a pod hovering about one speed bobbed its cockpit four metres; the engine glow's update is chained into the clips' (the animation branch replaced it, so the animated pods' glows never grew and their heat never rose), though a pod's glow still stands where every pod's always has, at the rear of the rest-pose box behind the cockpit and not on the game's own `engine_N` points (which `engineSpotsOf` does not take), so in the run it stays put while the cockpit rises; and another player's pod plays the same clips by its glided speed along its nose (`stepPodPicture`, `podSpeed`), which is how the rider's own game reads its speed, because a peer's figure is placed where their own game seated it, in that game's moving cockpit, and a picture that ran while the pod slid sideways hung its pilot four metres under the cockpit. **A pod is decided by what it is (`isPod`: the def's kind, not a creature), never by the kind it is tried as from the garage panel**: decided by the spawn's kind, a walker tried as a pod lost its walk and sat on the mesh guess while every other screen drew it standing still. `seatPod` is the whole seat path over a plain object a Vehicle satisfies, in the one order that works (pose the idle, chain the update, measure, hang with the bind taken in that pose): hung before the pose, every paste was 2.6 m off the cockpit. The walkers, the basilisk, the wheel bike, the MA-3 and the RIC-920 carry `player` points too and are left as they were.
- The scene draws into `postfx.sceneTarget`, which no effect writes into; the chain ping-pongs between two buffers with no depth. A pass may sample the scene's depth texture only while a target without that texture is bound: Chrome drops the draw otherwise (INVALID_OPERATION, and silently once its error cap is hit), which is why the god rays and the speed blur never drew before. Geometry products (heat, water mask, velocity) attach the same depth texture read-only, so they never sample it; a target sharing it must be the scene target's size, and is detached before being disposed on its own, since disposing a target disposes its depth texture. `__debug.fxCheck()` names a step that errors.
- A ship on a planet meets the ground through `src/vehicles/landing.ts`, not the springs: its foot is the hull's own `landing1` hardpoint kept between the hull's underside and `LANDING.gap` (0.3 m) below it (clamped both ways: the runtime's box is the assembled ship's, re-based so its underside is zero, and a point above that would hold the ship with whatever hangs lowest in the ground), the floor under it is a ray among what stands still (never the terrain inside a building), and the put-down fits a plane to five samples, refuses a slope past 20 degrees and eases onto the pose over 1.2 s, after which the hull is held (`Vehicle.hold`, a pose in a frame or in the world, written before every step; `release` lets go, and `teleport` and `launch` let go first). Stopped and powered a ship holds its height, but only while the pilot asks for neither up nor down: the old 1.5 m/s sink is what Ctrl still gives and what brings a hull low enough to set down, and without it a ship that has flown can never stop being airborne, so the whole spring and hover path goes unreachable. `SHIP_GROUND.rule = 'springs'` puts the old hover-only ride back for every ship and lets go of one already down. A ship in a building's rooms is followed through the portals as a mobile is (`World.trackVehicleRoom`, `LayoutStreamer.trackVehicleCell`, from the hull's middle with no walker's lift) and takes the inside filter, written into the filter half of each collider's groups so nothing loses its memberships; every number of the landing is invented and lives in `LANDING` and `SHIP_ROOM`, with `__debug.landing` as their knob.
- Another player's body is a sensor in a collision group of its own that collides with nothing (`Group.peer`, membership the peer bit and a filter of zero): the engine's rule is that a query sees a collider only when the query's filter accepts the collider's membership and the collider's filter accepts the query's, so every query that passes an interaction group misses a peer while a query that passes none is not group-tested at all and finds it — which is exactly the bolts, the blades and the aim rays. A sensor is never in the solver, so a body that teleports between messages can never shove a ship or a crate. Two things measured rather than assumed: rapier's character controller does **not** skip sensors (it stops dead on one the groups let through), so `Physics.isPeer` is a real second line and not decoration; and a capsule of zero radius does not throw but hands back a handle that is not a handle, so the live knob floors both shape numbers. The queries that pass no group at all are, almost all of them, the ones meant to find a body — a blade's sweep, an aim line, a hitscan, a flame cone — with three that are not: `Physics.groundDistance` is the one whose answer is a surface to stand on (it skips peers itself, or a peer standing under a speeder is its road), and the turrets' line of sight and `World.clearGround`'s set-down probe each pass `groups(Group.all, Group.all)` so a player standing in the way neither quiets a turret nor moves where a ship lands. Nothing on this side ever takes health off a peer: a blow goes out through `RemoteBodies.onBlow` and the player who was shot is the only one who subtracts — which is also why a peer joins `World.targets()` only while a blow struck here could reach their browser, since the local wildlife would otherwise chase a picture it can never finish.
- A vehicle's hull colliders leave out the terrain group (`HULL_GROUPS`): the springs hold the vehicle off the ground from their rays, and hull-versus-heightfield contact made every slope snag and jolt.
- An NPC ship is an ordinary garage vehicle flown by `vehicle.autopilot.drive` (`NpcBrain`, `src/space/npcBrain.ts`, every number in it invented). `World.spawnHull` builds its hull through the garage with its tier chassis's fit and `World.vehiclePrepare`, and leaves it out of `world.vehicles` hidden, held, weightless and ghosted, because nothing steps a vehicle outside the list and its live body would fall or be struck while the rest compiles; `NpcShipManager.spawnType` then compiles what the garage made after the vehicle (the clear glass stand-ins, `World.prepareExtras`), adopts its combat and puts it in the list with nothing awaited between (`ShipContacts.sync` drops a contact whose vehicle is not in the list and re-adopts it as neutral, losing its type). While play is paused (`world.simulating`, set first in `stepVehicles`, which `__debug.advance` also calls) every NPC ship is `held` and nothing thinks, fires or streams. `vehicleReach` returns Infinity for an autopilot hull, so nothing mounts or boards one, and the garage's clear leaves them. Its def is a copy of the garage hull's (same `id`), so closing the ship Edit page refits only the player's own spawned ships (`refitSpawned` skips an autopilot hull, as do `nearShip` and the console's nearest-ship picks), or every NPC X-wing would take the player's fit. `World.unload` disposes the manager before the vehicles, and a spawn still being built then finds it disposed and throws its hull away. The brain reads every ship's velocity as its nose times its speed (what flyShip sets a flying hull to), since `body.linvel()` makes an object per call.
- **Any garage ship hull can be stood as an NPC ship for a side and tier picked, and the big ones bring two things the typed ships never met** (`NpcShipManager.standHull`, the pure rules in `src/space/hullNpc.ts`; SOE complete, wave 5). It flies as a type of ours made from the hull's own fields (`standIn` on `NpcTypeDef`, which the family rows' count and clear step over) and is built through the very `stand` a typed ship is, so the faction, the ladder, the contacts, the taunts and the hold all reach it with nothing new. First, every room ship carries its rooms in its hull model (all thirteen of them, the corvette and the Star Destroyer among them), and only `World.spawnVehicle` hides them (`ShipInterior.fromHull`'s `reveal(false)`), so the NPC path hides every cell node past 0 itself, or the rooms stand out through the hull; they are still compiled behind the hold, which costs time and no frame. Second, the formation tables are laid out for fighters (their first three slots 43 m apart at the least), so a corvette patrol at the tables' own spacing stood three hulls inside each other and a lone Star Destroyer stood 700 m ahead had the player inside it: a group of a hull bigger than the tables fit spreads its slots by its own radius (`spread` on `NpcGroup`, one rule for a slot, `slotOf`, which the manager's `slotOffset` and the brain's `slotPoint` both call, 1 for every other group) and is stood out past its own sphere (`hullAhead`), live through `npcShips({ hulls })`. **Every other distance the NPC ships were given was a fighter's too, and each one contradicted the spread layout until it grew with the hull** (review): a ship was hidden past 4 km from the player measured from its middle while the layout stood a Star Destroyer patrol's wingmen 4.7 to 6.8 km out, so two of three were never drawn and a lone one blinked out on its own route; a pilot dropped its target past 4 km from where its group was stood while a claw's wingman's own slot stood 6.7 km from there; and a pilot fired only within its gun's range of the target's middle while its break-off kept it off both whole spheres, so a Star Destroyer (1.3 km to its bow, guns of 1 km) never fired and was never fired on. So the whole layout is one pure function (`hullLayout`, whose answers `standHull` stands its group by and nothing beside it), each group carries its own `drawReach` and `leash` (`HIDE_BEYOND` and `LEASH` for every typed group, to the bit), and the pilots' sight and gun reach count the part of each hull standing out past a fitted hull's 17 m sphere toward the other (`ShipContact.reachToward`, `reachBeyond`), which is nought for every one of the game's own NPC hulls (`HULL_FITTED_RADIUS`, read off the pack by the test), so a typed ship sees and fires exactly as before. The break-off still keeps the spheres apart, which is why fighters reach a Star Destroyer only off its bow and stern. A test that lays the layout out again in its own words proves nothing about the game's: the review broke four lines of `standHull` with every check still passing, and three of the side's four hand-overs, so the test holds `hullLayout`'s own answers and pins each hand-over's exact arguments. And `test:npcships` is not reached by `test:all`: the NPC ship suites run in `test:converter`, so a new one goes in both.
- A flying hull is driven by setting its velocity every step, so a contact the solver resolves is overridden the next step and the hull wedges into whatever it hit: `flyShip` compares the velocity it commanded with what the step left (`commanded`), takes a big loss as a hit, and then leaves the body to the contacts for a few tenths of a second. The hull collider ignores the terrain heightfield (`HULL_GROUPS`), so the ground is checked by height: below it is a crash and the hull is lifted out.
- Two ships that meet are both hurt, whichever of them measures it. That test is each hull's own, so a hull that cannot measure (held while play is paused, when the flown ship flies on and every NPC ship is held; in its own hit's moment; hovering on its springs, where ships have no hit test) was an obstacle that took nothing. Now the hull that finds the loss asks the narrow phase which ship's collider it touched (`Vehicle.shipTouched`, through a handle-to-vehicle map the constructor fills and dispose empties) and hands that ship what the contact's momentum says it lost, this hull's loss times this mass over its own (`partnerLoss`, `Vehicle.rammed`), with the same moment to the contacts, so it never measures that contact again. A ghosted hull is in no group and meets nothing. One collision with another ship (contacts with the same ship, keyed by its body's handle, within `COLLISION_BOUT` seconds of the last: two hull shells that met stay wedged and meet again each time the contacts let go) takes at most `COLLISION_CAP_SHARE` of the ship's own full front armour and chassis (`ShipCombat.collide`), so a ship at full health lives through meeting another; both numbers are invented, in `shipStats.ts`. A station, an asteroid, the Star Destroyer or the ground hurts only the ship and is not capped: a ship held into a station face strikes again about every half second and is destroyed, as it always was.
- Never give the effects' render target `samples`: three resolves a multisampled target at the end of every `render()` call, and the portal renderer makes a dozen calls a frame, so bloom alone dropped the frame from 144 to 11 a second. The target is single-sampled and FXAA smooths the edges.
- A NaN pixel from any material becomes, through the bloom's mip blur, a screen-sized black box whenever that object is in view (the owner found a plant on Tatooine that does it). The effects chain starts with a pass that zeroes NaN and Inf pixels; `__debug.blackBox()` scans the frame and names the object under the first bad pixel, which is how to find the material at fault.
- The motion blur merges two velocities per pixel. For everything still, the camera's reprojection of the scene depth, with a cut (25 to 60 m) pushed out past the far side of whatever the camera follows (`ctx.followFar`, from `App.followFar`), so the ship flown stays sharp at any size; last frame's view is taken with this frame's projection, so aiming's zoom is not motion. For movers, a velocity product drawn only for the objects `App.collectMovers` lists, and of those only the meshes that differ from the reprojection: what the camera follows draws only parts that move relative to it (wings opening, a weapon in hand); other movers draw everything while their root moves and only such parts while it stands. Limbs are not blurred on their own: no bone history is kept. The blur is a reconstruction filter (resolve, tile max, neighbour max, a depth-tested gather), so a sharp hull leaves no ghost in the ground behind it.
- Velocity draws go through `renderBufferDirect` inside an empty proxy scene's `onAfterRender`, one material per program variant (fourteen warmed behind the loading screen; fighters, peers and new mobiles prepared before they show), and must never throw there (three's render state would stay pushed). A direct draw skips three's own checks, so each mesh is checked every frame as `projectObject` would (visible up to the scene, actor layer, frustum) and drawn only after it passed on an earlier frame: a mesh never projected has no buffers, and drawing it saves a broken VAO for good. Records are checked every frame for removal, material swaps and disposed geometry. A variant's first real draw must not work its program out again (`side` is in three's program key, and a double-sided mesh first would compile a second one mid-frame), so `compileVariant` records the material version as three does after its own first draw. Remote players are listed with the relay's smoothed velocity: their glide between messages pulses at ten a second. A new thing that moves on its own must be added to `collectMovers`, or it blurs only with the camera.
- Three disables an AnimationAction when its fade-out finishes (`enabled = false`); an action reused after that shows its bones in the bind pose until `reset()` and `play()`. `rig.setState` restarts a legs action that has gone dead, and a saber move chained onto one already on the upper body stays there (`play(..., { upperOnly })`) rather than taking the whole body and fading the legs out; `__debug.split('BOTH_RUN2')` shows the halves and the live weights.
- Three's mixer writes a joint only when its clip's value moved since the last frame (`PropertyMixer` keeps the last value), so a joint something else turned after the mixer last frame still carries that turn on a frame the clip held it still. The head look turns the neck and head after the mixer and puts their clean pose back on its next call, but the first-person eye reads the head before that call, so on every frame the idle held the neck the eye stood where last frame's look had put it and the view shook back and forth by a centimetre. The rig now puts the look's joints back on their clean pose straight after `mixer.update` (`Rig.update`, `cleanLookBone`), and only a joint still holding last frame's turn, so a clip that wrote it this frame keeps what it wrote. Anything else that turns a bone after the mixer and is read before it next runs has the same trap.
- A clip's own event markers (a foot landing, a voice, a blow, a body hitting the ground) are read against the action playing the clip, never against a clock: each is a fraction of its clip and is mapped onto that action's own duration, since 863 of the packs' clips play at a time scale other than 1. Foot events come from a whole clip or a lower half and only from the one such action with the greatest weight (a walk blending into a run steps once); everything else needs a whole clip or an upper half at half weight or more. A loop's wrap, a negative time scale and a restart are each handled apart, an action met for the first time picks its clock up in silence, and a frame that crossed more than half a clip (a hidden tab, a clip shorter than a frame) moves the clock on without firing. `crossed` and `ClipWatcher` in `src/audio/clipEvents.ts` hold all of it, and the node test pins each case.
- What a foot lands on is asked in the client's own order: the water over the point (the player wades, everything else surfs; chest deep it is swimming and there are no feet at all), then the room the body is in (the interior table's surface, and a ship's deck, whose rooms are a physics world of their own that no ray of the planet's reaches), then the thing it is standing on (one ray down with the fixed-or-bodiless predicate, the streamer's collider-to-template map, and the planet pack's `surfaces`, which lists everything that is not stone), then the ground itself (the chunk's own shader-family grid, which is the array the ground mesh was painted from, never the terrain sampler, whose main-thread cache is mostly empty and would generate a whole block to answer). The surface then picks `footstep_<surface>` out of the body's own client data, falling back to the plain `footstep`, which for a creature is its size's effect and for a person the species' own boots, shoes and bare feet: nothing in the game chooses footwear, its data already did.
- A player aboard a ship's rooms has their body in that room's own Rapier world: before any world unload (travel, switching character, removing vehicles) the player must `leave()` the room, or the body is left in a freed world. `travel` carries a ship across as a `ShipCrossing` with a `ShipCrew` record (place in the hull's frame, heading, at the controls) and boards the new hull with it. The crossing carries the ship's fight too, as shares (`ShipCombat.shares`: shields, armour, chassis, each part and whether it is down, the boost), put on the new hull by `restore` right after `World.spawnVehicle`, which has adopted its combat by then, so a ship arrives as damaged as it left; a destroyed ship crosses nowhere (`goToSpace`, `landShip`, `carryAcross` and `Hyperspace.why` refuse it). A jump to another system is not a crossing: the hull itself is carried across the unload (`World.loadCarrying` takes it out of `vehicles` so the unload's dispose loop never sees it, reads its fight's shares before `ships.clear()`, puts it back in a `finally` so a load that throws still leaves it for the jump's abort to release, and adopts it again with them after `load`), with its body in the session's one Rapier world, its rooms in their own and whoever stands in them left there on purpose (the one exception to the rule above); `App.carryAcross` does what `arrive` does without the loading screen or the player's reset, puts the hull at the arrival's start at once so the world streams round it, and never sets `traveling`, so the frames (the tunnel, the crew walking) go on while the pack loads.
- A hull another player flies is made a place to stand in on demand (`src/net/remoteInterior.ts`), never for a ship merely in view. Its rooms hang under the peer's own picture, which is the only thing carrying that hull's pose off the wire, so they ride it with nothing copied and `hullCarrier` answers for whoever is in them by walking parents. The hull itself is a stand-in `Vehicle` that does nothing -- no model, no colliders, held, ghosted, weightless, not in `world.vehicles`, its body written from the picture each frame, its group marked `userData.noBox` so the rooms never grow the box a bolt stops against -- because `WalkableRoom.vehicle` is read as a Vehicle at some thirty places outside the rooms; `InteriorHost` in `interior.ts` is the little the rooms themselves need, and is where that stand-in would be dropped. A hull that is its own portal building has its cells copied out of the picture (`Garage.visualRooms`: geometry and materials shared, every mesh marked `shared`, cell 0 left out since the picture already draws the shell), and the copy is handed back **turned off**, because hanging it, measuring it and preparing it all yield to the frame loop and it is under a picture that is being drawn; a hull with an interior model of its own loads it hidden and prepared. The rooms are stepped by a sweep of the rooms themselves, called once a frame by the game and again by the peers' pass (a second call within `minStep` does nothing), never per peer: a room outlives the player whose hull it was, and a room nothing steps is a floor that has stopped under the person standing on it. A room somebody is standing in is never freed under them: it is cut loose from the picture and left where the hull was, still stepped, until they step out.
- The aboard path takes two kinds of room, and `player.aboard` is the interface both answer to (`WalkableRoom` in `src/vehicles/surfaceRoom.ts`): a ship's rooms, whose frame is the hull's, and a surface in space the gravity boots hold someone to, which keeps a frame of its own and re-anchors as they walk. Read a room's frame with `roomFrame`/`roomTurn`, never `aboard.vehicle.group.matrixWorld`, or a walker's picture, camera and thrown saber come apart the moment the room turns. A surface room is nobody's property: whoever lets go of it disposes it (the room's Rapier world is not freed by anything else), and it is cut out of one body's colliders only (the world's still ones, or one hull's), since a piece built from two bodies is wrong the moment either moves.
- A player standing in a hull somebody else flies sends **which** hull and where they stand in it, never a copy of the hull: a state carries `veh` or `in { ship, p, h }` and `cleanRide` (`server/vehicleWire.mjs`) keeps one of them, taking a browser that sends both at its word about the hull, because the copy is the one thing that must not go out — two crew each sending their own hull had every other browser build two ships in the same place, with two sets of glows and trails and two of everything to prepare and paint. The place is in the hull's own frame, which is what `Player.pos` and `Player.heading` already are aboard, and the figure is carried onto the hull's live pose **after** every clamped picture has been put on what it rides (`placeAboard` beside `placeDocked` in `src/net/remotePlayers.ts`): a world place eased on its own would swim a passenger about the cabin and out through the walls, because the figure and the hull arrive from two players on two clocks. Nothing answers "whose hull is this" in play yet — `hullCarrier` returns 0 for every hull, since a hull somebody else flies is not a place you can stand in — so the branch is reached only by `__aboard({ carrier: <a peer's id> })`; whatever makes a peer's hull boardable must hang its rooms on that peer's own picture, or `hullCarrier` is the one method to change.
- `Physics.cameraBlock` keeps only colliders that are fixed or have no body at all, so a room whose pieces hang on kinematic bodies (the boots' copied surface) gets no camera collision from it and the view sinks into what is being stood on. A ship's rooms escaped it only because their colliders are bodiless. Such a room casts its own block test in its own world, against its own colliders. And how far a walker has risen is not what says they jumped: the walk's plain jump only reaches `JUMP_APEX` (0.80 m, `jumpVelocity² / 2·gravity` in Jedi Academy's units), and a release height near that holds everyone but a Jedi with force to spend to the ground; the feet leaving the ground is what says it.
- Node's type stripping runs the tests, and it does not take a TypeScript parameter property (`constructor(readonly x: X)`), nor an extensionless relative import: a runtime module that wants a node test writes its fields out and imports `'../core/physics.ts'` with the extension. `src/core/physics.ts` and Rapier itself load fine under node, so a test can build a real physics world and try the thing rather than a mirror of it. And the file that breaks need not be the test: a display file that grows an import of its own takes down whichever suite reaches it while `npx tsc --noEmit -p .` stays perfectly clean, so every value import under `src/` carries its extension whether a test names that file or not.
- A `THREE.Sprite` is always upright on the screen, so anything fixed in the sky rolls with the camera: a space zone's stars are quads turned once at build time to face the sky group's origin (the group follows the camera's place and never turns), which costs nothing a frame and keeps the file's roll (`starQuadTurn` in `src/space/suns.ts`, mirrored by a node test). A sprite is worse than that for anything that must write depth: three's sprite program moves only x and y in view space, so the whole quad takes its centre's depth, and a wide sky body far off the view axis laid a screen-filling quad across the view a few hundred metres out and hid the zone behind it. Every space zone's environment file is one 348-byte ground sky copied into 23 files byte for byte, with none at all for the orbit added last, so in space nothing is built from it. Which star is a zone's sun is ours (`pickSuns`): the brightest by the flare's own ranking, and only ever a star with a glow, since the flare draws a star from its glow; a star within six degrees is folded in as a companion that takes the flare's second slot and no light of its own, so the scene's light count never changes. `SwgSky.spaceLightDir` is that star and is the very vector the world holds as `DayCycle.fixed`, so a live change of rule re-aims the sun, the cascades and the flare with nothing rebuilt. In space there is no ground for a star to set behind, so `World.sunInfo` skips the sun's height test and the effects' horizon fade is skipped too (four of the twelve zones' stars hang above the ship's horizontal in any case); the god rays count a pixel as sky only past a share of the far plane, and each sky planet writes depth through a stand-in out there — a cap of a sphere centred on the camera, which is the one shape whose every point is the same distance off in every direction. The depth buffer holds distance along the camera's forward axis, not distance from the camera, so that stand-in writes less than it stands at towards the corners of the screen and the band between it and the sky must be many depth steps wide (about 70 m a step at 7.5 km, 24 bits, a 0.05 m near plane). Every invented number is in `SPACE_SKY_TUNE`, live through `__debug.suns`.
- A space zone has no snapshot: its stations are `datatables/space/spacestation/<zone>.iff` (server names; drawn as `object/ship/shared_spacestation_<faction>.iff` by a table in `space.mjs`), its asteroid fields `datatables/space/asteroidfield/<zone>.iff` (centre, radius, count, seed, a style table of appearances with likelihoods, or a spline), and its sky is its `terrain/<zone>.trn`: SKYB names the six-sided skybox (`texture/<name>_<side>.dds`, 512 px; the ENVI cube map is what reflections see, not the sky, and drawn as the sky it is a blurry, misaligned mess), CLEA the clear colour, AMBI the ambient (alpha, r, g, b), PARA each parallel light (a byte, diffuse and specular as a-r-g-b, then yaw, pitch, roll in degrees, the same frame convention as the environment file's celestials), STAR the star ramp and count, DUST the motes' count and radius, CELE each star sprite (shader, size, a spare float, a byte, yaw, pitch, roll), PLAN the planets (the planet appearance, then the body's place from the camera, three angles, the halo's roll and the halo's scale, then a byte: the ground environment files' layout; the eighth float is not a size: it is 0 on all of Ord Mantell's bodies and on Kashyyyk's planet, which the old reading turned into size 0, and it does not follow the appearance's HALO chunk, since Dathomir and tatooine_moon_03 have none and a non-zero value, and Ord Mantell's haloed moon has 0). A body's size is its `.pln` appearance's radius (FORM PLNT > 0000 > SURF: a float, the shader, the radius; CLOD the same for the cloud shell; HALO the halo shader and a float), in the same metres as the place: the pack's `size` is radius over distance in the game's unit (`SPACE_BODY_FRAME` in `space.mjs`, world.ts's 240 m at 2600 m). In space the procedural ground must not be built at all: placed objects anchor the ground to their height, which raised a needle of terrain under every asteroid, and the ground plane three kilometres down filled the lower half of the view. The planets and moons wait on a picture each: `loadSpaceBodies` checks the load token after every one, or a travel or jump during them added the old zone's bodies to the next zone, where nothing tracked them, and they piled up trip after trip (a Tatooine in Endor's sky).
- A station's docking lanes are hardpoints on its hull (`dock_<lane>`, `dockradius_<lane>`, `approach_<lane>_<n>`, `exit_<lane>_<n>`), written into `space.json` per model by the space command. Three things about them are not what they look like. A dock's own forward is the way a ship **parks**, not the way in: on the five hulls that carry lanes it stands between 9 and 100 degrees from the direction the lane is flown (a row of parking bays all face along the row while their lanes come in from the side, and the capital ship's face back along its hull), so the way in is always the lane's last leg, and the parked turn is the hardpoint's unless it faces back out the way the ship came (`DOCK_FACE`, `dockingMath.ts`). The numbers on the points are not an order: one station keeps two whole corridors under lane `a` with their numbers interleaved and no `approach_a_1`, so the points are chained by geometry outward from the dock. And a lane's letter is not whose corridor it is: that same station files its third dock's approach under letter `a` and its exit under letter `b`, which left that dock looking like a dock with no points and had a ship parked at it on a direction borrowed from a neighbour, and had letter `a` parking one way down one of its corridors and 138 degrees round from it down the other. So each chained corridor is put back on the dock its innermost point is nearest (`regroup` in `lanesOf`), after which every dock on every hull has a real corridor, nothing is guessed, and the hardpoint's turn is within 100 degrees of every lane. A pack gives a placed object only a place, a turn and a bounding radius -- the capital ship's is 1881 m while its lane points are 615 m from its origin -- so whether a lane can be reached without flying through the hull is two bearings in the model's frame, not a trace: the point must be on the ship's own side of the hull and the leg must not arrive against the way the corridor is flown.
- One ship clamped onto another is ours entirely: the client has no ship-to-ship docking, no hardpoint names one and its one `canDock` string is read by nothing in the archives, so every rule is in `CLAMP_TUNE` (`dockingMath.ts`) and live through `__debug.dock({ clamp: ... })`. Three things about it are not obvious. A carrier's box top is not its deck: a tall thing standing on the hull sets the box, so the clamp spot is lowered onto the skin by one ray straight down the carrier's back (`lowerOntoSkin`, kept per pair of hull models), or a fighter would ride a bridge tower's height above a capital ship. The clamp is written at the top of `stepVehicles`, before any hull has moved, so the carrier's pose is read a step ahead from its velocity and turn rate (`leadPose`), or the carried hull rides a constant 1.67 m astern at 100 m/s and swings in every turn. And the hold's frame is the carrier group's own `matrixWorld`, not a copy of where it was, because `main.ts` only steps docking while play is simulated: with a copy, a hull left the carrier behind for as long as the ship menu was open -- which is the menu the pilot must open to undock. A driven tab never draws, so the clamp works that matrix out itself each step (`updateWorldMatrix(true, false)`, parents and self only). Both hulls of a clamp answer `Docking.docked` (`carrying` for the one carried, `carries` for the one carrying, which in the two-browser case is the grant it made), so wave 3's one refusal covers the jump, both crossings and the landing for the pair with no new code: nothing carries two ships across a zone change, and a carrier that jumped would take the other ship into a world that then dropped it. An answer from another player is checked again when it arrives, since it can be twenty seconds old and the ship may have flown off or opened the throttle since; the two clocks are the step's own seconds, which stop with a panel open, deliberately, because the ship menu is the only place a request can be answered. Crossing between the two ships' rooms names each side as a hull of this world or one another player flies (`CrossSide` in `docking.ts`) and answers for all four ways round. A peer's side counts only while their ship is really lying on the hull: the grant is written the instant the pilot says yes, which is before the asking ship has flown a metre of an approach that reaches fifty of them, and it stands until they say they have let go, which a pilot whose own clamp failed never says -- so the pair is gated on `clampClear`, the same measure an undock already uses the other way round, and never on the grant alone.
- Boarding a hull another player flies goes through a registry, never an import: `setPeerRooms`/`peerRooms` in `docking.ts` is the seam, the wiring in `main.ts` registers an adapter over `World.remoteRooms()`, and nothing in the docking or the menu code knows what builds a room. Which player a room belongs to is a `WeakMap<WalkableRoom, number>` filled as each room is handed over (`idOf`), so the answer is one lookup and an entry dies with the room rather than with a relay number that will be handed out again. Every way out of their rooms says `aboard(id, false)`, which is what clears the builder's `held` and lets the room be freed, and `close(id)` refuses outright for the room the walker is standing in, because freeing a physics world under a body is the one mistake here that is not survivable; `peerHullGone(id)` is the call that must come **before** such a world is freed, and it takes the body out and stands the figure clear. Their ship stays theirs while you are in it: `shipStatus` answers null aboard a peer's room so the ship menu opens nothing there, `handleMount` reads which side the room is before the pilot-spot branch so E can never take their controls, and `eject` refuses, since `eject` runs `travel` and a travel from in there would unload the world around a body that lives in a room's own physics. E at the way in is the way back out. `BOARD_TUNE` (`reach` 8 m, `wait` 20 s) is ours and lives through `__board()`.
- A space zone's pack is version 3: beside what it always held it carries the zone's nebulae, its asteroid fields' shapes, its stations' docking lanes and the sound a dock plays, all in the game's frame already (they are drawn rather than jumped to, so the converter mirrors X once instead of every reader doing it). The nebulae are `datatables/space/nebula/<zone>.iff` row by row, with the four colours as the table stores them (alpha first) and a row's lightning kept only when the row both names a `.ltn` and has a strike rate above zero: 171 of the 250 retail rows, from 4 of Endor's 41 to all 33 of Naboo's, and six rows name a rate with no file and do no damage. Every zone that strikes names the same appearance, `appearance/pt_nebulae_white_02.ltn` (`FORM LEFX > 0002`), whose picture is a 2x2 flip-book of four frames at ten a second; its two waveforms read as the bolt's width and alpha is ours, and the 81 bytes after the two effect names are counted and not read. Both nebula shaders and both `_no_z` shell shaders name the same two textures, so a pack writes two images and the shells point at them. A station's docking lanes are hardpoints on its model (`approach_<lane>_<n>`, `dock_<lane>`, `dockradius_<lane>`, `exit_<lane>_<n>`, the drydocks, and the `hangar*` points as `bays`; the `<name>_damage<n>` ones are damage effects, which every part of these hulls carries, and are left out) and must be read as the model is converted: a placed object is drawn as an instanced mesh with no hardpoint nodes, and a station a kilometre off may not have its detailed model at all. A point's number rises outward along a path, a lane may hold more than one path (the neutral station's lane a holds two and a stray, so its numbers run 226, 429, 576, 575, 114, 245, 441, 589, 76 metres out), so each point also carries how far it is from its own dock and where one path ends is the game's reading; a dock's `forward` is not reliably the way in, since the Imperial station's docks face its own middle while their approaches run outward. Five station models carry lanes and three carry no hardpoints at all, so Dantooine and Kessel have none. All six dock client effects name a sound and no particle, so there is nothing to draw at a dock. The field table's `Name` column is filled on 70 of the 214 retail rows and is a designer's note as often as a label, so a field is named for its place.
- `Line.computeLineDistances()` builds a whole new `Float32BufferAttribute` every call and three never disposes an attribute it merely replaces, so a dashed line worked out again each frame orphans a buffer a frame: the attribute is made once and the frame writes into it. Picking a mark on the space map is done in screen space, the nearest projected middle within a dozen pixels, because a mark is a point five or six pixels tall at any zoom and `raycaster.params.Points` is read only by `Points.raycast`, so setting it retunes the rock cloud and does nothing for anything else; the ray is still cast for the things one clicks anywhere on, a shell's wall and a belt's line. A field that runs along a spline is drawn as the belt alone: the table's radius is how thick it is, not how far it reaches. Every number the map draws and picks with is ours, in `MARK_TUNE` and `VIEW_TUNE`, live through `__debug.spaceMap`, whose `pools` counts must stop rising once a zone's marks are read.
- A space zone's nebulae are drawn from the pack's own rows as two instanced sets, the glow set added and the mist set blended, with `facingPercent` of the quads turned toward the camera in the vertex program so that nothing about them is worked out per frame. The mist is re-ordered far to near on a clock with a floor as well as a ceiling: at a jump's 900 m/s a distance test alone asks for an ordering every few frames and each one rewrites every instance buffer, and the sort is a merge sort down a scratch buffer made with the set, since a typed array's own `sort` with a comparator allocates both the closure and a working copy of the array on every call. A nebula whose middle is more than 6 km off is scaled toward the eye until that middle is 6 km away, which keeps its apparent size exactly (only the depth it is tested at changes) and is how it shows past the 9 km far plane instead of being cut by it; the price is that anything solid between 6 km and where the nebula really is now stands in front of it. From inside one almost none of its own sheets are drawn, since a sheet fades out as it fills the screen, so `alpha` moves nothing in there and the haze sphere about the camera is what is seen and what `shellAlpha` tunes. Everything of theirs is built while the loading screen is up, so the warm-up compiles it: the sheet buffers are made for the whole zone's cap and moving the density refills them rather than making a buffer, a material or a program, while the six numbers that are spent when the bolt pool and the strike records are built (`beams`, `beamSegments`, `waveAlpha`, `liveStrikes`, `liveSpread`, `liveMost`) are named in `BUILD_ONLY_KEYS` and take effect at the next zone load. Every number that places a sheet, sizes it or fades it is ours and lives in `NEBULA_TUNE`.
- **Nebula lightning is the game's own rule, and under it lightning is scenery.** Studied in the game's server and restated in our words (`nebulaMath.ts`, marked READ there): a row strikes only when it names a lightning appearance, a rate above nought and a density above nought; in a moment of `dt` seconds it strikes with chance `dt` times its rate, never over a half; both ends of the bolt fall anywhere in the cube three tenths of the radius either side of the middle, each axis drawn on its own; the bolt stands for half to all of the row's longest; it touches every ship it passes, the player's or an NPC's, each ship resting two seconds between strikes; it drains only the shield on the face toward its nearest point (ahead of the ship's middle the front, else the back) and throws the rest of the damage away, never reaching armour, chassis or a part (`applyShieldHit`, `ShipCombat.lightning`). Ours: the clock is ticks of `strikeTick` (0.1 s) on the clock the weather reads (`sharedClock.now()` less its `lagNow()`: the server's while one answers, walked rather than stepped when it is put right so a correction does not run it back over ticks already struck, and exactly `Date.now()` with no server set; it read this machine's own `Date.now()` until a review caught it, which agreed between two browsers only as far as their machines' clocks did), tick n striking when a hash of the nebula's name and n falls under that chance, so two browsers see the same strikes with nothing sent, exactly as `weatherSchedule.ts` keeps the weather shared, and at that tick the half cap never binds (no retail row strikes over once a second); a ship is touched when the bolt passes within `hitShare` of its bounding radius (the game swept a thin sphere against the hull's own collision); the owner's quarter share of the table's band and the settings switch that stops lightning hurting anything both stand. A strike is kept apart from the bolt drawn for it: it stands in one of a pool of records made with the zone and every one is tested against every ship each frame (a box test, then `segmentReach`, the ship handed the bolt's nearest point to its middle), so a strike too far off to draw, or one that came while both drawn bolts were busy, still strikes. The pool is sized from the zone's own rows (`strikeRecords`, ours: each row's rate times its longest life summed, four square roots of that over and four of the largest group in spare, `liveStrikes` the least and `liveMost` the most), because a fixed eight lost a third of Kessel's strikes and nothing said so, and rows that share a name share a seed and strike on the same ticks (Corellia's nine striking rows are three names), so a group counts as one storm whose every strike stands in each of its rows, and sized without that the pool still lost strikes there; sized so, ten minutes with a ship in every striking nebula of every converted zone loses none, which the node test drives through the real class. One that still finds every record standing takes the record of a strike standing in a nebula that holds no ship when its own nebula holds one (`bumped`), and is otherwise lost and counted (`crowded`); a nebula is asked for its strikes while it is within `strikeReach` of the eye or holds a ship that may be struck, and a ship in a jump or held where it is is never struck. Measured with the converted packs' rows, a fighter flying inside a striking nebula is struck about 0.16 times an hour at the median and 55 at the worst (Kessel's small one), where the old aim struck a ship inside one as often as every four seconds; `bendWithin` (0, the game's rule) above nought puts that aim back, moving a bolt whose far end falls that near the player's ship onto it. `__debug.nebulae({ strike: true })` stands one through the player's ship, half its reach beside the middle on a side the tick picks (through the middle itself the nearest point is the middle and every strike would land on the front), since `__debug.advance` does not move the wall clock. The node check that a tick which does not strike makes nothing once measured what was left after a `gc()`, so it passed with gigabytes of garbage a run; it measures the young generation with no collection in between now, over a million ticks in long windows (so the few bytes the engine may spend handing a call its numbers, which depend on what it has seen before, are nothing beside them) and the cleanest of three runs (a run caught while the engine is still optimising the loop boxes its numbers in the code it falls back on).
- **A nebula weakens a ship by the game's arithmetic with strengths of ours, because no retail row sets one.** The game summed the reactor, engine and shield effects of every nebula holding a ship, held the sums to [-0.5, 1], [-0.5, 1] and [-2, 1], added each to that system's efficiency held to [0.1, 10], scaled every speed, acceleration, braking and turn by the engine's, and scaled reactor power and shield recharge by the efficiency and again by one plus the effect (all READ). Those columns are nought on all 250 retail rows, so a row's own effect is summed only where it is not nought, and where a row left a column at nought ours stands in: `NEBULA_SYSTEMS` (engine -0.1, reactor -0.2, shields -0.5) times the density of the densest such row holding the ship, counted once and never once a row. The game builds one cloud out of several rows about one middle (a core, a body, a shell), so 210 of the 250 rows' middles lie inside two or more rows; summed per row, ours left the middle of a typical cloud a thirtieth of its shields' recharge and 94 middles none, which is how many sheets a designer layered and not how thick the gas is. Taken once, a density of 0.6 leaves the engines at 94%, the reactor at 77% and the shields' own recharge at 49%, the median row's middle 93%, 74% and 42%, and the thickest (density 1) 90%, 64% and 25%. What reactor power means here is ours too: the factor is held to [0, 1], takes the top speed and the refire from none of a reactor-down's slowing toward all of it, and multiplies the shield recharge with the shields' own. `ShipContacts.update` works the sums out into a record kept per contact before each combat's step (`shipSystemsAt`: nought for a ghosted or held hull, so a jump through a nebula does nothing), and `ShipCombat.setNebula` writes the spec only when a factor moves by more than 1e-4, which is on the way in and on the way out and never in between; with every factor at one the spec is the one it always was to the bit, which `towardOne` exists to guarantee (a plain lerp to one is not exactly one). A crossing carries a hull's condition and not its factors, which are worked out again where it stands. The player's ship says so once on the message line as it flies in (`NebulaNote`), naming the shield recharge it really gets (the reactor's share times the shields'), and nothing as it leaves, because the speed arc says that: it is scaled to the hull whole and outside every nebula (`ShipCombat.fullSpeed`, the flight view's `fullSpeed`), so a nebula's hold or a part knocked down moves its top tick down the arc and back. Scaled as it was to the tops as they stood, the arc shrank with them and the tick never moved. And a spec written again in one step drops the top in one step, which `flyShip` took two wrong ways: with W held it cut the cruise to the new top in a frame (ninety metres a second off a fighter in space at a nebula's edge), and with W up it kept the old speed for good inside the very thing that should slow it. A cruise between a top the hull's own numbers just lowered and the old one now eases down at the brake either way (`easeFrom`, cleared once the cruise is down), which also catches a part knocked down; a boost's coast above the old top and an autopilot's own cruise are as they were, and `shipCruise.test.ts` flies the real class. The environmental damage the same table carries is nought on every row and is not built.
- A lightsaber's appearance is an `.lsb` (form LSAT): BASE is the hilt's `.apt`, ASND its idle sound, a BLAD form holds SHDR (the blade's shader), LGTH and WDTH (metres), OPEN and CLOS (seconds), and an optional LGHT form the light (COLR as r, g, b, a bytes, RANG and TIME two floats each). The crafted sabers are under `object/weapon/melee/{sword,2h_sword,polearm}/crafted_saber/`; there is no `object/weapon/lightsaber/`. The blade colours are `palette/wp_lightsaber.pal`.
- A lightsaber is heard from the blade renderer, not from the code that swings it: `SaberBlade.update` is the one place that knows a blade is out, where it is in the world and how fast its tip is moving, so the hum, the ignition, the shut-off, the rain hissing off it and the boil under water all hang there and the player's blades, the fighters' and the mobiles' sound the same with nothing in `player.ts`, `npcs.ts` or `mobile.ts` to say so. A blade snapped on rather than ignited (the thrown saber leaving the hand and coming back) is switched quietly. Hanging a loop on a renderer means the loop outlives anything that stops drawing without switching off: a mobile is culled as a whole group (`group.visible`), so its blade renderer simply stops being called while the blade is still lit, and a mobile that dies off screen never gets a frame in which to put it out, leaving a hum in the air where it last stood for as long as the thing lives. So every blade stamps the time it was last drawn and a sweep (`SaberSounds.tick`, from the top of `SaberBlade.update`, which the player's own blades run through on every frame) lets go of any hum undrawn for half a second, leaving the voice lit so the blade hums again the moment it is drawn once more. A blow of the player's is placed at the drawn blade rather than at the body, because aboard a ship `player.pos` is in the hull's frame and the drawn blade is always in the world's, and a Force power at `player.worldPos`, which is the body in the world either way; both are given the ear's own room (`World.listenerSpace`), or a blade in your own hand is heard through the muffle the moment you walk indoors.
- Jedi Academy marks the frames its own moves sound on (its specials, katas, back attacks, spins and kicks; every ordinary attack of every style carries nothing), so a swing asks the clip-event pack first and lays one whoosh per mark on the audio clock at its own fraction of the move's real length, speed and all, and only a move with no mark whooshes once as it starts. Scheduling beats a timer here: Web Audio honours a start time to the sample, so a kata keeps its rhythm whatever the frame rate does, and a move cut short takes its unsounded whooshes with it. Its files have no `.snd` of their own, so a template is made for each role at the game's own lightsaber templates' settings and hung in front of the bank's lookup, with the sample path written `../jka/<path>` against the bank's own samples folder.
- Jedi Academy's torso does not face the view while moving: CG_PlayerAngles puts the legs at the movement offset (45 degrees for a strafe, 22 for a diagonal) and the torso at a quarter of it, so the shoulders lean a little the way the legs go. A torso turned all the way back to the camera read as twisted the wrong way. The special moves (katas, kicks, lunges, the jump attacks) are whole-body clips and must never be split off the legs; only plain swings ride the upper body over the run.
- A mesh's BOX chunk holds its two corners with the larger first: read as min then max, every pack's `bounds` came out with min and max swapped (`readBounds` in `msh.mjs` now takes them componentwise; packs converted before carry the swap, so game code must take extents as `Math.abs(max - min)` and never trust which corner is which). The weapons rack's "far end" was on the wrong axis because of it, which is what made a measured aim correction turn the guns 90 degrees.
- The game's blaster poses point close to the view once the barrel is read along the true long axis. `correctAim` measures the barrel against the crosshair each frame and turns the spine up to `AIM_SPINE_MAX`, the body's heading beyond that; the tuned turns (`gunTune`) only apply with the correction off. Prone is left to the game's poses.
- A mount's seat rides its skeleton: `hangOnBack` (`src/vehicles/saddle.ts`) hangs a guessed saddle or a bare seat on the spine bone nearest it (never a bone of the head, a tail or a limb, by its own name or any bone's above it: by name alone the ronto's saddle rode an ear flap and the kliknik's a pincer), and the place is measured on the posed, skinned vertices (`posedBack`, `getVertexPosition`), only those whose heaviest bone is the back's by the same rule (`offBack`): the ronto, bolle bol and sharnaff hold their necks up over the middle of the body, and the highest vertex there was the neck's, a saddle 2.3 to 2.8 m up on top of the head; the vertices are read after the idle clip has posed the skeleton once (`mixer.update(0)`): a creature's rest pose can stand two metres from its idle (the condor dragon's), and a rest-pose measurement puts the saddle inside the body. The camera's `forward()` is the look direction, `(-sin yaw, 0, -cos yaw)`; `__debug.advance` also steps the creatures, so powers can be tried headless.
- `__debug.advance(s, keys, true)` keeps its keys down until a later call names them without `hold`; a test that presses a key with `hold` and then calls advance with other keys still has the first key down (a crouch held rolls, then crouch-walks; a trigger held never releases a charged shot).
- Anything that happens aboard a ship's rooms must live in the hull's frame: the player's `pos` already does, so bolts fired aboard carry a `frame` (the hull's live world matrix and the room's own physics), the thrown saber is fed the hand and the aim in that frame, and the world-frame sweeps skip the aboard vehicle. A transient particle effect must end on its own: many of the client's hit effects loop for ever, so `Instance.update` finishes a transient after one run (and after one life for an emitter that never runs out).
- A template's integer parameters carry a delta byte (' ', '+', '-' or '%') between the int8 data type and the int32 value: `intParam` in `objtemplate.mjs` reads offset 2. A gun's `weaponEffect` (bolt, rocket, projectile_rifle, ...) and `weaponEffectIndex` name a row of `datatables/weapon/weapon.iff`, which names the shot's `.prt`, the muzzle flash and the hit and miss client effects per surface; the weapons command converts those into the pack and writes them as each gun's `fx`. The flame throwers are bolt 21 (a fireball), the lightning rifles rocket 3 (the lightning beam), the slugthrowers `projectile_*`.
- What a gun sounds like is two tables, not one, and they answer different questions. `datatables/weapon/weapon.iff` carries fifteen client-effect columns a row -- the fire, the hit on a creature, on metal, on stone, on wood and on anything else, the miss in water, on terrain, on a creature, on metal, on stone, on wood, on anything else and on nothing at all, and the ricochet -- and over its 70 retail rows 1,044 of those 1,050 cells are filled, every filled one names a `.cef` the archives hold, and every one of those names exactly one sound (1,002) or none (42) and never two. `datatables/weapon/combat_effects_ranged.iff` is the other: 271 rows, 267 keyed on a template's file name and four on a kind (`pistol`, `lightRifle`, `rifle`, `heavyWeapon`), 270 naming a muzzle sound and 129 a `Hit Target Sound`, which is the blow on what was aimed at and not on any surface (its commonest values are the flesh hits), and not one of the 271 naming a miss or a ricochet. So a gun takes its muzzle and its blow on a body from the per-weapon table where that table names them, and every surface and every miss from its weapon effect's own client effects; `combat_effects_melee.iff` is thinner still, 133 rows every one of which names a swing and only six a hit (three of them the lightsabers'), so a landed sword blow takes a sound of ours chosen by the kind of thing that swung. A bolt names what it struck by the collider that stopped it (`World.footSurfaces.templateOfCollider`), never by a ray cast down from the mark, because a mark on a wall is metres above anything such a ray could name; only a blast, which has no collider, asks what a foot landing there would, through the feet's own ray. A volley is keyed on who fired as well as on what, or six troopers carrying one rifle would be heard as one shot while a scattergun's eight pellets would be heard as eight. And the guns' clock is stepped beside the feet's rather than with the shooting, because that is the one place both the frame loop and `__debug.advance` reach every step: stepped with the shooting it stands still while the player is dead, in a panel or in the map, and every shot fired in that time after the first is swallowed as one volley.
