- Every sound the game can play is a template (`.snd`): `FORM SD2D > 0003` for a sound with no place in the world, `FORM SD3D > [FORM SD2D > 0003] + 0001` for one that has a place, 7,220 of them with SOE's final names list (3,178 2D, 4,042 3D; 7,171 before it, 3,115 and 4,056, a few later copies having changed kind), of which 528 voice-over lines carry a stray empty `0001` chunk at the top level that the generic parser refuses, so the reader takes the first root form. The `0003` chunk is an int32 sample count, that many sample paths, then exactly 112 bytes in every one of them: 28 four-byte fields (the start delay, the fades, the loop count and the gap between loops, the category, the play order, how volume and pitch vary with pitch in semitones, the priority, and the metres within which the sound plays at full volume). How loudness falls off beyond that radius is the client's audio library's and is not in the archives. The `sounds` command writes the bank under `assets-private/sounds/` (about 856 MB, every sample copied as it is; a second run copies nothing): the background score and the instrument parts are left out, but the music the world places in a room and the three sounds `scene/hyperspace.iff` names for the jump's stages are kept and marked, because those are things happening somewhere rather than a score. Where each sound is used comes with it, out of the client's own data (`clientdata/**`, `FORM CLDF > 0000`: `CEFT`, `CSND` and `EVNT` an event name then a path, `ASND` the object's own loop, a `DAMA` form a damage band, a `VTHR > VSND` a name then eight paths — idle, speed-up, slow-down and run, then four sounds of the damage that state stands for — a `VGEF > INFO` a byte that is 1 over water then the sound, and `ENGS > INTS > INFO` a slot name, a sound and six floats that are not decoded) and out of the room, interface, weapon, door, ship power and hit tables. A path may carry backslashes, one or two leading `@`s, a doubled extension, or the absolute path of the machine that exported it, whose tail from the deepest `sample/`, `music/` or `voice/sample/` folder finds 22 of those 27. 168 sounds the game names are in no archive at all and 27 more are there but left out as music; both lists are written, with the names an already converted pack asks for, and `sounds.json`'s `aliases` resolve the names that differ from a file only in where an underscore falls.
- What a ship, a ship part or a vehicle sounds like is nowhere on its object template. A part's sound belongs to the look a slot shows rather than to the component in it -- the crafted components name no client data at all -- so the key is the attachment template that both the fit and the stock tree keep: 230 of the 441 attachment templates reach a client data file (counted before SOE's final names list, which brings 456 attachment templates), 200 of those name a sound, 61 carry an engine set and 46 a booster, and an engine's `ENGS > INTS` rows are the undamaged run loop first and the 25, 50 and 75% damage loops after it. A `FORM INTS` at the top level is not the ship's interior: all 53 in the archives name one rocket loop, on the 46 booster parts and on the eight hulls whose booster is part of the hull, so a hull's room bed is the interior table's and never that. A hull's own client data usually carries nothing but the effect it is destroyed by, so its engine comes from its own looks table's `engine` column (the modal cell, as the stock parts are picked), which is the only answer for a hull whose engines are part of its mesh -- every TIE's column names an engine attachment with an empty hardpoint, so no model is ever hung for it and looking at what hangs on the ship loses the loop -- and that reaches 62 of the 63 hulls. Nothing names a vehicle's client data either: exactly one of the 63 vehicle templates names a file and the archives do not hold it, so a vehicle is reached through the same mount tables the rider poses use (`logical_saddle_name_map` to a logical saddle, then `saddle_appearance_map`'s `client_data_filename`), which joins all 60 of them. The idle, speed-up and slow-down loops are in no table at all: a run loop is named for its family with the word `run` in it and the archives hold the same name with `idle`, `accel` or `decel` in that word's place, tried both with and without the `_lp` a loop's name usually ends in, which is ours and finds a family for 55 of the 62 loops the fleet names; a run loop with no `run` word of its own is left without one rather than guessed at.
- The mixer (`src/audio/`) is one audio context with a master and seven layer gains (ambience, effects, creatures and voices, footsteps, vehicles, interface, music) made once and never added to or removed; a voice is joined to its layer only while its slot is silent, so nothing clicks. Slots are built on first use and then kept (40 positional, 8 with no place, 4 for the interface), and the panner only pans: its own distance model is off and the whole falloff is `distance.ts`, worked from the one radius the game's templates carry. Everything inside a template — the drawn delay, the gaps, the loop count, the fades, the drift and the offset a waiting bed comes in at — runs on the audio clock, handed in as a number, so a bed keeps its place whether anyone is listening or not and a hidden tab cannot throttle it (there is no `setTimeout` anywhere in `src/audio/`; the wait for a fade is a silent source of that length). Browsers let nothing sound before the first press, so the game unlocks on the first one it sees, falls silent when the tab goes unless the setting says otherwise, and starts nothing at all while `__debug.advance` runs, counting what it would have played. Nothing can be heard from a driven tab, so the helpers report numbers instead: `__debug.audio()` gives the state, the gains, every voice with the gain the listener would hear it at, and `recent`, the last dozen sounds asked for with what became of each, and `__debug.audioSelfTest()` builds a second mixer of the same class in an offline context and checks the rendered peak against what the template, the category and the sliders ask for. Every number of ours is in `DISTANCE_TUNE`, `VOICE_TUNE`, `GRID_TUNE`, `BANK_TUNE` and `MIXER_TUNE`, live through `__debug.audio({ … })`, except the pool sizes, which are read when the mixer is made.
- The world's own sound (`src/audio/ambience.ts`) follows the sky rather than working the mix out again: `SwgSky` blends up to four environment rows a frame and the ambience plays each row's own day and night beds at the very weights the dome is drawn with (`mixLength`, `mixBlock`, `mixWeight`), which is why a storm's bed rises with its clouds and a walk over an area boundary crossfades in sound as it does in sight. A bed has no place at all: it sits wherever the ear is, so walking into a cantina crossfades the street away instead of leaving the muffling rule to cut it off at the door, and every bed therefore takes a non-positional voice slot (363 of the bank's 406 `amb_` and `wtr_` templates are 2D, and so are 41 of the interior table's 45 beds), which is why that pool is the one that fills. A room's bed is the interior table's row for the layout the building's model key names and the cell's own name, falling back to the building's `default` row and then the table's, and a row whose bed the bank has not got is stepped over rather than played silently; a sound object the world itself placed in that room wins over the table's bed, or the chatter plays twice. A planet's placed sound objects come out of its own `sounds.json` in the snapshot's frame, so the runtime mirrors X and takes the layout's centre off exactly as `LayoutStreamer` does and a pack whose centre moves needs no rerun; each is one looping voice for the life of the planet, and one the snapshot put inside a building keeps asking for its room on the grid's own pass, because a building streams out and back as a new object with a new number. A particle effect's emitters have named a sound since emitter version 5 and nothing played them: a standing effect now keeps a loop that goes with it when it sleeps and comes back with it, and a passing one plays what it names once where it plays. The weather's channels are placed with `sound: false` and played by the weather instead, at the channel's own share of the mix, since a channel's effect sits on the camera and cannot know what share of the sky it has.
- **A loop that comes round again must never be stopped when its next pass is lined up.** Passes are scheduled `lookahead` (0.12 s) ahead of the audio clock, and the pass still playing was stopped with a bare `stop()` at that moment: up to 120 ms of silence at every repeat of every one of the bank's 644 gap-free endless loops, the saber hum's every 1.6 s. The pass before now runs out and is stopped at the next one's start only as a backstop, kept as the voice's `tail` so a silence or a stolen slot takes it to the cut node as well. Three faults sat beside it and are gone with it: every pass faded in from nought (only a pass that does not follow sound fades now, and a following one holds with `cancelAndHoldAtTime` so a fade longer than its sample climbs on); the next pass was armed from when the last was handed out rather than when it really began (`began(len, rate, at)`), so a loop started part way -- a late buffer, a virtual voice coming back -- restarted every frame until its clock caught up; and `writeVoice` threw away the template's own volume 33 ms into every sound (`passGain`), so every sound whose template volume is under one now plays at it. A loop whose pitch moves (every engine) cannot be seamless as passes timed at one rate, so an endless, gap-free, single-sample loop with nothing drawn per pass is one source with `loop = true` (`TemplateRun.seamless`, 603 of the 644) and the rest are retimed when their pitch is written (`retime`). A pass that `stop` ended before it began is only disconnected, never stopped again onto the cut node. The `seam` section of `audioRuntime.test.ts` drives the real mixer against a fake context that records every stop and every automation event with its time, which is the only way any of this shows without ears. A vehicle's loops each keep their own last place (`Voice.x/y/z` in `vehicleSounds.ts`): with one shared by the idle, the run and the water, the idle (held first) moved it and the run loop measured no distance, stayed where the rider got on and was gone 5 to 6 s into a run at full throttle. And a band part joined part way through somebody's performance is timed from where it began in the bar (`startsAt = now - offset`), or the part after it came in that much late.
- The mixer's nodes are made once and then only written to. One master and one gain per layer, made at start and never added to or removed; a voice's slot (its gain, its panner, the dry and muffled paths, the low-pass and the two echo sends) built the first time that slot is used and kept for the session, so a game that has played nothing holds no audio nodes; a slot's output rejoined to a layer only when the layer changes and only while it is silent. The only thing made per sound is the `AudioBufferSourceNode` Web Audio requires, which disconnects itself when it ends. A voice's parameters are written at most thirty times a second and only when they moved, through `setTargetAtTime`; the panner only pans (`rolloffFactor` 0), so the whole distance curve is one function in `distance.ts` that a node test sweeps; every looping sound, real or waiting, sits in a four-times-a-second grid and is distance-tested on a pass rather than on a frame, which means every rule about where a voice stands must answer the same on that pass as on a frame or the two paths drift apart; a voice that gives way is moved onto a cut node of its own and faded out there rather than cut dead on the gain its replacement is about to write. The samples go the same way: a WAV is decoded in a worker at the file's own rate and copied into its buffer in slices, nothing about sound is ever awaited on a visual path, and a one-shot whose buffer has not arrived within a tenth of a second is dropped rather than played late.
- A sound is placed against the ear's own room, not against its distance alone. The listener inside a building and the source outside it, or the other way round, moves the voice off its dry path onto a muffled one (a low-pass and a drop in level, both ours; whether the client muffled at all is not in the archives), and a voice standing in the ear's own room is sent to that room's echo -- one of two impulses of our own, the long one for the interior table's room type 7, which is six rows of the 265 (four capitol lobbies, one cantina and one station greenhouse), and the short one for the other 259 it names. It is one echo or the other, never both, and the send carries the voice's own layer gain, since the echoes are shared by every layer and return straight to the master. The returns stand open and each voice's send is what moves, so a voice that leaves a room rings out instead of being cut off at the doorway, and a slot handed to a new voice has its dry, muffled and send gains cancelled and written outright rather than ramped, or the new sound comes in wearing the last one's muffling. What is not in the world at all takes none of this, and that is the caller's word and never the template's category: the game's own interface table is 31 rows of three categories, five of which carry a distance, and a placeless sound with a distance is heard only in the ear's own building, so reading the category would have silenced a click indoors while the rows wired today were merely muffled. A voice with no place of its own is not echoed either -- an area bed stands wherever the ear does, so in a room whose only sound is its bed there is nothing for the echo to carry. Headphone placement is a setting, and the browser loads its head-related impulses the first time any panner asks for them, so one silent frame is pushed through a panner of its own the moment the setting is asked for rather than on the first shot fired; when that load finishes is the browser's own business and script cannot see it.
- In `iff.mjs`, `childrenOf(node, name)` needs the name and returns nothing without it; to walk every child, iterate `node.children`. A form's name is `node.type` (`node.tag` is always `FORM`).
- The number slots are the same machinery for both classes: a list of defs with id, name, cost, kind and blurb (`forcePowers.ts` for the Jedi, `gadgets.ts` for the Bounty Hunter), a loadout of ids per kit (`setLoadout`), the Skills tab (`forceUi.ts`, given `defs` for the class in play) and a field per class on the saved character (`powers`, `gadgets`). Bare hands is a toggle in both lists: `player.fists` hides what is held, and `Unarmed` in `unarmed.ts` plays the game's `unarmed_standing_ready_*` clips (or `BOTH_MELEE1/2` and the kicks) with a capsule sweep part way in. The grenade templates are the weapons pack's `thrown` class, listed on the rack but not held; a gadget names its model by substring.
- A species' `rig.glb` is animations only and about 200 MB (1228 clips): `Character.load` parses it once per URL and shares the clips (`rigClips`), or every fighter spawned stalled the frame for seconds. A wearable from one skeleton on another (the human wardrobe on a Rodian) must have its `skinIndex` re-pointed by joint name (`remapSkin`), since three computes a skinned mesh's bounding sphere from its bones every frame and an undefined bone throws. The species index records each species' wardrobe folder (`wardrobe`); `catalogue()` reads it rather than probing folders. New shader programs are main-thread work even with `compileAsync`: `World.compileReady` does a mesh at a time with a yield between, and a fighter stays hidden until then.
- A placed object inside a building is not contained by the building: it is contained by a cell object (`object/cell/shared_cell.iff`), whose `cellIndex` is the room and whose transform is always identity, and that cell object is contained by the building, so the room is read by climbing the containers. The object's own `cellIndex` is usually 0 and the cell object's is the room; where the object does carry one (130 of one planet's 5,102 objects inside a cell, 428 of another's 778) it always agrees with its container's, so either answers. An object template's `surfaceType` is an int and 0 (other) is its resting value, not 2: it is 0 on 765 of the 1,170 templates one planet places, so a thing with no surface of its own has no entry at all and the ground underneath answers for it.
- A building put down in play (`LayoutStreamer.place`/`unplace`, `World.placeBuilding`, `__debug.house`) is the constructor's own loop done once, and it works because nothing downstream is ever *told* a building exists: the portal renderer keeps its meshes in a lazy map keyed on the building, the interiors pass walks `this.buildings`, and a tier's object list is the very array the region holds. Two things about it are not obvious. A tier instances a model once for every copy of it in the region, and a copy cannot be taken out of an instanced mesh, so a placement made in play gets meshes of its own (an instanced mesh of one, which is what the pack's materials, the shadow rules and the portal renderer all already expect to be handed) and a `runtime` map keeps them so a removal is exact; `unloadTier` drops those records with the tier, or a later removal takes the meshes out twice and counts the instance off twice. And the model is not in the world's own pack: the snapshot packs hold what the game's own worlds placed, and the player buildings are the gallery's (77 of its 237 walk-in models, 63 of 219 before SOE's final files), so the streamer stands a second pack behind its own (`useGuestPack`, and `defOf`/`loadedOf`/`modelOf`, which is the whole of the difference) and that pack is disposed with the world rather than kept, since its materials joined this world's shadow cascades and the portal renderer's set when they were prepared and nothing but a dispose takes them out again. The flora exclusion a placement makes is not undone by a removal: flora is drawn into a chunk when the chunk is built, so putting it back would leave a bald patch on every chunk already standing. And a key is filed once (`placedByKey`): the same key placed twice leaves the first copy drawn, in its region and its tier, where no removal can reach it until the world unloads, so anything that stands a list of things across awaits must give each run keys of its own and check by identity that it is still the run in force after every await (`App.standSeats`, which first checked only the pack's name, so two of its stands overlapping on one world left a copy of every chair that nothing could take down). **The tier's object list is the region's own array.** `buildTier` keeps the bulk only for the instanced meshes; a copy there makes every runtime placement in that tier incorporeal, because the collider sweep walks the tier's list -- which cost every house, travel terminal, fitting and saved prop stood before its tier loaded its collision from pass 9 to Fix round I, while the comment on `place` still promised the invariant. The runtime loop checks after each await that the tier is still the one being built and that the placement is still filed (`placedByKey`), and the instance count an unload gives back is the tier's own (`LoadedTier.instances`), never its list's length. `placedColliders.test.ts` runs the real streamer against the real physics through `streamHarness.ts` and asks a ray. **`solid` is three-way (`collidesByRule`): `true` solid whatever the size, `false` never (our seats and tables under the people of ours, which the seats lesson forbids colliding), absent the size rule; a house always passes `true`.**
- **The ground under a house cannot be moved, and the test on it must not be one number.** Every building the game's own worlds place sits on terrain authored to receive it -- a town's ground is flattened by the building's own terrain layer, baked into the planet before it was converted -- and nothing here can add such a layer to a world already generated, so a house put down in play meets the ground as it finds it and a spot too rough is refused. The first rule was: stand it at the highest corner, refuse past `rough` metres out of level, and refuse past `slope` degrees. Measured over four real worlds (the generator run in node out of the pack's own `terrain.trn`, 900 spots each) that refused nine spots in ten and **every single refusal was the level test; the slope test never once fired**, because a fixed metre cap over a patch of fixed size *is* a slope test, and at 1.6 m over a 30 m house it was a 3-degree one standing next to a 12-degree one. What replaced it is read off the buildings themselves: every player house the archives held before SOE's final names list has a box whose bottom is sixteen metres below its origin, which is a foundation modelled to be buried (the eight the list brings reach 8 to 28.3 m below theirs), so the house is stood at the ground under **its own origin** (the last probe `patchProbes` returns, which is as near the doorstep as anything in the archives says) and ground falling away is allowed a long way (`sink` 6 m, the foundation covers it) while ground rising over the doorstep is allowed very little (`rise` 2.5 m, it laps up a wall). The slope stays a test of its own over every pair of probes, because neither of the others sees a step between two corners that both sit near the doorstep's height. All three now refuse real ground somewhere and a medium house finds 24% of Tatooine, 40% of Naboo and 40% of Corellia; every number is ours, in `HOUSE_TUNE`, live through `__debug.house({ tune })`, and the node test re-measures them over whichever worlds are converted. The client's own `.sfp` footprint is the authority on how much room a structure asks for and the converter does not read it yet: the patch comes from the model's box (`patchOfBounds`), which is fair and a little generous, and `patchOfFootprint` is there for the day it does. **The player's lift is judged at the floor**: the rise and the fall are measured from `door + lift`, where the floor really stands, so lifting clears a doorstep the ground laps over, lowering adds to the rise, and a house hung over level ground is refused in the fall's own words; **how far the door then stands off the ground is a `note` and never a refusal** (past `doorStep`, the player's own 0.5 m autostep, a jump; past `doorMost`, the walk's own `JUMP_APEX`, out of a plain jump's reach; a test reads both off the player's own numbers). And **the patch turns by three's own sense** (`turnLocal`: local `(x, z)` to `(x cos + z sin, -x sin + z cos)`), which every probe, the middle measured for what is in the way, the spot ahead and the deed grid's height samples use: they once turned the other way, agreeing with the drawn house only at 0 and 180 degrees, so a house turned a quarter was judged on the mirror image of its own ground, and the test now pins all of them against `Object3D` itself. What is in the way is measured from the patch's middle and the world is asked what stands round that same middle (`patchMiddle`), in the ghost and in `placeBuilding` alike: asked round the doorstep, which stands metres off a medium house's middle, a thing within reach of the far side was never handed over to be measured.
- **A prop a player puts down is furniture and is judged by two rules, not a house's five** (`src/world/propPlace.ts`, `propsPlaced.ts`, `PlacementGhost.placeProp`, the Props tab, `__debug.prop`). A house meets ground it cannot change and is refused rather than made to fit; a prop may go indoors where there is no terrain at all, may be lifted or dropped thirty metres on purpose (that is how a picture goes on a wall and a lamp on a shelf), and may be turned about all three axes. So the owner's rules are the whole of it: **not buried under the floor, and not swallowed by a wall**, neither of which is a distance. Buried is every one of its nine points (the box's corners and its middle) under the floor, which lets a rug lie flush and a foundation stone sink halfway; in a wall is `buriedShare` (two thirds, ours) of those points inside something solid, so a shelf bracket may reach into the wall it hangs on and a thing sunk halfway into a pillar still stands. **The turn is composed in the world's frame** (`r * q`, never `q * r`): a player pressing "turn left" twice means the same thing both times, and the other order turns about an axis the first press moved, so a crate laid on its side would spin about its own length. It is moved, turned, raised and put down by the placing hand, as a building is (below). A pick-up is one key and not a mode: the thing comes off the world, out of the store and back into your hands as a ghost in the same breath, so moving it is putting it down again. It does **not** go into the backpack -- the ledger keys a row by kind with a count, and a prop is a thing with an identity of its own, so dropping one in there is the very duplication `server/ledger.mjs` exists to prevent. With no server its rows are this browser's own per world (`propsKey`); with a server whose hail says `props: 1` they are the server's, as a house is (**Yours**, below). And the pack is a **guest pack**, as the gallery is behind a house: the streamer's one guest slot became a list, because somebody with a house up and a chair in it needs both behind the world at once, and the props pack is minted per world and let go with it (`PropCatalogue.release`) since its materials joined that world's cascades and the portal renderer's set.
- **A placed object's fire, spray, flame or glow is almost never in its appearance: it hangs from the object template's `clientDataFile`** (FORM CLDF > 0000), which the converter never read for props or worlds, so no brazier, fountain, tiki torch, streetlamp glow or harvester blinker in the game ever showed one. The chunks are CHLD (an appearance, then 17 floats: place 0-2, yaw/pitch/roll 3-5 in degrees), CHL2 (a template instead), HOBJ/IHOB (an appearance on a hardpoint of the object's own appearance), LOBJ (an int32, an optional `.prt`, 17 floats with the point at 14-16; the light itself is not decoded and the x/z order is inferred, y is certain) and HLOB (int32, `.prt`, 13 floats, int32, hardpoint). `tools/swg/clientfx.mjs` reads them; the `objeffects` pass writes a table per pack **keyed by object template, not model**, since one model is shared by templates whose client data differ (one streetlamp mesh wears four colours of lamp), to `objeffects.json` beside the manifest, converting each effect into that pack's `particles/`, and touches no model (3 s for every pack). `AssetPack.objectEffects` holds it, `LayoutStreamer.templateEffects` places it on every placed copy in both the tier and the runtime path, a thing put down in play carries its template as `effectsOf` (its `template` is its own key), and a guest pack's files are re-rooted with `relativeRoot`. The appearance route (a `.cmp` PART naming a `.prt`) is real but small: 85 prop templates over 44 appearances, and none of them a brazier. Measured over the converted packs: 598 effects over 439 templates, about 1,100 standing effects on Corellia. `ParticleEffects` resolves every file against **the pack of the world you are standing in**, so an effect out of a guest pack names its way out of that folder and its textures, meshes and carried effects are all prefixed with it (`packPrefix`).
- A building a player put down is the server's while a server keeps the player's houses (`server/homes.mjs`, the `houses` slot the store has carried empty since it was written; with none it is this machine's, **Yours** below) and the browser only stands it up (`src/net/homes.ts`). The division is the one thing to keep straight: **the server has no terrain**, so whether a spot will take a house is the placing browser's answer and the height comes up the wire with the place, exactly as a player's own position does; what the server decides is everything a second browser could disagree with -- how many houses there are, whose they are, and that two are not in one spot, which it keeps apart by the clear circle the placing browser's own patch asked for, capped. Every other browser stands it at the height the server wrote down and never measures the ground again, or two people would disagree about how high somebody's house stands the day the terrain moves. The id is the server's and never the browser's (a browser-chosen one could collide or be one of the language's own names), and the counter rides in the world file so a restart never hands out one it has used -- which is the trap the whole node test exists for, since a repeated id silently overwrites somebody's house. A world key is **not** a thing this file may have an opinion about: it is whatever `rooms.mjs` made of a browser's planet, a pattern on it refused every world whose name has a space, and it needs no prototype guard because worlds are held in real Maps and the only plain object a house is ever a key of is the store's, under an id the server made up. A placed building is filed in the streamer under its `template`, which for anything put down in play is a name of its own (`home:<id>`), so a removal is one lookup and can never reach a snapshot object. Where a house may **not** go is the browser's, since the world's own objects are the same in every browser and nothing in the archives marks a no-build zone (the real server's were a table it kept): a spot is refused when the patch's circle reaches something the snapshot already placed (`LayoutStreamer.objectsNear`, `blockedBy`, and the refusal names it) or when any probe is under the drawn water. **A snapshot object's size is its model's own box and nothing else** -- the third time this project has paid for reading the snapshot's `radius` as a size, after the flora and the colliders: taken as a fallback for a thing with no box it refused 100% of one world, and the commonest thing "in the way" on another was a cloud of insects, so an object with no box, and every particle effect whatever its box says, is in the way of nothing. Measured that way the world's own buildings refuse 1 to 4% of the open ground. The relay path makes every one of these tests and builds nothing (`tryOnly`), because there the house goes up when the server answers.
- The shuttles are the game's own and needed no conversion at all: every planet's `pois.json` has named its starports and shuttleports with their places since the `maps` command was written (64 ports over 11 worlds), and `galaxy.json` has carried the 30 routes between worlds with their fares and each world's own local fare, and nothing read any of it but the lines the galaxy map draws. The rules are `src/world/shuttle.ts`, pure and node-tested over the real packs: a **shuttleport** moves you about its own world at that world's local fare, a **starport** does that and also leaves it, and the fare is **directed** -- 8 of the 15 retail pairs cost different amounts each way (Corellia to Dantooine is 1000 out and 700 back), which is why the converter keeps both rows and nothing folds a pair into one. A ride lands you at the destination's own starport and not the world's spawn, which is `landingOn` over the destination pack's **raw** rows, because its snapshot centre is not known until its pack has loaded and the game's own `teleport` mirrors a raw row itself once it is there. A port in the packs is a **point** with `r` 0 on every one of them while the building it names is tens of metres across, so `SHUTTLE_TUNE.reach` (40 m, ours) is the radius of "you are at the starport" and not of "you are touching the terminal". A port wants the same key as a lift shaft, an elevator and a doorway, and is deliberately the last of the four to get it (they are underfoot and it is a wide point) while a speeder parked at the port does **not** take the key, exactly as a doorway does not lose it to one -- and `promptRules`' own test pins that order, so the game's dispatch (`handleElevator`, then `handleShuttle`, then `handleZoneGate`, then `handleMount`) has to agree with it. Nothing is charged yet: the fares are shown and credits do not exist. **A port is a building, one row each at its own origin** (`portKindOf` in `tools/swg/places.mjs`): a building under `object/building/` whose template says `starport` or `shuttleport`, and Theed's royal hangar, which says neither and is Theed Starport by name -- it was missing from every list for want of the word, and a starport *sign* in Mos Entha had taken that town's row 85 m off its building. A town's second and third shuttleport used to be dropped as a repeat of the first's name, so their terminals named no port or joined the starport down the street; each is now named the way the game named it, by the server's own travel point beside it out of the Core3 reference (`Theed Shuttle A`, `Mos Espa Shuttleport East`), and `status` asks for `pois` again while any port building has no row. Theed's transport parks in the hangar's room, and a transport trip there lands in it through the ship door on that pad's own `theed` branch (`padOfPort` takes a pad in a room only for a hull of the same rig whose rig has that branch, `RideRoute.landMood` picks the branch by destination, the passenger's room is kept across the seat and the step-off by `World.enterRoom`, and `RIDE_TUNE.roomPads` puts back the set-down); a lambda shuttle's trip, whose rig has no clip into the hangar, still sets the passenger down at the port. An arrival at a **port's** place whose building has a room's floor over the body and none under its feet is stood in that building's way in (`World.roomOverAt`, `roomOverhead` in `nav/navMesh.ts`), not on the ground under the deck; so is a character put back at a port's place (`portPlacedAt`), since a passenger saved in the air on the way to a port with no pad is kept at that port's place at the ground's height. Only a port's: a map jump to Yavin 4's Great Massassi Temple would stand under its ceremony hall by the same rule and is left where it always was.
- A deed is **four sources joined** and no one of them is enough (`tools/swg/deeds.mjs`, the `deeds` command, `src/world/deeds.ts`). The emulator's scripts say which building a deed makes, how many lots it takes and what it cost to keep: 0 of the 442 retail deed templates name a building, because that was the server's business. The client's deed template says what the deed is called. The client's **building** template says the portal layout, which is what the gallery keys its models on, so it is what turns "this makes a house" into "this stands up a model this game has". And the client's `.sfp` says the grid the game drew while you placed it -- which hangs off the **building**, not the deed: 0 of the 442 deeds set `structureFootprintFileName` although every one declares it. Measured: 111 deeds make a player building, all 111 have a retail template, 110 reach a footprint, 47 reach a model the gallery carries, and 24 of those are houses of 1 to 5 lots. A deed with no model is written with none rather than dropped.
- A `.sfp` structure footprint is `FORM FOOT > FORM 0000 > INFO + PRNT`: six words (cells across, cells along, the pivot cell's x and z, then the cell's width and height in **metres**) and one NUL-terminated string a row, one character a cell. All 93 in the retail archives parse to the byte (86 before SOE's final names list) -- the rows match the counts and the pivot is inside the grid on every one -- and the only characters are `F` (1,185), `H` (1,098) and `.` (50). Cell sizes really vary (3x2, 3x3, 4x4, 6x6, 8x8, 16x16, 32x32), so nothing may assume eight. **The origin stands at the middle of the pivot cell**, which is what makes an odd grid with a middle pivot come out centred and the Corellia medium house (3 by 4 of 8 m, pivot 1,1) reach 12 m one way and 20 the other from its doorstep. The arithmetic exists twice on purpose, `footprintPatch` in the converter and `patchOfFootprint` in the game, and the node test compares them over real shapes rather than trusting either.
- A building is put down by hand, not by a spot the game chooses (`src/world/placeGhost.ts`, `__debug.deed`): the ghost is the building's own model made see-through, with the client's own footprint grid drawn on the ground under it, green where it may stand and red where it may not. The client did this too -- a deed declares `useStructureFootprintOutline` beside its footprint's name. The model is **borrowed and never cloned**: the ghost swaps each mesh's material for a see-through copy and puts the originals back when it lets go, so a house is not built twice to be looked at once, and the copies go through `World.forgetMaterials`. The grid is drawn at the real ground under each cell rather than on one plane, so on a slope it reads as the ground it is testing. The wheel is taken **before the camera** reads it (the camera zeroes what it reads, so the two would otherwise fight); the rest of what the hand does is the placing hand's (below). Where it may stand is the same three tests every other placement makes, with the footprint's patch in place of the model's box; one taken by its model alone (the console, or the dev server's editor) is judged on the model's own box and draws no grid.
- **The placing hand is one block for a building and a prop alike, and while it holds anything nothing else fights** (`App.stepPlacingKeys`, `src/world/placeHold.ts`, the placing bar). R held (`placeTurn`) turns what is in hand and F held (`placeLift`) raises it while the mouse moves up and down -- the owner's words, one key to turn and one to raise -- Shift with R tips and rolls a prop about the camera's own level axes in the world's frame (never `cam.right()`/`cam.forward()`, which aboard a hull are the hull's), a quick hand moves it further than a slow one by a gain on its speed in pixels a second so the total is the same at any frame rate, and the wheel with a key held steps to the grid; what a held key does is `applyHold`, pure, which the game hands only the keys and the mouse. **While a key is held the mouse is the thing's**: the block takes the mouse's movement and zeroes it before the camera reads it, on the frame of a click too, so the view holds still and the view swinging never lets go of a thing picked back up. **The fight is muted by action, never by key** (`FIGHT_ACTIONS`: both attacks, the block, the saber's throw and style, the six ability slots, muted for the rest of every frame something is in hand, so the click that puts it down does not also fire and R does not throw a lit saber), and **the frame the hand empties, for any reason, the same set is muted until every key bound to it is up** (`muteUntilUp`), or a key still down from turning starts a throw on the next frame; R and F are borrowed defaults, which works only because their other owners are muted (the lesson on keys further down says why a raw read never is). **The ghost goes where the camera looks**, by the camera's own heading in the world (`viewHeading`), not where the body faces, which standing still lags the view by up to 45 degrees. **A thing picked back up is anchored where it stood** (`placeAnchor`, `anchorReleased`) until the player walks 0.3 m, looks 3 degrees away or rolls the wheel, so a small turn is a small turn and a pick-up put down unmoved stands where it stood. **Escape gives up what is in hand and does nothing more** (`giveUpHand`): the browser drops the pointer lock on the same press and sends the key and the lock's change in either order, so both routes reach it and whichever comes second finds the hand empty and the menu refusing for 300 ms; the lock is asked back only through `handBackMouse`, never while a panel, the map or a window that holds the mouse is up (an ask made regardless locked the pointer under the open backpack), a browser may refuse it until the world is clicked, and nothing promises the relock. Anything that must be done under the lock is a key or a click on the world, since the bar's buttons take a free pointer only.
- **What a world stands for good is two plain files that ship with the build, and nothing in play ever picks one of their rows up** (`src/story/placements.ts` reads them, `src/world/permanent.ts` keeps what stands, `__debug.placements`). `public/placements/story/<world>.json` is the story's dressing and `public/placements/world/<world>.json` the map's own edits, `<world>` the pack id; the owner's call is that story dressing ships to everybody with a launcher update like the rest of the game, so neither file is private, neither needs a server, and every browser fetches both from its own build (`cache: 'no-store'`, and the kind of answer checked as well as the status, since a missing public file is answered with the page and a 200). They are **plain JSON in `public/` because no module imports them**: Vite serves and copies `public/` as it stands, the release pack takes tracked `public/` files byte for byte and refuses `.jsonc`, and a save reloads nothing on its own -- the dev server's watcher says `placements:changed { layer, world }` and the page syncs by difference. A file there that is not committed stops a local release pack. On a planet a row is in the raw frame, the one `/loc`, a story's `near` and an area are written in, taken into the game's only when it stands (`toGame`, the very quaternion the streamer's constructor builds); in a space zone it is the game's own; and `y` is absolute and never measured again. **A row is filed under `place/<id>`**, a name no snapshot object and nothing a player puts down can have, so a story object names one as its `template` with nothing in the story, the wire or the server changed for it (`TEMPLATE` already took the slash, `StoryStands.where` asks for exactly that key and the streamer matches it), a removal is one lookup, and `npm run story:check` holds the object's `near` under `OBJECT_FIND` of its row (strictly: `LayoutStreamer.nearestPlaced` keeps only what is nearer than its reach, measured from the player with the height counting half again, so a `near` at the very edge is never found) and refuses a row the game never stands (anything in the world of copies, a building in a space zone, a world it has not got: `never` on the checker's row), reading this checkout's own two folders whatever `--root` names, since the placements a quest names are the ones its build carries. `status` names a pack the rows need and never asks for one, reading `public/placements` in a checkout and `dist/placements` only in a release (an install with no `public/` at all): a checkout's `dist/` is the last build's and keeps rows the editor has since taken out. **A world's rows are filed in one synchronous loop** (`PermanentLayer.setLayers`: one `syncPlan` over both layers, buildings before props), with nothing awaited between the check that this is still the run and the world in force and the filing, so a key is never filed twice; every call asks the world's generation, and a world that has gone takes its rows with it. **A row never enters `placedProps`, `homes` or `LocalHomes`**, so nothing in play picks one up and none is ever written into a player's own store. **A building filed takes what was filed inside it before it** (`LayoutStreamer.rehostAround`): furniture finds its host only as it is placed, and a story prop filed before the world edit's house round it was otherwise drawn on the actor layer through every wall. A props row whose thing is a seat or a table is **not solid unless it says `solid: true`** (`standsSolid`), because a permanent chair counts as the world's own (`World.dataPlacedNear`), so no chair of ours is stood there, and the seated person the data puts on it is a dynamic body a collider under it pushes off; every other row is solid unless it says `solid: false`. A story row is guarded `DRESSING_TUNE.guard` past its own turned footprint against a player's own prop. **The editor is the dev server's alone, behind three locks**: its endpoint exists only in a Vite plugin's `configureServer` (`apply: 'serve'`) and `vite.config.ts` is never packed; the launcher answers nothing but GET and HEAD; and everything of it in the browser (the switch, the list, the chip, L and its Controls row) sits behind `import.meta.env.DEV`, which a build strips. Its glue (`tools/dev/placementsEndpoint.mjs`, the rules in `src/world/placementEdits.ts`) is **loaded at the first request by a name worked out at run time**, so neither it nor what it imports is a config dependency and editing them never restarts the dev server; the price is that the endpoint runs the code it first loaded until the dev server restarts. It refuses anything but a POST from this machine's own page carrying the editor's header, reads a file as it stands and writes every other row back exactly as it read it, puts a file in place through a dot-named copy that never outlives the write, and takes a file away with its last row. **The dev server answers the page's GET for a placements file from the disk** (`placementFiles` in `vite.config.ts`): Vite's own list of the public folder learns of a new file only once its watcher has told it, so the page asking the moment after the editor first wrote a world's file was handed the page itself, read it as no file, and took down the rows the save had just stood. The switch between Yours and Permanent is never kept across a reload; the first save to a file each session and a refused spot each wait for a second click (the bar's buttons cannot be clicked under the lock); a row in hand cannot be edited from the list, since its save writes the row the hand carries; taking a row out of its file, by O, the bar or Delete, asks first; and a save stands the seats of ours again only when their plan really changed.
- **A player's own things are kept by a server or by this machine, never both, and which of the two is one pure rule asked a few times a second** (`keeperOf` and its neighbours in `src/net/propsKept.ts`, `server/propsKept.mjs`, `LocalHomes` in `src/world/myBuildings.ts`). With a server that has taken the claim, houses are the server's, and props are too when its hail says `props: 1`: everybody on that world sees everybody's, and only whoever put a thing down, or the admin, moves or removes it; the server never judges a spot (it has no terrain), the placing browser's verdict is the answer, and its prop counter rides in the world file as the houses' does. With none, both are this machine's, per world and shared by every character on this browser (`swg.props.<world>`, `swg.homes.<world>`), and they are never handed up to a server on their own. **The keeper changes only when a server answers a claim, the player puts the line down, a line that dropped comes back to a far end that keeps nothing, or an arrival with an address saved hears nothing for `PROPS_KEEPER_TUNE.waitMs`**: a line that merely drops keeps the server's things standing and refuses changes in words until it is back, since taken down and stood again they would flicker at every reconnect, while a far end that answers and keeps nothing hands them to this machine, or a drop would go to a server that ignores it. A change takes one set down and stands the other (`switchSets`) and **never calls `enterPlaced` or `PropCatalogue.release`**: the props pack's materials are in the world's cascades and the portal renderer's set and the streamer holds it as a guest, so freeing it mid-world frees every prop, permanent row and seat standing out of it; only an arrival may. A house this machine keeps clears the plants under it at an arrival only once this machine is known to keep it (`arrivalCircles`), or a session that starts on a server leaves bare clearings with no house in them. **A stand on its way is the only stand for its key** (tickets: `PlacedProps.standOne` and `land`, `KeptProps`' busy tickets, `Homes`, `LocalHomes.beginPlace` and `land`): a run of standing steps over a key still going up, and what lands decides once -- it stands if the rows still want it in the same world's load and comes straight back down otherwise -- where two quick changes of keeper inside one model load filed a key twice and left a copy nothing could take down. **A move or a removal the server will not make is answered naming the thing** (`propNo { id }`, `homeNo { id }`), so the mover's browser stands it again where it stood; answered in words alone it stayed hidden in the mover's browser while everybody else still had it. **Your own house is picked up where it stands** (O at its door, `takeHome`, then `dropHome`), anchored as a prop is and put back where it stood by Escape or Delete (Delete never takes a house down; the Housing tab does), and moved under its own key: this machine's through the same tickets, its row written where it stands only once it has gone up there and only while it is still kept (a take-down during the move's await, which the empty hand no longer guards, otherwise brought it back and saved it again), a refused move stood back where it stood by the placer itself (`placeLocalHome`'s `back`); a server's asked of it (`moveHome`, its own circle left out of the overlap test, `homeMoved` to everybody on the world, and the hail's `homes: 2`, without which the key at the door opens the Housing tab as it always did). The pick-up asks again after its await whether the world and the keeper are still the ones it began with. What was put down in the house's rooms stays where it stood and is counted on the message line (`World.thingsInside`), and what a move came to is `localMoveOf`'s answer over the landing and the list (`homes().lastOutcome`, `deed`'s drop `outcome`), never the ghost's verdict, which says only that the spot could take it: the world going is asked before the row, since a travel in the wait leaves a list that is the next world's and holds no row of the house, and `dropHome` writes the answer and its words afresh on every ending of the click, a refused one as `held`, so the two console helpers never read one click's answer beside another's words.
- Credits are **a number on a character and not a thing in a backpack** (`server/purse.mjs`, `src/net/purse.ts`), which is the owner's call and is also what the game did: nothing in the archives gives credits an object template. The one rule both halves exist for is that a balance may not be spent twice, so **with a server this side never takes money off**: a spend is asked for, the server's answer is what may be acted on, and the thing that was paid for happens in the callback the answer fires -- a browser that subtracted first and told the server afterwards would be a browser deciding what it can afford. A spend that would go below zero moves **nothing at all** rather than going as far as it can, and a refusal clears what was waiting so a later word cannot set off a journey that was already refused. With no server the number lives on the saved character exactly as its clothes do. The fares are the game's; the starting amount, the cap and the rate are ours, in `PURSE_TUNING`. Nothing in the game earns credits yet, so the starting amount is the whole of anybody's money and is set to cover a good many fares rather than to be a challenge.
- **A travel terminal is a thing you use, not a place you stand in**, and E is a general use key -- the owner's own correction to a first cut that made a forty-metre circle round a starport the trigger. The terminals are not in any snapshot: Corellia's places 413 terminals (mission, bank, bazaar, newsnet, elevator) and not one travel terminal, because the whole of travel was the server's. What the server did is a `childObjects` block on each starport and shuttleport **building template** (`tools/swg/travel.mjs`, the `travel` command), so a terminal stands wherever that building stands: 11 templates carry them and the converted worlds come to 189 things. Corellia's starport declares the game's whole arrangement in six children -- four **terminals** inside cell 4 where a ticket is bought, one **ticket collector** outside where it is taken, and one `player_transport`, the shuttle itself. Two things in the numbers are not what they look like: a child is written `x, z, y` with **z the height** (every terminal comes out 0.64 m off its floor and 48 m down the building, which is how you know), and a snapshot names a building by its **shared** template while the scripts name the server's, so both spellings are written and the join is a plain string match. A thing indoors keeps the building's own frame and its **cell**, and the cell matters: two starports on one world are the same building and their terminals carry identical numbers, so what a player reaches is asked of the room the streamer already tracks and never of a point.
- **What the converter reads from the emulator ships with it, and nobody installs the emulator** (the owner's decision: it is referenced, not taken). `travel`, `fittings`, `deeds`, `spawns`, `snapshot`, `pois` and `mobiles` read `tools/swg/core3ref/` -- the twelve Core3 readers' answers (names, places, turns, counts and stats, never the scripts, credited to SWGEmu Core3 under its AGPL-3.0 in the folder's `index.json`) -- and read a folder live only when given `--core3=<dir>` outright, never from `.env`, so a checkout and every launcher convert exactly the same thing. `core3-reference @CORE3` writes it, and compares every answer, over every zone the screenplays name and one for any other, with a fresh read of the folder before it keeps anything; measured, `travel`, `fittings`, `deeds` and `spawns` write byte-identical packs either way. Run it when Core3's scripts change and commit what it writes. The launcher's release carries it (`pack.mjs`), and the launcher test fails if a file of it goes missing, since a folder read by path is invisible to the import check. `regions/regions.json` was the first file of this kind. Older comments saying nothing read from the emulator may reach the repository are about the converted packs, which still never may.
- **SOE's final names list is the difference of two mounts, applied after the install's own indexes, all of it or none, and everything converted says which mount it came from** (`tools/swg/finalnames.mjs`, `finalnamesBuild.mjs`, `openVfs`'s late phase in `tre.mjs`, the list `tools/swg/manifests/final-names.json`; SOE complete, wave 1). An NGE install holds every archive SOE shipped, but its four indexes stop at publish 54, so 7,063 files and 2,815 newer copies sat unreached in archives the player owns. The list is SOE's final four `.toc` files mounted over the same archives less the install's own (9,880 `set`, 8 `deleted`, 2 `gone`), keyed by twelve hex of the SHA-1 of its text with every line ending folded to LF, written only by the maintainer's `final-names` and checked row by row against an install by `final-names --verify`. The gate is exact: the indexes hash to the list's `against` and all 17 archives it reads stand at the final-client manifest's size and SHA-256, or nothing is applied and the mount is the one before to the entry (`none`, `refused`; a folder holding SOE's own indexes is `final`). Two rows are held back, the Life Day snow terrains of Tatooine and Talus. Every command that opens the archives writes `late` (`{ key, state, rows, v }`) into the file `status` reads for it (`LATE_STAMPED`; `LATE_EXEMPT` says why each other command needs none), and every path that keeps a model because its file is there asks `keptStale`, never `materialStale` alone (a text pin in `finalNames.test.ts`). Seven things learned. **`status` hashes no archive**: `probeInstall` reads the install's four index files and its archives' sizes, the install is `--swg`, else `SWG`, and an empty `SWG` names none, which PowerShell cannot say (`$env:SWG = ''` deletes the variable), so every test that runs `status` over fixture packs hands its child `env: { ...process.env, SWG: '' }`, or on a machine whose `.env` names an install the probe says serves it sees the late asks as well. **A late ask never names `--jka`**: the plan reads an ask that names it as one that needs Jedi Academy and skips it on a machine without. **A test that holds a pack's pixels or counts to the archives opens them as that pack was converted, which its own stamp says** (`mountForPack`, `mountForLate` in `materialsHarness.ts`), and a count a re-issued file moves is pinned per late world (the props pack's size cap, the dye floors, the saddle count and the armoured varactyl's saddle, Dathomir's radial affectors, Tatooine's tiers and furniture, the hair grids), or the first suite run between one conversion and the next fails on packs that are right: the normal-map test found a re-issued fan texture 18 degrees off a weapons pack converted before. **The mobiles' source key takes the late world only where the list applied** (`sourceStampOf`), so a mount it does not serve keeps today's key to the bit; and because the code stamp re-keys no unit, nothing `mobilesCodeStamp` reads may change once the real run has been made. **The rewritten check counts a file as named only by a JSON at or above its own folder** (`rewrittenCheck`): by name anywhere in its top folder, 798 female meshes an older run left in the human male wardrobe read as named by the female wardrobe's JSON, and a run that was right failed. **A refusal records what it saw, and `status` asks again for it only once that has moved** (the stamp's `seen`, each refused archive's size and when it was written, against the probe's `archives`; `lateAsk`): the probe reads sizes and the mount SHA-256s, so on the probe's `serves` alone a byte flipped at SOE's own size, or an archive this machine cannot open, was "this install can now mount SOE's final files" in every `status` and a whole conversion redone in every `convert`, refused each time; a folder whose only packs are such refusals says the stamp's why on its line, and one with no pack converted says nothing is converted rather than "mounted in every pack". **What makes a walk grid stale is one function** (`gridLayoutStale`), which `status` and `navgrid --skip-existing` both ask, since a resumed bake that read only the version, the angle and the rules kept exactly the grids `status` then asked for again.
- **What a menu shows of SOE's final files is asked of the menu's own function, keyed on the pack's own stamp, and four of the menus' rules met the late tables only through that** (`src/ui/lateReport.ts`, `__debug.late()`, `tools/swg/tests/lateMenus.test.ts`; SOE complete, wave 2). The console's `late()` reads only what the game has already fetched (a wardrobe through `Character.wardrobesNow`, the garage's `late`, the props', the deeds' and the creator table's own) and asks the very products the node test asks of the packs on disk, where a pack with no stamp is a note and one stamped `applied` fails when its menu does not show the late thing. **SOE's final table gives each Wookiee and Sullustan gender the other's styles on the very same meshes**, so the other gender's group is deduped by mesh as the untabled one always was, which empties it for both Wookiees and for a Sullustan man (a Sullustan woman keeps his beards and his styles 20 to 27) and moves two grids nobody asked about, four styles each way out of the Bothans' other gender's group and the Zabrak men's 25 out of a Zabrak woman's, every one on a mesh an earlier cell already shows (`hairGrid.test.ts` holds every species' grid to the pass's base: the base's less only such cells of the other gender's group, and nothing new); the own group stays the table's rows to the letter, so the two rows the table itself names on one appearance (the Zabrak man's 21 and 25) stand twice there, and the test says so as a note. **A beard is numbered from 1 as the styles are**, so twelve of a Sullustan man's cells read what twelve others read until `styleLabel` said "Beard N". **The one hair entry `isHairKey` still missed was the Singing Mountain Clan's bangs** (`singing_mountain_clan_bangs_s01`, under a hair folder of its own), found by holding every wardrobe's hair entries to it. **A dial's line could not write a tier**: `movedArgs` passed over a path part that was not a name, so the NPC ship ladder's `grade.8` would have vanished from the line the menu copies out; a whole number is a key it writes now (`src/space/tierDials.ts`). And the give tab puts an entry no id pattern places under its arrangement's first slot (`slotGroupOf(id, slots)`, the slots' groups ours), while the Clothes panel's group "take off" asks the same (`WardrobeUi.groupOf`), or a piece listed under Back by its slot would never have been taken off by Back's own button; and since a group then gathers several body slots (a ring and a cybernetic hand under Hands, a wrist bracelet under a bracer), a double-click on a worn piece takes off that piece alone (`takeOff`), where taking off its whole group would have taken the cybernetic hand off with the gloves. The test reads the packs through the code's own rules and never a looser copy of them (the garage's `garageGalleryRows`, the NPC tab's `readinessOf`), and a ships pack whose `combat.json` is missing does not show the tier 10 type, which `late()` tells from a file not yet landed by the fetch's own `ShipContacts.loaded` and names in `missing` (`missingMenus`). The check of the final table's two new hair groups (the Wookiees' fur, holding the pattern, and the humans' `hair_trim`, whose colour only nine of the men's styles and twenty of the women's carry) found nothing the page drew wrong: the grid stands in the first hair group (`gridGroupId`, which the page now asks) and a later one shows its own rows or nothing.
- **SOE's own people for Kashyyyk and Mustafar are a local overlay laid over the world's wildlife pack after everything else, and nothing of the copy they come from is in the repository** (`tools/swg/nge.mjs` reads, `tools/swg/ngepack.mjs` rules, `src/world/ngeOverlay.ts` fetches and merges; SOE complete, wave 3). `spawns --nge=<folder>` writes `assets-private/nge/` only, a target git would take refused, a copy inside the repository refused, an `@` value refused (the cli expands `@NAME` from `.env` before it parses, so the word is read off the raw command line first, `ngeArg`), and `--nge=none` takes it away. Where the overlay may be written is asked of the repository that really holds the folder, from where it really is (`gitPlace`: the part that exists carried through any junction, and git asked from there), because git asked from this worktree about a path in a sibling checkout answers only "outside this repository", which read as "no repository" let a worktree write into a sibling checkout's tracked `src`, and a worktree's `assets-private` is a junction into another checkout that is that checkout's to ignore. The code names the copy's table paths, its column and objvar names and the few generic spawner scripts and templates a row's kind is told by (format knowledge, as `core3.mjs` names Core3's fields) and nothing else of it: which spawners a script stands rather than the data (`gated`) is told by shape -- shaped as a spawner, not a building, and naming nobody in `strSpawns` -- never by a list of the copy's own template names, and the index lists them by name read at run time, which is local. Its tests write a tree of `test_*` names into the temporary folder at run time and read every file back for a stray name; no count, row or creature name of the copy goes into a committed file, this one included. Every name the overlay writes is `nge:`-prefixed and the merge refuses an overlay that names anything else (`strayName`), and its people go **after** Core3's rows and the heralds (`standingList`), because `stood:<world>:<n>` is a place in that list and every other browser must keep naming everybody else the same; a world Core3 holds nothing of starts from an empty pack named for the overlay's world, or every row is `stood:here:`. Its creature numbers go through `gameOf` by way of `ngeMob`, so a level means one thing whichever source stood it, and a spawner more than `NGE_RULES.lift` over the pack's ground (a canopy walkway) stands its people in a still knot on itself, since a body drawn across its disc perches on nothing and nothing of ours walks one along a walkway. **A row is named by its `objid` only where its table writes one**: some areas' tables have no such column and every row reads nought there, and keyed by that nought every spawner of the area drew the same creature at the same offset, respawn and heading and stood under one shared name, so such a row is named by what it says with its twins counted (`rowIds`, as the crews' rows are). Its patrollers walk their rounds whatever they may be fought (`walks`, `setGoing` beside `walksRound`), since the server's own patrol spawner walked its fighters, where Core3's towns left their combat walkers at their posts. Five traps. `WildLife.load` parses Core3's JSON only where it will use it (a world the index does not list reads exactly what it read before, parse and all). `status`'s ask for the overlay is a line of the report and never a `need()`, since a to-do step is one the launcher's plan would run and nothing a launcher runs may pass `--nge`. Where a server others share holds the world the overlay is left out unless `where` says `always` (`serverHoldsFor`: the props keeper's rule from an arrival, the relay that came before counted as shared, and a session's first world with a server saved counted as held, because its connect comes only after that loading screen and nothing can answer before it lifts -- waiting on it would hold the screen for good). `WildLife.retune` lays the world out again from the merged pack it holds and must carry the overlay's people across, which `adopt` empties. And **a world's arrival point is ours and can land in SOE's own hostile camp**: the flattest spot near a layout centre stood, on Mustafar, among aggressive people the data placed there, and with no cloning facility on that world the respawn came round at the same spot and died again. So the overlay's people and herds who pick a fight on sight are kept `NGE_OVERLAY_TUNE.arrivalClear` (64 m, ours, live) clear of where the game brings a player in and back (`World.arrivalPoint`, the App's own `spawn`, which a teleport moves too), by their spot, their roam, every point of their round and a herd's spread, and one already standing there when that place moves onto it is put down; nobody else's row is touched.
- **Restuss is one world in two states, and nothing that stands in only one of them moves anybody's index** (`tools/swg/restuss.mjs`, `src/world/worldState.ts`, `src/world/restuss.ts`, `server/worldState.mjs`; SOE complete, wave 4). SOE's final client places no city there in any copy of `snapshot/rori.ws` from publish 40 on (the late camp, the two faction cantinas and a bunker stand in its place); every copy up to `patch_15_02.tre`, which every retail install holds, has the launch city, which is read out of that one off a rectangle (raw x 5050..5540, z 5470..5915) with its raw ids kept, because the emulator named its rooms by them -- 63 buildings, 659 top-level objects and 820 with their children on the owner's install, none colliding with the final snapshot's or its buildouts' -- and **appended after every other node**, so no layout index of the world's moves and the auto centre stays the Rebel outpost's; the ruins are a swap of template names on the launch city's own places, each of the 35 buildings with a ruin of its kind among the retail `*_destroyed`/`scorched` statics getting one on its place and turn, a filler block's variant by a hash of its id (ours), and nothing read from the server's event tables. What is the city's is tagged `restuss=living` or `restuss=ruins` (`<key>=<value>`) and anything untagged stands in every state (`standsIn`); Rori's layout carries a `states.restuss` block (its version and the city's middle, the client's own region) **after** `center`, since `poisCentre.test` reads the centre off the head of the file. **What is filed is never what is placed**: the streamer files every layout object and puts the ones not in this state in a dormant list (`fileByState`, a contained object's `hostIndex` worked out through the layout index), keeps their flora exclusions and answers `objectsNear` over both lists, so a house is refused against a building of either state and no tree grows through a ruin while the city stands. The people are a mask, never a list: Core3's town inside `RESTUSS_TUNE.circle` (700 m, the emulator's own no-spawn circle) is skipped in the ruins by a `dormant` predicate the people take at adopt, Core3's 37 indoor rows that find their rooms only because the city is read in are appended after every other row of the world and tagged `restuss=living` (`restussLast`), as the fittings in those rooms are tagged from their building, so they never stand in the ruins whatever the circle is moved to (untagged, a circle under 340 m stood them in the air where a dormant building was), and the First Brigade of ours goes after the heralds and before the local overlay, always in the list and stood only in the ruins by its tag, so a `stood:` name is the same in both states and in every browser. **A post is kept clear of every ruin's footprint, not only its own** (`rosterRows`: each footprint grown by a body's 0.35 m, a side so blocked passing to the next round from the one drawn, a ruin hemmed in on all four given none): the filler blocks stand packed side by side, and a post drawn onto the side two blocks share stood three people in the neighbour's rubble. **Ports are named once per state** (`namePortsInStates`: one pass per tag value with the untagged ports in each, refusing an untagged port that would come out two names), and pois, travel and fittings rows carry `state` and are filtered as the game reads them (`inState`, `fileInState`, every cache keyed on `worldStateStamp`), never by taking a row out; of the untagged fittings the ruins skip only what the server stood on no building inside the circle (`fittingDormant`), since the late camp's cloning facility stands inside it in both states and its terminal with it; the walk grid is baked over the union of both states. **The `sounds` command is a reader of the snapshot too** (`loadPlacements` in `soundplaces.mjs`), which the first cut missed, leaving the living city's bands, crowds and humming props out of Rori's `sounds.json` (77 emitters): it reads the city in, each emitter carries its node's state, the file says so (`states`, which `status` asks with `restussSoundsStatus`), and the ambience puts down only the state in force. **An arrival takes its states before the pack becomes the world's** (`this.pack = pack` after `takeWorldStates` in `loadPack`), because the frames drawn during the wait for a hail hand `this.pack` to whatever reads it -- the placed sounds are put down the frame `readySound` first sees it -- and those must read the states this arrival takes, not the ones it would have taken before the hail; a pack loaded for a world a travel in the wait left behind is the wait's to dispose, since no unload ever saw it. **A state is taken only at an arrival** (`takeWorldStates`, released between worlds): the server's word, else the setting (`swg.world.restuss`), else the ruins, the owner's default (2026-10-19). With a server saved an arrival on a world with states waits for the hail, and the first world of a session cannot wait on the real line, whose connect comes only after that loading screen (wave 3's trap), so it opens a throwaway line of its own to read the hail and closes it (`peekHail`, bounded by `WORLD_STATE_TUNE.waitMs`). A permanent placement row carries `state` too, written into its hash only when present so every row's hash before is unchanged, the editor's State button cycles it, and `story:check` warns when a quest names a `place/` row that stands in one state only. The server keeps the admin's word as a record of its own (`worldState`), hails it as `states`, takes `worldStateSet` from the admin alone and tells everybody connected as `worldStates`; a browser built before reads neither and keeps its own setting. **Every test that reads the ports over the packs reads a world with states once per state, as the game does** (`statePasses` in `rideRoute.test.ts`, off the places' own `states` mark): each state holds one port of each name, so read whole a converted Rori names Restuss's two ports twice and `padOfPort`, which finds a port by name, takes the living city's for the camp's. And every hand-over is pinned as text (`restuss.test` reads `cli.mjs`, `travel.mjs` and `soundplaces.mjs`; `worldState.test` reads `world.ts`, `main.ts`, `ambience.ts` and `galaxyMap.ts`), since every behaviour test builds the rest for itself and a review deleted each of them with the suite green. And any build without this wave stands both states at once on packs converted with it (an older streamer files every layout object, so a building and its ruin stand on one spot, and its places name each Restuss port twice): a checkout reading the same packs must have the wave merged before it reads Rori.
- The shuttle's timetable is **ours**: nothing in the archives says how often one came or how long it waited, that was the server's. It is built the way this game already keeps the weather and the nebulae's lightning shared -- straight off the wall clock with nothing sent between browsers, each port's line cut into rounds of `every` seconds and each round's landing moment hashed from the port's own name, so two people at one starport watch the same shuttle land at the same instant and two ports do not all land together (measured: 200 ports at one instant spread over 430 s). A ticket is bought at the terminal and handed in at the collector, and boarding is refused in the shuttle's own words until it is really waiting. Every number is `TRAVEL_TUNE`, live through `__debug.terminal`.
- **A shuttle is drawn on the game's own rig, and its placeholder mesh is not the shuttle.** Both `player_shuttle.sat` and `player_transport.sat` carry a mesh of four and eight vertices; the hull is static appearances their client data hangs on joints by name (`HOBJ`): the shuttle's one piece on `root`, the transport's hull, three struts and door each on a joint of its own. So the `travel` command converts each rig once into a folder every world shares (`<out>/travel/`: `convertSat` with `rigOnly`, the skeleton and its clips and no mesh, plus each piece as an ordinary model), and a shuttle row names its rig and the branch of its clips (`rig`, `mood`; pack version 3). The transport's table has two branches, Theed's (a longer path out of the royal hangar) and one the rest share; which a starport played was the server's and is ours (`moodOfRow`: the Theed hangar plays Theed's). The rig is posed as a pure function of the shared clock -- `shuttleAt` times the round by the rig's own clip lengths (`rigTimes`: the shuttle lands in 20.8 s, the transport in 26.6 s and Theed's in 32.3), and `rigPose` says which clip and where in it -- so the collector's words and what stands on the pad are one answer, and the landing plays paused with its time set, never on a mixer's own clock. Parked on the ground pose both hulls sit on their pads (the shuttle's underside at 0.0 m, the transport's struts reaching 0.9 m below); the clips come in from 250 m up and 1.8 km out (the shuttle) to 8 km up (the transport), and the shuttle's lift-off ends only 67 m up and 320 m out, where it is simply no longer drawn. A parked hull is solid: its collider is its pieces as they stand on the ground pose, added for the wait and taken away for the flight. `ShuttleRigs` (`src/world/shuttleRigs.ts`) stands, poses and takes them down, drops a stand begun for a world that has since gone, and clears on every arrival and on the way to the select screen, because the scene and the physics outlive every world. **What a shuttle sounds and shows is its client data's, timed by its own clips** (pack 4): each landing and lift-off clip carries `FORM MSGS` marks named `hpevent_<joint>_<event>` whose events are exactly the client data's `CEFT`/`CSND` names (the transport's engines start, it lands, touches down and takes off; the shuttle only lands and takes off), so nothing about when is invented. A `.cef` is PSND (sounds), CPAP (a particle, then a float read as how many seconds it burns -- the calm landing lights its engines at 6.60 s and 6.60 + 15 is 21.6, against a touchdown at 22.33) and CAMS (four numbers read as a shake's amount, rate, seconds and radius, ours). Marks on the frame after a clip's last are dropped, since they would fire the take-off at touchdown. A flame is a **standing** effect lit for its mark's window in the clip playing now and put out after (a transient one is ended by `ParticleEffects` at twice its particles' life), drawn out to `fxReach` times the files' own distances (every emitter of the game's is gone at 200 m and a transport's engines light 360 m up), and every sound is prepared when the shuttle stands, or the first landing of a session is dropped for want of a buffer. Theed's transport stands at **child yaw 0**, our correction of the emulator's quarter turn (`SHUTTLE_TURN` in `travel.mjs`, assigned on the row, never added, and `core3ref` left as the emulator wrote it): it is the only turn at which the hangar's one ship doorway, at its +Z, lets the Theed clips in and out without the hull passing through a wall, and it puts the ramp 2.8 m from the ticket collector. The outpost starports' quarter turn is on the right axis and may be the wrong way round (the lift-off grazes the pad's tower by about a metre); that waits for the owner's eye. A child's turn is read from a quaternion of any length (`childYaw`).
- **A shuttle that carries somebody is its rig made a vehicle, and the flown hull and the stood one have to be one picture.** The hull joint (`root` on the shuttle, `hold_transport` on the transport) is pinned at the model's origin by a carrier whose matrix is that joint's rest inverted, the clips are played with the chain's own tracks taken out, and the vehicle's pose is `P = Pad × J(t) × T(offset)`, `offset` being the recentring `frameModel` gives, measured at the ground pose: that is what makes the swap on the pad a millimetre (`rigHull.ts`, `rigPath.ts`). The game's take-off and landing begin and end in stretches no ship could fly -- kilometres a second, from eight kilometres up -- so where the clock hands the take-off over (the cut) and where the landing takes the hull back (the join) are worked out from the clips themselves each time the game runs (`takeoffCut`, `landingJoin`) and never typed in; the calm and Theed joins sit exactly on `joinAccel` and are pinned by windows, not frames. The hull is ghosted in every leg but parked, and says so again every frame, before anything else in that frame can leave it: a dynamic body at 150 m/s is stopped dead by the contacts against a tower, `flyShip`'s hit test cuts its cruise, and the passenger is stranded -- and the jump's own release un-ghosts the hull it lets go of in the very frame it says it has come out. A crossing up with nowhere to fly across to faces away from what a jump's arrival faces there (`zonePlace`), since the jump after it is flown straight on along the nose for up to 3.75 km, and a jump's own facing at Talus and Rori is their station's middle 782 m ahead.
