The passenger is nobody's pilot: `pilot` is null while riding, which in one line takes away the flight cursor, the dock, the guns, `playerShip` and every rider's hurt path, and the hull flies on its `autopilot.drive` with `unpaused` so an open panel does not coast it; the jump, which flies every vehicle in its transit, is handed that hull as `jumpHull` for the jump leg alone, and the trip lets go of its stick while it does. A pad's own shuttle is kept out of the picture by counted holds that survive `ShuttleRigs.clear()`, because `arrive` clears the rigs on every crossing and the far pad's hold must be there when that pad is stood. A crossing is an ordinary `travel` with `passenger: true` -- no word to the group, no launch, never `lastShipDef`, a `try` round the spawn -- and the trip keeps a crossing token, so the hull going with its world is not taken for the hull lost and a hull that comes out after the trip has ended is thrown away; nothing on the way may leave anybody in space, so a crossing up that gets nowhere and a jump refused or failed twice become a skip (`replanSkip`), and anything worse sets the passenger down at the port, once. Parked, the passenger's view is pulled in by whatever stands still between it and the hull (`blockDistance`, which never meets the hull itself) and framed close and low, or it stood on a walled starport's roof. The pilot's hand leads its own error (`errorLead`): a hull that answers in 1.2 s chasing a point two seconds ahead swayed about every final approach. The world's list of what a server holds takes down only what it stood itself (`sweptByList`): a saved server address with nobody answering emptied that list on every retry and took every ticket collector with it. A loading screen waits for a world's shuttles to be stood (`settle`, `TRAVEL_STAND_WAIT`), or a starport's rigs stood after it lifted built their programs in front of whoever had just arrived. A hull's effects are made ready (`ShuttleRigs.ready`) before it is shown at boarding, and after a crossing through the crossing's own `ready`, which `travel` waits for before its settle compiles; and the portal renderer's own two draws (a doorway's stencil polygon and the depth reset) are scenes of their own that no sweep walked, so the first lift-off of a session built both on a live frame as the view rose over its own starport: `World.compileEverything` compiles them (`PortalRenderer.ownDraws`). The crossing down comes out on the landing's own line, `downReach` from the pad, on a 6-degree glide of ours (`RIDE_TUNE.downGlide`) raised over the far ground where that is known, and a start on that line is flown straight in (`RIDE_PILOT.straightIn`): from 700 m over the pad and back along the way in from the pad rather than the join, the course dived 500 m and flew a whole circle to reach its gate. A passenger kept at or put down by a collector stands `collectorClear` toward its pad, never inside the droid. And `__debug.shaders().builtInPlay` names a program after the first material that ever built its key, not the one that built it now: two programs "of the shuttle's hull" built in play out in space were an NPC Z-95's body, whose key the plain shuttle's hull had built first, so what built a program is asked of the scene, never read off that label.
- **There is no world music in this game and none is wanted**: the owner's decision is that the only music is diegetic, the sound of players playing. The background score in `music/` (about 460 mp3s) stays unconverted, and what the `music` command reads is `player_music/`, which is a different thing and a better one. The game wrote its player music as **one track per instrument per song** -- `song06_khorn_main_lp` -- so a band is not a mix anybody has to make: 20 songs, six stems (`drum`, `nlrg`, `mand`, `khorn`, `shorn`, `xantha`), each with an intro, a main loop, an outro and eight flourishes, 1,210 parts in all, and 10 of the 20 carry all six. Hold a kloo horn and you play the kloo horn's track; stand beside a mandoviol and the two tracks are the two halves the game wrote. **A song with no track for your instrument plays nothing at all** rather than falling back on another stem, which is the whole of what holding a particular instrument means. One naming trap, already paid for: song 6's drum writes its flourishes `_1_lp` to `_8_lp` where every other stem of every other song writes `flourish01`, so read strictly it is eight flourishes silently missing with nothing to say so. What is **not** in the archives is which of the fourteen instrument templates plays which of the six stems; that is `STEM_OF` in `tools/swg/music.mjs`, one line of reasoning each, and the ones it cannot place are printed rather than given somebody else's part. Instruments come through the **weapons** pack as an `instrument` class (the owner's call: it is the one thing in the game that puts a model in a hand, names it and racks it) and fight with nothing. The samples have no `.snd`, so a template is made per part and hung in front of the bank's lookup through `SoundBank.offer` -- the same trick as Jedi Academy's sabers, with the sample written `../music/samples/<file>`. And **everybody's loop starts where the song is** rather than where they pressed, off the shared wall clock modulo `BAND_TUNE.bar`, so two players who press a minute apart are still in the same bar with nothing sent between them.
- An owned item is **what it is and which one it is**, two fields and not one: `id` is the catalogue id every copy shares and `thing` is this one, minted when it was got and never reused (`mintThing`). They were one field for years, which is exactly why two of a kind could not be told apart and why colours, stats and crafting all waited -- those are things a *thing* has and not things a *kind* has. A thing carries its own `tint` (the customizer's own variable names, cleaned by `cleanTint`), so two of one shirt may be two colours. **The dedupe moved with it**: `normalizeOwned` now keeps duplicates and drops only two rows claiming the same `thing`, and keeping one per kind and id is `migrateInventory`'s own rule, where it is right -- bringing a character into the backpack gives it one of each, and getting a second afterwards is a second thing. That move is the one thing here that could quietly cost a player: the migration builds its list from three that overlap, so without the collapse a character would have gained a duplicate of everything it was wearing, and the inventory test is what caught it. **The server and the backpack key by thing too.** `server/ledger.mjs` keeps a row per thing and each character's table by the thing's name, so two of one shirt are two rows, two backpack cells (a cell's key is the thing's name) and two lines in a trade, and a colour stays on the thing as it changes hands. A row the server made itself, and every row a server before this one wrote, is named on read `<world epoch, base 36>.<row id>`, never written down: a browser's own names always hold a `|`, so the two cannot meet, and nothing on disk was rewritten to get there -- the second copies an old server's reader left on the disk and out of memory simply come back. The row counter those names are made from lives in the world file (`itemSeq`, as the houses keep `houseSeq`), carried past every id by the very records that write a row, so it is in the log the moment the row is and a row dropped and a restart never hand its id or its name out again: worked out from the ids still in the file, the newest row's name came back the moment that row was dropped. A list from a browser that names nothing is still folded by kind and id, and an `add` with no name still only makes sure there is one, which is why the story's rewards needed no change and still give one of each. **A list from the server is paired and never minted**: `pairOwned` lays the server's rows over the backpack by name first and then by kind and id, oldest with oldest, and a pair takes the server's name, so a name and the colour on it survive every reconnect and every trade (`reconcile` used to run the rows through `normalizeOwned`, which minted fresh names on every list and wiped every colour). A thing the server hands over on its own (a reward, `Equipment.receive`) waits its turn in the equipment's queue and is here already only by its name *and* what it is: written at once, it landed before the list the server sent ahead of it was laid over the backpack, which read it as gone and took it out while the server held it. **`give` still gives one of each**; a second copy is `giveAnother`, the developer's alone, which stops in words at the server's cap (`ITEMS_MOST`, held equal to `LEDGER_TUNING.items` by a test) rather than make a copy the next list would take away. Which copy of a worn piece is the one in use is `wornThingOf` -- the record's `wornThings` choice, else the oldest -- the one answer the backpack, the trade window and the server's worn list all read; a weapon is a hand's (`heldThingsOf`, the next lesson). Two of one item being two things moved the worst hand-up: it is no longer four hundred different ids but four hundred copies of the longest (46 characters, whose name carries the id again), which written out as objects is 66 KB against the 64 KB a server takes from one browser in a second, so to a server that says `items: 2` a row goes up as the short array `[kind, what, got, thing]` (56 KB; `wireRow` in `ledger.mjs` reads both) and `itemsRelay.test.ts` measures that case and no kinder one; `mintThing` cuts the id in a name at 60 characters so no name is ever past the 80 a server takes, since a name refused is no name and two of an item with no names are folded into one. **A colour's time is never later than the server's own clock**: the newest colour wins (`Ledger.tint` refuses an older one), so a browser whose clock ran three hours fast, taken at its word, would have held that thing's colour against every other for three hours; the server takes any time past its clock as now (`cleanTintAt`), `Trade.noteTint` sends no time past `serverNow`, `OwnedItem.tintAt` is stamped on the shared clock, and `pairOwned` holds a local time to the server's clock as a list arrives. Against a server whose hail does not say `items: 2` the browser keeps to one of each and sends no colour. It holds the other way round too: a line whose hello says less than the sixth wire (`NAMED_FROM` in `relay.mjs`, told to the ledger at the claim and again at every hello, since a claim can come first) folds its list by kind and id, so a trade that would hand it a second of something it has is refused whole in the old words (`Ledger.namesThings`), or the server would hold a shirt its player could never see. The creator's old duplicate (a kit shirt it was dressed in, given twice) is collapsed once on load and the record marked `named: 1`, deliberately not `inv: 2`: a build from before reads any `inv` but 1 as a record from before the backpack and would hand it the kit again.
- A structure's portal layout comes from `portalLayoutFilename` anywhere up its template chain (`resolveTemplateString`), and many templates share one (the same cantina on three planets, `object/ship/` stations naming the hull as their appearance and the rooms as the layout): the gallery's Interiors section keys on the layout file, converting it with `resolveAppearanceToMesh` rather than the template.
- Every cell of a portal building names a `.flr`, the walkable floor the client authored for that room in the cell's own frame, and for years the converter read the name and threw the file away. `tools/swg/flr.mjs` reads it and the pack carries it as `floors.json` beside the manifest, keyed by model then cell index, merged onto `def.cells[i].floor` and `.graph` when the pack loads; a pack with no such file steers as it always did. A 60-byte triangle record is three corners, the triangle's own index, three neighbours (-1 at the mesh's edge), a normal stored pointing the opposite way to the winding, three per-edge crossable bytes, one undecoded byte, a part id derivable from the neighbours, and three per-edge portal ids that index the **cell's own** link list -- which a range test cannot show, since both that and the building-wide reading are in range on all 14,569 portal edges: what shows it is that the edge's midpoint sits on the polygon the cell reading names (median 0.00 m) and 91.83 m from the one the other names. A path graph's doorway node names the *building's* portal instead, which the converter turns into the cell's link index so one word means one thing. Every index the pack carries is checked against the list it indexes, and a triangle with a corner outside the vertex list is walled off from both sides, since crossing is decided from the flag of the triangle being left and every edge with a neighbour is crossable. The graph is inside a `FORM PGRF` on 0005 and 0006 and is chunks of the floor's own version form on 0004, in records this does not read: it is reported, not dropped. 17 of the 3,429 floors the game's cells name (3,332 before SOE's final files) have no graph at all, and 199 of the cells the archives place are the older versions, which are the same format with the counts left off and are read. `BEDG` is exactly the boundary-edge set `TRIS` already implies and `ECNT`/`ESTR` are a per-node index into `PEDG`, which stores every edge both ways: neither is read. Floors are a file of their own because the manifests are written indented and a planet's floors would take its manifest from 3 MB to about 30; the file says `mesh` for which shape it is, a building this run redid never keeps the last run's, one the pack no longer carries is dropped, and `--no-floors` removes it.
- **A door stands in a portal, and is drawn and solid by the portal renderer's own rules.** A `.pob` portal side names a door style and, from 0004, a hardpoint whose twelve floats are written whether or not its flag is set (all 7,642 portal sides of the 291 retail files read to the byte so, 7,422 of 281 before SOE's final files); the style is a row of `door_style.iff`. The `doors` pass writes `doors.json` beside every world's and the gallery's manifest and the door models once into `assets-private/doors/`; `src/world/doors.ts` stands a building's doors with its rooms (`LayoutStreamer.doorHost`) and takes them down with them. A door between two rooms is on the rooms' layer and shown when either room is seen; one on an exit is on layer 0 for the world's pass and takes the rooms' layer only for its own building's pass (`PortalRenderer.showInterior`), so it is compiled for both (`markFurniture`). Each leaf hangs on one kinematic body made once and moved (`doorBody.ts`: slid with `setNextKinematicTranslation`, and put in place outright with `setTranslation` on its first step, which would otherwise be a whole slide in one step), never added or removed as it opens, in `Group.interior` (a wall indoors and out) and marked `Physics.markWall`. **Anything that stops only at what stands still and must also stop at a shut door asks `Physics.stillOrWall`, never its own fixed-or-bodiless predicate**: the fighters' controller, the camera's block ray, both gunners' lines of fire (`Mobile.lineTo`, and `segmentBlocked(..., walls = true)` for the fighters and a lamp's sight), or a gunner stands and shoots a door it cannot see; a wall's body is never one of the movers the ragdoll filter keeps a corpse from, so a corpse lands against a shut door; and the doorway test of who can reach whom (`wallBetween`) and the indoor nav deliberately do not see doors. The room's daylight beams ask `Doors.openShare` and fade with the leaf, and a moving leaf is listed for the motion blur on any layer (`FxMoverList.anyLayer`, since it is not on the actors' layer). Who opens a door is pure and node-tested in `doorMath.ts` (`gatherOpeners`: the player unless aboard, every ready living mobile, every fighter and every peer; `openersNear`, whose `reach` knob scales the radius and never the building's size; `doorWanted`, the style's own trigger radius, the release while open, and `Doors.mayOpen`, the one seam a lock or a slicer answers through, which the corvette's locked rooms now answer). The second leaf of a double door hung half a turn round and the ease are ours (`DOORS_TUNE`, `__debug.doors`).
- **The dungeons are one world of copies, and its ground is the ground the client never drew.** `dungeon1` is a planet in the archives like any other: its snapshot places 48 Corellian corvettes, sixteen of each of three templates 150 to 400 m apart at height nought, and its buildout table adds ten each of the heroic Star Destroyer, Axkva Min's prison, IG-88's arena, the Tomb of Exar Kun and the meatlump hideout beside the tutorial's stations; the server handed each group a copy. Its terrain is flat and its one shader family is `null_a_punchout` over `texture/null.dds`, so the ground is hidden by that rule (`emptyGround` in `terrainTextures.ts`, read off the families and never off the world's name) and the world is kept off the travel lists, the galaxy and the creator (`PlanetDef.instances`, `GALAXY_LEFT_OUT`). Which copy a group goes to is a hash of the group's id, or the character's, over the copies in order of place (`copyIndex`, `copiesOf` in `src/world/instances.ts`), so a whole group lands in one copy with nothing sent and the server never needs the idea of a copy at all. Nobody stands in that world until the player is inside a copy (`PeopleDeps.scope`, `World.inCopy`): its 6,488 rows over 48 copies would otherwise stand the two caps of forty in whichever copies lie nearest the empty sky between them, and with the player in one, only that copy's crew is ever nearest. A player who comes out there in no copy -- a character saved between them, which the smoke test found -- is sent straight back out (`leaveIfStranded`), since there is nothing to walk to.
- **A building that names a portal layout is drawn as the layout, even when it names an appearance too.** Two of the 3,500 templates the worlds place name both, and read by its appearance the heroic Star Destroyer was a hull with not one of its 59 rooms in it; the other is a small Tatooine player house, which gained its two rooms and a door the same way. The rule is `resolveTemplateMesh`'s and holds for `object/building/` alone: a ship's or a station's template names its rooms too and is flown past as a hull.
- **The corvette's crews are the emulator's rows by room name, and its faction is who runs it, not who is aboard.** `readCorvette` reads 268 people in 42 named rooms over three crews; a row is a place in a named room, and each copy's own cell objects say which cell carries that name, so the rows are written once per copy (`corvetteCrewRows`, 4,288 over the 48) under keys of their own. The template's word names the run, as the emulator's `getBuildingFaction` does: the `_rebel` corvette is the Alliance's run against an Imperial crew, the `_imperial` one the Empire's against a Rebel crew, the plain one the neutral run against CorSec, and each of the three ticket takers (`takes` on its row, read from the screenplays) sends a player to its own run. The Star Destroyer's crew is ours (`OUR_CREW`: the corvette's own people sorted by the kind of room they stand in, laid on the Star Destroyer's rooms of that kind on the room's own floor triangles, marked `ours`, 2,200 rows), and the heroic dungeons stand empty, because the reference holds nobody for them. Every crew row's `respawn` is nought, the server's own: played alone a crew killed stays down until the world is left, and with a server the death is held for the longest it holds any (`STAYS_DOWN_SECONDS`, an hour) under the row's own name, so a group sent back to the same copy within the hour finds whoever it killed still down. No row can be keyed per visit instead, because every member of a group must name the same body the same way and nothing they share counts visits.
- **A copy's locked rooms are exactly the rooms its keypads and panels open.** The emulator's list of locked rooms is the very seven its keypads and panels name, so a room is locked in a copy while any of that copy's fittings opens it (`InstanceLocks`, keyed by the `PlacedObject`, which outlives the building streaming out and back, and let go with the world) and the lock answers `Doors.mayOpen`, refusing the crew inside as well as the player; the lift menu skips a stop in a locked room. A copy's fitting carries the room it opens by name and by index (`opens`, `opensCell`), so the use key at a keypad is one lookup. A copy is locked **once**, so its locks are made only from the whole list of its fittings: made from the empty list a world holds while its `fittings.json` is still on its way, the copy stayed open for the whole visit, so `lockCopy` waits for `fittingRowsIn` and `enterCopy` waits, bounded, for the rows before it stands them. A room may be opened before its copy's locks exist, and `InstanceLocks.open` holds it until they do: the room a player comes back into a copy standing in (a character saved in a room it had opened, which was otherwise locked round them), and a room another member of the group opened. That last is a word of its own (`unlock`, `server/copyWire.mjs`), sent at the keypad to the group and only the group and held nowhere, because each member keeps the locks in their own browser: kept there alone, one member's keypad left the door shut on every other screen while the one who opened it walked through it. A member who comes into the copy after the word was said finds the room locked, and the relay must be restarted before the word crosses at all.
- **Every way into a dungeon keeps the way back, in this browser.** A `WayBack` (`swg3js.wayBack.<character>` in local storage) is where the player came in from: beside the ticket taker, the spot outside a heroic entrance, or the ship left docked at the Star Destroyer with its dock and lane, which `Docking.park` docks again on the way out as if it had been flown in; lost, it is the dungeon's own way in at that world's arrival (`fallbackBack`). A travel into a room stands where it says and is never lifted onto the ground (`arriveIndoors`, read in `arrive`'s ground lift): the Star Destroyers hang 625 m up and their hangar floors stand nearly 800 m over the ground plane. A copy's crew is stood in one forced pass (`World.standPeopleNow`) and the loading screen waits for their bodies before its settle compiles them, so arriving builds no program in play (measured nought on the corvette, the Star Destroyer and the tomb). Five things about the way back were learned by losing a player. **It is kept before the copy is looked for** (`enterCopy`'s `given`), or a copy that cannot be stood in sent the player to some other dungeon's way in, ship and all. **It never leads into the world of copies** (`wayBackNowhere`, refused by `setWayBack` and read as none by `keptWayBack`, with `keptKind` still naming the dungeon for its own way in): out there is the empty sky, and the doorless building's `E enter`, which every copy offers from outside because the corvette's and the Star Destroyer's one outside portal is not passable, once wrote exactly that over a good way back. **The way out is the copy's own arrival**, found again by the room the dungeon names (`World.roomSpotOf`) for a player who came back in some other way -- a session started inside, following the group -- and never the spot they happen to be standing on. **A death in a copy comes round in that room**: `respawn` stands the player in it again (`enterCellAt` with the arrival's cell), because a reset is a move further than a walk, which the streamer takes for a teleport out of every room, and outside every room the crew go (`World.inCopy`) and so does the way out. **A member following the group in goes on foot whatever the leader did**, and the ship they fly or walk -- docked, flying or with them in its rooms -- goes into the way back with its fight and its dock (`wayBackFromHere`), so a docked ship is no refusal; a dock still repairing is finished as they leave (`Docking.finishRepair`), since the dock it is stood at on their return repairs nothing. A way back in space kept with no ship comes out in a ship at that very spot (`arriveInSpace` is handed it), never at the zone's arrival with the player then stood out of the seat.
- **Never tell a driven tab it is visible.** The game compiles with `compile` rather than `compileAsync` in a hidden tab because a hidden tab never reports a program linked; a harness that overrides `document.hidden` gets `compileAsync`, whose poll never finishes, and every dressed body stays `loading` and the next loading screen waits for good, which reads exactly like a hang in the looks. Stand in for `requestAnimationFrame` and the timers, never for the visibility. The stand-ins must be in place before the app boots, and an ES module already evaluated in a window is never evaluated again: so the tab goes first to a same-origin page that is not the game (any missing file under `assets-private/`), installs them, puts the address back to `/` with `history.replaceState` and only then writes the fetched `index.html` in. Written over the running game instead, the page comes up empty with the old `__debug` still answering.
- **A material made after the loading screen must have the program key it will keep, from its first draw.** A blaster bolt's two materials are made per colour the first time that colour is fired, and the first frame draws them before the quarter-second scan adopts them; adopted afterwards they gained the cascades' defines and hook, which are in the key, so every colour after the warm-up's two built a program in play, and a room's crew firing a colour of their own built two under the room's lights (the "MeshBasicMaterial · 1 dir, 12 point" that walking into the corvette showed: traced by wrapping `createProgram` and the prototype's `onBeforeRender` in a driven tab, which names the mesh being drawn when a program is made). They are marked `unlit` and `dry` now, so no scan ever changes their key and the warm-up's two colours build the one program every colour draws with, in the world's pass and a room's. Anything else made lazily in play -- a colour, a beam, an effect -- is the same question.
- Indoor pathing (`src/world/nav/`) is ours, no library, and it degrades in three steps rather than two: null outdoors and for anything flying; the doorway polygons alone, which are a room-to-room graph the pack has always carried and which needs no reconversion; and the real corners once a cell has a floor. A body walks a path by what it **faces** and never by its goal point, because `moveTo` is also the arrival test and a body given each corner as its goal stops a stride short of every one and never reaches the one after. Three things are not obvious. The converter's adjacency is checked and not believed (taken only when it is exactly three per triangle and every link points back across the same two corners, else rebuilt from the shared edges), since a floor read wrongly is a body walking through a wall with nothing to say so. A path is keyed on the loaded model **and** the placed building, or two copies of one building down the street from each other share corners and a body walks at the other one's. And a lift shaft is named by `lifts.ts`'s own `LIFT_CELL` and no route ever goes through one: a shaft's stops are doorways with nothing between them, so a route through it walks a body into the air, and now that a fighter falls rather than being teleported that is a visible fall. Every invented number is `NAV_TUNE` in `navMesh.ts`, live through `__debug.nav`; the agent radius is not ours, it is the player's own 0.35 and the node test fails if `player.ts` and the table disagree. A body outside a building finds its way in through `src/world/nav/doorway.ts`: each doorway to cell 0 is turned once into two points in the world, a step outside its plane and a step inside it; the door taken is the shortest walk inside (over the room graph, `doorCosts`) plus outside; the body is walked to the outside point on the outdoor grid, through to the inside one, and the building's floors take it from the moment its followed room flips; and a doorway whose sill stands well over or under the ground outside it (a station's door in the air, a dungeon's opening under the sand) is not a way in on foot. Where the goal is, is the caller's to say: a goal nobody can place steers by the old rules exactly.
- A fighter has the player's own character controller and the player's own numbers, so a wall, a step and a slope stop it, which is what finally makes its stuck check fire. Two things had to be decided rather than copied. Outdoors the planet's own height stays a floor it may never go below -- only a floor, so a ramp still lifts it -- because the heightfield colliders exist only within three chunks of the player and a controller alone would drop a wandering fighter out of the world; indoors there is no such floor on purpose, since dungeon rooms go far below the terrain, and a room whose collision the streamer has not built holds the body at the height it had (`LayoutStreamer.cellsSolid`, the `NpcDeps.cellSolid` hook) rather than letting it fall through. The collider is the player's 1.6 m capsule while `FIGHTER_BODY.halfHeight` stays 0.9, because dozens of places read that number to aim at a fighter's middle and the collider and the aim point need not be one number. What stops a fighter is fixed or bodiless colliders only: another fighter, a creature, a mobile, a vehicle, the player, a peer and a corpse all pass through, or a crowd jamming on one another would each report itself stuck and give the fight up. Its stance is the player's three-way carry chosen from what it has to fight rather than from a mouse button, and the barrel is measured against the point the next bolt is going to and folded into the spine by `Player.correctAim`'s own arithmetic; what the spine cannot take goes on the drawn body and never on the heading, which is the brain's. The aim is **held** through the recoil window and a stagger, not eased to nothing, which is the player's own early return (`aimMode` in `fighterStance.ts`): eased, the barrel never settles on anything.
- A ragdoll (`ragdoll.ts`) is built from any skeleton without a parts table: a capsule per bone long enough, ball joints at the bone origins, and the bones posed back from the bodies each frame (parents first, each local transform read under the parent's fresh world matrix). Its colliders carry `ActiveHooks.FILTER_CONTACT_PAIRS` and `Physics.hooks` keeps only their contacts with fixed or bodiless colliders, so corpses never trip the living; the character controller skips them by predicate. A corpse's own pieces meet each other only while `RAGDOLL.selfCollide` is on, and then never two a joint holds together and never two that already lay inside each other in the pose it died in (both would be thrown apart on the first step), and never the pieces of two different corpses. Rapier's JS `World.step(eventQueue, hooks)` runs the hooks only when an event queue is passed as well: without one they are silently skipped, which had every ragdoll piece colliding with every other and shivering for ever, whatever the springs were set to (`__debug.ragdoll().hooks` counts the calls), so every deliberate step outside the loop goes through `Physics.stepOnce` and never the raw `world.step()`. Nothing inside a contact hook may call into the engine: the world is mid-step, so `world.getRigidBody(handle)` from inside `filterContactPair` throws "recursive use of an object detected", which unwinds out of the filter's own callback rather than anywhere a frame can catch it — the pair is then refused by the accident of the throw and nothing the filter counts is counted. Whether the other body moves on its own is therefore worked out before the step (`Physics.movers`, refreshed only while there is a corpse in the world) and the filter touches nothing but its own two maps. The engine's per-axis joint motors and limits on a ball joint were tried and dropped: the springs are torques applied in `Ragdoll.spring` each frame, fading out after the fall, and a body near still for a second (or slow for a few) is stopped and put to sleep; every number is live through `__debug.ragdoll({...})`. A piece carries its capsule's own angular inertia, three times harder to swing across than to spin about itself (a thin one, twenty), and a wanted angular acceleration is turned into a torque through that piece's own tensor (`jointTorque`) and never through one scalar: through the mean of the three, `RAGDOLL.maxAccel` was 2.4 times itself about an ordinary limb's long axis and the damping was over-applied by the same factor, which on a thin-boned skeleton put the explicit damping term past the step size at which it grows instead of settling. `RAGDOLL.inertiaBlend` at 1 puts back the single ball every piece used to turn as. Aboard, the ragdoll lives in the room's physics with the hull's matrix as its frame. The terrain's `floor` reset (fallen out of the world) must not fire inside a building: dungeon rooms go far below it.
- The dances are the skill loop's third branch (`loop_skill:speed2:dance_N`, N the performance table's visual id, odd novice and even full) and the flourishes `skill_action_1..8:dance_N`; `nameLocomotion` must not re-rank loop_skill's branches by speed. Emotes are the 270 `emt_` clips; the `_ag` ones are NPC ambient idles.
- A terrain boundary's **feather distance is a share of the shape for a circle, a rectangle and a polyline, and plain metres for a polygon**, and one shared reader clamped every kind to [0, 1]. The consuming arithmetic was always right -- the circle takes `radius * (1 - f)`, the rectangle `0.5 * min(side) * f`, the polyline `width * (1 - f)`, while `BoundaryPolygon.isWithin` compares `f²` against squared world distances and divides by `f` -- so only the clamp was wrong, and it turned every polygon's ramp into one metre. The retail terrains carry 605 polygons with feathers up to **700 m**, 268 of them over a metre, and the ground they bound moves: mountain ranges, plateaux and the flattened basins towns sit in all stood at full height right up to their own outline, which is what made cliffs "terminate early", left the game's own props hanging on slopes that had collapsed, and popped whole squares of ground up. The oracle that proves it is in the file and nobody had read it: **from terrain version 15 a `.trn` carries the ground height at every 16 m flora tile** (`PFPM`, packed fixed point at 0.03125 m), written by the game's own editor, which is a far better witness than a placed object's authored height because plenty of objects are meant to stand off the ground. Measured against it the busiest world was wrong over 4.22% of itself, worst 348 m; it is now within half a metre everywhere that map has an opinion and not one of its 207,106 tiles is out by more than 2 m. The ground is generated in the browser from a file the packs already carry, so nothing needed converting again.
- **The ground's families come in 8 m cells, every painted pole keeps an alternate of its own, and the colour map is painted rather than read and thrown away** (`src/swg/terrain/generator.ts`). The engine finishes every chunk by giving each pole the family of the 8 m pattern corner nearest it, a tie going to the corner above (in a block of ours, which starts two poles before a corner, pole i takes pole (i & ~3) + 2's), and does it again before any layer that has affectors of its own and reads the family map through an active shader filter, whenever a family has been painted since (`snapFamilies`, `Layer.readsShaders`); our port did neither, so up to 15% of a world's families stood where the client never put them and, through those filters, so did 35% of Tatooine's collidable flora. Every pole a constant, a road or a river paints keeps a choice among its family's alternates, a byte drawn from the first float of the engine's fast random generator seeded with the pole's coordinate hash (`childChoiceAt`); a replace keeps the choice it finds, the pattern never moves one, and `childIndexOf` walks a family's children by weight to the one it picks. A legacy terrain -- every retail one but Mustafar's -- drew that choice from a generator stepped per chunk instead, and the hash is the one rule built, named `TERRAIN_GROUND_TUNE.legacyChildren` because `GROUND_TUNE` is the NPCs' ground skill. The colour map starts white and the three colour affectors paint it in single precision, every step truncated to a byte; **"multiply" is not a multiply**, it fades toward the average of the old and the new, so v on white is (v + 255) / 2, and the height ramp reads the height as it stands when it runs. Its ramps are TGAs in the archives that the terrain file only names, so they reach the generator as the bitmaps do (`attachRamp`, sent to the worker at init, from the pack's `terrain/colorramps.json` in `colorRampFile`'s shape), and a ramp that never arrives makes its affector do nothing, the engine's own rule. Every fractal any layer item reads is worked out once a pole per run (`fractalAt`, the engine's per-pole value cache), which holds the colour map to about half a millisecond a block in the workers; `colorIfCached` and `childIfCached` read cached blocks only. Measured with the generator at HEAD beside the new one over every converted world, the heights are bit for bit what they were, and the client's baked flora map agrees better everywhere: Tatooine 97.5 to 98.4% of the tiles both plant, Talus 80.4 to 82.3%, and the share of all tiles, planted or bare, up on every world (on Rori the planted-only share fell 98.33 to 98.16% on one more mismatch among nine more matches, which is why "never worse" is judged on all tiles). A far tile's samples are none of this: each is what a 16 m grid of its own paints at its own place, which already differs from the near ground's family at a quarter of Tatooine's samples (the slope and shader filters read the coarse grid), and on every world whose layout centre is off the pattern (all but Tatooine) the pattern cannot lay them on its corners at all and their choices are drawn from places no pole has; a far tile drawn the client's way has to sample on the corners itself, and moving its mesh by the centre's remainder mod 2 puts it on poles, not corners. `terrainSnap.test.ts` holds the ground to the client's own baked heights (PFPM) to within that map's rounding on every world but Talus, which is pinned where the generator before the pass left it; comparing the heights with the pattern on and off only proves the pattern moves no height, since both runs are the new code. `__debug.ground({ at: true })` says what the generator made of the pole underfoot.
- **The ground's every picture is in the pack now, and the pictures an older build reads are still the ones it drew** (terrain shaders version 4, `tools/swg/terrainShaders.mjs`, moved out of `cli.mjs` so a test runs the very writers a conversion runs). Every child of every family is written -- colour, bump map decoded by its slot through `normalDecode.ts` like every other normal map (every retail ground shader's is NRML, plain), and gloss -- in the family list's own order with its weight, so the index `childIndexOf` gives is the index into `children`, and each family carries its place in that list as `priority` and its shader's MATL specular colour (white on every retail ground shader but 23, which are black; power nought on all). The version-3 fields (`file`, `normal`, `specular`) are the **heaviest** child's, not the first-listed one's as the design said: twelve retail families (five of them Dathomir's) list a lighter alternate first, and the heaviest is what the converter drew before, so an older build draws a new pack exactly as it drew the old one (`primary` names it). The bump map's alpha is a height and goes into the gloss picture's **green**, never an alpha, because a browser drawing an image into a 2D canvas premultiplies; red and blue stay the AUX0 mask the old loader reads, a shader with a bump map and no gloss (thirteen of Kashyyyk's, whose effect is plain dot3) gets red nought and keeps its height, and one with neither gets no gloss file. The client's three border masks are read through the shaders its executable names (`shader/blnd_onequarter.sht`, `onehalf`, `threequarter`) and its cloud tile through `shader/terrain_cloudtile.sht`, all four written as their **alpha as grey**, the only channel either is drawn by. Their alpha is four bits, steps of 17, so "the shared edge profile within 5" can only hold on average: sampled every 32 px the three-quarters mask's edge reads 170 where the others read 187, and along the whole edge it meets the half's within 1.1 levels. `terrain/colorramps.json` is `colorRampFile`'s shape with the world's `reference` beside the ramps (`readColorReference`): the medians of the colour map's luminance and of each channel over the places that are not black, the generator run once over the whole map as one grid every 64 m (half a second a world); measured over every world it sits within 0.038 of the median of 128 random blocks of 2 m poles, it is null on dungeon1 (black everywhere) and 1 on Mustafar and Kashyyyk's pob dungeons (white). A run leaves `terrain/shaders/` holding exactly the pictures its list names, now named for the shader rather than the family. Measured: 785 children over 324 families on nineteen worlds, 2,265 pictures and 179 MB where one picture a family was 927 and 60 MB, 29 s for all of it; `groundTextures.test.ts` runs the writers over five worlds out of the archives into a folder of its own and holds every picture to the archives' own texel for texel, and does the same for any pack on disk at version 4 (`SWG3JS_PACKS`). Three holes its first cut had, each of which let a broken writer pass every check: a child the archives hold that was never written dropped out of every comparison (which only reads what was written), so a child whose shader and texture are there must now have its pictures; a null reference passed as "a world black everywhere", and the reference's per-channel medians were only range-checked while a 0.045 window on the luminance could not tell a median from a mean or Rec. 709 from Rec. 601, so the reference is now worked out again in the test from the same 64 m grid and held to the file to the fourth place, luminance and each channel, null only where that grid finds no place that is not black; and none of the three worlds had a black place, so the rule that leaves them out was never exercised, which is why Kashyyyk's Rryatt trail (a fifth of itself not black) and dungeon1 (none) are among the five.
- **The ground stands on the client's height points, and `groundChunkOrigin` is the one function that says where** (`src/world/groundTiles.ts`). A pole's terrain x is a multiple of 2 and game x is the layout centre less it, so a vertex every 2 m from a multiple of 64 stood a metre off every pole on the twelve worlds whose centre is odd in an axis, and the client's fans were never drawn there (Naboo's ground out by up to 3.8 m, Talus's by 24 m, the physics heightfield with it). A chunk's ground now starts at its bucket's corner moved by the centre's remainder each way (`groundShift`); the mesh, the heightfield (`Chunk.origin`), the far tiles and their cut all ask `groundChunkOrigin`, and the streaming buckets -- which chunk a place is in, for the flora, the props and the layout -- keep their own multiples of 64, a ground a metre over its bucket's edge being still that bucket's. **The far tiles move with it**, which the design did not ask for: moving only their cut opened a metre-wide sliver between the near and the far ground at the edge of the detailed ground, while moved too their 16 m quads stay square with the chunks' edges and their samples stand on poles; `heightIfCached` finds a far grid by the same shifted corner. `groundAlign.test.ts` builds real chunks over every converted world's own `SwgTerrain` in node and asks a real Rapier heightfield; the next wave's grass must stand on this mesh, so it imports the same function.
- **The ground is drawn the client's way by one program for every planet, and every triangle carries its tile whole** (`terrainTextures.ts`'s tile program, the composition mirrored line for line in `groundMath.ts`; terrain shaders version 4). Each 2 m cell lies in one 4 m tile, so the chunk builder works each tile out once from its four corner poles (`tileLayers`: the families bottom first by their place in the family list, each the child its first corner's choice picks, each overlay's mask the corners it holds) and writes it on all three vertices of every triangle in it, as bytes: the texture-array layers, the overlays' mask codes (one plus four times the kind plus the quarter turns), the vertex's own place in its tile (in halves, so a fragment's place is interpolated exactly and no `fract` of a world coordinate ever breaks a mip chain) and the colour map at its own pole, read from the cached block in one lookup (`groundIfCached`), never generated. The masks are as the converter wrote them -- the quarter covers the image's top-left, the half its left side, the three-quarter all but the bottom-right -- and `turnTile` turns a tile's corners onto them; two opposite corners take the stronger of two quarters (ours). The pictures are sampled along the terrain's own x (`mirror`, inferred, a uniform), so a bump's slope toward the picture's right is toward the world's -x and its sign follows. Derivatives are taken once at the top of the colour block and handed to `textureGrad` in every branch, since most tiles hold one family and the program branches on it. Nothing in the program depends on a world, so the material is the session's (`World.groundTilesMat`, never disposed), each world hands it its textures (`bindTiles`), and a travel builds no ground program (measured nought on every leg Tatooine, Naboo, Tatooine, Corellia, Dantooine, Mustafar). **A version-3 pack, or `swg.ground.tiles` '0', draws the program of before to the character**, which `groundShader.test.ts` holds against a copy taken before the tile program came, and a chunk is built with tiles only while the world holds a tiler (`Terrain.tiler`), else with its corners' families as always. The tint's modes, the gain cap, the shine, the mirror, the masks, the opposite rule, the clouds and the view are uniforms; the order, the children and the child's corner decide what a chunk is built with, so `__debug.ground` builds the ground round the player again for them. The cloud shadows lay the client's grey over the colour as it would be shown, which in an HDR frame we can only estimate by holding the lit colour to one (ours), and never brighten. Two traps found on the way: a `BufferAttribute` that is normalised hands its bytes back over 255 from `getComponent`, so a test reads the array; and `World.unload` runs before the first world has a terrain. Three more from review. The loader reads every picture at its own size: a canvas sized to the colour layers cut the masks and the cloud tile to nought past its edge on the copies' world, whose colours are 4 px and masks 256; and a terrain whose tiles are not 4 m (`tilesFit`: the copies' are 16) keeps the program of before rather than laying its cells on tiles of the wrong size. What the loader makes of the pictures is pure (`tileEntries`, `assembleTileArrays`, `tileLookups`, `makeTiler`) and `groundLayers.test.ts` runs it over the packs' own pictures and the planets' own shader groups, as `groundAlign.test.ts` runs the game's own tiler over real chunks and far tiles and the world adds a chunk's heightfield only through `chunkHeightfield`; the black-specular rule changes no byte of the converted packs today, every black child's mask being written nought already, so only a child made for it shows it. And `groundSelfTest` holds the program to `groundMath.ts` on everything a node test cannot compile: the gain cap binding (`capBinds`), the cloud tile's place and drift both ways round, the bump's lean and gloss (`view: 'bump'`, the lean kept before the rain's rings touch it) and the flat shine under one light, each of which a deliberate break of the shader was seen to fail in the browser.
- **`player`, `parts` and `species` stamp no format on what they write**, unlike the mobiles packs, so `status` cannot tell an old rig from a new one and nothing will ever ask for them again on its own. That hole has now bitten twice: once leaving nineteen of twenty species unable to swing a lightsaber, and once leaving every rig without an aimed blaster pose. The answer both times is to name a clip the new conversion must produce and look for it (`jkaClips` for the first, `loop_*combat_standing_aimed` for the second -- and *standing*, because the prone aimed loops survived the `DIR ` bug and searching for "aimed" alone finds twelve clips and proves nothing). Any converter change that alters what those three commands write needs a check of that shape, or the owner's install silently keeps the old thing for ever.
- An area's environment family is painted by the terrain's AENV affectors exactly as ASCN paints shader families (DATA: int32 family, int32 use-override, float clamp; the family's own clamp from EGRP > version form > EFAM > DATA: int32 id, name, three colour bytes, float clamp), and `SwgTerrain.environmentAt` reads the map the generator fills. The environment table's family names match case-insensitively, because one planet's terrain capitalises names its table spells in lower case. Life Day's families are seasonal: one planet's Life Day layer is active in the archives and covers a town, so seasonal families are kept in a second map and only win in season, leaving the place underneath known the rest of the year. **A top-level layer the file switches off is never prepared, so it paints nothing** (`TerrainGenerator.prepare`, the engine's own `isActive` check): ours prepared every one, which snowed over Wayfar and Dearic (the publish-54 Tatooine and Talus terrains SOE's names list holds carry their Life Day layers switched off) and stood Talus's ground up to 2.6 m off the client's own baked heights through its switched-off `paths`; Talus now meets them everywhere, and `terrainSnap.test.ts` no longer pins it. **And a layer that paints Life Day's area runs only in season** (`TerrainGenerator.runs`, `TERRAIN_GROUND_TUNE.lifeDay`, ours: the client ran Corellia's Doaba Guerfel snow all year): the main thread sets it from `Weather.lifeDayToday()` before a world's terrain is made and hands it to the worker, so the ground's snow follows the Life Day setting at the next arrival while the sky follows it at once; the converter runs out of season, so the walk grids and the colour references are the rest of the year's. A family id outside a byte is noted and paints nothing, and a family from a building's own layer file is cleared rather than painted, since nothing remaps that file's ids onto the planet's. A cached read is free (a sixth of a microsecond); an uncached one generates the covering 64 m block on the main thread, one to four milliseconds, which is what `environmentIfCached` is for and why `environmentAt` counts itself in the "sync grids" figure. A pack with its colour ramps (terrain shaders version 4) adds about half a millisecond to that, since the ramp affectors then paint every pole: measured in node over 200 random blocks a world, Tatooine's median went from 2.4 to 3.0 ms and its slowest twentieth from 3.1-3.5 to 4.2-4.5 ms, Naboo's and Corellia's median from 1.7-1.9 to 2.0-2.2 ms. The main thread keeps its ramps all the same, because the block it generates is the one cached, and the ground and `colorIfCached` read their colours from it; a main-thread block made without colours would need a mark of its own and a second generation before the ground could use it.
- `convertParticle` caches per output pack. Before, a run over several planets wrote later planets' manifest entries for particle effects without their files.
- A water shader's CUBE and `_SKY` slots are never sampled by any retail effect: `water.eft` reads MAIN, NRML and an ENVM placeholder the engine filled at run time, most likely from the area's day and night map. **What every reflective surface and the water reflect is our own sky dome, captured every four seconds, and not the client's cube maps** (the owner's call, 2026-09-26, and made to be undone: `REFLECTIONS.source` in `envmap.ts`, `localStorage['swg.reflections'] = 'game'` or `__debug.reflections({ source: 'game' })` puts the areas' own cube maps back). Each capture is a whole PMREM render target, kept and freed as one (`World.useEnvironment`: disposing only its texture freed nothing, one target leaked per capture); the interior cells' material copies are registered like their originals so none holds a freed map; a disposed material leaves the registry by itself; and `World.reflectionsReady` captures the sky before the loading screen compiles, since the first capture landing on a live frame rebuilt every reflective material at once. The per-shader cubes are converted into `water.json` all the same; `await __debug.waterFx({ env: 'shader' })` shows them, dimmed at night by the sky's clear colour against its brightest. The client's water pixel shader is unlit, `(main·a·0.5 + spec + env) / (a + 2)` with alpha `0.5 + a/2`, which is where each body's opacity comes from; ripple is the normal map's mean slope against the reference map and drift its first TSNS scroll rate (TSNS is a form holding one 20-byte chunk: a tag, then four floats). Lava is the same data path with no colour and no cube: `isLavaWater` takes the terrain's water type, then the pack's entry, then the shader's name, because five of the lava tables are typed 0.
- `envMapCubeUVHeight` is in three's program cache key, so a reflective material whose environment changes height recompiles mid-play. Every environment a water body can see is therefore filtered from a 128 px cube (a PMREM 512 high): the water cubes, the planets' sky maps, the dome (`fromScene` with `size: 128`), and a black stand-in made at startup that every body carries from the moment it exists, so the first real map to arrive swaps a texture and nothing more. A sky map of any other height (a space zone's 256 px maps) is never given to water. `setEnvironment` also no longer overwrites each material's own `envMapIntensity` — the water's authored 0.55 was being forced to 1.
- Everything alive is one kind with one key: the player, the creatures and the fighters all implement `Living` (`src/combat/kit.ts`), the world hands out one list of them (`World.targets()`), and `damage(amount, from, push, source)` carries who struck so that what is hurt can turn on them. Keys start at 2 (`nextLivingKey`) and the player's is a named 1 (`PLAYER_KEY`), so no key is ever 0 and "nobody" can be stored as a falsy number without the player being the one thing that vanishes. Anything with a timer runs on `World.simTime`, which only `stepLiving` advances, so `__debug.advance` exercises it.
- A shot between two players is decided on the shooter's screen and subtracted only on the screen of whoever was hit (`src/net/combatNet.ts`, `server/combatWire.mjs`): everybody is drawn ten times a second and glided in between, so the two screens never agree exactly, someone has to be right, and having the one who was hit take the number off means nobody can ever disagree about how much of anybody is left or that somebody is dead. Every other browser flies its own copy of the bolt `inert` — drawn, heard, whining past the ear, able to be turned away by a blade, and never reaching `damage`, `takeBolt`, `onPlayerHit` or the bolt's own `onHit`, or one trigger pull would be paid for twice — and an inert copy's ray passes over every peer's body as well, since the muzzle it left is inside the shooter's own. Where a bolt stopped is not each browser's to work out: each traces its own streamed world, so the one who fired sends the point and every other cuts its copy short there (`Bolts.cutShort`), which is what puts the mark and the burst in the same place on both screens. A blade turning one of those bolts away is a word and not a second bolt: today's block makes the bolt the local player's own and is local-player only, so on the shooter's screen the blocker is a peer capsule and the deflection would never happen there at all — the blocker's `blocked` cancels that shot on every screen, the shooter's own real bolt included, and the blocker fires its own from the block point, one bolt in and one bolt out. A death is announced by the one who died, with whoever struck inside the last eight seconds, and plays as a clip, never a ragdoll (nothing carries bone motion across, so a ragdoll would end in a different heap on every screen). Health goes over five times a second from a timer and only when it has moved, never from a frame, and is what fills the group roster's bars. Damage between players is the server's own switch (`--friendly-fire`), off unless it is asked for, with the game's own duel inside it (`COMBAT_DUEL` and `COMBAT_PEACE` at the game's own 128 m); the browser refuses to send a blow it knows cannot land and the server drops it too, so a browser on another build cannot hurt anybody in a peaceful world either.
- A fighter's body is kinematic and its update writes its height from the terrain every frame, so a Force power that only set a position was undone before it was seen: holding and throwing one takes a branch in that update (held, falling, or on the ground) and a knock hard enough to lift it, not just a `holdAt` method.
- `AnimationUtils.makeClipAdditive` rewrites a clip's values in place and leaves `clip.blendMode` behind as the mark. A clip shared between rigs (the species packs have been shared since the fighters stopped parsing them each) must only be converted once, and on the rig's own path, where the clip object itself is converted, `blendMode` is the whole guard and no marker of ours is needed. It is not a guard for a caller that wraps a clip: a wrapper built from `clip.tracks.slice()` shares the track objects, so the conversion lands on the shared values while the mark lands on the throwaway clip.
- A material that is disposed must be taken out of the portal renderer's `Set` and the shadow cascades' `Map` (`World.forgetMaterials`); both are strong. The portal renderer's is the one that costs per frame -- it is walked once per stencil change, about a dozen times a frame -- and the cascades' is a leak that shows as a stutter when the shadow distance changes. A material must also join the cascades (`csm.setupMaterial`, inside `World.adoptMaterials`) before its program is compiled, because the defines and the `onBeforeCompile` are part of the program key; an unlit material (`userData.unlit`) is kept out of them altogether for the same reason in reverse.
- A vehicle's own materials (the glow sprite's, each trail's, a refit's new trails included, each clear pane's) go on `v.ownedMaterials`, and `World.disposeVehicle` forgets and disposes them once (`v.disposed` guards a second call): every way a vehicle leaves goes through it (the destroyed branch, the garage's clear, the NPC tab's clear, a patrol put away by distance, a spawn thrown away, `unload`; a refit whose ship went while it staged has its spare trails' materials forgotten by `World.refitVehicle`, since they were pushed after the dispose), or the portal set walked a dozen times a frame grows with every ship that ever came and went. A clear-glass stand-in shares the cached hull's geometry and carries `userData.shared` (so `Vehicle.dispose` leaves the geometry) and `userData.paneStandIn` (so a spawn finds it to compile). A custom paint's copies are `ShipPaint`'s own, forgotten and disposed by it.
- The colour grade (`src/core/fx/gradeMath.ts`, pass `colorGrade`) runs in linear HDR just before the OutputPass. Every tint keeps the pixel's luminance, and its contrast turns about 0.18 divided by the exposure and leaves anything below a thirty-second of that and above thirty-two times it untouched, so it cannot crush shadows or clip highlights; a raise in saturation is scaled by how grey the pixel already is, so strong colours never clip. A planet's mood (gloom, heat, warmth) is hand-set in `GRADE_LOOKS`, not read from the sky: a planet's environment blocks differ region by region (one planet's keys run from dim to bright), so a mood read from whichever block is in use is an accident of block order. The sky supplies only the hues and how hard the key stands against the fill. Rooms and ship interiors take 30% of it for the whole view, eased over a second both ways; an exits mask from the portal stencil was worked through on paper and dropped, because it dipped the street on the way out and flickered on the door plane. `__debug.gradeSelfTest()` checks the shader against the JavaScript mirror that `npm run test:grade` sweeps.
- Mustafar's lava is terrain water: a table is lava when its water type is 1, when water.json's entry says `kind: 'lava'`, or, with no entry, when its shader names lava (`isLavaWater`; all 102 of Mustafar's). World skips lava in `applySwgWater` and draws it in `loadLava` with `createLavaMaterial` (emissive, unlit, opaque, polygon offset so the ground wins ties), one merged mesh per look, never the water material. A lava shader's MATL is four ARGB groups and a power: the third is loopTime then the flow, the fourth (a, colorScale, colorBias, tcScale); flow × loopTime is a whole number, which is why the loop has no seam. Its TFNS entry BLUM (stored `MULB`) is the bloom factor; the glow is on ramp alpha × that factor, and 0.203 to 0.213 lights about the top tenth. The ramp goes inline in water.json (a PNG's alpha may lose colour in the browser's decoder); the crust and the 128³ noise volume (`water/noise_volume_lava.r8`, 2,097,152 bytes) sit under `water/`. The texture coordinate `tc0` is read as world (x, y, z), an inference (`__debug.lava({ axes })`). The noise volume is mipmapped and the index fades to its mean where the veins are sub-pixel, or kilometre-wide flows sparkle through bloom. A pack converted before the lava look draws a hand-made stand-in, and every missing piece falls back on its own. `loadLava` warns with the water command, and `status` asks for it, when an entry has a lava effect and no `lava` block. An entry whose block is null (an unreadable MATL) asks for nothing, since reconverting does not mend it. `World.leave()` bumps the load token, since it unloads with no load after it and an in-flight lava or sky load would otherwise commit into an empty scene. Lava takes no ripples or spray: `emitRipples` skips a point over a lava table, since the lava planet has a global sea (hidden) and the ripples would otherwise run over the lava.
- Anything that floats reads the **drawn** sea and chases it (`src/world/afloat.ts`): the hulls have ridden the swell since they learned to, but the player's swim and every swimming creature read the flat table underneath, so a crest rose over the head and a trough dropped out from under the feet while the body held perfectly still. Reading the surface is only half of it -- holding a surface that moves is no longer standing still in y, so both now ask `afloatVelocity` for the speed that keeps them on their line. Three things are not obvious. The creatures' own hold (`(want - y) * 2.5 - vy * 0.3`, capped at 6) was **not** reused as it stood: 2.5 is a good number against a target that does not move, which is what it was really tuned for (a flyer's hover, a flat lake), and it is 27 cm out of step on an eight-second swell a metre and a half trough to crest, so floating takes `track` 8 and the flyers keep 2.5 and their own line of code. The answer is written straight onto the body (`vel.y`, `setLinvel`) rather than accelerated toward, so it is a first-order chase that cannot ring -- which is why the player's swim must **not** ease it again as it eases the dive and the jump, since two lags in series put the body a quarter of a metre behind the wave it should be riding. And it never commands a step that would carry the body past the line, because the stiffness is a console knob and an explicit step whose gain times the frame is over two oscillates for ever. The surface itself comes from `World.seaSwellOverFlat`, which takes the flat height the caller already has (the swim's lava-filtered column, a creature's terrain table) and answers 0 on a lake, in the shallows, on a planet with no sea and wherever the sea is off, so everywhere but the open sea nothing changed at all. `__debug.afloat` is the knob and prints how far under the drawn surface the player is lying, which should sit at the swim depth and stay there while the wave moves.
- Water writes no depth, so its reflections come from a mask: every body's twin (the same shader compiled with `WATER_FX_MASK`, in `WaterBodies.maskScene`) is drawn into three targets sharing the scene depth: normal, view depth and coverage (the occlusion reads that alpha); the environment term the lit water leaves out, weighted by its blend and fog; the weight a traced colour gets. While `WaterBodies.active`, decided in `drawFrame` from `postfx.passWanted` before the scene is drawn, the lit water's environment term is multiplied by 0 and the `waterReflections` pass adds it back mixed with what the trace found, in place, and its `enabled` returns that same decision. `passWanted` therefore must never ask a pass's own `enabled`, or the water shuts itself out the first frame it says no. `await __debug.waterFx({ view: 'envOnly' })` must look like the pass off.
- The swelling sea is double-sided, transparent and writes no depth, so at low angles one pixel blends several layers of it (a crest's front, its back, the next crest). A mask that keeps one surface a pixel holds one layer's environment term and the picture darkens at the crests with the pass on; the mask's second and third targets are therefore blended exactly as the lit water is (`src + dst (1 - a)`), and the first writes alpha 1 so it is simply replaced (it keeps the last surface drawn, not always the nearest). Blending makes draw order matter: each body has two one-sided twins, back then front, which is what three draws for a double-sided transparent material (a single double-sided twin draws the same but has three flip its side and look its program up twice every draw), and `syncTwins` orders the bodies as three's transparent list ordered the lit meshes (`sortByDraw`: group order, render order, clip z of the bounding sphere's centre far first, id). A fixed order put a lake over the sea off by up to 25/255.
- Whether water is on screen at all comes from a WebGL2 occlusion query of the twins drawn into the scene target with every write off (`WaterVisibility`); WebGL answers a query only after control has returned to the browser, a frame or more late, so a test that draws frames in one synchronous loop never sees an answer. The mask's targets (88 MB at 1440p) are given back after ten seconds of effects time undrawn: detach the shared depth, dispose, attach it again, and three rebuilds the target on its next bind.
- A screen-space reflection hit must be a crossing (in front at one sample, behind at the next) confirmed after refinement within a depth-scaled thickness; a thickness that grows with the coarse step turns every ray passing behind a post into a hit and smears silhouettes down the water. The half-resolution depth texel `t` holds full pixel `2t + 1`, so the half-resolution trace and its upsample read the mask there too.
- A lit blade's light on what is around it is the `bladeGlow` effects pass: it rebuilds each pixel's view-space position and a normal from the scene depth and adds every blade as a segment light (inverse square with a soft core and a smooth knee, wrapped Lambert, the surface's hue, zero at 3.5 m), and the tuning is `BLADE_GLOW_TUNE` in `src/core/fx/bladeGlowMath.ts`, mirrored by a node test. A pixel brighter than the frame's lights could make any surface (`litCeiling`, from `World` and `Effects.litIrradianceNear` and the torch) is taken for glow and its hue is not used. It cuts the light at walls with an 8-tap march in screen space toward the blade (`BLADE_GLOW_MARCH`): a sample counts as blocked only when the depth is a little in front of it, not more than a thickness in front, and not nearer the eye than the path's nearer end by more than 0.3 m, which is what keeps a body between the camera and the path from shadowing the ground; one march per pixel, the player's blades together toward the strongest, or the strongest fighter's. While the pass is switched on the blades ask for no pooled flash lights (`App.bladeGlowOwnsLight`, which reads the setting, not whether a blade is on screen, so a blade crossing the screen's edge never swaps one light for the other). The pool serves the latest request when full, and flashes age only in `effects.update`, so what must keep a light is asked for last: the ship's room lights come after `world.update`, fighters' glows just before them. Blade light is read from the renderers (`SaberBlade.glowing`, `drawnBase`, `drawnTip`, `ignition`), not `saberSegments`, which skips the thrown and orbiting blades. A fighter's blade ends live on the fighter; they were module-level and every fighter's glow sat at the last one's blade. The frame context starts with `UNSET_BLADES` and the pass warns once if the game never hands its own list over.
- Another player's lightsaber is a `SaberBlade` like the player's and every fighter's, drawn from the holder already hanging on their hand bone and joining the frame's blade list through `collectBlades`'s `more` list, so with the blade glow pass on it lights the world through that one pass exactly as the player's does. With the pass off it is not the same: the pool is four flash lights, the player's own blade, the fighters' glows and a ship's room lights all ask earlier in the frame and a flash holds its light for its whole life, so a peer's blade (which asks last) takes only one standing dark and often goes unlit. A second `SaberBlade` builds no program: three keys a shader stage on its source text, and the player's own blade groups are in the scene from the start with their ribbons hidden, so `compileAllAsync`'s `traverse` builds that program behind every session's loading screen. Which blades a peer has out is read from what is in their hands and never from a style on the wire, and which hand a holder is on is the hand it names, never its place in the list (the peers skip an empty hand). A peer who leaves hands their renderers on to the next peer rather than disposing them: a blade's materials are in the portal renderer's set and the cascades' map, and people churn all evening.
- Three's renderer counts a frame per `render()` call, and the portal renderer makes several a frame: `scene.updateMatrixWorld()` runs once per call that takes the scene as its root (up to eight outside a building, three inside), and `Skeleton.update()` once per call for every skinned mesh drawn, re-uploading its bone texture each time. So `PortalRenderer.render` turns `scene.matrixWorldAutoUpdate` off after the first pass that walks the scene and puts it back in a `finally` (`matrixOnce`; `__debug.mobileTune({ passMatrices: 'every' })` measures without it). Nothing may move an object's transform between the passes of one frame; `onBeforeRender` may rewrite vertices (the engine trails do), never a matrix. Only not drawing a thing saves its skeleton, which is why a mobile is culled as a whole group.
- A converted actor is culled as one group against a sphere of its own (`plan.cull`, in its frame above its feet), by setting `group.visible`, and each of its meshes is then culled by three in every pass and shadow cascade against that same sphere, grown by `SKELETON_TUNE.sphereScale` and set once in the mesh's own frame at `attach` (`fitCullSpheres` in `src/world/bodyCull.ts`; a fighter's from its measured height): `Frustum.intersectsObject` reads a mesh's `boundingSphere` in that mesh's own frame and a skinned mesh computes one from its posed vertices only while it is null, so one set by hand is kept. The sphere is a standing body's, so anything that lays a body along the ground turns `frustumCulled` off for as long as it lies there (`cullsOneByOne`): a ragdoll, a body dead in its held death clip (every death on a world a server holds, and every one waiting in the ragdoll queue), a knockdown, a fighter prone, and an idle lying at full length (`LYING_IDLE`: the dead and restrained moods), each of which puts the head outside the sphere; every change of those asks again (`applyCull`). The manager owns `visible`, `castShadow` (at 2 Hz, by size class and screen, and never for a body whose sphere reaches none of the shadow cascades' light boxes, `reachesCascades` against the frusta three itself culls each cascade's casters by; never a distance from the camera, since the cascades' reach is a depth along the view and their boxes reach past it toward the sun) and `frustumCulled` on every mesh from `attach` on, whatever the prototype's preparation set. A clone shares one skeleton between the meshes that shared one in the model (`cloneShared`), and inside the portal renderer's passes a skeleton is worked out and uploaded once a frame (`src/core/skeletonOnce.ts`), which is one more reason nothing may move a bone between the passes of one frame.
- A mobile pack clip may have no track for a joint that never leaves its bind pose: three blends such a property toward the value it had when the binding was made. So nothing poses a mobile's bones before its mixer binds them, and nothing stands a body up again in place: one killed is taken away, and whatever comes back at its post is a new body, cloned, hung and bound fresh, so a ragdoll's pose can never be left on the joints no clip keys (the rest pose a respawn once put back is no longer kept at all).
- The mobile catalogue is fetched once at boot and is usually still in flight when the first planet loads, so everything reads it through a getter (`MobileCatalogue.loaded`, the manager's `catalogue()` dependency) and treats "not yet" as absent. A manager that captured null at load would be dead for the life of that planet.
- A mobile ignores a blow from its own side from one that would never pick a fight with it (`Mobile.damage`): a pack spitting at the player through its own ring otherwise turned on itself and feuded for the rest of the fight. A creature's spit falls, so it is aimed over the target by the drop over its flight.
- A mobile's speed is two numbers, the acceleration's ramp and what the chosen clip's feet show (`stepGait` in `mobiles/gait.ts`): the gait is chosen from the ramp, and the body moves at what the gait shows. Kept as one, the ramp was pulled back every frame to the walk clip's cap (1.8 times its speed) and a two-gait pack whose cap sits under the walk-run midpoint never broke into its run: a bantha chased at 1.5 m/s against its 5.2.
- A mobile's building room is followed through the portals as the player's is (`LayoutStreamer.trackCell`, from the manager four times a second and every 2 m), never read from the rooms' bounds: rooms overhang their hull, and a body on the street beside one was taken to be inside and lost the terrain from its colliders. The terrain-floor reset skips a mobile inside, as it does the player. Mobiles are in `World.targets()` once their model is up (`m.ready`), so the powers, the Bounty Hunter's weapons, the fighters and each other find them.
- Turning shadows off must flip only `renderer.shadowMap.enabled` and leave the cascade lights' `castShadow` alone: with no shadow-casting directional light, three's cascade shader applies all three cascade lights unshadowed, three times the sun. With `castShadow` kept, it takes its "one light" branch.
- Ambient occlusion (`src/core/fx/ssao.ts`) darkens only the ambient share of each pixel's light. It re-evaluates, at the reconstructed surface, the lights that drew it, from `ctx.lights` (`src/core/fx/lights.ts`): `World.fillFxLights` gives the sky's hemisphere, fill and cascaded sun (one shadow tap) and the rooms' ambient, parallel light and lamps (each lamp with the cell `updateInteriorLights` lit it from), and `drawFrame` adds the flash pool and the torch. A light added to the game but not there makes AO too strong where it shines. Which set a pixel takes comes from the stencil the portal renderer leaves: inside a building 1 is rooms and 2 the world through exits; outside 1 is the world and 3 is rooms seen through doors (the door restore increments 2 to 3 rather than writing 1). It is read by an Equal-tested draw into a target sharing the scene's depth-stencil texture. Anything brighter than a surface lit that way could be is left alone, which keeps blades, bolts and glows from darkening, and water is skipped wherever the water mask's coverage is set. `__debug.ssao({ split: 0.5 })` compares, and `{ view: 'region' }` shows the rooms' pixels.
- A `sampler2DShadow` uniform must never be left null: three's empty shadow texture is never uploaded (its version stays 0), so null binds no texture, and ANGLE drops the whole draw with INVALID_OPERATION, silently once its error cap is hit. Bind a real 1x1 `DepthTexture` with a `compareFunction` and `needsUpdate = true` instead (the ambient occlusion's `noShadow`). The half-resolution depth samples half texel i at full texel 2i + 1 (its centre at 2i + 1.5), so everything upsampling from it places a half texel there.
- The heat haze is the client's 2d_heat_composite: sources draw into a half-resolution RGBA16F buffer as (r, noise × r, noise × r) with additive blending, so overlaps average; the composite moves the UV by (noise − 0.5) × r × 0.01 × strength only where the landing pixel is hot, and not onto or from anything nearer than the farthest depth in the pixel's bilinear footprint. Sources sit in `HeatSources` (lava from World, plumes from providers that push numbers and a phase they integrate themselves; one provider that throws loses only its own plumes) and draw on layer 30 in the heat product's own scene, depth-tested by texel against `linearDepthHalf`. `gather` runs only inside `PostFX.end`; console helpers read stats. The client's noise volume has mean 0.49 and spread 0.077; the runtime 32³ noise matches it.
- A particle effect placed with a `frame` (a hull's live matrixWorld) simulates in that frame and is carried into the world as its quads are built: anything held or played aboard is placed that way, and the planet's ground and the weather's kill are never applied to it. A held flame's heat is stamped each step and ignored when not held within 100 ms; `BountyHunterKit.coolDown()` (class switch, leaving a ship) drops the flame's heat and its held effect.
- The lens flare follows what the sky draws, not the light: a flare body is a `sun_*.sht` with a glow whose two sprites loaded (Yavin 4's sun slot is the gas giant and has none, Dathomir draws no sun, Mustafar's moon slot is a second sun and flares at night, in slot 1), and in space the brightest star sprite group, which is mostly nowhere near the zone's main light; each keeps a fixed slot, and `SwgSky.flareLights` reads the sprites' own position, opacity and `visible`. The sky's sprites hang at 3500 m and are depth-tested, so the occlusion counts depth as sky from 98% of that distance along each pixel's view axis, compared as `1 - depth` (exact near the far plane, unlike a linearised distance); fog does not dim the sprites, so it does not dim the flare. The occlusion is smoothed in a 2x1 target the element draw reads in its vertex shader, so nothing is read back.
