The composite never lifts a pixel past exposed luminance 0.9 (display 0.874, under the 0.888 where white begins) and adds nothing above it, which is why by day the flare is mostly a streak and ghosts; `__debug.flareProbe()` counts it, and `__debug.flare().sources[i].turn` is how to steer to a star when flying.
- Inside a building the world seen through its doorways is drawn after the rooms (the exits' stencil, a depth reset, the world pass), so anything additive drawn only with the rooms (`INTERIOR_LAYER`) is painted over wherever a doorway is behind it; outside, the world pass draws actors over the whole screen. The room motes (`RoomAir`) are on the actor layer, drawn in both passes and clipped by the stencil, and hidden on any frame the portal renderer draws from outside, since `cameraBuilding` can flip for a frame on the door line. The daylight beams are the doorways extruded along the sun, evaluated per pixel in the effects chain (`lightShafts`), where the scene depth stops them at whatever stands in them.
- A point sprite under about 3 px changes brightness up to eightfold as it crosses pixel boundaries (the Gaussian at 1.5 px; a hard disc twentyfold), which reads as twinkling: draw motes at 3.5 px or more with a Gaussian and no discard, and scale the colour by (true size / drawn size) squared.
- A third of a building's point lights sit outside their own cell's box, many in the ceiling above it, and exit polygons stick out of their cell's box by up to half a metre (Mustafar's crashed ship's torn bridge opening stands 3.8 m off its own plane): anything that clips to a cell box or casts a sight ray to a lamp must pad the box with the doorway's own vertices or clamp the lamp inside first.
- A light moved by setting its `position` keeps last frame's `matrixWorld` until the scene is drawn, and the effects' light records (`fillFxLights`) read the matrix: anything that reads the lights before the draw (the room's air does, because its motes are in the scene) sees last frame's places on the frame the room's lamps are handed out again, so `RoomAir` holds its lamps for that one frame and reads them all as new on the next.
- Which weather level is active was the server's and is not in the archives: `weatherSchedule.ts` walks each planet's levels from the wall clock (an anchor drawn from the planet's climate every two hours, one-level Metropolis steps every five minutes between), so every player on the relay sees the same weather without a protocol change. The sky blends up to four environment rows (two levels, two areas while crossing a boundary; `SwgSky.setMix`), both gradients crossfade on the dome, and the clouds are a fixed pool of four sheets whose drift is integrated per image, never computed as time times speed, so a strengthening wind never makes them jump. A sheet samples its image at `xz / repeat + scroll`, so the picture moves against the scroll: the drift takes the wind's heading off the scroll (`driftScroll`) to move the clouds the way the rain leans and the dust blows.
- The time of day and the weather can be run off a clock a server hands out (`src/world/sharedClock.ts`), and neither may ever be made to jump. The clock is `off` with no server address (`Date.now()` exactly, so nothing in the game differs by a line), `shared` while a server is answering, and `adrift` once the socket goes, where the last offset is kept so the day carries on at its own rate rather than springing back to this machine's wall clock; the offset is the median of the last nine round trips (a round trip slower than `maxRttMs` tells us nothing but its own delay and is thrown away, as is an answer to a question nobody is waiting for, and a `pong` after the clock has been put down must not open sharing again — only a greeting may), and it is eased as a pure function of the wall clock, so nobody ticks it, nothing is allocated and a dropped connection needs no one to come and finish it. `DayCycle` takes the shared time **outright only where nothing was drawn**: a `dt` of zero behind the loading screen, or frames that have been away longer than `gapSeconds` beyond the step they reported — which is how a hidden tab is told from a slow frame, since the loop clamps `dt` to a twentieth of a second and a two-minute absence looks identical in `dt` alone. Everything else is walked, by the shorter way round, between a quarter and four times the day's own rate for a drift and thirty times for a correction, so the sun never crosses the sky in a frame and never stands still; a correction by the shorter way can wind backwards, which is deliberate (racing forward through a whole night would take four times as long) and is `catchUp` on the knob. Move the day by the error alone and never by the step plus the error: the error is measured before the day is advanced and already contains the step, so `step + err` settles exactly one frame ahead for ever. `Weather.clockSeconds` reads a second, *walked* copy of the same clock (`walkSeconds`), because the schedule has no easing of its own and a correction taken in one go turns the wind's heading, the rain's lean, the clouds' drift and the step of the schedule together in a single frame; the walk sheds at under real time so the weather's clock is never seen to run backwards, and a difference past `weatherEaseMaxMs` is still taken at once, because two players agreeing on the weather matters more than one player's smooth wind. Anything that writes `day.time` by hand (`__debug.time`, the room air's sun aiming) holds the day until `__sharedDay({ release: true })`, and the held note on the screen says so; a greeting that *opens* sharing and being told there is no server hand it back by themselves, a reconnect does not. Where a planet sits in the shared day is `phaseFor(azimuth, maxElevation)`, worked out from the planet's own sun so it is the same number in every browser with nothing on the wire — and a hash of two numbers spreads them no better than chance, so the salt is chosen by measuring the closest pair over the real planet list and the test re-measures it. A parameter property in a constructor stops node's type stripping dead, so any `src` file a node test reaches must write its fields out.
- Weather particles are in `weather.scene`, which has no lights and no fog. They are drawn once after the portal renderer (stencil 2 when the camera is inside, so only through exits) and compiled with `renderer.compile(weather.scene, camera, weather.scene)` at the end of `compileAllAsync`. Compiled against `world.scene`, the program key carries its lights and fog, and the first rain compiles during play. They die, and their fragments are discarded, below the top of their column: a grid of rays cast down around the camera (`RoofGrid`; rooms left out by collision group; lake surfaces included; no casts until physics has stepped since a reset, `Physics.steps`). The camera-following half runs after the camera and the physics step (`world.updateWeatherView`). Storm rows that turn shadows off fade `shadow.intensity` and skip the shadow pass (`portals.shadowsWanted`); clear rows always keep shadows. Never toggle `castShadow` or `shadowMap.enabled` for weather. The storms that blow sideways (dust, the snow storm) shoot along the effect's -X, so they are yawed by the wind's heading plus a quarter turn (`DUST_YAW_OFFSET`).
- The aimed depth of field keeps its focus on the GPU in a 1×1 float ping-pong: the median of five half-resolution depth taps at the crosshair, eased in dioptres. The sky and anything nearer than the shooter's plane (`cam.orbitDistance + 0.4`, 0.6 m in first person) do not count. There is no lens until a surface has been under the crosshair this aim, and the last surface is held after. The aperture widens as the square root of the focus only to 40 m, since an unstopped aperture blurred the whole foreground whenever the focus went far. The lens-grid texels carry the raw circle of confusion, which rises with depth, so the gather orders taps without a depth fetch. The grid strides to a quarter on buffers taller than 2000 px, so the radius never outruns the ±3 tile dilation. Glows that write no depth (gun and ship particles, bursts, blade cores) are drawn into a `dofGlow` R16F product (1/z, MAX-blended, particles masked where bright), so a bolt at the focus does not take the blur of the town behind it. The game clears the aim on any frame the player is not simulated (dead, a panel, the map), or the shoulder view and the lens hold on under the death card. The wardrobe doll takes its lens pass (one render into a 4-sample half-float target, then a premultiplied gather, tone curve and sRGB onto its see-through canvas) only once zoomed in enough for a pixel of blur, with hysteresis to 0.7 px. Both program variants are compiled when the panel opens.
- Wetness is shared uniforms (`WEATHER_UNIFORMS` in `wetness.ts`) read by the ground (its own `injectWetness(shader, 'ground')`, `userData.wetBuiltIn`), the water (rings in its slope) and every world material wrapped by `wetWrap` in `World.adoptMaterials`, right after `csm.setupMaterial` and in the same iteration, so the wrap is in the program key before anything compiles and rain only moves uniforms. The wrap is judged once per material by the first mesh the scan meets it on and is idempotent (a hook the cascades replaced is wrapped again around the new one). Never wet: skinned meshes, anything under a bone, under a skinned mesh or under a `userData.weatherDry` group (the player's figure and thrown sabers, another player's ship), and materials marked `interior`, `dry` (every ship's own materials, hull, wings, engines, guns and cockpit frame, marked by the garage when it loads the model and by the model's own kind, never the kind it is spawned as, since a shared material is judged once for every copy; the garage's per-model copies of a hull's room materials; ship interiors), `glass`, `water`, `wetBuiltIn`, `invisible` or `unlit`, or transparent. Shelter is the roof grid sampled with `texelFetch` beside a wall (out along its normal), not above it, so walls are wet down to the ground and a covered floor stays dry. `localStorage['swg.weather.wrap'] = '0'` turns the wrap off for a cost baseline.
- The dressed NPCs are mobiles, not fighters: a fighter is stood by the spawner and leashed to where it was stood, and a cantina NPC must follow the player out of the door and back in. A person is a "look" (`mobiles/look.ts`): a parts character (a species body, or the NPC's own `mobiles/models/<app>/parts.json`) built once per entry with `Character.load(..., { dir, skipRig: true })`, dressed with `wearItemFrom(<wardrobe folder>, item, part)`, coloured (the template's `custom` set as shared, which reaches each private copy; each piece's values as `<part>|<name>`), and cloned per spawn after `customizer.settled()`. No rig is fetched: the pack's clips bind to the clone by joint name.
- A ray for "the floor under a body" must pass only what stands still (`physics.topSurface` with a fixed-or-bodiless predicate): cast from inside a capsule, it finds that capsule and calls its middle the floor. A fighter rose a metre a frame that way.
- **A light that must light rooms carries the actor layer, set once before anything compiles.** Three leaves a light out of a draw, and out of a compile, whenever its layers are not ones the camera sees, and the rooms pass sees only the interior and actor layers: the flashlight sat on layer 0 alone, so it lit the street through a doorway and no wall, floor or body inside any building, and the round of fixes that only moved and capped it changed nothing indoors. It is `markActor(this.torch)` straight after the torch joins the scene in `main.ts`'s constructor, so every warm-up builds the room programs with the spot already in their light set; both the draw camera (`PortalRenderer.renderLayer`) and the warm-up camera (`World.withLayers`) enable the actor layer, and changing a light's layers in play would rebuild every room program. Ambient occlusion re-evaluates the torch in `spotDirect()`, added after the region choice, so a room's pixels count it as well as the world's.
- **A person the data stands on something raised keeps the row's height, and is held there until what they stand on is solid** (`src/world/mobiles/perch.ts`). For two passes an outdoor row was handed over with no height and `World.groundAt` outdoors is the terrain alone, so every Ewok on Endor's tree villages, a sniper on a Corellian bridge and Veers and Thrawn on their Naboo balcony stood on the ground under where they belonged (184 of Endor's 411 outdoor rows are more than 0.3 m over the terrain, 531 over the ground worlds); the pass 6 note that the manager "finds a platform" was never true, and lairs stand on the terrain only because their spot is moved clear of the camp's pieces. Now the height goes to the manager through `perchSpawn`, never below the terrain, and a body more than `lift` over the ground is perched -- held like a body in a room with no floor -- until one probe that ignores the terrain finds its floor (`landTop`; a hit far under the terrain is something buried and counts as nothing). Whether that floor can still come is asked of the objects whose own turned footprint covers the point, never of whole regions (`LayoutStreamer.builtAt` over the pure `floorAt` in `placedTiers.ts`): `built` lets the body down at once, `coming` waits with a safety clock that never counts behind a loading screen, and `far` holds it with no clock while the player is too far for the streamer to build it. A driven body is never perched and is decided again when handed back, and a body whose floor loses its collision while the world runs is perched again (`onFloorGone` to `perchOver`). Every number is `PERCH_TUNE`; `__debug.people({ perch: true })` counts held, far, landed, fell, timed out and perched again. A body knocked off a platform in a fight is still not held.
- Whatever is stood before the pack's terrain arrives -- the people the warm-up stands behind the loading screen (`World.warmUp`), a lair stood on the first passes -- stands on the stand-in heights; when the real ground comes in it can be metres above them. Mobiles are lifted back onto the terrain from the manager's 4 Hz pass (`Mobile.liftToGround`), and a frozen-tier body only sleeps when grounded: one put to sleep in mid-fall hung 200 m under the ground for good.
- **The emulator's rows are in the snapshot's frame, and every reader has to carry them into the world's.** Every placed object goes through `LayoutStreamer`'s mirror -- `gx = -(x - centre.x)`, `gz = z - centre.z` -- and a yaw goes to its negative with it. The lairs always did that (`intoWorld` in `wildLife.ts`); the standing people never did, so for two passes all 4,619 of them stood on the wrong side of their planet: the Tuskens of Fort Tusken on empty sand kilometres off, and nobody indoors at all, since a cantina's floor is not where its mirror image is (measured over five worlds, 541 of Tatooine's 622 rows stood within 30 m of a placed object as stored and none as the runtime read them). It escaped every spot check because `__debug.people({ go })` walked the player to the same wrong place and always found somebody there. `StandingPeople` now keeps the pack's rows raw and carries them once, route points and heading with them, on the first pass that knows the layout's centre (`frame`); a building's own children take `childInWorld` instead. Anything new that reads a converted row with a place in it must say which frame the row is in before it stands anything, and its console helper's `go` must use the same carry as the thing it is checking, or it checks nothing.
- **`readLua` reads tables and steps over everything else; it never throws.** The emulator's town screenplays define their spawn tables beside functions (`spawnMobiles`, and inside them `local pMobile = spawnMobile(self.planet, mob[1], ...)`), and the reader threw on the first `function` in 32 of the 35 city files; `core3.mjs` caught the error and moved on, so for two passes no town had anybody standing in it and nothing said so. `tools/swg/lua.mjs` now steps over a `function ... end` body and over any top-level statement it cannot read, counting each (`skipped`), which reads all 35 and took the worlds from 4,619 standing people to 10,358, and the screenplay's own `self.planet` is read from the file's table rather than guessed. A file of the emulator's that reads to nothing is a count in the converter's report, never a caught exception. Two more traps came out of the same run: `getRandomNumber` folded to a constant stood 216 people on one point, so a row that asks for a random place is drawn apart once from its own key; and a creature flagged `KILLER` or `STALKER` is not one that attacks on sight -- read that way, 864 of the towns' people opened fire on everybody walking in.
- **What a town's screenplay says about the one standing there is the server's, and most of it is not hostile.** Every city row is stood `AI_STATIC` (its brain off: it never wanders and walks home after a fight, `post: 'still'`), a stationary commoner gets `pvp 0` and no AI at all (part of the furniture: `peaceful`, `essential`), a stationary row names nobody and draws four in five from the town's `stationaryCommoners` and one in five from its `stationaryNpcs` at each spawn, a guard's row carries both sides and a world nobody holds stands the Imperial one (`city.lua`'s `spawnGcwMobiles`), and a patroller starts 40 to 60 s after it is stood and lingers 30 to 60 s at a point the route flags, 5 s at any other (`setupMobilePatrol`, `mobileDestinationReached`). Those are the server's; every other number of the standing people -- how near they stand, the two caps of forty, how far a post's person steps about -- is ours, in `PEOPLE_TUNE`.
- **A catalogue body is one creature's numbers out of every creature drawn as it, so a row's numbers come from the row.** The mobiles catalogue takes an entry's stats from the first Core3 mobile that wears its look (`mobiles.mjs`), and looks are shared: 3,445 Core3 creatures are drawn on 1,496 bodies, 1,474 of them differ from their body's level by five or more and 833 disagree with it on temper, and all 35 kinds of Tusken, levels 8 to 263, stood as the level-8 child, 192 health, defensive and unarmed. So the spawns pack (format 2) carries each creature's own `game` block -- level, health, damage, temper, ranged attack, whether it may be struck -- computed with the very `core3StatsFor` the catalogue uses, and its weapon groups, and a standing person, a lair's creature and a camp's body each wear their own creature's numbers over their body's (`overridesOf`, `weaponsOf`, `SpawnOpts.overrides`); the entry answers only for a row whose creature says nothing. Nothing that stands a body for a row may read its level, its temper, whether it may be struck or what it holds from the catalogue entry.
- **A camp is what its point of interest's client data hangs on it, not its template's appearance.** 263 of the 270 theater lairs drew `nests/rock_pebbleclump_a3.glb`, because a camp template's own appearance is a pebble: its tents, cots, stools, terminals, banners and fires are `CHLD`/`CHL2` children of its `clientDataFile` (`clientdata/poi/*.cdf`), the very form `clientfx.mjs` reads for a brazier's fire. All 107 camp templates the lairs name have one, 1,156 children over them, 1,145 of them models, median five pieces and at most thirty, none missing from the retail archives; the spawns command converts them into `spawns/nests/` and writes them as `camps[template]`, and each piece stands on its own ground with a collider of its own, since the template's `terrainModificationFileName` (`poi_small.lay`) flattened ground that cannot be flattened now (the houses lesson) -- which is also why a site too far out of level is refused, as the emulator refused it. A theater camp is not attackable and has no condition (`spawnTheater`); a nest is, at its building level's condition (`spawnLair`). Half of the 160 creature-nest templates carry one child, nearly all of them fog or insects, which is the nest's own effect.
- **The physics engine can panic inside its own step, and the world is lost for good when it does.** Rapier 0.35 (`@dimforge/rapier3d-compat` 0.20) keeps, for its continuous collision pass, a list of every fixed collider with its box while there are 512 or fewer (past that it reads the broad phase's own tree, which is why it struck in open country and never in a town), and throws the list away only on a step that both runs that pass (something is fast) and carries a user change. A collider taken away on a quiet step is never struck off it, and the next fast step with nothing written looks it up by a dead handle: a panic in `World.step`, after which every call into that world fails as "recursive use of an object" -- most easily after killing creatures and teleporting a long way. `Physics.keeper` is a fixed body with no collider, in no query and no island, written to its own value before every step, so every step carries a change and the list is made afresh whenever it is read (about 0.03 ms on a fast step); never call `world.step()` directly, only `Physics.step`/`stepOnce`. Upstream fixed it in rapier 0.36 (compat 0.21), an upgrade wave of its own. Should a world break anyway, `isEngineFault` knows the panic wherever it surfaces, the world refuses every later call (`broken`), and the game saves the character where it stands and loads straight back in (`Physics.onBroken`); `removeBody` never removes twice, and `tools/swg/tests/physicsStep.test.ts` builds the failing sequence in a real world.
- **From inside a building the game draws only what its doorways show, and what moves is drawn only in the passes of the rooms it stands in.** The portal renderer used to draw the whole outdoor world, its shadows and its weather whenever any exit's sphere touched the view, every room of every building near enough, and every body and indoor chair in every pass, up to six times a frame with its bones uploaded each time. `portalVis.ts` floods the rooms outward from the camera's own room through each doorway's own outline on the screen, once a frame before any pass: only rooms really seen are drawn, with no exit in sight the world, the sun's shadows and the weather are not drawn at all, and when one is the world pass is cut to that exit's rectangle. It is conservative on purpose -- the projected polygon and never its plane (Mustafar's torn bridge opening stands 3.8 m off its own), padded rectangles, a camera standing in a doorway handing the room beyond the whole screen, every room whose box holds the camera starting the flood -- because every mistake it can make is a hole in a wall. `portalCull.ts`'s `ActorRoutes` then draws each body only in the pass of the room it stands in (outdoors: the world's and the shadows'), and also beyond any portal its sphere reaches, grown by how far it has gone since its room was last followed, so a body half through a doorway is drawn on both sides; a record with a light under it is never hidden, since a light hidden changes the light count and rebuilds every program; and a body left out of every view pass is kept hidden through the effects too, because the motion blur draws a mesh on its own only when it believes three drew it the frame before. A building's furniture is drawn with that building's rooms (`furnitureHost.ts`), and a body in a room nobody can see stops animating and casting. The Mos Eisley cantina went from 50 to 15 ms a frame and the street outside it from 59 to 24; every change is a switch `__debug.perf()` can flip back and forth on the same screen.
- **The stencil reference every material tests is one value read through an accessor on `THREE.Material.prototype`, never on a material.** Writing the portal renderer's reference into seven or eight thousand materials about nine times a frame cost 0.7 to 0.8 ms, so moving it is now one assignment (`PORTAL_REF.value`) and a registered material reads it through an accessor installed once, the first thing `main.ts` does, before any material exists (`src/world/stencilRef.ts`). On the prototype and only there: V8 turns an object whose data property is redefined as an accessor into a dictionary for good, and every field of that material then reads at about twice the cost (measured in node 22 on three's own materials: 60-80 ns for 25 reads in fast mode, 157-172 after an own accessor, and the same after a plain value is put back). The constructor's own `this.stencilRef = 0` goes through the setter, which writes two fields of the material's own in the same order for every material, so every material keeps one hidden class. A write from anywhere else is not taken, is counted and is said once in development, because a reference that silently would not move is the fault nobody would look for; `localStorage['swg.stencilAccessor'] = '0'` is the old way at the next load.
- **Three keeps an attribute's upload ranges until it uploads the attribute, and uploads it only when something draws it.** `addUpdateRange` pushes a new `{ start, count }` every call and only an upload empties the list, so a buffer refilled every frame and drawn only some frames -- the weather's own batches, which a room with no exit in sight never draws -- grew its list by an object a frame for as long as it went undrawn, and three sorted the whole pile the frame it was drawn again. A writer keeps one range of its own per attribute and hands it over once per upload, widening it in place while three has not taken it (`keepUploadRange`, `src/world/uploadRange.ts`); the marks keep their windows the same way. Anything that writes a buffer every frame and is not drawn every frame needs it.
- **A world key off the wire is kept byte for byte, NUL and all, and a body every browser stands for itself is shared by saying it has been seen.** The server keys a world as its planet, a NUL and its zone (`roomKey` in `server/rooms.mjs`); the browser read a spawn row's world through `readWords`, which strips every control character, so `endor\0` came out `endor` and every record an admin stood was refused on every browser as another world's. A key is only ever compared, never shown or parsed, so `readRow` in `src/net/owned.ts` keeps it as sent. And the world's seeded bodies (a lair's creature `wild:`, a person at a post `stood:`, a nest `camp:`) are named the same in every browser and never sent to anybody: a browser says `seen` when it has stood one and `unseen` when it puts its body down, and the server offers it only to a browser with a body for it (`seers`), however far off, since every browser that has one stood it because it was near. A seen one that died stays dead for its row's own respawn, told with the death as `back` seconds, and is then forgotten so the next browser stands it whole; one nobody keeps or sees for two minutes is forgotten too; a `seen` for one already down is answered to that browser alone with `fresh`, so its copy goes quietly instead of playing a death nobody saw or counting as a kill at its post. A grant handed back must tell the browser to let go (`keep { drop }`), or it goes on keeping what it gave back, alive on its own screen and frozen on every other; an unattackable body (a ticket collector, one of ours) is never shared at all, and a follower leaves sharing with `taken`.
- **A node test that reads a shader's text has to compile something of it too.** The blade glow pass once declared `own` twice in one scope of `bladeLight`, which every GLSL compiler refuses: the whole pass stayed unlinked in every browser, and every node test passed, because they matched expressions in the shader's source and node compiles no GLSL. `bladeGlow.test.ts` now builds the real source and scans it for a name declared twice in one scope -- following braces, and a function's parameters, which share its body's scope in GLSL ES 3.00 -- after checking the scanner on the very fault it was written for. A test that pins a shader by its text pins its words, not its program; whatever it cannot compile it should at least scan for what a compiler would refuse.
- **A tree is solid at the shape the client authored for it, and a canopy is not a trunk.** Every planting stood on one upright cylinder guessed from its model's box, and a wide tree's box is its canopy: anything not three times as tall as it was wide was taken for a rock and blocked at 80% of its width, a 9 to 27 m column under a birch or an oak, and whole forests were walls. `tools/swg/extent.mjs` reads each appearance's own collision extent (null, cylinder, sphere, box with its corners taken componentwise, composite, detail and mesh) and the `floracollision` light pass writes `flora-collision.json` beside each ground world's manifest with no model reconverted; a tree's extent is its trunk, a rock's a few cylinders, a fallen log a box, and a NULL extent -- a living wroshyr, a giant lickbush -- is walked through, as the client walked through it, with a knob to put a trunk back. It cuts both ways and is the owner's to judge: the forests opened up (the share of a forest tile that blocks went from 49.7 to 14.8% on Yavin 4 and from 50 to 0% on Kashyyyk's main zone) while other worlds block more where the client's rocks are bigger than the guess (Kashyyyk's hunting grounds 2.1 to 36.3%, the southern dungeons 1.8 to 34.1%, Mustafar 10.5 to 16.6%, Tatooine's boulders); `__debug.flora({ collision: { rule: 'guess' } })` puts the old rule back to compare.
- **An expansion zone's map picture covers the buildout area's composite rectangle, not the terrain.** The `maps` command drew every picture over the terrain's whole width about the origin, which is right for the ten launch worlds (their composite is 0,0,0,0, which means none) and wrong for every expansion zone: the client draws one over the composite rectangle of its row in `datatables/buildout/areas_<planet>.iff` whose `allowMap` is 1 (`kashyyyk_main` 2048 m where its terrain is 4096, `kashyyyk_hunting` 2844 m, `kashyyyk_dead_forest` 1000 m, `mustafar_main` 8000 m about (-2880, 2976) where its terrain is 16384), moved by `map_adjustments.iff`'s offset for that zone. The one offset the archives hold (Kashyyyk main's 56) is in the picture's own pixels and its sense was measured, by fitting every placed object and place onto the picture, not read off the client's code. `map.json` carries `centre` and `frame`, one pure helper (`mapFrame`) places the map window's picture, the galaxy map's dots and the terminal's dots alike, a `map.json` with no `centre` reads as the origin, and `status` asks for `maps` again while a composite world's map has no frame.
- A running Vite dev server holds the files it has served open, and on Windows that makes a conversion's swap of a pack folder fail with `EPERM` -- once it deleted a mobiles unit outright. Stop the dev server before converting anything.
- A parts pack's `customize.json` (5 MB for a wardrobe) is fetched and parsed once per folder for the session (`loadCustomizeFile` in `customizer.ts`) and shared by every customizer: each dressed NPC's look builds its own customizer, and re-parsing it per look stalled the frame for every new entry. Nothing may write to a parsed recipe or palette; key per-character state by the recipe object instead.
- **The appearance page is laid out by the game's own creator table** (`customization` command, `tools/swg/customization.mjs`, `characters/customization.json`; `src/ui/creatorModel.ts`). `customization_data.iff` is a row per species, tab and slider or colour in the client's own tab and row order: the tab, `hslider` or `color`, the row name (which keys `ui_cust.stf`'s words, so the words are read and never typed), one or two variables (a `blend_skinny,blend_fat` pair is one slider as an `_0,_1` pair is), `IS_SCALE` the height, `REVERSE` a slider run the other way, `DISCRETE` whole steps, `COLOR_LINKED` the variable a colour follows, `COLOR_LINKED_TO_SELF_0/1` the variables it also sets with the same index (a female's skin sets her lips and eye shadow, whose palettes begin with the skin's creation colours: no make-up), `IS_VAR_HAIR_COLOR` a variable on the hair object; `customization_group_shared` marks the hair tab, `allow_bald` the species that may not go bald, `hair_assets_skill_mods` each template's styles, and `palette_columns` how many of a palette's colours the creator offered and in how many columns. That last table has the client's interface palette as a row, and every row matching `PALETTE_SKIP` (`^ui(_|$)`) is passed over before its file is opened: the interface rule. The file carries `format` **and** `status` looks for a named row (the human male's `color_eyes`), so it is never the no-stamp hole `player`, `parts` and `species` are. Which customizer key a row writes is decided in `creatorView` alone: a body row every body mesh's key of that bare name plus the shared one, a hair row the worn hair's keys plus the remembered `hair|<name>`, a follower hidden behind its source and written with it, a row that writes exactly what an earlier row writes dropped (the males' torso is their muscle), an untabled **body** morph in a group of ours and a garment's own fit morph (a jacket's belt, the Mon Calamari and Wookiee females' shirt-only flat chest) left out. "Writes exactly what an earlier row writes" is the kind, the variables **and the object they live on**: a bare name is not a variable, and the first cut, keyed on the names alone, took the Zabrak's horns, the Bothan's and Rodian's hair trim and the Rodian male's frill tips (each `index_color_2` on the hair) for copies of the eyes (`index_color_2` on the head) and dropped all of them. What a pick writes and in what order (`pickWrites`), which colours stand in their own mesh's section after the groups (`ownSection`), the bald rule (`hairNone`: no hair is offered to **every** species, the owner's call once he had looked and found that taking the hair off leaves no hole in any base head; the table's `allow_bald` is still read and said beside it, with `keptFromBald` naming the species it kept from going bald, and decides only that a new character of one starts in its first creation style) and the page's own lists without the table (`packSliders`, `packColours`) are pure functions there too, so the node test runs what the page draws. The pack's variable names are bare (`hum_m_head_l0|index_color_2`), not `/private/...`. Without the file, or for a character with no live recipes, the page is the packs' own list as it always was (`variableLabel.ts` names it).
- **A look's colours scoped to a mesh are kept before anything reads them.** `applyAppearance` sets every `|` key whether or not a recipe reads it yet, and `applyLook`/`applyLookPrepared` await `catalogue()` first: a fresh rig is coloured before it is dressed and the wardrobe's recipes join only with the wardrobe, so filtering on "a recipe reads it" dropped every garment and hair colour a peer wore the first time they were seen (confirmed against the old code in `lookValues.test.ts`). A loaded piece stays "active" while it is off (its materials are still in the character), so a value set for it renders at once and none goes stale; `putBackLook` (look.ts, run by the select screen before each figure's look and by the creator, which reuses the last figure's rig when the species is the same) therefore puts every key this record does not set -- shared or scoped -- back to the pack's own value or its recipe's default and lets it go once that has rendered, and forgets at once what nothing reads, has nowhere to go back to, or is at its own default, or one character's skin and shirt colour stay on the next one and are saved into its record (measured on the owner's own two human males). **A private copy of a shared colour goes where its shared colour goes**, never to its own default on its own: the head's `hum_m_head_l0|index_color_skin` is what the head's texture reads and the customizer keeps it equal to `index_color_skin`, and putting the copy back alone left the head in the default skin and the body in the last character's. Nor is a copy ever forgotten while its value is not its own default, or the next render of the head reads the default. `applyAppearance` sets nothing the customizer already holds at that value, since every colour a peer moves sends their whole look again. A colour change alone sends a hello (`saveAppearance` queues one), and the hello carries only shared keys and keys of worn meshes (`lookKeep`): the saved record keeps every shirt ever coloured and the wire keeps the first 120 numbers. A peer's name label is written again into the label it already has, and only when the name changed: one made anew per hello left a material in the portal renderer's set every time a peer moved a slider.
- **Every worn piece says what colour it takes, and the wardrobe asks two things of its shaders the rest of the converter does not** (`tools/swg/dye.mjs`, the wardrobe case in `cli.mjs`). First, a palette counts in **any** pass (`shaderNeedsBake(shader, tag, { allPasses })`, which the wardrobe sets as `ctx.everyPass` and which reaches both the GLB's own bake in `skinnedTexture` and `convertWearableMesh`'s recipe test, so the picture and the recipe agree): the `h_color2w_*` family lays its palettes on after a plain first pass, and read at the first pass alone 65 pieces of each human folder and 22 of each Ithorian one -- the Mandalorian, Rebel and GCW armour, the Kashyyykian hunting gear, the Ithorian bodysuits -- had their colours thrown away. Only a pass a **variable** reaches counts (a factor a palette sets, a texture a choice picks): read literally, "a factor or another texture in any pass" also takes the black specular factor of the `a_specmap_*` effects' second pass, 152 shaders more that would have been baked for nothing and lost the plain path's glow split. A shader baked only by that rule whose plain look glows through a mask (the two Ithorian GCW helmets' visors, `EMIS.a`) has its glow split **out of the bake** by that mask (`bakeGlowSplit` in `cli.mjs`: `paintGlow`'s reading, surface.mjs's `splitGlow` in linear light, at the bake's own size and not capped at `GLOW_MAX`), so the GLB's base is the lit half: the first cut laid the plain path's glow over the whole bake, which is the emismap lesson's "twice as bright by day" all over again (base 202 under a glow of 202). The recipe carries how it glows (`glow`, a ship paint recipe's shape), and the character customizer splits a recolour the same way (`runPaintJob`, on its own thread and in the shared renders), putting the glow only where a glow map already is; the characters' own worker carries that split too (`Customizer`'s `putsGlow`: a glowing recipe goes out whole when its materials have no glow map, and its glow comes back to be put where one is), and any other renderer that takes the wardrobe's recipes must as well, or the first recolour doubles the visor again. Nothing else in the bake path changed. The mobiles' wearables share `convertWearableMesh` and leave the flag unset until the Materials pass turns it on for them. Second, the pieces the game gave **no** colour (230 of each human folder, 79 and 78 Ithorian) carry a dye of ours: decided per **piece**, from the recipes on all its meshes, never per material (a jacket whose cloth takes the game's palette keeps its buckle as the game had it), and written per **mesh** as an ordinary recipe -- a plain pass laying the GLB's own base down, then `2 x grey x colour` under a mask -- so the renderer, the worker and the shaders needed nothing new. The mask and grey are computed from **the images the GLB embeds** (`glbImages` in `glb.mjs` is the one place that decides them, and `buildGlb` reads its images through it), never from the archives' textures, which is what makes "undyed is the GLB" true to the byte by construction whatever a later conversion does to those images; the test proves it by rendering every dye recipe against the GLB's own base. The colour comes from `swg3js/dye`, one entry with an alpha of nought, so every index (the 0 every value starts at, the 0..11 a fighter's look throws) leaves the piece alone and only a colour carried whole will dye it; that palette **must be set after `exportPalettes`**, which writes `[]` for a palette the archives have not got, and an empty palette decodes as opaque white and dyes everything white. The appearance page shows it as a row called Dye whose picker opens on every garment colour at once. A piece's `colour` is worked out once every piece is converted, from the recipes the run ends with, because a mesh two templates wear is dyed by whichever meets it first (the two `appearance_invisible` bracelets wear the bracelets' own meshes). `status` asks for the folder again on `dyeFormat`, on there being **no** `colour: 'dye'` garment (the named product, so a stamp written by a broken run does not pass), and on hairstyles with no pictures in a folder that has pictures, each its own reason and none chained to the `materialFormat` check; `--no-dye` and `--no-icons` are the owner's choice and are not asked for again. And a run now leaves only the images its customize.json names: the registry numbers its files in the order it meets them, so a run that meets one image more early on renames nearly every file after it, and every earlier run's copies had stayed on disk unread (1,839 files and about 165 MB in one human folder when the sweep first ran).
- **A character's colours render in a worker of their own, a colour private to a mesh keeps a texture of its own, and any colour at all is a negative palette value** (`src/player/recipeWorker.ts`, `customizer.ts`, `texrender.ts`, `src/ui/dyePicker.ts`). Every character but the dressed people the mobiles share renders through `characterRender`, a worker apart from the ships' paint worker (so a recolour never queues behind an 800 ms hull), which hands back the normal map the values pick as well, put on exactly as the main thread's render was, so no material gains a normal map it would not have gained there; `__debug.dye({ worker: false })` puts the renders back on this thread to compare, and `__debug.dye(item, variable, colour)` counts `compiledOnSwap` against the programs held before the first key moved, since the renders land one at a time with frames drawn between them and a baseline taken after the last would hold whatever an earlier one built. A render that comes back after its recipe was queued again is not put (the newer one is), and one that lands after its customizer was let go puts nothing. A recipe that reads any private variable (`perMesh`) keeps its textures under `<material>|<mesh>` and puts them only on that mesh's materials (`materialsFor(name, mesh)`, matching a loaded mesh by its name or its name less a `_<n>` the loader added; measured: every one of the 3,921 recipes over the species and wardrobe packs (3,665 before SOE's final files) names a mesh the loader makes, and none ends in `_<digits>` of its own, which the node test reads again over every species, wardrobe and mobiles pack on disk, 4,450 recipes over 98 packs today; the rule is `isRecipeMesh` in `customizer.ts`), because a left and a right glove, or the Ithorian chest plate and its leggings, share a shader name and keyed by the material alone one recoloured the other. A recipe of shared variables alone keeps the material's one texture. A ship's paint is the second kind except for two of the YT-2400's patterns, whose texture choice is marked private to the mesh `ship`: harmless, since a ship's paint answers `materialsFor` by name whatever mesh is asked and its customizer finds a texture through its recipes (`textureOf`), not by the bare material key. A colour carried whole is `-(0xRRGGBB + 1)`, -1 to `RAW_COLOUR_MIN` (-16,777,216), decoded in `paletteColor` before the palette is looked at, so it reaches a shader's palette factor and a blueprint's palette op alike, and nothing else: a texture choice clamps it to its first, and the picker never offers one; the game's own picks stay indices, and no palette, recipe default, pack value or mobile's colour on this machine is negative (measured). A ship and a painted speeder take one on their colours and never on their pattern (the lesson on ships taking every colour, below). The picker (`DyePicker`, `pickerTabs`) gives a worn piece, a hairstyle and our dye every garment palette the wardrobe's recipes carry, every creator palette the creator's table carries and their folded union (1,244 colours on the human wardrobe: 1,555 distinct, folded within four levels a channel), and a body colour (skin, lips, markings, horns, lekku) its own palette only; an eye colour its own and **every species' eye palette** (the owner's call: every race may take every eye colour). Every species but the Ithorians has an eye row, and every one reads it on its head through a palette factor (measured, and pinned by the node test), so a colour carried whole reaches all eighteen; the Ithorians have none and get nothing new. The palettes come only from the recipes' own lists and the table, never a sweep of `palette/`, so the client's interface palettes cannot appear. Every block is one canvas picked by position, never a button a swatch. A worn piece's colours stand in one section a piece under the game's name (`itemSections`), one row a bare variable, and a pick writes every one of its meshes' keys: in the game a private variable belongs to the object.
- **A garment's colour is its thing's, by bare name, and a weapon in hand is a hand's thing** (`OwnedItem.tint`, `tintValues`, `moveTints` and `heldThingsOf` in `src/core/inventory.ts`; `Equipment.setTint`, `colourPiece`, `tintLook`). A tint is keyed `index_color_1`, `index_color_dye` and never by a mesh's key, since meshes are a gender's (`_m_l0`, `_f_l0`) and a shirt traded between a man and a woman keeps its colour. **Applying one writes every variable the piece's recipes read** on the meshes it is worn under, the recipe's default where the tint says nothing, or putting on the undyed of two shirts leaves the dyed one's colour standing on the shared mesh; only values that differ from what the customizer holds (under either spelling of a key) are set, so a colour already showing renders nothing, and it is set after `loadPiece` and before the settle the piece waits on, so it shows in its colours and builds no program. The select screen and `play` draw a record in its things' colours by laying them over its look (`App.recordLook`), as non-default values only, so `putBackLook` puts the last figure's back where this one has none and nothing undyed is rendered. The look no longer keeps a garment's mesh keys (`appearanceOf(c, false)`, once the record is marked `tints: 1`, a mark of its own for the reason `named` is), and **what the others see is read off the live character** (`helloNow`: `variableValues()` through `lookKeep`), which already carries every thing's colour, so the wire needed no new field. Saved records moved once on load (`moveTints`): each owned piece's mesh values went into the worn copy's tint by bare name and out of the look, with no time stamped, so a server's colour, which has one, wins over a moved one; the session adopts the moved record's new mark without counting a change (`Session.noteRewritten`), and the creator's colours go onto the new character's things the same way when it is made (`outfitTints`). **The move takes every garment's key out of the look, owned or not** (`garmentMeshesOf`: every non-hair piece of the wardrobe and the species pack), and so does every save (`lookWithoutGarments`, over `Character.garmentMeshes`, which counts pieces never loaded on this rig): the old look kept the colours of every piece ever coloured, given away or not, and a stale key left there came back on the day another of that piece arrived with no colour of its own. **A garment's copy of a body colour is the body's, never the thing's**: the customizer links every private variable to a shared one of the same bare name (`Customizer.followed`), so on a Wookiee, whose fur is a shared `index_color_1`, every garment's own `index_color_1` follows the fur and the page hides it (measured: 814 wardrobe garments on the Wookiees, 127 on the Trandoshans through `index_color_0`, 46 on the Twi'leks through the head's `index_texture_1`). `tintValues` takes a `follows` that writes such a copy at the colour it follows (`followedValue` live, the record's own look for the select screen's `tintLook`), the move and the creator's carry pass it over, and a thing that carries a colour under that name from another body keeps it for whoever wears it next; reading it as the thing's would have pinned the fur on the shirt, reset it to the shirt's default on connect, and drawn it differently by whichever path ran last. A piece put on with no owned thing behind it -- the give tab's wear, which gives the thing only once the piece is on -- goes on in the game's own colours, never the last copy's still on the meshes. A pick equal to every mesh's own default is no colour (`pickedTint`): five pieces of each human folder rest at different defaults on their meshes (a dress's two halves), and taking the first mesh's default as "none" sent the other back to its own under the colour just picked. `play` draws the body from its things once more after the record is read (`colourWorn`). The character mark carries a `t:` digest of the tints by kind and id, only while any thing has one, so marks from before are unchanged, a server's rename moves nothing, and a colour set offline is a change. **The hand-up carries no tints** (400 rows of the longest id are 56 KB of the 64 a server takes in a second): a colour goes as a paced `tint` word (`ITEMS_TUNE`: 800 ms of quiet, at most six a second, a refused one tried again a second later). Six and not the design's ten: the server takes eight words a second from a browser on a window of its own that starts with whatever word comes first, so it never lines up with the browser's seconds, and at ten the hand-up and the first burst fell in one window and the ninth colour was dropped without a word (measured through a real relay in `itemsRelay.test.ts`), and **newest wins both ways** -- `pairOwned` takes the server's colour unless the one here was set later and names every thing whose colour it kept over the server's (`resend`), which `reconcile` sends after the list, so a colour set while no server answered survives connecting. Colours are stamped on `sharedClock.now()`, never `Date.now()`. A backpack and a trade cell show a 10 px swatch of a thing's first colour (`itemSwatch`, a game colour set on the element, framed in the palette's edge). **Two copies of one hilt can be held one in each hand** (the owner's call, overturning the design's "planHold unchanged"): `planHold` empties the other hand only for the very same thing (`sameHeld`: by thing where both are named, by id otherwise), and `heldThings` is now the thing **in each hand** (`{ right, left }`, written only where it is not the rule's: the right takes its choice else the oldest, the left its choice else the oldest copy the right does not hold), so `inUse`, `destroy`, the backpack's hands, the server's `using` list and a list taking one of two copies away all read the hand's own thing, and one copy still cannot be in two hands. Nothing else needed to change for it: the player, the peers' `held` (`{ r, l }` by id, checked by `wire.mjs`, which allows the same id twice), `applyHeld` and the peers' blades hang and light each hand on its own (the hanging is `hangHeld` in `src/net/peerHands.ts`, out of `RemotePlayers` so `remoteBlades.test.ts` runs a hello naming one hilt twice through it to two lit blades), and the thrown saber is the right hand's as ever; a blade's colour is still the character's, so both blades are that one colour.
- **A hairstyle is a cell of the converter's own picture, and it goes on the way every worn piece does** (`src/ui/hairGrid.ts`, `App.wearHairPrepared`). The grid is the creator table's own lists: this gender's styles in the table's order with the creation ones first, then the other gender's (ours: no row of the table crosses a gender), then what the wardrobe converted that no row names, and a cell for no hair leading for every species (`hairNone`), the Twi'lek, Zabrak and Trandoshan the game kept from going bald included. A wardrobe folder holds every species' hair and decides the cut, so the Sullustan's `_f` and `_m` templates wear **one mesh** a folder and are one style: what no row names is deduped by its meshes, never by its id. `Character.wearHair` put a style on at once and compiled it on the live frame; the game now loads it hidden, carries its colour, settles, prepares each mesh and only then swaps it in one `putOn`. "Prepares" has to mean something in the creator too: `prepareRoot` used to return outside play, which stopped being right when the creator learned to stand the figure in a captured place (a world the main renderer draws every frame), so it now prepares whenever `scene3d` is up as well, and a garment the creator dresses gains the same. A change still loading is tied to more than the character object, which `useSpecies` hands to the next character of the same species: `dropHairChanges` is called wherever the equipment's epoch moves (the select screen, into and out of the creator, a record played), the creator's default style takes its turn before it reads anything and is dropped the moment the creator is left, and the page saves only when the worn style really changed. The colour rows stay live while a style loads, so the carry is asked again just before the swap and a pick made meanwhile goes on first (`HAIR_TUNE.recarryRounds`). The colour is carried **before** the style shows and **onto the new style's own keys** (`carryHairColour`, its inputs gathered from the live parts and customizer by `hairCarryFor`, which the node test runs against the real customizer: the remembered `hair|<bare>` first, else the old style's key of the same bare name), because a hidden style's recipe renders only for a value set under a key it reads, and a new style with no value of its own wore the default until the next pick. The Sullustan's ids are `sul_hair_s<nn>_<f|m>`, not `hair_...`, and every `^hair_` test in the game took them for wearables -- owned, put in the backpack, and never taken off when another style went on: `isHairKey` (core/inventory.ts) is the one test of a part name, and `Character.hairWorn` reads the part's catalogue kind. A Sullustan played before that still owns the style it wore as a row: the row is kept (an owned list never loses anything) but the backpack does not show it and `Equipment.use`/`wear` refuse a hairstyle outright (`HAIR_ELSEWHERE`), because with hair out of `wornPieces` nothing there would take the style already on off, and the backpack would have put a second one on beside it. The Sullustan's hair tab and its colour row are ours (`withOursHair`), in the words the table gives every other species' `color_hair` row, only on a creator table converted before SOE's final names list: SOE's final table gives the Sullustans hair rows of their own (19 a woman's, 39 a man's with his twelve beards), and with it the table's own tab and colour row stand and `withOursHair` adds nothing, since it adds a tab only to a species whose table has none. A new character of a species that may not go bald starts in the table's first creation style, in the creator only (`openCreator` and a species picked while creating), never on an existing character.
- **The creator always starts from a human male's fresh body, a new character is dressed by the appearance table, and every species stands at its own template's height** (`App.freshBody`, `starterOutfit` in `core/inventory.ts`, `Character.resetShape`, `HEIGHT_RANGES` in `character.ts`). The creator opened on the figure last stood on the select screen (`this.characterId`), and `useSpecies` hands back the very rig that figure wore, dressed, shaped and sized, with only its colours put back, so a new character was the last one looked at under a new name; it now opens on `human_male` and a fresh body puts the colours, the shape sliders (where each first loaded, `restMorphs`) and the height's middle back and dresses the pack's own dress less what the table blocks or hides for the species, judged by the table alone and never `packOwn` (which keeps an old record's pack shirt on): a new Wookiee wears nothing, as the game's own new Wookiee did (`creation/default_pc_equipment.iff`'s Wookiee rows are empty), and the kit a new character is given is judged the same way (`Equipment.kit`'s `look(ctx, true)`). The game's own starter outfits (that file and the seven professions' `creation/profession_defaults_*.iff`) are not read yet. A species' height range is the `scale` range of `object/creature/player/shared_<species>_<gender>.iff`, per species **and gender**, copied into the table: the old table was keyed by species alone and nobody's numbers, so a Wookiee male stood 1.91 m at the slider's middle where the game stood him 2.20, Bothans and Rodians too tall, Sullustans too short, every woman at a man's range; a saved character keeps its 0..1 and changes height once. The player's capsule does not scale with it.
- **A film over an eye is drawn blended** (`EYE_FILM` in `surface.mjs`, rule 5): the Trandoshans' eyelids (`trn_[mf]_lid`, a shell a few millimetres over the whole eye whose alpha is a third over the middle) and the Mon Calamari's eye shine (`*_eyetrans`, whose pass blends with no alpha test at all). As half cut-outs the lid was a pale opaque cap over 85% of the eye and the shine was thrown away. `species` stamps no format, so `status` reads each rig's models for an eye-film material that is not `BLEND` and asks for `species --only=` those species.
- **A ship and a speeder take every colour a garment takes, on their colours and never on their pattern** (the owner's call). A hull's colours are its palette variables (`index_color_<n>`, the PAL operations on the hull, the wings, the carriers and the parts) and open the appearance page's own picker on the ship edit page (`pickKind` `paint`: Own, the hull's own palette, writes its index as the page always did; Garments, Creator and All write a colour carried whole, from the character's own wardrobe's garment palettes and the creator's table). Its choice of pattern (`index_texture_<n>`) stays a choice and refuses one everywhere a fit is kept or checked: `clampVariable` (resolveFit and ShipPaint), `packFit`, the page's `pickPaintValue`, `__debug.paint` and the relay's `cleanShip`, which cannot look a hull up and so asks the key's own name (`isColourKey`, one expression on both sides of the wire; measured: every one of the 135 paint variables over the hulls (123 before SOE's last hulls) is a palette exactly when its name is a colour, which `shipDye.test.ts` reads again). **On the wire a colour carried whole rides beside the paint and never in it** (`fitForWire`, `fitFromWire`, a hello fit's `colours`): the paint carries the nearest colour of the hull's own palette (the default kept as absent, the pattern untouched), which every relay and browser built before reads exactly as it always has, and only a relay whose hail says `paint: 2` (`PAINT_VERSION`, beside `items`, with no wire version of its own) passes `colours` on, for a browser built for it to lay back over the paint. The first cut put the colour in the paint itself, gated on the hail: the relay hands one cleaned hello to every peer and nothing a browser says tells it which build that peer runs, so a browser built before drew the ship in its palette's first colour, which this way round cannot happen. A browser says once on the message line that the others see the nearest colours, but only once the claim is answered (`Session.paintVersion`, read in `onClaimed` too), and the palette the nearest colour needs is read off a parse that has already landed (`customizeFileNow`), never awaited, since the hello is built synchronously. The paint worker, the glow split and the preview needed nothing: `paletteColor` decodes the colour, and a glow's mask is the chosen pattern's. **A speeder is a ship with a fit of its paint alone** (`paintOnlyFit`: no chassis, no slots, a flight computer it never shows), so the edit page (its paint section alone, opened by a `paint` button in the garage), the kept fit, the spawn, the refit and the hello take it with no new path: `def.fit` is how everything that carries paint finds it, and a painted speeder ridden is the ship the hello names. What a fit means beyond paint is decided by its slots, never by there being a fit: a fit with none arms no gun from a slot (`armGuns`), so a painted walker spawned as a ship keeps every gun and fires its own weapon, where read as a fit of no weapons it lost them all, and its chassis row is still named after it (`chassisNameOf` takes an empty chassis as none). Its paint comes from the gallery, which the converter now writes as the ships pack writes a hull's: a vehicle's paint shaders baked at their defaults through every pass (`convertOne`'s `paint`, and for a skinned walker `convertSat`'s `paint`, which hands a paint shader to `textureFor` exactly as a static model's), their recipes in the gallery's own `customize.json` through the very function the ships command uses (`tools/swg/paintrecipes.mjs`, moved out of the ships case unchanged, which `galleryPaint.test.ts` holds to the ships pack's own recipe), each model's `paint` on its manifest entry (`vehiclePaint` in `gallery.mjs`) and `paintFormat` on the manifest, which `status` asks for again with `gallery --only=vehicles` (`galleryPaintStatus`). Measured over the retail archives: 24 of the 62 vehicle templates (none of SOE's last five), 20 models, read a colour (15 of them `vehicle_base` and `vehicle_trim`; the basilisk the astromech's palette, the AT-RT the starships', the v35, flash speeder and XP-38 a garment's and a creator's), 32 recipes, 68 images, 15.1 MB, and 52 of the 159 vehicle materials' base pictures change, to the game's default colours. The 33 that read none get nothing: a dye of ours on them needs the wardrobe's mask over a static model and a conversion, and is not built. NPC ships take no paint and are untouched.
- A mobiles model that fails with "no mesh survived" is one the game's own archives strip at every detail level (the gubbur): a rerun fails it again, so `status` lists it apart and does not count it as work (`permanentFailures` in `mobiles.mjs`), but only while the failure was recorded from the archives the catalogue was made from and for the unit signature it plans now. A skinned mesh's level list (`.lmg`) gives the finest level that has triangles, not merely the finest that exists (`lmglevel.mjs`, in `convertSat`, the wardrobe's worn meshes and the mobiles plan); on the retail archives that changed no pick (4,642 lists before SOE's final names list, 4,882 with it, and the only one whose finest level is empty is empty at every level).
- A particle can carry other effects (a `PATT` in its `PTCL`: path, a start-percent range, kill-with-particle, and spawn on creation, death, a percent of life or ground contact). `exportParticle` converts them through its `attach` callback (`convertParticle` recurses, with a `{ pending: true }` placeholder in its cache against cycles) and writes each one's `file` onto the attachment. The game places them as transient effects in the parent's frame, one level deeper (at most 12), follows the particle each frame and, with kill-with-particle, sets their `rateScale` to 0 when it dies; a stopped transient ends when its particles are gone. At most 256 live per `ParticleEffects` (Mustafar's lightning chain peaks between about 75 and 140); `ParticleEffects.status` counts what was skipped. A light dust storm, the forest's falling leaves and Mustafar's lightning draw nothing except what they carry. `status` asks for the sky again while a sky's effects carry one the pack has no file for.
- **A carried effect named `.swh` is a ribbon, not a particle effect** (`tools/swg/swoosh.mjs`, `src/world/swooshTrail.ts`). An entertainer's ribbon stick is its mesh and one quad written with alpha 0 for its whole life, and that quad exists only to carry the ribbon, so while every carried effect was read as a `.prt` the ribbon failed with "expected FORM PEFT, got FORM SWSH" and the stick was bare. The file is `FORM SWSH > 000N { FORM PTEX, 0000 }`: the particle texture, then RGBA, a width in metres, an effect's name, nine fields nothing reads yet (written into the pack in file order) and, from 0001, a rate, a point count and a subdivision -- all 93 retail files read to the byte (89 before SOE's final names list), and that last reading is ours, made because it gives each effect a sensible length where reading the first unread float as a length would have made the skill-up swirl eighty seconds long. Told apart from a flip-book shader by its extension, never its root tag (an `.sht` can be `FORM SWSH` too). At run time a ribbon is a `SwooshTrail` on a carried handle: its carrier moves it like any carried effect, it keeps a ring of points, cuts its tail exactly at its length, curves each span (Catmull-Rom), and writes a camera-facing strip -- V down its length, U across, which is how the game's gradient textures are laid out -- into the particle batch its texture already has, so it makes no material, program or draw call. Every number of ours is `SWOOSH_TUNE`, live through `__debug.ribbons`; `status` asks for the weapons again while a ribbon its effects carry has no file.
- A flip-book shader is a root `SWTS` form: `NAME` the base shader (sometimes an exporter path like `c:\a_exported\shader\x.sht`, which lives at `shader/x.sht`), a timing form and one `TEXT` per frame (slot tag, texture path) that replaces the base's main texture. The timing forms are the client's switcher classes (their RTTI names are in the executable): `DTST` DeltaTimeSwitcher (count, seconds per frame), `DRTS` DeltaRandomTimeSwitcher (count, min and max, drawn per frame), `DPPT` DeltaPingPongSwitcher (count, min and max; plays forward then back), `DFST`/`DRFS` frame switchers only on samples. `SWSH` is the same with whole shaders as frames; the Theed `SWSH` frames are not in the archives, and the falls the world places are the scrolling `a_splitalpha_scroll` shaders.
- Texture scrolling is `TSNS` (slot tag, then u1, v1, u2, v2 per second) and only matters when the effect's vertex program adds `textureScroll` (`a_scroll.vsh`, `a_scroll_rgb1_a2.vsh`, the water programs): the offset is `frac(rate x seconds)`. Split alpha (`a_splitalpha.psh`) takes colour from set 0 and alpha from set 1 of the same texture; the converter writes an opaque colour image and an alpha-as-grey image (three's `alphaMap` reads green), each on its own glTF sampler so that GLTFLoader, which shares a `Texture` per image and sampler, gives every scrolling material its own offset.
- An effect pass's DATA has a heat byte after dither from version 10 on (and a pixel-shader byte first before version 5): reading blend and test at fixed offsets made every version-10 effect a cut-out. Only the scrolling, split-alpha, flip-book and unlit-alpha-screen shaders among the 2,113 that blend and test with z-write off are converted as translucent, in a new `translucent` field that leaves `alphaMode` alone because the sky's celestial sprites read it; the rest (foliage, hair, fences) stay cut-outs, because three sorts transparency per object and an instanced field would draw far over near. Translucent placed surfaces take `renderOrder` 3, or the terrain water, which follows the player and so always sorts nearer than an instanced mesh's centre, is blended over them. A shader can write its effect inline (`FORM EFCT` inside the shader, the whitewater and a few dozen holograms, particles and UI shaders); its pass is read like a file's.
- In the client a fully masked emissive texel is the texture colour exactly: the `a_emismap` family lerps the lit texture toward the unlit one, and the rest add the mask inside a light that `saturate` caps. three adds `emissiveMap` after lighting, so the converter splits such a texture in linear light into a base colour `rgb x (1 - m)` and an emissive `rgb x m` (image names carry the mask's source); laying `rgb x m` over the full texture made every lit sign twice as bright by day. The mask is read from the pixel program, and two shapes misled: `a_specmap_bump_emismap` declares a local `sample` in two blocks, the diffuse map (its alpha the gloss) and then the normal map, whose alpha is the glow, so a local is resolved from the read nearest before `emisMask`; and the envmask assembly programs `lrp` toward the environment cube (ENVM) by their shine mask with the same instruction the emismap ones use toward the texture, so only an `lrp` toward a sampler on MAIN is a glow. `a_emis_full` and its alpha variants are unlit (`KHR_materials_unlit`, `userData.unlit`); `emisadd` is unlit and additive, with alpha forced opaque only when the source factor is One. The game applies flags that are in a program's key (`alphaTest`, `alphaMap`, additive blending, `fog`) in the GLTF plugin's `afterRoot`, before anything compiles.
- Animated surfaces (`src/world/surfaces.ts`) swap `map`/`emissiveMap` between textures of one kind and move `texture.offset`, neither of which is in three's program key; every GLTFLoader that loads converted static models must be `surfaces.withPlugin(new GLTFLoader())`, and a material's link to its flip-book is the string `userData.swgTrack`, never a texture, because `material.clone()` copies `userData` through JSON. The plugin only registers; a material joins its track in `World.adoptMaterials`, when it is in the scene, and leaves on its `'dispose'`, because `AssetPack` swaps a cell's original material for a clone and never disposes the original. `World.unload` sweeps the records no material joined. The clock runs on `World.simTime`, so `__debug.advance` steps it.
- An item's name and description are string ids on its template (`objectName`, `detailedDescription`: a presence byte, the table, a presence byte, the key); take the first along the chain whose table and key are both set, since the base templates carry the table with an empty key. About one in ten points at `static_item_n`, a table no index before SOE's last publishes reached: SOE's final names list brings it (and `static_item_d`), and on a mount without the list those fall back to the table with the item's id as key, then to words made from the id (`name: null`).
- What an item occupies is its template's `arrangementDescriptorFilename`: `FORM ARGD > FORM 0000 > ARG ` (three letters and a space), each `ARG ` one alternative of NUL-separated slots (`ring_either` is `[ring_l]` or `[ring_r]`); a shirt is chest1 and a jacket chest2, so they are worn together. The player's slots are `abstract/slot/descriptor/player.iff` (`SLTD > 0000 > DATA`). SWG's arrangements put every lightsaber and polearm in both hands.
- Who may wear what is `datatables/appearance/appearance_table.iff`: a row per `shared_<id>`, a column per species and gender (`Male Human` to `Female Sullustan`), each cell `:default`, `:block` (cannot wear), `:hide` (worn, takes its slots, not drawn) or that species' own appearance. The wardrobe converter takes its own folder's column (the men's bracelets, the Ithorian cuts) and writes the rest of the gender as `fit`; a cut the archives hold stripped (six Ithorian camouflage pieces) falls back to the template's appearance, or the item is lost; `fit.own` never names the folder's own species, which wears the entry's `sat`. The Ithorian folders hold the table's `:hide` items as entries with no meshes (`sat` null, `parts` empty): anything that dresses from a wardrobe must expect them.
- Item pictures are baked at conversion by a software rasterizer (`tools/swg/thumbnail.mjs`, X negated like the GLBs) and shown as `<img>`: a live 3D cell compiles a program per material on the main thread, which is the stall the whole game is built to avoid. The texture entries carry a reduced copy of their main image as a non-enumerable `thumb` only while `wantThumbs` is set (the weapons and wardrobe commands, unless `--no-icons`), taken before any glow is split off, so no run decodes a PNG it has just encoded and the snapshot, gallery and ships runs pay nothing.
- Three skips an object with `visible = false` before the shadow pass looks at it, so hiding something that way removes its shadow too. What first person hides of the player goes on `FX_LAYERS.shadowOnly` instead: the portal renderer's shadow camera enables every layer and its view passes only 0 or 1 plus 31, so the shadow maps draw it and no view does. Layers are not in a program's key; nothing compiles on the switch, because `World.passesOf` treats the layer as an actor's, or a warm-up made in first person (an Effects switch, an arrival) builds the head's world-pass programs only and zooming out indoors compiles on the frame. `markActor` leaves a shadow-only mesh as it is, so nothing that prepares or marks the player un-hides the head; both it and `passesOf` ask `isShadowOnly` in `fxRegistry.ts`, which the node test calls. A draw with `FX_ALL_LAYERS` (the debug mask) sees shadow-only meshes too.
- What is "on the head" (`headRule` in `src/player/headHide.ts`) is decided per worn item from the client's data, never from mesh names: hair and the `hair/`, `wearables/hat|helmet|goggles/` template folders go whole; an item whose triangles all lie in head zones (skull, face, sideburns, neck) goes whole; a garment (it hides a zone below the neck) only ever loses its head triangles; an item hiding only head zones goes whole; otherwise the skin decides. A triangle is on the head when any vertex is weighted at least half to `Head` or below. Zones alone fail (some helmets have none, the Kashyyykian chest plates hide `skull`, the Mon Calamari body hides `skull`) and the hooded robe's arrangement does not take the `hat` slot; the skin is what tells the hood from the robe. Skin alone fails too: the Mandalorian Imperial helmet is skinned to `spine3` and only its zones hide it.
- A partly hidden mesh has its index reordered once so its head triangles are last, and its `onBeforeRender` narrows the geometry's draw range for the eye's passes while `onAfterRender` puts `Infinity` back; the shadow pass calls `onBeforeShadow` and draws it all. The range lives on the geometry only for the length of one draw, because the wardrobe doll's clone shares the geometry (and `clone()` never copies the hooks). The per-triangle zones are reordered with the index, and `Character.cull` culls through `cullIndex` (headHide.ts), which keeps the order and counts the kept triangles ahead of the head, so the kept head triangles stay last and the eye's count stays right.
- `Object3D.copy` deep-copies `userData` through JSON: never put a typed array or per-triangle data on a mesh's `userData` (the doll clones the character every time the wardrobe changes); per-triangle data belongs on the geometry's `userData` or in the owner's own structures.
- SWG's arrangements put every lightsaber and polearm in both hands. This game keeps Jedi Academy's dual style for the off-hand classes (`OFF_HAND_CLASSES` in `src/core/inventory.ts`, the one list the rack, the player and `planHold` read); a gun or the double-bladed saber empties the left hand. `Character.wearItem` honours the appearance table's `hide` for everyone who dresses (an empty part: it takes its slots and draws nothing), `Equipment` refuses `block`; the species packs are dressed in `shirt_s03`, `pants_s01` and `shoes_s01`, which the table blocks or hides for Wookiees, so an item a species pack carries as a part is worn as that part and counts as wearable (`packPartOf`), or every Wookiee would own a shirt it cannot put back on.
- Anything put on or taken up in play goes: `Character.loadPiece` (hidden), `customizer.settled()` (a recipe can add a normal map, a new program), `World.prepareActor` a mesh at a time, then `Character.putOn` in one synchronous step that also takes off what it displaces. `prepareActor` calls `markActor`, so `putOn` (whose `applyOcclusion` ends in the first-person head layers) must come last. A weapon is prepared on every hold, not once per file: a program is compiled for the target and scene of that moment, and the Effects setting or a travel changes both. A peer's clothes and weapon go on the same way (`dressPrepared` in `look.ts`, `RemotePlayers.applyHeld`), and a peer's rig stays hidden until its look is on and compiled.
- The outfit, the items and the hands are written by `Equipment` on every change: before the backpack, clothes changed in the world were never saved, never reached other players, and the weapon in hand was forgotten at logout. `Equipment.reset()` empties the hands on the way to the select screen and into the creator (`useSpecies` keeps the rig when the species is the same, hands and all), and every queued operation is dropped if the character changed under it, or one character's weapon is saved into the next one's record. `restoreHeld` puts the saved hands back unlit (`Player.equip` lights a Jedi's blade) and does not switch the class; `setClass` writes the class to the record.
- The hyperspace table's points (`datatables/space/hyperspace/hyperspace_locations.iff`: name, scene, X, Y, Z) are in the same frame as the station tables, so the game mirrors them alike, `(-X, Y, Z)`: each point's description gives its distance to its system's stations, and the unmirrored pairing matches all 41 exact distances (mean 164 m) while the mirrored one is kilometres out. The `space` command repeats the check on every run (`hyperspace.frameCheck` in `space.json`). `space.json`'s `hyperspace` is an object; the points are `hyperspace.points`, and the world keeps the whole pack as `World.spaceData`.
- `scene/hyperspace.iff` is HYPR > 0000 > DATA (a float, 1.05, unresolved), STG1 and STG3 (the stage's seconds, 2.2 and 5.2, which match their sounds; the warp `.prt`; a `.cef` whose only content is a sound; a float, 3.0 in both, unresolved, where the older one-stage `hyperspace_enter.iff` has 4.0; a `.snd`) and STG2 (35, 900, 0.4 and a `.snd`, read as the longest wait for the new scene, the speed the ship leaves at and the hand-over fade; a reading, not confirmed). A float after a string starts right after the string's terminator: read at a four-byte column in a hex dump it comes out wrong (STG3's 3.0 looks like 2.0). The client's `HyperspaceIoWin` has nested stages and listens for the scene change, which is why stage 2 is a wait.
- The jump's look is in its two `.prt` files, not the scene: the enter streaks are brightest from 50% to 80% of their 4 s life, an untextured 90 m "black drop" quad darkens the view behind them from 0.85 s, and the exit effect is a white-to-dark-blue untextured quad that dissolves at 5.0 s with its stars bursting at 4.0 s (`warpTimings` in `space.mjs` reads those times off the emitters). Untextured quads are drawn only for handles placed with `solid` (a 1-texel white texture on the shared particle program, in a batch of their own at render order 10). The jump places both warp effects without it: the black drop and the white-to-blue backdrop were made for the client's camera fixed behind the ship and showed from ours as flat boxes in space, one at each end; the streaks and the stars still play, and our own tunnel (`hyperspaceTunnel.ts`) covers the move. Every space zone prepares both warp effects at load, so their batches compile behind the loading screen; the converter writes the timings (`hyperspace.effects.timing`) and the jump runs on them.
