- A space zone is a system: `PlanetDef.space` is a planet's id only when the zone is that planet's orbit. Kessel (space_light1), Deep Space (space_heavy1) and Ord Mantell have their own truthy values there, so everything that goes down to a planet asks `planetBelow()`, never `planetById(zone.space)`.
- The `sandbox` command writes a system that is nowhere in the game: an ordinary space pack at the same version, with the same files, whose every place, size, distance and count is drawn from one seed and is ours. What it borrows from the archives is only what it is drawn with, through the readers the `space` command already uses: one converted zone's sky, that zone's own `.pln` surfaces for the bodies to wear, and the style tables the real asteroid fields are scattered from. The pack says so on every piece -- `source: 'invented'` on its points, `invented: true` on its fields, and a `sandbox` block naming the seed, how far it reaches and whose sky it took -- and that block is what everything downstream reads to know that nothing in the zone can be mistaken for the client's. It is optional: `status` lists it when it is there and never asks for it, and the zone is in `PLANETS` whether or not it has been converted, which is why the converter's list of the client's zones is no longer simply the game's.
- A space zone's ground is a plane at its terrain's base, 3 km down (`terrain: { base: -3000, amplitude: 0 }`, no `.trn`), never built, and `terrain.heightAt` still answers it: whatever a ship's flight reads from the ground must be guarded by `space`, or it is an invisible floor at y -3000 with half the stations under it (Tatooine's at -5872). `flyShip`'s slow hold, its stop at the floor and its landing all skip in space, as its ground easing, ceiling and crash always did. Separately, the physics engine caps a body at 400 m/s (times `integrationParameters.lengthUnit`, 1 here; measured): a speed commanded past it is cut every step, which the hit test read as a crash, so a boost in space (440 m/s) hit an invisible wall anywhere. `flyShip` never commands more than `BODY_SPEED_CAP` (399); the cruise and the readout still say 440. A ghosted hull in a jump is not tested and flies at the cap.
- Kessel's and Deep Space's hyperspace points, Deep Space's fields and its Star Destroyer are made up (`INVENTED_*` in `space.mjs`, `source: 'invented'` in the pack); Ord Mantell's one point is space_nova_orion's row, whose station, fields and sky are Ord Mantell's own. The Star Destroyer is `object/ship/shared_star_destroyer.iff` (the exterior only; the `spacestation_stardestroyer` template adds the 59-cell interior).
- A retail zone's planets are pictures hung on the sky at a fixed reach with no true distance, which is all any of the game's own zones ever needed. A body that stands somewhere (`place: 'world'` with a true `at` and `radius`) is placed every frame at its true direction from the camera, pulled in to no further than the sky's reach and scaled by the same ratio, so the angle it covers is exact at every distance and it grows with true parallax. Its depth cannot be a sphere cap built for one angle, so it is written by a flat quad hung near the camera whose fragment program traces the ray against the body and writes `gl_FragDepthEXT` at the true surface: the sphere it traces must be the DRAWN one, read off the picture's own matrix and never the quad's, because a true middle two hundred kilometres out, squared in the ray-sphere maths, is past what a float in a shader holds, and the depth found is multiplied back out. The deepest it may write is kept strictly inside the god rays' sky threshold, or a far planet reads as open sky. One program serves every such body in a zone (three keys a shader material's program on its source), built behind the loading screen, and the placement is a few vector operations into one kept record.
- A ship's hit test compares the velocity it commanded with what the step left, so at jump speed a slow frame's damping alone (5.4 m/s at 900 m/s over three steps) reads as a crash: a ghosted hull (`setGhost`) skips the test, and the jump commands its cruise through `Vehicle.jumpCruise`. Everything the jump holds (the hull, the tunnel and the camera's far plane, the crew hold, the effects) is released by `Hyperspace.abort`, which `travel`, the map's teleport, leaving for the select screen and death all call first; P, M (the map and its teleport) and N (noclip) are locked from the enter stage until control returns and E too, except in the transit for whoever is aboard the rooms (the lifts and the controls, never the door: `pressJumpE`), and I and B from the countdown on, since closing the ship edit page refits the hull in place. The jump's tunnel is one mesh for the session, in the scene hidden from the start so every loading screen compiles it, drawn at render order -0.5 (after every sky layer, which write no depth and some ignore it, before every ordinary opaque) and depth-writing, so the world behind it fails the depth test; fully closed, the camera's far plane comes in to its tip so the world is not drawn at all, and goes back (with `World.onCameraResized` for the cascades) the frame it opens. A walker who steps out of a door from the enter stage until control returns is boarded again at the entry (`keepCrew`), since the move would leave them behind.
- A run across a system at kilometres a second is held, not flown: Rapier caps a body's velocity at 400 m/s and the hull's own hit test reads that cut as a crash, so the run writes the pose itself through the hold API and ghosts the hull. The work is in what must not happen at that speed. The placed-object streamer is held where it is for the whole run (`World.streamHold`), and the hold must cover the NPC patrols as well, which stream themselves in: a sleeping group within a few kilometres wakes, spawns its hulls and compiles their programs on a live frame, and a few kilometres at that speed is a fraction of a second. Nothing new is drawn but the zone's own warp effect, already prepared at load, laid on again while the speed holds because a transient ends itself. The motion blur is handed the run's own step (`setReprojectionCarry`), or the reprojection of the scene depth pushes every pixel off the screen and the picture is a grey wash instead of a sense of speed. The others on the relay are told to hide the ship (`j`, as a jump does), since ten places a second a kilometre apart is a teleport on their side. The one moment a program can be built on a live frame is the stop, where the streamer is freed and `World.readyAround` waits at the stopping point with the ship already still, which is why `__debug.cruise` counts `programsWhileSettling` apart from `programsWhileFast`, and only the second must be 0.
- The loading screen in space waits for every tier in range, measured from the player's world position (aboard a hull `player.pos` is hull-local), and a jump waits `World.readyAround` inside its closed tunnel (at least `TUNNEL_TIMES.min`, 4 s), which also links every program still queued; a jump to another system then waits for the new zone's reflections (at most `ENVIRONMENT_WAIT`, 5 s) and compiles the whole scene once more (`World.settleCarried`), since there is no loading screen's settle to do it and a reflective material compiled before its cube landed would otherwise rebuild on the frame the tunnel opens; otherwise a 2 km landmark streams in and compiles after the screen lifts. Arriving from the galaxy map, `travel` streams from the zone's arrival (`arrivalAt`), where `arriveInSpace` puts the ship, not from the zone's origin.
- A group crosses together as a set of separate crossings, never as one: the leader's own `travel` and the jump's countdown both call `TravelTogether.leaving`, which offers the rest of the group the same trip, and whoever says yes makes the crossing their own way (flying in space after a jump they jump too, which is the only crossing that keeps a hull, its rooms and whoever is walking them; anywhere else the ordinary travel with the ship they are flying carried across). Where the leader came out is known only to their own browser -- a `state` goes to the world its player is standing on and no further -- so one word of its own, `cross`, goes to the sender's group and only to the group: `going` as a crossing begins, `here` with the place when it ends. `going` is also what makes the last place stale, so a group returning to a world it has been to before is never sent to the point somebody came out at the time before. The follower waits for that word under its own loading screen, bounded (`waitMs`) and before anybody steps out of a ship, and comes out at that place with a fixed step to one side taken from their own member id, so two ships never arrive inside each other. Whether a trip can be kept at all is asked **before** the yes goes out, because the server counts an acceptance once and for all and one spent on a refusal could never be said again; a member standing in a hull they are not flying is asked to step out first rather than carried, since nothing carries a passenger out of another browser's rooms.
- Objects wider than the streamer's 600 m collider cap in a space zone (the Star Destroyer) are kept in a set of their own: their collision is built with their tier, not by distance (the pack's radius counts from the model's origin, not its middle), in pieces of at most 4,000 triangles (`splitTrimesh` in `src/world/trimeshPieces.ts`) through `cleanTrimesh` and `TRIMESH_FLAGS`, about 3 ms a frame, and `settle`/`readyAround` wait for the pieces (in a hidden tab, where no frame runs `update`, they build them as they poll). Measured on a 120k-triangle sheet with the flags, 8 pieces took 76 ms in all (worst piece 17 ms) and 52 pieces 68 ms in all (worst piece 2.1 ms). Only in space (`LayoutStreamer`'s `hugeColliders`): a planet's snapshot gives thousands of ordinary objects a radius of 1024 m or more, which is not their size.
- A hull the world has disposed has no Rapier body: on a removed body `numColliders()` throws `unreachable`, and a throw inside `Hyperspace.update` (which `step` calls near its top) skips the rest of every frame after it. `Hyperspace.abort` releases a hull only while `alive` (still in `world.vehicles`), a crossing that comes back empty releases the old hull if it is still there, and `Vehicle.setGhost` checks `body.isValid()` first. A ghosted hull's colliders are the only ones in no collision group (`setGhost` is the only thing that empties a collider's groups): the bolts' world ray skips every collider in no group with a filter predicate (`Bolts.passable`), which covers a hull in a jump and one still being prepared before it joins `world.vehicles` alike, and every target choice (the NPC brains, the auto-pick, Tab) goes through `targetable` in `src/space/shipCombat.ts` (alive and not ghosted); any other ray cast without a group filter still hits one.
- The interface's colours are eighteen names declared once on `:root` in `src/style.css`, between the `palette` markers, and read from there by both sides: the panels' `var(--…)` uses and `src/core/palette.ts`, which hands the same strings to the overlay canvas and the same four layer colours to the hull flash, so a bar, a pip and the flash on the model cannot drift apart (`palette.test.ts` fails if the two tables disagree by a name or a value). The overlay (`src/ui/hudCanvas.ts`) is a plain 2D canvas, the first child of `#ui`, that touches no WebGL context and so compiles nothing; it draws no text ever (canvas text costs a shaping pass a call and gives up the browser's subpixel rendering), allocates nothing in a frame (colours are indices into a frozen table and alpha is `globalAlpha`, so no `rgba()` string is built; a polygon is read out of the caller's own array), strokes everything twice — once in `void`, two pixels wider, which is what makes a hairline read over a noon dune and over a starfield — and clears only the union of what it drew last frame, which a resize must clear rather than merely forget unless the backing store was reallocated. Every size is one number in `src/ui/hudMath.ts`, which is pure and node-tested, and `hud.css` states a handful of them again, which `hudMath.test.ts` checks against the table the way `palette.test.ts` checks the colours. Each piece of the display counts its own DOM writes and `__debug.hud()` sums them as `writes`, which should be 0 in a steady frame; a single shared counter was tried and taken out, because a window that only rolls when it is read reports whatever time has passed as one second. Every colour a panel wears is one of the same eighteen, either directly or as `color-mix(in srgb, var(--x) N%, transparent)` at exactly the alpha the literal had; `hudPage.test.ts` reads `src/style.css` as text and fails if a colour is ever typed into a panel by hand, and the three deliberate literals (the travel curtain, which must reach black because it replaces the world rather than being drawn over it, and the two lit wells behind an item's picture and the creator, which are a light rather than a surface) each carry a comment saying why.
- Everything the game says once goes to the message line (`src/ui/messages.ts`), never to the prompt: the prompt is rebuilt and rewritten every frame from outside every guard in `main.ts`, so anything written to it by an event lived about seven milliseconds and was never seen. Twenty-eight sites used to write it; the crash line, the relay's connection and every notice the ultra cruise sends were among them. The line holds what it is told for eight seconds in a fixed pool of eight elements, merges a repeat within two seconds into a count, and writes `textContent` only when it differs — so it never parses HTML, and a line moved there from the prompt must have its `<b>` tags taken off (`plain()` does it). The long prompt line is what is left and is now behind a setting; its cost was never its words but its conditions, which ask the world for the lift underfoot, the elevators near, the doorless building near and the nearest vehicle every frame.
- The overlay canvas is the first child of `#ui` so that every panel draws over it, and neither it nor anything the display builds on the page carries a `z-index`: two positioned boxes in one stacking context paint in tree order, so the page paints over the canvas. A shape meant to be seen *on* an element of the page must therefore be drawn by the page. A number slot’s cooldown is one custom property on the cell (`--cool`) and a conic gradient in `hud.css`, written only when it has moved by a step of `HUD_TUNE.cooldownSteps`; drawn on the overlay it sat under a cell whose own background is `--plate` at 0.7 and contributed about a fifth of a near-black, which is to say nothing. Drawing it on the page also takes away the `getBoundingClientRect` measuring, the resize listener and the whole question of what happens when the browser moves the row. A second a cooldown is running in writes about fifty times, which is meant to; a *steady* second is still 0.
- **The minimap's name and coordinates are page text laid over its canvas, and the coordinates are the map window's own pair.** The top-left block wears no plate (`#hud .top-left` in `hud.css` takes the panel's off it, in space too) and the planet's tagline is gone. The world's name and the coordinates are two elements after the canvas inside `.hud-minimap-face`, placed by the stylesheet with no `z-index`, so they paint over it in tree order and the canvas never draws a word; the mark for north is the canvas's own, turned with the picture and drawn before the player's arrow, because `minimap.test.ts` reads the last `rotate` as the arrow. The coordinates are the map window's own point in its own frame: the point is `App.mapPlace` -- the hull's place while you ride, fly or stand in its rooms, the figure's otherwise -- which the map's source, the minimap's middle and the corner's `/loc` on a planet all read, because the figure's own place stands metres off the hull's aboard and a seat's height off it riding, and read from the figure the two said different numbers whenever you sat in anything; the frame is `gameToRawX/Z` about the layout centre. They are rounded, written only when the rounded pair has changed and at most `MINIMAP_TUNE.locHz` times a second, so a still player writes nothing, a hull bobbing a few tenths of a metre on its springs writes nothing, and a pair that moved too soon is written by the first frame after the wait, still or not, leaving the last words where you stopped. The corner's own `/loc` line, which stands aside while the minimap shows, is handed that pair by `main.ts` before `hud.update`. In space it is the game's own frame and the figure's own place, as it always was, and not the space map's: that map's line mirrors x (`Math.round(-p.x)`, the client's frame) while a story's place, a waypoint and `__debug.teleport` all take the game's frame out there, and the hull the gravity boots name may be nowhere near the walker; so in space the corner and the space map differ in the sign of x alone, on purpose (the design asked for "the game's frame" and "matching the space map" in one breath, which are two different things). On a planet the corner's pair is the client's, which is what `teleportSwg` and a story's `raw` place take and **not** what `teleport` takes: a `/loc` line pasted into `teleport` lands somewhere else. What `__debug.minimap()` reports as `writes` and `locWrites` is the minimap's own count rolled on the wall clock, and `minimap.test.ts` stands that clock in and checks the count against what its stand-in page saw written every frame, since a write that missed its counter leaves the console reading nought while the page is written. The nearby name is a fixed-height line of its own at the block's foot, written on a change of name. The help block's place is a sum stated in both stylesheets -- the block's top, then either the name and `/loc` lines or the circle, the city's line and the wrap's margin, then the nearby line -- and `hudMath.test.ts` adds both up again from the two files, so a line added to the corner must be carried into both sums; a gap between two of its lines is padding and never margin, since two margins in a row collapse into one and the sum then comes out a few pixels long (measured: the nearby line's margin vanished into the wrap's).
- Anything on the screen that shows a key must be told when one is rebound, and must read the key of the thing it stands for rather than its own place in a row. The note is `onBindingsChanged`/`notifyBindingsChanged` (`src/ui/hudPage.ts`, re-exported by `src/ui/menu.ts` so the design’s import path works), fired wherever a binding is written — the Controls page’s `assign`, its reset button, and `__debug.bind`/`__debug.resetBindings` — never in a frame, with each listener in a `try` so a display that cannot redraw its caps does not take the menu down with it. An ability cell takes `slot<n>` from the slot the ability is in (`KitSlot.key`), because both classes drop an empty loadout entry: counting the cells instead put the wrong key on every cell after the first hole. Caps are spelled by `keyLabel` in `src/ui/hud.ts` (`Ctrl`, `Shift`, `LMB`, `↑`), never by the Controls page’s `keyName` (`Left Ctrl`), which is a keyboard diagram and does not fit a 22 px cap; the action bar imports the same one, so a key is spelled the same in both places two centimetres apart.
- A key is not free because `DEFAULT_BINDINGS` lacks it. One panel still listens for a raw code of its own outside the bindings table, the trade window (`TRADE_UI_TUNE.panelKey`, T while a trade stands), which the Controls page does not show; and at a ship's controls two raw keys are read with `justPressed`, J for the engine cut (`CUT_ENGINES_KEY`) and O for the ultra cruise (`CRUISE_KEY`), on the very keys the journal and the pick-up are bound to, so both of those carry `&& !atControls` (and `hudWiring.test.ts` reads both gates). The group's panel had the full stop as a raw key until its roster became a tab of the map, and Y before that, until the Waypoints window took Y and one press opened both; the full stop is now the `group` binding, so T is the one raw panel key left. Read that tune and those two keys before giving a new action a default. **A default that moves onto a key a player has chosen for something else gives way, and has it back when the choice moves** (`loadedBindings` in `src/core/input.ts`): an action nobody chose loses any of its default keys that a chosen binding of another action holds and that action's own default does not, so a player who had put an emote on J does not find J opening the journal as well when the journal's default moves there; keys two defaults share on purpose (the placing keys borrow R and F from the saber throw and the torch, and right mouse is both the other attack and the block) are left alone, because the chosen action held that key by default too. A borrowed key works only because its other owner is muted while something is in hand: `Input.mute`/`muteUntilUp` mute by **action**, never by key (R must still read as the placing turn while the throw is muted), and only `held`/`pressedAction` -- a raw `isDown` or `justPressed` is never muted, so anything new that reads a raw key or mouse button fires while a thing is in hand. **Only what the player chose is stored** (`Input.chosen`, through `chosenBindings`, which also drops a choice that is merely the default), and the bindings are worked out from it again on every change by the very rule a load uses, so what is pressed now is what a reload gives and a player who never rebound something moves with the defaults. The first cut stored every action that differed from its default, which wrote an action the rule had emptied down as a choice of no key the first time anything else was rebound, and that action never had its default back. **An empty binding asked for is kept** (`bind(a, [], { empty: 'none' })`, and a stored `[]` reads as a choice of none), because the plain `bind(a, [])` restores the default, which is what `__debug.bind` documents and still does. The Controls page rebinds through `Input.assignKey` and nothing else: the key pressed comes off every other action the player chose keys for (left with what it has left, none included, and kept that way), an action on its defaults is left to the rule (so it has the key back once the choice moves off it), a key two defaults share stays shared when one of them is put back on it (`takesKeyFrom`: the first cut took right mouse off the other attack for good when the block was put back on it), and Backspace on an action's last key leaves it with none.
- Our own marks are one file, `src/ui/hud.svg`: a `<symbol>` per glyph on a 24×24 box, each carrying its own `fill`, `stroke`, `stroke-width` and joins, because `<use>` clones the symbol’s subtree and whatever the file’s root says is left behind. It is fetched once at boot through `new URL('./hud.svg', import.meta.url)` — which the builder rewrites and which a node test never reaches for — and put in a hidden `#hud-icons`; a sheet that does not arrive costs the marks and nothing else, every cell keeping its key, its cost and its border. Which mark an ability wears is keyed on its name with everything but the letters taken out (`hudIcons.ts`), with word tests under it and a last-resort mark under those, so an ability added later still shows something. Every glyph stays inside 3 to 21 of its own box: one that ran to the edge touched its neighbour at 28 px. The name and health over what the crosshair rests on (`src/ui/nameplate.ts`) needs both `hp` and `maxHp` on the living thing — a body that keeps only `hp` shows a name and, silently, no bar, which cannot be told from a full one.
- What the screen offers you to press is gathered a few times a second into one kept struct (`App.gatherPrompt` into `newPromptState`, at `HUD_WIRING.promptHz`), never per frame, and both the short action bar and the long prompt line read that struct. The words were never the cost: the *conditions* were, and the long line asked the world on every frame for the lift underfoot, the elevators near (twice on one line, once to test and once to read), the doorless building near, the nearest vehicle — through three separate walks for "in reach", "has a room" and "on its back" — and the ship the boots could reach. The gather asks in the long line's order, not the bar's, so the lift underfoot is asked for while a bridge's controls are held; the bar's rules read the same struct in their own order. The bar is emptied on the frame the game stops simulating rather than at the next gather, as the overlay is, and the long line is written only on a frame that simulates, since half its branches read the struct and half read the player and with a panel up it would otherwise disagree with itself; a jump's own line goes over it either way. The two words the struct has no room for (the lift's level count, the doorless building's name) are kept beside it, and are put back when the world is left, as the struct's own reset does not reach them.
- A panel whose own `canOpen()` asks `anyPanelOpen()` must never be added to `anyPanelOpen()`: it would then read "something else has taken the screen" about itself and shut a quarter of a second after every press of its own key. The trade window frees the mouse without joining that list. The group's roster used to as well, and stood down on its own when the pointer lock went back to the game; it is a tab of the map now and does neither (its `canOpen` only says whether the chevrons over far members may be carried, and the map tab has no `canOpen` of its own), so nothing about the roster holds the mouse or listens for the lock. Escape out of a typed line is the same thing from the other side: the browser takes the lock back on that very press, so the line asks for it again and holds the game's keys aside for a moment afterwards, or the one press that closed the line also opens the menu. A loop that carries a marker over a head is armed only while there is a marker to carry and disarms itself when there is not — a group in which nobody is far enough off runs no frames at all.
- Every window moves by its header and sizes by the grip in its bottom-right corner and its right and bottom edges (`draggable` in `src/ui/drag.ts`; the arithmetic, the minimums in `WINDOW_MIN` and the reading of old records are `src/ui/windowBox.ts`, node-tested). `localStorage['swg3js.panels']` is `{ id: { x, y } }` for a window only moved and `{ id: { x, y, w, h } }` for one sized, so the records from before sizing read as they stand. A sized window carries `win-sized`, and every "fill the frame" rule hangs on that class, so a window at its own size is laid out exactly as it was; double-clicking the grip drops the size and keeps the place. The grips are the panel's last children and carry no z-index. The click that ends a drag is swallowed in the same task, or a drag let go over a panel's backdrop closes it and one let go over the world outside the trade window takes the pointer lock. A WebGL canvas's buffer is compared with its box the way three sizes it (`Math.floor`), or at 125% and 150% scaling the map set its size again on every frame; a canvas sized from a ResizeObserver is drawn again inside the observer, with its camera placed first, because the observer runs after the frame's draw and before it is shown. `__debug.windows()` reports every window's box, its minimum and whether it fits. A press on a scrollbar is never a move or a size: the header's own (the Escape menu's nav scrolls once the window is short) is skipped by the move, and a grip or edge strip lying over a list's scrollbar steps aside (`win-pass`, pointer-events off) while the pointer is on that bar. A drag ends on the first of `pointerup`, `pointercancel` and `lostpointercapture`, or a capture taken away with no release left the window busy and unmovable. Every injected stylesheet (each `const <NAME>CSS` template string under `src/`) is held to the palette rule by `hudPage.test.ts` with no exceptions. Two things are never windows: the select screen, which is a whole screen, and the creator's full-size panels (`.creation` on the overlay), which are neither moved nor sized and have their inline place and size cleared while the creator holds them; `windowBox.test.ts` fails if the select screen is ever handed to `draggable`.
- **The Waypoints window and the group's roster are tabs of the map, and a list tab never touches the map's one WebGL canvas.** The roster that moved is the group's panel (`GroupUi`); the strip of members down the side of the display (`Roster` in `hud.ts`, with the jobs followed hung under it) is another thing and stays where it was. The map has four tabs (`src/ui/mapTabs.ts`): Here and Galaxy are pictures on the one canvas that is passed between them, and Waypoints and Group are lists, pages built by their own files and handed to the map with `MapUi.adoptBody`, which shows and hides them and tells each page once as it comes up and once as it goes (`listShown`). Showing a list cancels the frame loop and calls `galaxy.hide()`, and never `attachCanvas`, `setSize`, `setPixelRatio` or a move of the canvas: it waits, its context whole, in whichever picture's body had it last, and the pictures' own draws already skip a canvas with no size, so going to a list and back costs the pictures nothing (`mapTabs.test.ts` builds the real window against a stand-in page and counts every size written, every move and every frame asked for). The waypoints' page is made before the map exists (`wireWaypoints` runs first) and adopted once it does; the roster is adopted where the group is wired. Group is offered only while a server holds a group for this browser (`MapSource.groupTab`, `App.groupHeld`), and asked for with none the map shows Here. Y and the full stop go through one rule (`tabKey`): the map shut opens on that tab, open on another switches to it, already on it shuts it, and a tab not on offer makes its key do nothing. A plain opening (M) opens on the tab used last, kept in `localStorage['swg3js.mapTab']` and read inside a `try`, since a page can refuse storage outright. Being tabs, both lists pause play as the rest of the map does and hold the mouse through the map: neither is in `anyPanelOpen` or `mouseHeldElsewhere`, neither is a window (`draggable`, `WINDOW_MIN`), and the map's own minimum width is the waypoint row's 600. The chat line does not open over the map, so the roster's hint says to shut it before typing `/g`; a trade's window waits for the map to be gone, so the roster's Trade button shuts the map before it asks. Y and the full stop go through `pressTabKey`, which carries `tabKey`'s answer out on the map, and `mapTabs.test.ts` drives that one function on the real window; `tabKey` shuts the map when its key is pressed on its own tab before it asks whether the tab is on offer, so a Group tab left showing when the group ended is still shut by the full stop. Each page hands the map its own `shown` (`WaypointsUi.shownFrom`, `GroupUi.shown`) and `main.ts` adds nothing of its own, so the test that adopts the real pages into the real window sees what the player would. And because the lists are no longer panels, whatever answers a key while play stands still under the map must ask `map.open` itself: the pick-up does (O behind the Waypoints tab took a prop into hand under it), and the inventory and the spawner shut the map as they open, as the journal and the ship menu always did, or their own key again took the pointer back with the map still on the screen.
- The select screen (`src/ui/characterSelect.ts`, its rules in `src/ui/selectModel.ts`, node-tested) stands the chosen character up by the very steps `App.play` takes (`useSpecies`, the look, the outfit) through one queue in `main.ts`, so Play and Create wait behind the load in flight and find the rig already dressed, and two rigs can never race onto the player; a choice waits `SELECT_TUNE.dwellMs` before loading (a species rig is about 200 MB of clips) and a generation number drops any load it overtook. The figure is a clone in a `CharacterPreview` of its own context, playing the mood's idle on the clone's own skeleton through the preview's `onFrame`, framed afresh per character (`reframe`) and let go (`release`) the moment the game starts; releasing disposes the clone's geometry, which the live character shares, so the world renderer uploads those buffers again behind the loading screen, as the wardrobe's doll always has. `applyAppearance` sets only the colours that differ from the pack's defaults, so a rig a second character of the same species puts on keeps the first one's colours wherever the second keeps a default: the wiring puts every colour back first (`putBackLook`), and the real fix belongs in `look.ts`. Deleting asks in the page and the answer stays disabled for `SELECT_TUNE.armMs`; `window.confirm` is used nowhere. `__debug.select()` reports the figure, the loads, the boxes and `stretch`, which must be 1.000 in a visible window.
- Where a blow on the player came from reaches the game by two different paths, and both have to carry it. Everything that strikes calls `damage(amount, from, push, source)` on the target, and `PlayerTarget` hands that on to the callback the frame loop sets; but a creature that is aggressive and *unprovoked* does not call `damage` at all — `Creature.update` calls the `onAttack` it was handed, which is `World.hurtPlayer`, which calls `playerTarget.hurt` directly. Anything reading the direction must be fed from both, or the commonest bite on a planet arrives with no side to it. So `onAttack` carries `from` the whole way down (`Creature.update`, `CreatureManager.update`, `World.update`, `World.hurtPlayer`) and `PlayerTarget.damage` hands it to its callback. `App` keeps one wrapper round `world.playerTarget.damage` as a second source, installed in its constructor — the record is one object for the life of the session — held for the length of the call in a `try`/`finally` so a direction cannot be left standing to be worn by the next blow that has none, and read by the frame loop’s damage closure as `from ?? this.hurtSource`. Every one of the nine places in `main.ts` that hurt the player goes through `App.hurtFrom`, which raises the red flash and, with a direction and within `HUD_WIRING.hurtRange`, the arc on that side; the direction is measured from `player.worldPos`, not `player.pos`, because aboard a ship's rooms `pos` is the hull's frame.
- The relay grew into a small server in place, and every part of it had to be added without breaking a browser built before it. The server sends `hail` the instant a socket opens (the clock, how long a day is, a nonce, whether a join word is wanted, whether damage between players is on); an old browser has no case for it and drops it silently, and `welcome` stays exactly where it always was, sent once in answer to the first hello — a browser that ran `case 'welcome'` twice would re-fire `onJoin` for every peer and re-fetch a rig, a look and a `prepare` for each of them mid-play. A browser that hears no hail within a second knows it is talking to the relay that came before and turns everything new off. Adding fields to `welcome` is safe; sending it twice is not. The version a hello says is the other half of the same promise, and it bit when the wire went from three to four: the third wire's relay records a browser's version only when it is exactly its own and passes a creature's bite on only to a browser it recorded, so a newer browser that said 4 in a hello sent before the hail was recorded as nothing and lost every bite on itself while `speaks(3)` still said yes. A hello now says the lesser of the browser's version and the server's once the server has hailed (`Session.helloVersion`), a hello that went first is said again in the server's version when that is the third wire or later, and every relay since the fourth takes the lesser of the two itself (`Math.min` where it records `c.v`), so a browser and a relay a wire apart keep everything the older of the two speaks and lose only what is new.
- A claim can fail in two quite different ways and they must not be answered alike. A proof that does not match and a wrong join word are the end of the line: that browser is not who it says it is, asking again cannot change that, and it is sent `denied` and closed. A character that belongs to another player is the end of that **character** only: the browser is sent `refused`, keeps its line and can offer a different one. A character id can genuinely collide, because the maker falls back to a time plus six random characters on a page with no `crypto.randomUUID`, and closing the line over it would lock that player out of the server for good, on every reconnect, with nothing telling them why.
- The server's news goes only to the world a player is standing on, but a peer is taken apart only when their line actually closes. `join` is the expensive word — `RemotePlayers.remove` then `add` → `dress`, a rig fetch, the wearables and `World.prepareActor`, which is a shader compile on a live frame — and `hello` is the cheap one, which the browser already handles correctly when it says another planet: keep the peer, stop drawing them. So the server keeps a set per connection of the peers that browser holds, sends `hello` for anyone it holds and `join` only for a first meeting, never sends `leave` merely because someone travelled, and sends `leave` on a closing line to everyone who holds that peer wherever they are now standing. What is saved is the stranger on another planet, who is never mentioned at all; a friend hopping to another planet and straight back costs nothing, which is what the first cut of the routing got wrong.
- The world the server keeps is `world.json` plus `world.log` (a line of JSON per change with a sequence number, appended and `fsync`ed as it happens; the snapshot is written to a temp file, flushed, renamed over the real one, and only then is the log emptied). Three Windows facts decide its shape: `ftruncateSync` on a handle opened for appending throws `EPERM`, so the log is emptied by closing the handle, writing the file empty and opening it again; a rename over a file another program holds open throws `EPERM`/`EBUSY`, so it is tried a few times and then left to the next ordinary write with the log keeping everything; and a failed rename must schedule nothing of its own, or the server writes and fsyncs the whole world about fifteen times a second for as long as somebody has the file open in an editor. Every table keyed by something a browser chose is `Object.create(null)`, and is rebuilt that way when a file is read back, so `__proto__` is a key and not a way into the language's own objects; the wire refuses `__proto__`, `constructor` and `prototype` outright as the near half of the same belt.
- A server started with a join word listens to nothing but a claim until the browser has said who it is (`relay.mjs`), so the hello cannot go first: with a word set the browser waits for the hail, claims, and then says hello, and with no word it says hello at once exactly as it always did. A browser told its character was opened somewhere else must clear `wanted` as well as closing the socket, or the reconnect takes the character back and the two browsers kick each other for ever. The player's key is never sent: what a server keeps is a second hash of it (`server/identity.mjs`), and what answers the server's challenge is an HMAC of the nonce under that.
- **`receive()` in `src/net/net.ts` is a switch with no default: a server word with no `case` there is dropped in silence.** The server's `purse` answer had none for as long as the purse existed, so with a server every fare waited for ever and the balance read nought while every purse test passed (they handed the purse its words by hand). `tools/swg/tests/netRoutes.test.ts` now reads every server module for the words it sends (anything spelled `t: '<word>'` that is not a `write(`/`.change(` record) and fails for one with no case, and the relay's header must list it too. A test of anything the server says goes through the browser's own `Net` against a real relay (`purse.test.ts`, `storyRelay.test.ts`), never only through the module's own `word()`. And a tie between two copies of a character that is answered from memory is answered *before* the `claimed` word arrives (the server sends `settle` first), so whatever waits on the tie must ask `session.ask` (is a question still standing?) and never `keep === 'ask'`, which the claim reports even when the answer has already gone; the relay keeps the verdict on the line (`c.keep`, rewritten by `onSettle` and by the wait running out) for whatever settles after the character. Two more traps of the same settling: a part of the character that `characterMark` reads and that a settle moves (the story book's `local`, which a settle takes back to nought) must adopt its new mark through `Session.noteSettled`, which moves no counter, since a settle counted as a change makes this browser's copy the newer one at the next claim, written over a server's copy the player had just chosen; and nothing that settles may speak before the line has the claim (`authority === 'server'`), or a word left over from the last line reaches the server after the claim and is answered for a tie the player has not yet been shown (the server refuses a `sync` while `c.asking` stands, as the near half of the same belt).
- **The story held in this browser (`src/story/localHost.ts`) works nothing out until a set has been read, and asks `BookClient.holdsHere()`, never `host`, before it does.** A book read before its sets arrive (the sets are fetched at boot and usually land after the character's book) would have every running job revised against an empty set and held as "paperwork revised"; and `host` is only what the last `derive()` found, so a server that took the book over a moment ago still reads `local` -- a batch worked out then is refused by `applyLocal`, and a refused batch pays nothing and says nothing, or a reward the book never recorded would be handed over and paid again later. The book records a reward before the purse or the backpack is touched, never after. The detectors only raise; the step machine decides, so a level detector repeating itself (`repeatEv`) costs nothing, and anything that reads the view after the detectors have run must read it again, since what they raised may have moved a job on. Three traps found in review: an observe step's watch is closed by the word of leaving its area and by nothing else, so the detectors say every area left (`StoryWatch.leaveAll`) when a travel begins, on a death and before a new world's reset, and only the select screen forgets in silence, because the host closes every watch itself there; the character leaves the world for the story (`LocalHost.leave`) first on the way to the select screen, while its purse, its backpack and the line are still its own, and leaving never builds a loop of its own over a book in hand, or a book a server held until the line was put down would be settled there and its rewards recorded as paid to nobody; and a server that answers but holds no story still keeps the character's credits, which only its admin may add to, so the jobs wait there (`jobsWait`) rather than record a payment that never arrives.
- **The server's story host (`server/stories.mjs`) knows where a player stands only two ways: from its own state about a layout centre it was told (`--assets`, each world's `pois.json`), or from what the browser says, so every story word a browser sends carries `at` (the place in the story's frame, the room, the hour) -- the job words and the console's as well as the events.** The parity test caught a console grant on the server measuring a job's relative place from nothing, because only events had carried it. Three more: `HostCore` holds the store's own book object, which a book taken whole replaces, so the loop is rebuilt whenever `bookOf` is another object (`coreOf`); the server's story modules may import only node's built-ins and `src/story/` (`storyImports.test.ts`), so what it knows of worlds (`server/storyWorlds.mjs`, over `src/data/planets.ts`) and the sets' files (`storySet.mjs`) are handed in by the relay; and a thing a job pays while the ledger does not yet hold the character's backpack must wait for it (`Stories.held`), because the first backpack handed up replaces whatever is there.
- A group is held by the server, and a member of it is a **character, not a connection**: the key is `c:<character id>` once a browser has claimed one and `s:<connection>` otherwise, which is what lets a reload keep its place. A line closing holds that place for ninety seconds — the group is told the member stepped out, the roster reads them away, the place still counts against the eight so a reload cannot be locked out of its own group, and the leader changes hands only when the hold runs out, to whoever has been in the group longest. The one closing that must not look like stepping out is a takeover: the same character opened in a second browser closes the older line, so `Groups.absent` ignores a line that is no longer the one holding the member and `present` takes the older line out the moment the newer one claims, or the whole group is told somebody went away who is standing right there. Turning an invitation down takes back that one invitation and no other — only somebody actually going takes the ones they sent with them, and whoever sent one is told, rather than being left reading that it is still out while the person they asked has already been dropped.
- The group's words and its distances are the game's, read out of `datatables/player/radial_menu.iff`: an invitation reaches 90 m (`GROUP_INVITE`), a trade 8 m, a duel 128 m and a death blow 10 m, and 16384 m is that table's mark for no limit rather than a distance, so an invitation to somebody on another world is refused for being on another world and never for being 16,384 metres away. `GROUP_RANGES` in `server/groups.mjs` and `GROUP_RANGE` in `src/net/groups.ts` each hold the rows and neither is tunable. Everything beside them is ours and is one object apiece — how many fit in a group, how long an invitation stands, how many one browser may have out, how long a place is held, how often it may ask its group for anything, how many lines of chat a second — printed by the status page and moved for a run with `--set group.<name>=<number>`. A chat line is sent back to the speaker by the server rather than shown where it was typed, so everyone reads the same words in the same order and what is shown is what the server let through; a distance is measured in the relay, which is the only part that knows where two players are standing, and the state a browser sent from a world it has since left is thrown away rather than measured against.
- A label over another player's head hangs on where their figure is being **drawn** this frame (`RemotePlayers.peerAnchor`), never on where their last message put them: the figure glides toward that point over the tenth of a second between messages, and one riding in somebody else's hull is placed from the carrier's own pose, so a label hung on the message's place swims off the head and snaps back ten times a second. The roster's distances and the invitation's 90 m read the message's own place, where that lag does not matter.
- **The emulator's conversations are kept as structure and played only once adopted.** A conversation file declares a screen and then adds it to its template, and seven files declare a second screen under a first one's name, so `readConversations` reads a file a statement at a time (`readStatements` in `lua.mjs`): read by name, as `readLua` reads, the first screen is lost and the second added twice. It keeps screens, links, the client's string ids and only *where* a handler acts, never its code, and a line in the emulator's own English is null and `core3-literal`. `src/story/core3Trees.ts` folds a tree into the same `TalkDef` a story's own is, marks `needs` wherever the handler did something the structure has not got, and a tree with any reachable `needs` is nobody's; even a complete one is given to a creature (`voices`) only through an adoption in `src/story/core3/`, the heralds' first. **No herald is in any pack**: the server stood them from a table of its own (`heraldScreenPlay`) that the spawns readers never read, so the `conversations` command stands them itself, as rows of the packs' own shape (`speakers.json`'s `stand`), and a world's load appends them. One of the game's own people is `row:<key>` in the book, but what gives them a conversation is the creature they are stood as this life (a crowd's is drawn), which goes up with the talk word (`who`) and lives in `TalkState`. The game's conversations list no set of their own (`sets` empty), so joining them never makes a host "read a story": a library holding only them would read every job in a book as revised away. A server that reads no set still lets the heralds speak, through a host loop of their own that only ever opens and answers a conversation and is never loaded, swept or given a new library (`Stories.talkCoreOf`). A herald names a place with a client string reference longer than a waypoint's 48 characters: it is kept whole only on a waypoint a story set (`by`, `cleanStoryWaypointName`) and shown in the client's words only there, so a name a player types is cut and never read as a reference, and a reference a host from before cut short is still the same waypoint to the herald (`sameStoryName`) and is shown as a plain "Waypoint".
- **The story is one rule book with two hosts, and a book only ever moves by a closed list of changes.** `src/story/` is pure TypeScript the server imports under node's type stripping (`server/stories.mjs`), so the browser with no server and the server with one run the very same step machine through `HostCore`: every event is worked out on a draft (`draftOf`, which copies the tables and never the records in them, so a record is only ever replaced and never changed in place), the watchdog runs over it, and what it changed is applied as a batch of records `applyChanges` knows, each cleaned and refused with a reason when the book will not take it, the revision rising once a batch. Anything the rules clamp is written as the whole clamped record (`trackSet`, `npc`, `companion`), never as an amount to add, because two hosts whose tunes differ would otherwise each clamp an add their own way and the two books would part; the record the rules worked out is what is stored, on either side. Deadlines that fell while the character was away are settled in the order they fell, each at its own moment (`settleDeadlines`), before the character comes in. Every story word that crosses the wire is rebuilt field by field by a cleaner on arrival (`storyWire.ts`, the view's cleaners in `view.ts`), and each has a test through the browser's own `Net` against a real relay, as every server word must (`netRoutes.test.ts`, above). The last wave of the pass added no word at all: the hail's story version (5) is what tells a browser it may send the companion's event, since a server from before would refuse an event kind it does not know.
- **Everything a story hands over is paid once, keyed by where it is written, and the key is recorded before anything is paid.** A reward is `<quest>#<completion>#<step>` and an action that hands something over (`pay`, `give`, `xp`, `standing`, `trust`) is a reward of its own at `<quest>#<completion>#do:<step>.<phase>.<i>` (in a conversation `<tree>#1#do:<node>[.<reply>].<i>`, once to a character however often the answer is given), so a restart, a drop and a fresh grant, or a failure and a retry reach the key the first try wrote and pay nothing, while a quest done and taken again is a new completion and pays again. A price (`charge`) and a fine are taken every time, keyed only so the host can tell them apart. The console's `run` has no quest to key by and keys by the moment, so two actions in one call must each have a place of their own (`console<i>`): with one shared place the second payment of a call was swallowed as already paid, and two calls in one millisecond share a key still, which is why the tests move the clock between them. The purse is read once for a batch, so everything that takes money in one batch counts against `spent` and a fine takes no more than what is left of it, the rest owed. The purse can still refuse the charge when it comes to be paid (it moved since it was read, or a server keeps it), so a fine's charge names its debt (`PayOrder.owe`) and both hosts owe a refused one there in a batch of their own (`HostCore.owe`), saying it as owed: a fine ends taken or owed, never neither.
- **The client's own words are never quoted: not in the repository, not in the test set, not in a story the server holds.** A story names them `@table:key`, the `conversations` command writes the owner's own tables into `assets-private/`, and only the browser resolves a reference (`strings.ts`, `text.ts`), showing `[…]` until its table lands. The emulator's English is not the client's and is left out the same way (null, `needs: 'core3-literal'`). A conversation's transcript keeps a client line as this browser read it out, handed up when the window closes, or as its bare reference when it was never resolved, never as a guess; every committed test asserts on its own `TEST:` words or on a reference, never on a line out of the archives.
- **A document is worked out once and frozen the first time a character opens it.** The page -- its version chosen by the first `--- variant` whose condition holds, its slots filled, `{player}`, `{npc:...}` and `{date}` written in -- goes into the journal as it was read, by the hash of its words, and is read back from there ever after, whatever the file says later; so two characters who chose differently hold two different letters, an author's rewrite never changes what anybody already read, and a document handed again is a new handing, read and frozen again. The journal only grows (no change takes an entry away, and a book handed up whose journal is shorter within one timeline is refused), and its words are kept apart by hash, once for everyone, so a page whose words were lost says `NOT_KEPT` rather than showing the file's current text; one the journal is too full to keep is shown as it reads now and never frozen.
- **Rank is written down, never worked out, and Trust moves only on a choice made under pressure.** Crossing a rung's thresholds only makes `rankReady(t)` true for the promotion beat the owner writes (or takes the rung by itself when it is written `auto`), and only while the track's status is `active`, which nothing but the story's own `useTrack` and `activate` reaches; so a rank can be lost (`demote`, `suspend`, `burn`) and a stranger who happened to do a track's favours is not made one of its cell. The checker holds the data to the pressure rule (rule 5, `movesTrust`), for a track's Trust and a named person's alike (`trust()` and the third number of `npc()`). What the interface reads (the view) carries Standing only as a bar whose steps are cut by `barStep` and Trust only as one of five words, and that is all anything that draws may read; the numbers themselves are the book's, so a browser's copy of the book and every `ch` batch a server sends hold them, and `__debug.standing()` prints them. A rank changing is said on the message line in words, and neither Trust moving nor a file entry ever is. `historyMax` is held to the book's own `trackHistory`: turned past it, a track's history outgrew what the book takes of one and every move of that track was refused from then on.
- **Only the story's named people keep a record, and what is true of them is kept apart from what the character knows.** A Standing, a Trust, an access or a life is kept for a cast member or for one of the game's own people a cast file promotes (`row:<key>`), and for nobody else: anybody else spoken to keeps only the stamps of having met them and learned their name, the rules write nothing more for them, and a server cuts a book handed up to that (`namedOnly`), since ten thousand townsfolk each holding a Standing toward every character is a book nobody can carry. `alive` is the truth and `known` is what the character has learned (the page that told them, or the companion falling beside them), and the People tab reads only the second, so a person the story killed off-screen is still somebody the character expects to meet; the world reads the first, so they are never stood or spoken to again. A track that burns the character refuses its own named people too (`accessOf`), until somebody vouches. That rule reads a cast member's side out of the sets, which a browser on a server does not hold, and somebody with no conversation of their own is greeted without any host being asked, so who will not speak is handed over in the view (`mute`) and the greeting reads it. Everything that writes a person's record goes through `whoOf` first: `introduce` once did not, and a promoted row introduced by its cast id was named under a key nothing reads, shown by the name they go by until named and listed twice on the People tab.
- **The companion is a follower who goes down instead of dying, and the browser is believed about it.** It takes follower slot 0 with the owner kind `companion` (so `FollowerSet.full` never counts it against the three), is stood again beside the player at every arrival behind the loading screen (`FollowerSet.clear` drops it with the world), and is aboard and undrawn in anything the player rides. A `downable` body at nought health lies in `loop_incapacitated_face_up` where its body has one and in its held death clip otherwise, in a knockdown phase of its own (`out`) so the cull draws it whole, and leaves `World.targets()` so nothing goes on hitting it. Getting it up is the use key held beside it, in the E dispatch before the person's own talk, or its own clock with nothing within `selfClear` still in the fight (`isThreat`: hostile to the player on sight, or still fighting or holding a grudge against the player, a follower or the companion), which is never asked of the companion's own temper, since one who cannot fight is passive and nothing would ever count against it. **It is one body, handed across**: the cast stands the companion before they are taken on and after they are told to wait or let go, and the keeper stands them in between, so taken on in their own conversation or come back when spoken to where they wait, the cast's body is handed to the keeper where it stands (`StoryStands.release`, `CompanionDeps.adopt`), and told to wait or let go, the keeper's is handed back where it stands (`FollowerSet.giveUp`, `StoryStands.adopt`), got up first if the story told them while they lay, since nothing but the keeper gets a body up and nobody can speak to one lying in a fight's state. Stood afresh at each owner's change, the person spoken to vanished under the open conversation and ended it, and a body was stood on the player's own feet, since `waitAt` is where the player stood. So the companion is stepped before the cast, whether or not the game simulates; a fresh body is stood at the first spot round the player that has a floor and nothing solid between (`standSpot`, `floor`), never blind behind them into a wall; the cast never stands anybody within `STAND_TUNE.clear` of the player; and what the keeper told the host goes with the body it told it of, or the next body is announced up again for nothing. Only the story's `kill()` kills it. Its kills are credited to nobody, and it is this browser's alone, so the server takes the browser's word for down and up (a `companion` event) and never stands it.
- **Every number progression can change is read through one resolver, and an empty record must hand every read site the very number it held before** (`src/progress/`, `src/player/playerStats.ts`; pass Progression I). A stat's base is the literal its read site held (the catalogue in `stats.ts` keeps it; the read site now holds the kept value whose base it is), a stat nothing grants to has no entry, and `apply(id, base)` hands back the base itself, not a value worked out again; a read site may multiply by a kept factor of exactly 1, never reorder its arithmetic, since `(a * b) * c` and `a * (b * c)` part in the last bit. `progressIdentity.test.ts` holds it by `Object.is` against today's literals written out by hand, drops the real `JkaMovement` from forty heights with and without the new fields, and reads the read sites as text for a stray copy of a literal -- pinned to the exact line, because `camera.ts` has two more `* 0.15`s (the chase's and the cockpit's release) that are not the aim blend, and `bountyHunter.ts` a `radius * 0.8` that is a cloud's sting and not the chain. **The Bounty Hunter's kit resets a charge, a held stream and a beam whenever the gun profile it is handed is another object** (`gun !== this.lastProfile`), so `PlayerStats.gun()` hands back `GUNS[type]` itself until a gun stat first has an entry, then one copy per gun for the life of the stats, rewritten in place whenever the grants change (a respec writes the bases back into it) and never replaced; grenades the same, by spec. And `update(rec)` first builds a key of what grants anything (the rules' stamp, the owned nodes sorted, each built skill's whole rating) and does nothing at all while it is the key it has, so a payout that crosses no whole rating makes no object and bumps no version. **Every file of `src/progress/`, this browser's client, store and levels reader included, imports only its own folder and `src/net/hash.ts`**, its storage, clock and fetch handed in: the release ships the folder whole by one rule (`PROGRESS_SOURCE`) and refuses a shipped file naming a file it does not carry, which `progressImports.test.ts` and the launcher test hold. A node granted from the console brings what it needs above it (`grantChanges`), or `cleanRecord` drops it as an orphan the next time the record is read and the grant does not survive a reload. Steady Arm scales the throw's speed and its lift together, because the throw adds a fixed lift: the speed alone by the square root of 1.25 takes a level throw 17% further, and `steadyArm.test.ts` pins the doc's 25% as a range through `src/combat/demoMath.ts`, the very lines the throw runs, never as a factor. **A comparison of the new code with itself proves nothing about today**: the day-one proof holds the falls to today's harm written out and a hash of every step measured before the pass, and builds the camera and a saber for real, because a field the game writes only when the stats move is, on day one, nothing but its own initialiser. What machines take is Demolition Expert's only at a grenade's or a charge's detonation (`detonate` hands `blast()` the factor): read in the shared `blast()` it gave every gun's splash half again and opened the vehicle damage `machines > 1` gates to all of them. And a limit is never moved past the backstop (`tuneProgress` holds it there and says so), or a list this host grew past it is cut the next time the record is read back and a payment cut so could be paid twice. The challenge table is converted by `customization` into `assets-private/characters/levels.json` and its numbers never go into a committed file or a report (the tests use a TEST table); `status` reads it in an `if` of its own outside the species gate, which is why `customizationConvert.test.ts`'s "asks for nothing" check now writes a levels file beside the creator's.
- **A fight is noticed by looking at its members, never by counting blows, and what struck is held for the length of the blow** (`src/progress/encounter.ts`, `src/combat/strikeTag.ts`; Progression I wave 2). The player is hurt three ways and only one goes through the record's own `damage` (the App's wrapper, which a bite reaches through `World.hurtPlayer`): a bolt calls `onPlayerHit` and `player.takeDamage` and never `damage`, so the bolt's path tells the fight itself (`struckBy`), and the peers' path is no fight's. And a member dies in ways the hit watch never sees -- a burn finishing it in its own step with no `damage` call, a follower's or the companion's blow (the watch reports the player's own only), a group member's on a body this browser keeps -- so the fight looks at every member four times a second through a `look` the game hands in, writing into one kept struct, and a death however it came closes the fight won. The game's `who` and `look` are `src/world/encounterMembers.ts`, out of `main.ts` so `encounter.test.ts` runs them over bodies of the game's own shapes with the real `isFollowerSource` and `isThreat` (a stand-in that refuses a peer for itself proves nothing about the game's), and reads the wiring that feeds the fight out of `main.ts`. **Every way out of a world breaks the fight off and leaves the trainer behind** -- a travel, the select screen, and a jump to another system, which swaps the world under the hull in `carryAcross` and never goes through `travel`: missed there, a fight held the last world's bodies, kept last stand armed, and could never go quiet, since the new world's `simTime` starts again at nought. What struck is not in the `damage(amount, from, push, source)` contract at all, so each of the player's striking sites sets `STRIKE.kind` (a gun, a launcher, a grenade or a charge, a blade, a turned bolt, the Force), makes its call and puts it back in a `finally`, and the world's hit watch reads it inside the call; a loop of blows (a flame's cone, a blast) holds it round the loop. `blast()` takes the kind as an argument, never guesses it: a grenade's and a charge's are Demolitions, a rocket's splash the launcher's, every other gun's splash and the flechette's mines a gun's. **What a creature is, for diminishing returns and a first win, is its row's own creature** (`Mobile.creature`, from `PersonPlan.who`, the lair's creature, ours' drawn person), never `npcId` (a world name every body has its own of, empty for one this browser stood) nor the body (`entry.id`, which many creatures share) -- and `PeopleCreature.id`, which the design named for it, is the body a creature stands as, not the creature. `Player.takeDamage(amount, force, kind, source)` is god mode and then `takeHarm` (`src/player/harmRule.ts`, the glue and the rule both, which `progressFeel.test.ts` runs over a body carrying the real `JkaMovement` and `PlayerStats`, and holds to the old line to the bit with nothing earned): a roll's first `roll.iframes` seconds refuse a `blow` (never a fall, the player's own blast or cloud -- `'self'` at both of `bountyHunter.ts`'s sites -- the surroundings, or the console's kill) and name its source as a dodge, and last stand, armed as a fight opens once Fortitude reaches 75, leaves one point under a lethal blow, fall or tick of the surroundings. **The surroundings are a kind of their own (`env`)**: what reaches the player's record with nothing that struck and nowhere it came from (the lava, drowning, a fire's tick: `recordHarmKind` in both of the record's hurt paths) and a crash; read as blows, a roll timed to the lava's one-second tick crossed a flow unhurt, and standing in it bought a fight's Fortitude and kept the fight from ever going quiet, since only a `blow` counts toward heavy and holds the calm off. What struck is pinned site by site in `strikeTag.test.ts`, the blade's sweep run in a real physics world: every skill but Acrobatics and Fortitude is paid only through `STRIKE.kind`, so a site that drops it pays nothing and nothing else fails. **The client applies a batch to a copy of the record and swaps it in** (so a batch that would take the record past `RECORD_MAX` is refused whole), which means `client.record` is a new object after every batch: read it again after an apply, never hold the old one (the console's `debug` did, and asked its second change of the old record). A batch worked out by the rules is made on a draft too (`payout.ts`'s `Batch`), each change applied as it is written, so a trim is decided against the list as the changes before it left it. The quest bonus is a `PayOrder` of its own (`bonus { xp, since }` under the reward's own key, pushed only for a quest run's reward), which every host that does not know it answers true and pays nothing for -- so the story's own tests that count a reward's orders count three where they counted two, and a server from before wave 3 that holds the story pays no bonus. The Skills tab's Progress section draws only on the tab's own render and on a change of the record (`ProgressUi.renders` says how often), and a trainer is anybody whose row's creature `TRAINERS` names, reached by E and Train, the nearest on the world found over the rows' world places (`StandingPeople.nearestOf`); `progressRules.test.ts` holds `TRAINER_WORLDS` to the committed reference. **The game's own trainers stand on the `hostile` side** (the catalogue tags them `hostile` and `passive`), so the talk rule refused every one of them until `whyNotTalk` was handed `trainer` (`App.isTrainer`, at every call that decides whether somebody may be spoken to, the console's lists included, which `promptRules.test.ts` holds call by call): a trainer who would not pick a fight may be spoken to, and nobody else on that side. The teacher is dropped beyond `teachReach` even while play stands still under the Skills tab, since that is the page that says "while you stay near". **A node is given back one at a time**: a server's purse keeps one answer waiting and a second spend asked before it is answered takes its place, so both fees go and the first node is never given back; `surrender` refuses while the purse is `busy` (a spend unanswered, under `PURSE_TUNE.answerWait`), and a give-back the record refuses once the fee has gone is said in words that say the fee went. And never `done?.(this.settle(...))`: an optional call that is not made does not work out its arguments, so with no `done` the node was never given back at all.
- **With a server the browser only claims, the server works every amount out by the very same rules, and a batch is applied everywhere under the backstop** (`server/progress.mjs`, `server/progressData.mjs`, the line half of `src/progress/progressClient.ts`, the cleaners in `src/progress/wire.ts`; Progression I wave 3). The server imports only node's built-ins and `src/progress/`, and keeps no number of a rule but its own copy of the rules (`copyRules`, moved by `--set progress.rules.<name>`, and `progress.limits.<name>` its own limits, held under the backstop); `progressImports.test.ts`, now in `test:net`, reads both modules for it. That import rule is why the record's text is cut and put back by `wire.ts`'s own `chunkText` and `Reassembly`, the story's written out again: the folder may not import the story's wire, and the server's module may import nothing but the folder. **A batch is made under the host's own limits with its trims written into it, and applied under `PROGRESS_BACKSTOP` everywhere else** -- the store's `applyProgressRec` at the change and at the replay, and the browser at its revision -- because a replay after a restart without the `--set`, or a browser with its own limits, would otherwise cut one record two ways. **The rules' own caps have a backstop of the same kind** (`PROGRESS_RULE_BACKSTOP`: the Skill Point cap, a core skill's XP, a pool's bank and the rested bonus at the top of the range anybody may move them to), and every read of a record another host decided goes through it -- the server's file and its archive read back, a `progressRec` applied at the change and at the replay, the browser's `takeRecord`, and the browser's storage: cleaned under anybody's own rules, a server run with `--set progress.rules.bankCap` moved up had its records cut on its next start and in the browser's copy, each its own way, at one revision. A host's own rules decide only what it pays and what it takes from a browser handing a record up, and a payout never lowers a skill another host's caps put above its own (`gainInto` holds at what was). And a test that two copies stay one proves nothing unless it runs the browser's real client and moves the limit or the cap **up**, past this browser's own: moved down, a client cutting to its own limits arrives at the same record. **What a claim says dead is believed by its world name** (`PayCtx.witness`): a body the world shares only on its keeper's own `dead` word (the relay hands it to `progress.death` beside the story's, with the ownership's `back`), on the line's own world, credited once to a character, and only while the body stays down -- `back`, never less than `deathFloor` (room for the fight's claim to arrive behind its death) nor more than `deathKeep` -- because a seeded body comes back under the very name it died under, and kept for ten minutes its last life's death let a claim of its next be refused (the character already credited) or another character's be believed while it stood alive; a name the world does not share, and a member with none, against the character's own `localKillsPerMinute`; and only for a won claim, so a fight lost or broken off spends nothing of that rate. A claim naming a shared body dead whose death cannot stand for it yet (none heard, one from the body's last life, one this character was paid for) is parked, one park per claim id on the clock of the first hearing, and run by the death word that completes it or by the tick after `claimPark` with what was heard -- the claimant that keeps a body sends its death and its claim by two paths nothing orders; a claim already paid is answered at once. The relay's half of that (the death word, `end.world` against `rooms.keyOf`, the 250 ms tick) is tested only by a real relay with a browser keeping a body and saying it died, in `progressRelay.test.ts`. **The browser paces its own claims and asks again once**: the server takes two claims a second from a line and drops the rest in silence, so the queue goes up one every `PROGRESS_TUNE.claimGap`, a claim unanswered for `claimWait` is sent once more with the same id (`paid` makes it safe) and one never answered is given up and said; claims made while the record is held wait in the queue, and a line that goes away puts what went up unanswered back at the front, for whoever holds the record next to pay once in its own copy. A `no` naming a claim or a task is the console's (`onAnswer`, kept beside the fight), never the message line's; a learn's or a fee's is said; and a claim that pays nothing says why in its first pool's own words (nothing it fought died, held to its bucket) rather than "nothing to pay". **The XP buckets are the server's, by character, for its whole life**, so a reconnect never fills them again. **A fee and its node go in one step** on the server: `purses.spend` first, the batch only when it took the money, and the purse's own word goes back with it, which is how the browser's purse learns the balance. **A quest's bonus on a server** is `stories.pay` handing the order to `progress.bonus`: paid at once while that character's progress is settled on the line, otherwise owed (`progressOwed` under a key of the server's own, a restart keeping it) and paid then, keyed, before it is struck off. The store record `progress` and the wire word `progress` share a name, so every `this.write({ t: ... })` stays on one line (`netRoutes.test.ts` reads a `t:` on a `write(` line as a record), and the status page lists the tuning as `progressTuning` because `progress` is the host's own report. The relay test (`progressRelay.test.ts`) runs with `--assets` naming an empty folder, so nothing of the game's own challenge table is read or printed, finishes a test-set quest through the story's admin `complete` (whose reward is what pays the bonus), reads the relay's own console for a rate warning, and measures the worst hand-up on one claim -- four hundred of the longest id, a book of three pieces, a record at every cap -- at 105 KB in its first second, all of it the backpack and the book's first two pieces: the record's first piece waits `offerDelay` and adds nothing to that second, and the line stays open.
- **The grappling hook is our own constraint on the player's kinematic capsule, and a frame of it runs in one order the node harness runs too** (`src/player/grapple.ts`, the rope `grappleRope.ts`, the arithmetic `grappleMath.ts`, the swim `swimStep.ts`; Grapple wave 1). Rapier's joints never move a kinematic body, so the rope is a length the body's velocity is held to after the controller, no body or joint is ever added for it, and the dart is a ray; the order in `Player.update` -- `swimStep`, `jka.step`, `grapple.beforeMove`, the snap, the controller, `grapple.clip`, `computedGrounded`, `grapple.afterMove` -- is the harness's (`tools/swg/tests/grappleBody.ts`) to the call, and `grappleWiring.test.ts` reads both, so a step moved in one and not the other fails there rather than in a swing nobody can see. **Rapier moves a kinematic body's collider only at a physics step and the controller starts from the collider**, so at 144 fps the capsule runs up to two frames behind `pos` and a 35 m/s swing went 0.40 m into a wall: while attached the collider is put where the body is before the controller (`syncCollider`); the fault is the whole game's and is mended nowhere outside the hook. **Ground is a collider whose membership is exactly the terrain's bit** (`isTerrainCollider`): `membership & TERRAIN` matched every collider left in the default groups as well, whose membership is every bit, so a flora trunk counted its bends against the ground's cap. **The rope's casts take the still family by the engine's query flags, never a predicate** (`STILL_FLAGS`): a predicate is called back into script for every collider a ray's box passes, a few hundred bytes a call, and a town is many. **Nothing in the hook's frame allocates, and four things that look free do**: `Math.hypot` hands back a fresh number even from optimised code (about 5 bytes a call, so every length goes through `len2` and `len3`, or `Math.sqrt` written out); a number handed to a call, or handed back from one, that the engine did not build into its caller is boxed, and a big method builds few of its calls in, so the constraint, the haul and the ribbon take kept objects and arrays, and the mark's probe reads a kept `view` its caller writes, its segment and cast read their numbers from kept fields and the water test is handed a kept point (the probe alone made 18 to 50 bytes a frame before, for every player on foot); a `let` of a module or a closure written with a number allocates on every write; and the engine's own wrappers cost far more than the design reckoned (a cast 570 to 1,200 bytes, a fan about 1,000, a contact read 305), which is theirs. **Measuring it is a trap three times over.** Node's observer is told of a collection a turn or two of the loop after it ran, so a window's collections are read only after two turns (after one, a window that collected read as clean, and what it made as whatever the young generation held at its end). A full collection throws away optimised code built round an object that has since died, so each window is measured after the calls that optimise it again and a minor collection (`gc({ type: 'minor' })`, which throws no code away) that empties what they made: straight after `gc()`, windows of 2,000 frames read 16 to 59 bytes a frame of re-warming.
