And a process that has run the real engine and stand-ins both has made the hook's call sites meet two kinds of object, and a call no longer built in boxes what it hands over, so the hold on the hook's own code (test 17's (d), `grappleAllocOwn.ts`, under 0.2 bytes a frame over every branch of a frame and the mark) runs in a node process of its own against kept answers small enough to be built into the call, and the whole path against the real engine and the mark against its two casts are sanity checks with a tenth of slack, never the hold (one of them passed an object of 150 bytes a frame made in `track` three runs in five). The sampling heap profiler is no shortcut: it leaves out what the collector took unless asked to keep it, and charges an engine wrapper built into the hook's frame to the hook. The timing behind `__debug.grapple`'s `cost` is off until the console first asks for it. **The controller hands back tilted normals for a body walking on flat ground** (seen at 30 fps), so a walkable contact (a normal at least cos 55 degrees up) is never clipped and counts only the speed straight down into it, or a walker on the line was kicked off the ground every few frames; and the rope carries the body along its turn only while it swings or floats, since a carry on the ground walked it a few hundredths of a metre a second into the earth. **A swing scraping the ground is held a few centimetres inside its length by the ground itself**, which reads as slack, so the slide's latch holds within `latchSlack` (0.25 m, ours) of taut; **a rope sliding along a heightfield grazes its own face's back**, so the bend off the first of two opposed faces (`opposedLean`) is made only when the far face is a plane of its own more than `wrapOffset` off; and **the haul's lip-over starts a step early** (`minFree` plus the larger of `lipTrigger` and one frame of the haul) and ends only once the bend has come off and the feet are over the lip, or a 30 fps haul overshot it into the wall at 17.8 m/s. **The haul's last step stops at `minFree`**: aimed at the pivot itself, a step of 0.3 m at 60 fps and 0.6 at 30 that ended inside the shortest drove the capsule into the face it was hauled to at 16 and 10 m/s, and the controller's clip of that step took the speed off before the arrival rule could, so the rule was unobservable there; what the step falls short by goes on the move as the constraint's correction does, never on the velocity, which keeps the haul's own speed for `haulArrive` to take off on the frame the haul ends, at every rate. **The mark is worked out only where the camera reaches it**, and `placeCamera` returns early for a shuttle's passenger, a ship in flight, the cockpit and Alt-look in a ship, while its flash and snap hold run down only inside the probe: `updateCamera` stands the mark down whole (`markOff`) on a frame the probe did not run, or a press refused on a speeder left a red diamond at the crosshair for the ride. **The movement's command on the rope is the hook's own `command`**, which the player's update and the harness both call, and `grappleWiring.test.ts` reads every other rope line of the update and of the harness side by side (each guard, the snap, the sync and the correction, the swim's two write-backs): every behaviour test runs the harness, and a line taken out of the player alone passed all of them. The swim's depth is one constant, `swimStep.ts`'s, which the player, the harness and the wading drag all read, and the water test's copy of the old swim block carries the old block's own literals, never the module's. The harness's `Input` gets a stand-in page only while it is made, because the physics engine's glue reads `window` when it finds one and a stand-in with nothing of a page's in it panics the first step. Every number is `GRAPPLE_TUNE`, live through `__debug.grapple({ tune })`; the swim's `jka.holdApex(pos.y)` on every swimming frame is the one change a player who never fires can feel, and "Where things stand" says what it changed.
- **The hook's danger and look ride the frame it already had, and nothing of them runs for a player who never fires** (Grapple wave 2: `slamAt` and `look` in `grapple.ts`, the arithmetic in `grappleMath.ts`, the arm in `src/player/armAim.ts`, the sound in `src/audio/grappleSounds.ts`). A slam is judged in `clip` on what the clip measured, against what was struck, only off a face steeper than the climb and never while F is held, and goes through the player's own `takeDamage` as a fall's (the view `GrappleBody` gained `hp` and `takeDamage`); the fast flight after a let-go (`released`) runs the sync, the clip and the slam and nothing else, set only by a let-go from the rope faster than `slamFrom`, so the hook keeps a reference to the body's velocity for the let-go's speed, and every way out that moves the body (`endsFlight`) stands everything still easing down at once (`calm`). The stagger, the jolt, the lean, the arm and the camera's pull are stepped by one call after `afterMove` behind `lively`, which is false by `Object.is`'s standard for a body that never fired (the camera is handed the literal 1). **Three things were learned the hard way.** The constraint takes a catch in two frames -- the last of the slack lets some of the speed through and the frame after takes the rest -- so a frame's `caught` reads anywhere from nothing to the whole catch by the phase it fell on (12.9 m/s at 144 fps for a 20 m/s catch); the jolt sums the two frames from the one the rope ran out of slack, which reads the same at every rate. With the haul's last step cut at `minFree` (wave 1) the haul's own push never meets a face from straight out, so the design's 17.3 m/s contact no longer exists; the rule is proved with `haulArrive` off, in the air, since on the ground the movement's own friction takes the carried 18 m/s under `slamFrom` before the face is reached. And the hook's frame boxes a number wherever one crosses a call the engine does not build in, which a field written every frame with `Math.min(1, ...)` did too (the arm's weight, sixteen bytes a frame until it was written only when it moved): the lean is handed its direction and its step in kept records (`leanOf`, `leanToward`), and `joltFor` is asked only past `joltFrom`, so a steady rope's frame makes nothing and a real catch boxes a number once. **The arm's aim is worked out in the figure's frame as `placeVisual` will stand it this frame** (its heading, then the lean, then the rig's own turn under the group), because the rig is posed before the group is placed and `aimArm`'s answer depends only on the turns between the bones, never on the group's stale matrix; at a weight of 1 it is the rig's old arithmetic to the bit (`grappleLook.test.ts`). The lean turns the drawn group about the rope point (`leanedAt`), never the capsule, at a rate that takes the largest lean upright in `leanOut`, and the whole turn goes to other players with that origin while it leans and for `leanTail` after it is level (`leanSends`): sent only until level, the last turn a receiver heard still leaned by the lean's step between two states (about 21 degrees at ten a second), and a receiver built before stands a figure upright in one frame the moment `q` stops (17 to 20 degrees measured; 0.1 with the tail). **A peer's turn is glided by `src/net/peerTurn.ts` and never by its Euler angles**: three works a whole turn's Euler angles out in XYZ order, whose yaw is 180 degrees less the heading for any heading past a quarter turn, so the old glide, which read the heading off `rotation.y` the frame `q` stopped, drew such a figure mirrored (150 degrees drawn as 48) on every browser built before it; a figure whose turn last came off the wire whole is eased upright on its heading as a whole turn and handed back to the heading's glide once upright, and only a friend on this build sees it mended. `grapple.test.ts`'s test 37 sends a let-go at the wire's own rate through both receivers. The slam's gap and the tail are clocked by `look`, so each keeps the hook `lively` while it runs (a console slam that left nothing else to ease held the gap shut for good), and the sound step, which runs every frame of the session for a player who may never fire, is handed the velocity record and asks the sabers' pack for the whoosh once a pack (a speed worked out at the call was 16 bytes a frame, and a pack without the whoosh built a name to look it up with on every frame). The daze is one frame input into the vignette's own branch (`vignetteKick`) and the shake and the jolt the camera's own register. **Jedi Academy's melee whooshes are played by nothing in the game** (the feet return on a Jedi Academy file pattern), so the boost makes its own template over them from the sabers' pack (`sabers.jkaFile`, the `.wav`/`.mp3` fall-back) and stands in the game's own unarmed swing without it; every sound is played one for one from a count the hook keeps, the ratchet from the held reel's alone at most `tickMax` a second, and asked for at every arrival.
- **A hooked prop is a state of its own (`holding`), never `attached`, and the hook's progressable numbers are read through the player's stats at the hook itself** (Grapple wave 3a: `attachThing`, `stepThing` and `placeThingEnd` in `grapple.ts`, `whoMoves` and `turnByQuat` in `grappleMath.ts`, `hookablePropAt` and `HookableProp` in `src/world/looseProps.ts`, the five rows in `src/progress/stats.ts`). Every line of `Player.update` that touches the body is behind `attached` (or `released`), so a rope on a thing the mass rule moves (`whoMoves`: still is the player's, at or under `tether.strength` the thing's, up to `heavyRatio` times the player's 80 kg a tug-of-war nothing answers yet, past it the player's) lives in `holding`, which only `out` reaches: the movement's command, the sync, the clip, the slam and the snap never see it, and a walk dragging a crate is the walk with no shot by `Object.is` (`grappleMass.test.ts`). Nor do its keys: the reels lent the walk's and the crouch's keys do nothing to a prop, so while it holds one only the torch (F, the yank) and the emotes are muted (`grappleMutes`' fourth list) and its let-go owes the torch alone (`THING_RELEASE_MUTES`); muted as on an anchor, Shift ran and the crouch, and Jedi Academy's landing roll with it, were lost for nothing. The rope's end is kept in the prop's own frame and read off its mesh, never its body, and **the mesh is a physics step behind the body in the player's update** (the world steps the pool after the player and the physics after that), so the drawn rope is carried onto the drawn crate again in `drawGrapple` (`placeThingEnd`), while the yank's own `holdAt` reads the body as it carries it. The pool stands every crate unturned, so a test that never turns one cannot tell the prop's frame from none: it is held by a crate turned before the bite and turned and tilted again on the line, where no frame at all and a turn the wrong way round end 0.3 and 0.5 m off. **Anything the hook does to the prop at its mesh's place is a step out of date, and a hold there is a velocity back along the last step**: the yank's end first held the crate at its mesh's place to take its speed off, and in the game's order that threw an arriving crate back out at the grip's 14 m/s, 7 to 27 m (60 and 30 fps; at 144 most frames carry no step); its end now stops the body itself (`stop`: its speed and spin to nought, nothing read) and releases it with no lift, so it drops where it came to, at the feet from 15 m and 2 to 3 m short when the 1.5 s clock ends a yank from past 21 m. The test had stepped the pool after the physics, where the mesh is fresh, and passed at every rate; the harness now steps it where the game's world does (`TestBody.beforeStep`), and any test of something the world steps between the player and the physics must do the same. The guard against a place written without a reset measures the move against the speed before it (`speedBefore`, kept in `beforeMove` as the anchor's is), since a landing takes the fall's speed off first and measured after it a hard landing read as a teleport. Two more things were learned on the crate (measured, `grappleMass.test.ts` and the scratch runs behind it). A yank ended by `release` alone left the crate at the grip's 14 m/s (8.8 at 30 fps), and it struck the player and shoved the body 4 to 13 cm, which is why it ends stopped. A tether drag carried along the rope's line rides the crate up toward the hand -- its middle 1.5 m up after a 76 m walk through the grip's weightless hold, and still 0.9 m with its weight kept, since a velocity set every frame beats gravity whatever share of it points up -- so the drag keeps the weight and carries the prop across and not up (`holdAt(point, dt, false)` keeps the body's own vertical speed unless the point stands above it by more than it does across), and it rides the ground at 0.3 m. And a prop's body is gone with its pool, so nothing is asked of it (its weight given back on a let-go included) unless `propAt(handle)` still answers that very prop and the world is whole; a crate the pool recalls is the same body on its stand, which is why the hook notes `recalls` at the hook and lets go the frame it moves, and the drag lets go `tetherStandMargin` short of `PROPS.lost` so it never drags a crate into a recall. **The stat reads**: the hook carries `stats` (`NO_STATS`, the identity, until the player hands it its own) and reads `stats.apply(id, GRAPPLE_TUNE.x)` at each site -- the range in the mark, the dart, the bite and the reel-out cap, the reel, the haul, and `grapple.swing` (base 1) times the sway's push and cap -- so the tune stays live, a number times exactly 1 is itself, and an empty record steps a swing, both reels, the haul and the mark by `Object.is`; `grapple.range` and `tether.strength` were rewritten where they stood in the table, and `progressIdentity.test.ts` reads the hook as text for any bare tune read beside them and for any 100, 6, 18 or 40 written out in its own code. Comparing an empty record with no stats compares the new code with itself, so `grappleMass.test.ts` also holds that swing to a hash of the very trace taken through wave 2's code before the table was read (`WAVE2_SWING`), and a test that moves a stat must show it reached where it is read: a range past 100 m bites a wall 120 m off and reels out past it, a slower haul starts the lip-over nearer the lip. The prop's frame boxes nothing: the turn into its frame takes kept records, the straight span's check goes to the cast through the rope's kept arrays (`anchorSpanClear`), its lengths are worked out in line, the mark's range test takes its numbers in a record (`MarkReach`), and `grappleAllocOwn.ts prop` holds the tether, the yank and F let go mid-yank to 2,272 bytes over 200,000 frames in a process of its own. **That measure was right only most of the time until its stand-in cast was made too small to leave out**: the hook hands the engine's cast its reach as a number, and whenever the compiler's budget for building calls in ran out before it reached the stand-in (with `segment` and `castPassing` built into `probeMark`, or `castCs` into `stepThing`), that number was boxed, 16 B a frame in about one process in twelve, found with the sampling heap profiler round the measured window and the inlining trace of the bad processes. The boundary is the engine's cost in the game and not the hook's, so the stand-in is one function of 25 bytes of bytecode, which the compiler builds in whatever its budget has left (its small-function rule, 27 bytes), keeping the reach in a record and asking its rule with none; and its call to the rule has met two rules before anything runs, because a function's own optimised code counts toward its size (built with `near` in, the 25 bytes counted as 122), and a function records nothing it calls until it has been called a few times. Measured after: 96 processes of each measure 24 at once, none over its line. A test that takes a collider away steps the world before the next query, or the query meets a collider the engine has dropped.
- **A rope on a vehicle rides the hull's pose led to the frame's own time, and a hull that moves is met by a test of its own, never by the character controller** (Grapple wave 3b: `src/player/grappleFrames.ts`, the time-aware rope in `grappleRope.ts`, `Vehicle.pulled`, `toldDown`, `refits` and `wingStateOf`, `Physics.behind`). Three clocks meet in a frame: the bodies stand where the last 60 Hz step left them, the player's update runs ahead of that by the accumulator's remainder at its start and by that plus the frame at its end, and a flown ship's turn is written by its drive in frame time; so every hull near the rope is read once a frame and led to both (`leadPose`, the clamp's arithmetic), every point on it is kept in its own frame, every cast against it is made in its frame and taken to where the query pipeline has it, and the mark, worked out after the step, reads each hull again and leads it by the remainder alone (B is kept in the hull's frame, so the dart is aimed true at it). A test must place what rides a hull against its true pose too: placed against the body where the step left it, a body beside a 100 m/s hull started 0.7 m astern of where it meant to and swung along the hull, 2 cm out in its hanging height. A roped moving hull is marked walk-through (out of the controller and the capsule's solver pairing) and a second controller of the player's meets it in its own cast pose after pushing the capsule's start out of it along each hull collider's own `contactShape`, which needs no hull shape kept; its contacts are clipped against the hull's led point velocity. **The hull test is its own landing clamp**: the player's controller walked through the hull, so nothing else takes a fall off a body resting on a moving hull's walkable face, and with only the walls clipped a body lying on a pod's top piled up 58 m/s of fall in 3 s while the test held its place (a let-go then struck the top at that speed); a walkable contact there takes the speed down into the face off relative to the hull and holds the fall's apex from it the next frame (`onHullFloor`), and the body is still not called grounded, since on a moving hull it never stands (§3.6) and the ground's friction and keys are the still ground's. Who is marked is decided on every attached frame the frames were stepped (`framed` in `beforeMove`, after `hullsHold`, in the rope's own world), never at the end of the hull path alone: there a hull the rope had just unwrapped off stayed walk-through, out of the controller with its top no ground, for as long as it stayed near. The teleport guard judges each read of a hull against the last alone (`jumped` cleared at every `leadSlot`): kept, a hull that jumped while merely near the rope froze a bend made on it seconds later and let a later bite go. The hull test's controller is one maker the player and the harness both call (`makeHullController`), held to the player's own controller's offset, slopes and mass by `grappleWiring.test.ts`, which also reads every hand-over of the wave in the game (the collider lookup, the vehicles to the mark and the hook, the mark's motion to the fire, the terrain height before `afterMove`, the refit's count): the harness makes each for itself, so a line lost from the game alone passed every behaviour test. **A pull is told to both of the hull's hit tests or the hull reads the rope as a crash**: `pulled` adds the speed it gave to what the next step's commanded-velocity test measures from, and keeps its downward part for the hard floor, which reads the fall less it -- held each update to the hull's own downward speed and let go once the hull falls no more, because the floor meets a corner the pull pressed down a step or two after the pull (the design's "nought after the next update" told the floor nothing and the 8 m/s downward pull still struck the hull). **A day-one test of a shared class is held to the class as it stood before the change, never to itself less the change**: grappleVehicles' test 1 reads `vehicle.ts` whole out of git at the wave's start (`651848074c`, `git show`) and loads it beside the class, and both are held to a hash of that old class's trace written into the test, so a later change anywhere in the stepping fails it even where git cannot hand the old file over; built first as the current file with the wave's two blocks taken back out by text, it passed a `* 1.001` put anywhere else in `update`. The floor's own control (a pull told to the step's test and not the floor's) is that text-removed class, which is a fault injected, not a day one. A parked hull's brake is a budget carried between physics steps and latched only at the rope's first pull, at rest and undriven; at 144 fps, where the rope pulls hardest on the frames that carry a step, two or three apart, the carried budget meets 89% of the brake a second (a gap of three frames is a fifth over one step's worth) where spent per pull call it met 41% -- not "the same at every second or third frame", as the design had it. **Every number that crosses a call in the hull's frame goes in a kept record, and the hull's branch of anything a still rope calls every frame is a method of its own.** The hull path first made 36 B a frame of its own (the rope's `relativeMost`, `unwrap` and `tighten` handed the hand as numbers, `castHulls` its length, `hullTest` the capsule's half height and the filter, `pullHulls` the tension, and the clip's contact on a hull, which comes on few frames and so is never built in, took `clipInto`'s answer back as a number); fields and arrays took it to a hundredth of a byte. A new call on the still rope's frame costs the old calls their place in the budget: `stepFrames` asked on every frame pushed `teleported` out of `beforeMove` (16 B a frame) until it was asked only with a vehicle about or a point on a hull. Then `pointAt` grew a branch and a call for a point on a hull, `look` built it in through `pivot`, and a still rope's `look` boxed 23 B a frame -- found by bisecting against the committed tree exported to a scratch folder (`git archive`, the modules joined by a junction), since the sampling profiler only named `look`: `look` now reads `pivNow`, which `afterMove` keeps, and the hull branch is `pointOnFrame`. And `grappleAllocOwn.ts`'s own loop was compiled on the stack and built the frame's step into itself, so whether the hook's helpers were built in turned on when that compile happened (one process in two over the line); the loop is kept out of the optimizer (`%NeverOptimizeFunction` under `--allow-natives-syntax`, which every test that runs it passes) and the step is compiled as itself, as the player's update calls the hook. The whole path against the real engine is measured in a process of its own too (in the suite's process, after every other section had stepped its hulls, it failed), against the engine's calls measured on their own in the same scene -- a fan about 800 B and twelve a frame on a tow, the hull test's controller call 250 to 290 B, a contact test 300 -- with two fifths of slack, since those move by a tenth from run to run and the same calls make 1.12 to 1.28 times as much inside a frame as weighed alone; the design's 260 B a cast and 50 B a hull test call were never what the engine costs. That whole-path check weighs only what its windows ask -- the misses and the fans of a slack tow and a hang, no pull and nothing that meets the pod -- and its allowance is calibrated through the rope's own cast and fan, so it says so in its line and the hold is the stand-in measure. **A measure holds only the paths it runs**: the hull modes of `grappleAllocOwn.ts` ran neither `look` nor the mark, which the game runs every frame on foot with a vehicle about, and run with them the mark's hull cast of the hand's line boxed its length (`hl + 0.05` handed to `markCast`, 16 B on each frame it ran) until its length and filter went into fields (`markLen`, `markFilter`); the stand-in answers the frames' own kept ray (`hullsRay`) by the slab test against the hull's box, so its hits and misses both run. **The hull path calls its rules through kept records** (`grappleMath`'s `…In` forms, the hook's `nums`, `payOut` reading its length from it, the sway one method both paths call, the frames' lengths by `Math.sqrt` written out): which of a big method's small calls the compiler builds in turns on when it reaches them, and one run of the harness in six left the slip's or another out and boxed 6 to 30 B a frame; the plain forms stay for the tests and the console, through a record of `grappleMath`'s own. The same holds for `len2` and `len3`, which the still rope's frame may call: in `beforeMoveOnHull` and `pullHulls` every length is `Math.sqrt` written out, since with the look and the mark in the measure about one process in fifty under load left a length out of `beforeMoveOnHull` and boxed 16 to 48 B a frame (named by the sampling heap profiler round the measured window of the bad processes; 192 of 192 clean after). Two things about the tests' scenes: a rope fastened at once (`attachHull`) with its first span through the hull (a top anchored from below its plane) bends on the face itself and crawls along it, which no dart ever makes, so a scene hauls to a hull's top from a rope dropped over its edge; and the slip's allowance comes back on every frame the rope does not pull, so a drag the rope catches on every second frame (a swimmer's own ease behind a pod at 144 fps) is taken whole and handed to the hull rather than paid out. A vehicle nobody ropes steps as before by `Object.is`; `pulled` has one caller, the grapple's own vehicle source.
- **A rope on another player's vehicle rides the box their game keeps, by arithmetic, at a pose of its own worked out from their states, holds the body off that box by the same arithmetic, and is drawn on their picture** (Grapple wave 3c: `PeerFrame`, `PeerHullSource` and `HullFrames.peerTest` in `src/player/grappleFrames.ts`, `peerPoseIn` and `peerMissIn` in `grappleMath.ts`, `rayBoxIn`, `spanMeetsBox`, `triangleMeetsBox` and `pointInBox` in `grappleBox.ts`, `RemoteBodies.hullBoxes` and `stampHulls`). A peer's hull is a sensor in the peer group, which no cast of the rope's ever sees, and asking the engine of it would mean a collider made for the rope; so every cast, fan and depth test the rope makes on a peer's frame branches to the slab arithmetic against that box in the frame's own coordinates (`RopeFrame.boxMid`/`boxHalf`, null on every other frame, a hit's collider null), held to Rapier's own cuboid by grappleVehicles' test 13: its rays agree to 6e-6 of their length (the engine keeps poses and answers in single precision, so a check in metres fails on a 20 m ray), its spans to the engine's own solid ray along them, and its quads and the fan's own `triangleMeetsBox` to the engine's triangle shape test, a meeting of ours the engine denies a failure and only a miss within the 2 mm its GJK allows excused (a collider's `castRay` answers the time of impact, or -1 for none). **Neither their picture nor the relay's velocity taken raw is a pose to ride**: the picture glides toward each state, ten a second, in a sawtooth, and a rope fastened to it at 40 m/s towed the body at 13 to 50 m/s; and a state's place is the sender's when it sent it while its moment is when it was heard here, so each state's few milliseconds of network and frame jitter moves the relay's velocity by a metre a second or so, and handed whole to a dead-reckoned pose (its step and its lag) that swung the same tow 14 to 49 m/s. The wave's first stand-in sampled the truth at the frame the state was heard, so no jitter could reach it and the fault passed every test; test 13's stand-in now sends from the sender's own frames, samples the place there, and hears it 50 ms on with 5 ms of jitter, stamped as it arrives (as the game stamps it, `performance.now()` as the message is handled) or, a thread busy all frame, as the next frame starts. The pose dead-reckons the newest state by a velocity of its own, the relay's eased over `peerCorrect`, corrects toward the states over the same, and is held back by `peerLag`, the picture's mean lag, which is the glide's time constant plus most of half the gap between states, `dt(1 - k)/k + (0.1 - dt)/2` (0.143 s at 144 fps), so 0.14 where the design took 0.1. `peerCorrect` went from the design's 0.3 to 0.5 for the jitter: at 0.3 a tow over the ground dipped 1.1 m/s under the vehicle, and with every state stamped a frame late at 60 fps the rope went slack past the tow's own hold and dropped it to 31 m/s; the price, from a one-dimensional model, is a pose run on past a vehicle that stops by 5.3 m braking at 15 m/s² and 9.4 m stopping dead (2.2 and 5.5 at 0.3) before it eases back. **The list is stamped with the clock the frame already read** (`stampHulls(tFrame)` before the peers are moved): read in `hullOf`, `performance.now()` handed back a fresh number every frame for every player riding anything, whether anybody fired or not, and `remoteBodies.test.ts` 11b holds the peers' path to a tenth of a byte a frame beside a clock read that makes sixteen. **The body is held off their box while the rope rides it**, by `peerTest` after the controller and any hull test (the box grown by the capsule's reach along each axis and the controller's offset, the start pushed out at most `hullPush`, the move cut at the face it enters and slid along it, each face a contact measured against the pose's point velocity as a hull test's is): the controller never meets a sensor, and with the hand let into the box the rope wrapped it from inside, fourteen bends under Shift and thirty-two and fouled as the vehicle stopped dead. A span that ends inside a box is not met by it either, so no bend is ever made from inside one (`fromInside`, which the console's `blocked` count asks without, so a rope through their vehicle is counted); `hullTest` false turns the box test off as it turns the hull test off. The pose's turn is the picture's own led by the glide's step, the rope's points ride the pose and are drawn through the picture's transform, and the box is copied from the entry every frame (their game measures it again when wings settle). Peer slots come after the hull slots, and every loop that weighs, pulls, brakes or marks a hull is bounded to `MAX_HULL_FRAMES`: nothing is put on a peer's vehicle (mass Infinity, `takesPull` false), and it is never still for the haul, which ends hanging at the free length. A jump is a state further than `peerJump` from where the last state, carried on by its velocity, said it would be, less what the moments it was heard at account for, `|v|·|gap − PEER_SEND|` (`moved`): judged by the moments alone, a sender stalled 0.2 s at 100 m/s missed by 14.6 m and let go. Every number that crosses a call on this path goes in a field (`rayBoxIn`'s length in its answer, `peerHitOut`'s share, the rope's spent length at the frame's start, `measureSpentAtStart`, and the words' slack test, `slackWithin`), each of which boxed 16 B a frame in one process or more of `grappleAllocOwn.ts peer` and `peer hang`. A slam that comes of a peer's vehicle harms only where `CombatNet.mayHurt` says they could hurt you; the stagger, the shake and the daze stand either way. **A tow over the ground pays rope out where a tow in the air pays none**: the capsule meets the heightfield's own seams and loses a frame's travel to them, and the hand's correction pays that out (to 3 m at 30 fps; wave 3b's pod tow paid 2.36 m the same way), so "nothing paid out" is held hung under the belly, where it is nought, and the tow over the ground by its speed and its slip.
- **Another player's rope is a picture of what their game sent, and every point of it that rides a vehicle goes over in that vehicle's own frame and is put on this browser's own picture of it** (Grapple wave 4: `server/grappleWire.mjs`, `src/net/peerRope.ts`, `src/net/remoteRopes.ts`, `wireRope` in `grapple.ts`, the gear in `src/core/inventory.ts`). A state carries `gr` (the dart's place and velocity, or a held rope's slack and its world points from the anchor, at most the wire's sixteen, cut at the anchor's end so the bends nearest the hand are the ones that go) and `grf` (each point riding another player's vehicle as that player's relay id, the point's index and its place in their vehicle's frame); the relay drops a `gr` of the wrong shape whole and a `grf` entry that names its sender, repeats an index or reaches past the count alone (`WIRE.ropePoints` is the wire's shape and refuses `--set`). Sent only as world points, a point on a vehicle at 40 m/s stood 6.6 m off the drawn vehicle by the time a watcher drew it; put on the picture it is under a millimetre, and the rider's own browser puts it on the real vehicle. The watcher (`RemoteRopes`, a `PeerWatcher`) glides each point at the peer glide's rate, flies a dart on by its velocity at most `peerLead` past its last state and stops it at the first thing a cast meets, hangs the rope from the drawn left hand by the slack it was sent, aims the arm up it through the rig's own `aimArm`, sounds fire, bite and let-go at the hand only for a peer shown now and before, and hands a leaver's ribbon and dart on to the next, since their geometry is all they own. Five things learned. **`Net.receive` rebuilds every `state` field by field, and a field it does not name never arrives**: `tb`, the thrown saber, had been sent since thrown sabers were written and was dropped there every time, which nothing caught because nothing ran one through the browser's own `Net`; it now names `tb`, `gr` and `grf`, and `grappleRelay.test.ts` runs all three through two real `Net`s and a real relay. **A table of numbers a frame reads stays under 128 keys**: the engine makes an object literal of 128 keys or more with every key out of the object (in a property array, where one of 127 keeps all of them in it), and the one key this wave first added to `GRAPPLE_TUNE` took it to 128 and the hook's own frame from 0.09 to 4.2 B, every run alike (grapple.test's own-code measure, `grappleAllocOwn.ts`), and boxed the watcher's dart lead read off it besides; the lead is `PEER_ROPE_TUNE` in `peerRope.ts` now, the hook's table holds 127, and `remoteRopes.test.ts` counts them, so a new number of the hook's goes in a table of its own. **A watcher's frame boxes numbers where the hook's did**: a cast answered as a number (it writes into a kept `Float64Array` now, `seg[6]`), three's `normalize` (its length handed back) and `Math.min`/`Math.max`; the sampling heap profiler, kept for objects the minor collector takes, named each. **A kind of owned thing is named once** (`OWNED_KINDS`, `isOwnedKind`), because `normalizeOwned`, the trade's row reader and the ledger each held a list of their own, and a thing of a kind one list lacked was dropped by that reader in silence. **A server that keeps fewer kinds than the browser must neither be handed them nor take them away** (`pairOwned`'s `keeps`, `Trade.keeps`): a ledger whose hail has no `gear` lists no rig, and a list read as the whole truth took the rig the browser gave itself out of the backpack at every connect; there the rig is given by the record's own mark (`gear: 1`, in one save with the row) and stays this browser's, and only a server whose hail says `gear: 1` decides it, from its first list of a session (`Equipment.gearFromList`), which asks for one where there is none. **Who keeps the rig is asked of the props' own rule, and nothing is given while it is not known** (`App.gearKeeperNow`, `keeperStateOf` with the hail's `gear`): a character loads before its server is connected to, so a grant at load always found "this browser" and gave a rig that a server keeping gear and already holding the character then took away with its first list, saying "one thing went" to every character on the first session after the relay's restart; with a server saved here the grant waits (`pending`) until the far end has said it keeps no gear, is the relay that came before, or has not answered within the props' wait, and is then made from the frame's keeper step (`grantRigHere`). A rig given by the mark before such a server was met is still taken by its first list and asked for again straight after, and the words leave it out (`reconcile`). **The grant is not a change the player made** (`rewritten`, as the garment colours' move is): counted, every character's counter rose once for a rig, and a copy a server held one change ahead tied with this one and was put to the player as a question.
- **A pod, and a bike in racing mode, is driven by a race drive the garage hangs on it, which holds the hull instead of the springs and plans its height over the physics' own coming steps** (Racer handling wave 1: `src/vehicles/raceDrive.ts`, the pure arithmetic `raceMath.ts`, every number `raceTune.ts`, the view `raceView.ts`, the kinds `vehicleKinds.ts`, `Physics.stepsAhead`, `World.raceGround`). The drive is attached by the garage (`attachRace`) and never by `Vehicle`'s constructor, so a vehicle made without one, and every vehicle while `RACE_TUNE.on` is false or `localStorage['swg.race'] = '0'`, steps exactly as before. `Vehicle.update` asks `race.wants(v)` (the switch, `drive: 'racer'`, not a ship, not adrift, held, ghosted or destroyed, and a pod or a bike in racing mode) and while it says yes runs `step` and nothing of the springs; the first step takes the hull (`adopt`: the speed shared out into its parts, gravity and damping to nought, the race box in, the command not trusted for one step, every mark begun afresh) and the first step it says no hands it back (`release`, then `Vehicle.handBack`: gravity, damping and the hull's own colliders back, the springs' hit test skipping a step, and the top held at the speed the hull has so `HOVER_TUNE.easeCap` brings it down at the brake), except a hull destroyed while the drive held it -- by a flow's tick, a bolt or a blast between its steps -- which stays the drive's until the game takes it away later in that same step, so its rider is thrown no harder than `flingMost` however it was wrecked (`raceFling`; handed back, it threw them at its racing speed). **The race box and the hull's own colliders are both built at spawn and swapped by enabling** (`setRaceHull`), never added or removed, so the body's mass never moves and nothing is left behind; but a collider laid on a body adds its mass at once whether it is made enabled or not, and the engine takes a disabled one's off only at its next step, so the body is weighed again from what is enabled the moment the box is on it (`recomputeMassPropertiesFromColliders`), or a spawn's first update, which comes before any step, read the body at twice its mass. **While play stands still the drive holds the hull where it is** (`RACE_CLOCK.live`, written from `stepVehicles`' own `simulate`, which `__debug.advance` passes as true) and comes back at the speed it had, since the frame loop steps the vehicles and the physics behind every panel. **The body moves only on whole sixtieths of a second**, so every frame the drive asks how many of them the coming `step(dt)` will take (`Physics.stepsAhead`, which works the accumulator out as `step` will and changes nothing) and plans exactly that travel in sub-steps; a frame on which the physics takes none (most frames at 144 fps) plans nothing, and every sweep along the way -- the strikes, the bridge's odometer, the climbing judge -- runs from a mark of its own (`sweptTo`) rather than from this frame's start, or a plan made on a frame the physics then did not take would sweep the same metres twice and a step taken with no plan would sweep none. **The height is set, never sprung**: the posed lowest point is held at its ride over a line read off the ground under the hull and raised halfway toward the ground half a second ahead; a rise is taken at once (a faster upward speed eased toward the target at `riseRate`, never a plain `max`, which overshot and flew the hull up faces), within a band it settles, above it it falls hard. Under its line the hull never takes the line's fall ahead (the rise adds the line's rate only when it climbs): a hull reaching a short bridged ledge partway down a descent, where the line holds flat while the line a tenth of a second on has already dropped past it, was driven on down through its ride at the descent's speed and struck the ledge's far rim, which is what raceGround's false dives on hilly ground were. The real-ground test measures with research5 §4.3's own reference pits (the raw ground closed at `max(L, 0.6 × bridgeTime × speed)`, the very metres `0.15 × speed` closed at the first ride's speeds, the same with the bridge on and off): a hull high over one is gliding and only time high over open ground counts against the bridge, as the design's half point means it. **The ground never pulls a raced hull down harder than the world's gravity, and the first ride's magnet is one switch away** (wave 2b, after the owner's first ride; `stepLooseOf` beside `stepVerticalOf`, `RACE_TUNE.hold` 'loose' or 'firm'). The loose hold asks the firm rule what the cushion would do -- rise at once under its line, settle within its band, take back over its band any upward speed at the firm fall -- and puts a floor under the answer and a brake over it. **The floor**: the hull's vertical speed never drops faster than `looseGrip` (20 m/s²) on the ground or `looseFall` (20, the world's gravity) in the air, and never below `keep`, the upward speed the ground itself gave it, faster than either; so off a crest it flies on what the slope gave and lands where a thrown thing would, and over a hollow wider than the bridge it carries on and comes down where it comes down. **The brake**: what the cushion lifts of its own, beyond `keep` -- toward a line the look-ahead raised ahead of a rise -- is never more than the firm fall could stop at that line, or at where the ground under it is about to rise to (`ahead`), and the rest is taken back at the firm fall, never below nought. Six things were learned getting there. A first cut that held the rise itself to what the grip alone could stop at the line climbed every rise slower than the climbing judge reckons (it reckons the firm rise, up to `riseMax`), so faces the judge called climbable struck as crashes and a 60-degree face at full speed was flown; the rise is the firm rule's again and only the brake bounds it, so the judge's reckoning stays true (`raceHold.test.ts`'s ramps meet nothing at 144, 60 and 20 fps under either hold, and with the rise held to 30 m/s nine of fifteen are met). Bounded by its line alone, the brake hung a bike over a pit 6 m deep and drove it into the far wall, which stands straight up: `ahead` is the bridged ground's rise over the next `feedAhead`, so the hull may lift for a wall it is about to meet. `keep` is fed the raw ground's rate under the hull over the last tenth of a second (`rawNow`, before the bridge closes it; the plans' heights kept in a ring, `pastRate`), taken up no faster than `looseFeed` (80 m/s²), so a kerb or a pit's far rim, the ground's whole height in one sub-step, is a nudge and never a launch; fed from the bridged line, the bridge's glide over a pothole kept upward speed the ground never gave, and the bridge's time high over open ground on rolling ground stood 0.6 of a point over the bridge off's, where fed raw it is 0.3. The rise and the settle take the line's own rate while it climbs and otherwise the line's fall between two sub-steps (`looseRate`, the plan's first sub-step taking the hull's own vertical speed while it rides its line): the line's rate half a second ahead reads nought down a descent wherever the footprint then overlaps the one now (at the halved speeds a stock pod's whole range), and the hull flew off its own slope, while the slowly fed `keep` in its place lifted a hull walking off the foot of a slope (fifteen of fifteen in the control). A lift is moved by its end speed as the firm rule moves it, so a rise is climbed to the centimetre as the judge reckons, and anything slower by the mean of its end speeds, which is gravity's parabola exactly: moved by the end's alone, at 20 fps, one sub-step a plan, the body's speed stepped by half as much again as gravity allows. And the pitch is left out of the lowest point the loose hold holds, since its attitude reads the raw ground and at a pit's rim the nose's tip hopped the hull two to nine metres. The firm hold is the first ride's rule to the bit at the halved speeds: `raceHold.test.ts` loads `raceMath`, `raceTune` and `raceDrive` out of git at the wave's start and holds the firm traces to them by `Object.is`, the first ride's speeds laid back. Measured over the converted worlds' gentle, rolling and hilly ground (`npm run test:race-ground`), the loose hold is pulled down harder than gravity nowhere, where the firm one is pulled down 0.3 to 5.3 times a kilometre at up to 511 m/s², its crashes are no more than the firm hold's on any world and its strikes at 144 fps within 20 fps's; the price is time in the air (a full pod on hilly ground off the ground 27% of the way in 1.3 jumps a kilometre against the firm hold's 16% in 1.2, and flying up to 43 m over its line where the firm hold reaches 22). The faces differ by hold, and the tests say each per hold: a 20 m face at full speed is crashed at 60 degrees under the firm hold and at 80 under the loose, which at 60 crashes or flies it and is never under it; a crevasse 25 m deep at a full pod's top is crashed at 120 m wide under the firm hold, carried at 100 m and crashed at 160 under the loose. **The speeds were halved in the numbers themselves**, never by a factor where they are read: every speed of the handling, the ceiling, the rise and fall's most, the impacts and the hit's least loss, and the look-ahead's reach (half a second at the ceiling, as before); the turn rates, the times, the accelerations, the heights and the lengths a face, a rise and a slope are judged over are kept, the bridge's time doubled to keep its metres, and every cost a metre a second doubled, so a wall, a scrape or a landing at the same share of the speed scores and costs the same. **The ground is read over fixed lengths and at the terrain's own poles**, never per frame, so the rules answer the same at 20 and at 144 fps (`raceGround.test.ts` runs every converted world at four rates); a world read beyond the built blocks comes off the far grid's lattice (`World.raceGround`, `nearHeightIfCached`, `farSampleAt`), and never generates a block. **The bridge glides over a pit crossed in under `bridgeTime`** from samples kept behind and ahead of the hull on the poles' own spacing: a gap closes only when every window that holds a sample has an answer and is in its row (`closedAt` is strict and answers NaN otherwise, since an open window read as closed laid a bridge over a canyon on the strength of one sample), and the ground ahead is read bridged too, or the look-ahead saw the pit the line had just bridged and dived into it. And the bridge lifts only where its closing raised a sample (`bridgedBetweenOf`): read between two samples it leaves as they are, an interpolated closing stands over the true ground at the foot of every slope (0.29 m on 30 degrees), which lifted the hull over every valley floor it crossed, pits or none, and a pit wider than the span was not followed as with the bridge off. **A speed is shared out before it is written**: the physics cuts any body at 400 m/s, so the drive holds the whole velocity under `BODY_SPEED_CAP` with the vertical part kept first (`shareSpeed`), and the pod's ceiling stops at `CEILING_MOST` (1,368 km/h, 380 m/s), where the height line still finds its vertical room; past it is a wave of its own. Everything the drive does to the speed is in its own command, so its own cuts are never read as a hit. **The grapple puts no pull on a race-driven hull** (`VehicleSource.takesPull` is false while `raceDriven`), as on a flown ship: the drive's command wins at its next step, so a share of a catch handed to the hull was simply lost and every catch only partly taken; the player takes the whole of it, the hull's turn is the drive's yaw rate in frame time (the drive writes its rotation and sets its turn rate to nought, so the body's own reads still), and the hull test steps over a disabled collider. A pull that reaches one all the same is told to its hit test (`told`), so a hooked racing hull is never hurt by the rope. **What a vehicle is spawned as decides everything only the rider's game runs, and what it is decides everything another screen draws** (`raceRoute`): the drive, the bike's two modes and the box follow the spawned-as kind, the pod's seat and clips, any vehicle's own clips and a creature's saddle follow the def, because the wire carries no kind and the two screens must never disagree. The gallery's 62 vehicles (and the exhibit of ours) are sorted by a table of ours keyed on the garage id (`VEHICLE_KINDS`), the name rule kept as the fallback, behind `kinds: 'names'` and whenever the pass is off (the console sorts the garage's list again whenever `on` or `kinds` moves; a vehicle standing keeps its kind), and the whole of what a spawn does about the race -- the drive it attaches and the hull and box it writes into the spec -- is one answer, `raceSpecFor`, which says nothing at all with the pass off. **The drive allocates nothing a step, and it was the compiler that decided it, not the code**: a number handed across a call the compiler does not build in is boxed (8 to 32 B), so every per-step entry point of `raceMath.ts` takes and answers through a kept record (`hitVerdictOf`, `walkLineOf`, `stepVerticalOf`, `climbVerdictOf`, `lagBlendOf`, `stepYawRateOf`; the plain forms stay as wrappers for the tests), and the step is a list of a dozen small parts called in a loop (`RaceDrive.PARTS`) so each is compiled alone and none pushes another out of the budget. Three smaller traps: `Math.hypot` allocates in V8 where `Math.sqrt` written out does not; an object built by a keyed loop (`for (const k of keys) o[k] = …`) becomes a dictionary and every write to it allocates, so `newBase()` is one literal; and a field that will hold a fraction must start as one (`0.5`, then set in the constructor), or it starts as a small integer and the first fraction written boxes it from then on. `raceAllocOwn.ts` measures it in a process of its own, cleanest of three, because about one process in six fails to build the test's stand-in ground into the step and boxes its answer: in the game every read of the world's terrain boxes its numbers as the springs' always have, about a hundred reads a step at 144 fps, which is the world's cost and not counted. The race view (`raceView.ts`) is the camera's own when `view` is 'race' (`placeRaceView` in `main.ts`): a heading target the mouse moves at most `steerLead` ahead of the nose, the camera trailing and leaning into the turn, pulled out and widened with speed, pulled in at once by what blocks it and let out over half a second, and never closer behind the seat than the hull's own length and `raceClear`; widening refits the cascades only every `fovRefit` degrees, and only after the camera has written that frame's field of view (the cascades read it as they fit). Every ease of its camera half reads the frame's step off the record the game hands it (`raceInput` writes it on every call): left at the half second the record was made with, every ease ran near-instant at every frame rate and no node test could see it, since they all hand the step themselves. A day-one test of a class the pass changes reads the old class at the fixed commit the pass started from (`PRE_WAVE`), and where git cannot answer holds today's to a hash of that class's own trace written into the test, never to a copy of itself (`git merge-base HEAD HEAD` is HEAD, so once the wave is committed it compared the camera with itself). Nothing new is stood or put away near a player racing past `livingAbove` (`World.livingHold`, read in the three held hooks), because each new body is a clone and a compile on a live frame; measured on a perf flight through a Naboo wood, the bodies the hold keeps behind go on reading the ground where nothing is cached any more and made 0.9 terrain blocks a frame on the main thread, which is the hold's own price and the next thing to look at if racing through wildlife hitches. **A raced hull is reached from its lowest point** (`vehicleReach` reads `race.low`): its lowest point stands over the highest ground under its footprint, so parked on an 11-degree slope a pod's middle stood 5.5 m over the ground at its lower side, past E's 4 m, and could not be mounted.
- **A raced vehicle's engine parts are the kind it was spawned as, its glows are the kind it is, and both are decided once and never by the step** (Racer handling wave 2: `src/vehicles/drivetrain.ts`, pure, every number `ENGINE_TUNE`; `raceSpots.ts`; `engineFx.ts`; the race keys in `shipFit.ts`). The parts follow the drive, so a pod has two engines of three (left, +x, parts 0 to 2; right 3 to 5) and anything raced as a bike one, a pod-shaped def raced as a bike included; the glows follow the model, so `def.raceThrusters` (and `def.thrusters` with them) are written by `raceThrustersFor` only for a gallery def whose **own** kind is a pod or a bike, at its first spawn, from its own `engine_N` points within `rearTolerance` of each side's rearmost (every point on a bike; never `engine_sound` or `engine_damage`), and cleared when the pass goes off: written by a spawn as another kind they would have moved a flyer's glows for good, since a def is shared by every spawn of it. A glow is sized down by the square root of half its points and scaled by its side's thrust each frame (`markGlowSides`). **The engines' top is a share laid on the resolved top** (`vFull` kept whole, `handling.V = vFull × top`) and handed out through `share()` and `writeSpec`, so a part going out eases the speed down at the brake with no hit read, exactly as the hull's own top does; written only when the share moves. **That one new caller of `share()` boxed 17.5 B a frame in a part that had made none**: `thrustStep` worked a reverse thrust out on every step, and with `share` reached from one more part the compiler built fewer calls in and boxed its answer; found by bisecting against the committed tree in a scratch copy (`git archive`, Windows' own `tar.exe`, the modules joined by a junction), and mended by working the reverse thrust out only in the reverse branch, which takes the drive back to 0.6 B a frame with a part damaged, a fire lit and a repair held once every 8,192 frames in the measure (`RACE_ALLOC_ENGINES=0` leaves them out to compare): a part burning out or a repair beginning moves the top, which shares the speed out and writes the spec, a few numbers an event and nothing on the steps between. **What the rider holds decides the repair key, never the key**: R is the saber's throw and the placing turn as well, so `drive.repair` is written only inside `race.wants` and only with no hand placing anything, and the bar offers it only while a part is damaged or burning. A scrape now costs the hull a third of what it did (`hullShare`) and the rest goes into a part on the struck side (`hitSide` by the loss's direction against the hull's right), a strike from below into any part, and the overheat that ends a run dry sets one part burning chosen uniformly (held by a chi-square over 6,000 seeded rolls; seed 7 of the first cut failed it at 15.75 on a fair rule, which is why the test names its seed). Two things about the sounds: the vehicle's damaged thruster sets are the game's own (levels 0, 0.5, 0.75, 1; the last with no engine loop at all, its four fire loops in the order idle, speed-up, slow-down, run), chosen by the parts' mean damage and worked out again only when the level moves, so the fire is a third voice written into a record kept per vehicle; and a part crossing into one of the client data's damage bands sparks **once a step however many bands it crossed**, and never on the first read after the drive takes the hull, or a remount would spark for damage it already had. **Sebulba's pod is an exhibit of ours, and every number of it is worked out when the converter runs**: the one rule (`satMeshFile`: a named `.mgn` the archives lack, with a `.lmg` of its stem beside it, is read as the list) changes nothing else the converter makes, measured; the exhibit is appended to the vehicles only on a whole run (a `--limit` run is its first templates and nothing more); its `player` point is borrowed from Gasgano's mesh in `body`'s frame and its engine points are ours at each engine joint's rear middle in that joint's own frame, from the rest pose; its sound is lent (`VEHICLE_SOUND_LENDS`), because the converter's sound join walks only the archive's own templates; and `status` asks for it while the gallery lists Anakin's pod and not Sebulba's, since `status` mounts no archives and both pods live in one archive. **The upgrade levels are `race_*` keys in the kept fit's components** (`race_top: 'race_top_3'`, absent at stock), guarded three ways: `resolveFit` steps over them without a note, `fitForWire` strips them in both its branches, and every save but the race writer's lays the kept ones back over what it saves (`keepRaceKeys`), or a painted speeder's edit page, which saves its whole fit with none, erases them; they are applied right after `World.spawnVehicle` returns, against the base of the kind it was spawned as. **Over a flow means over one**: the column's own answer (`World.lavaAt`) is finite on every column of the lava world, whose whole sea is a flow, so the heat drain asks first that the liquid stand over the ground under the hull and that the hull's lowest point be within the reach the world's own burn gives a ride (`LAVA_HARM.rideReach`), and only then whether the liquid is a flow; and whether liquid stands over the ground is measured against the ground alone, never against `footAt`'s, which is the higher of the ground and the water and against which wave 1's drive never once found itself over water (so a raced hull never said it was on water and the travel's water lag never ran). The smoke and fire work their glow point's world matrix out afresh before they read it (`updateWorldMatrix(true, false)`: the drive wrote the hull's place this step, and three works matrices out only as it draws, so read as it stood the effect trailed the drawn engine by a frame's travel), and `EngineFx.clear` runs wherever a world goes (an arrival, a jump's swap, the select screen), since the ship effects they stand in outlive every world.
- **A race track is a file that ships like the placements, and a run is timed on the body's own steps by a watch the drive hands its hull to** (Racer handling wave 3: `src/race/` -- `trackFile.ts` the file, `gates.ts` the crossing, `raceRun.ts` the run, `trackEdits.ts` the editor's rules, `trackTune.ts` and `trackDials.ts` its numbers; `src/ui/raceHud.ts`, `src/ui/trackList.ts`; `tools/dev/tracksEndpoint.mjs`). A world's tracks are `public/tracks/<world>.json`, plain JSON a build ships and every browser fetches (`cache: 'no-store'`, its content type checked as well as its status, since a missing public file is answered with the page), in the raw frame on a planet and the game's in space, and the editor is the placements editor again: the dev server answers the page's GET from the disk and says `tracks:changed { world }`, its `POST /__dev/tracks` glue is loaded by a name the config works out, one track is put or taken out at a time with every other written back as it was read, the file goes through a dot-named copy, and it goes with its last track (`writeWhole`'s `prune` 1, the tracks folder and nothing above). **The crossing is a segment, tested inside the drive's step** (`RaceDrive.watch`, a `DriveWatch`, called as each live step begins, before any part moves the hull, never while play stands still), from the watch's own mark to where the body stands, against the next gate's window alone; its instant is the mark's clock plus the share of the way through, so a crossing is timed within the steps that carried it at any frame rate (worst 0.0000 ms against the body's own recorded steps at 144, 120, 60 and 20 fps, 3,200 jittered trials with none missed, 9 ns a test). **The run's clock is advanced only by the steps the physics is about to take on a simulated frame** (`advanceSteps(Physics.stepsAhead(dt))` in `stepRun`, a whole count, since a fraction handed to a call the compiler did not build in is a number made each frame), so it stands still behind a panel exactly as the hull is held, and a countdown of 180 sixtieths summed lands at -1e-15, which held go back a whole step until a microsecond of nought was taken as nought. **What still differs between frame rates is the drive's, not the run's**: the drive works its speed out once a frame and holds it over every fixed step the frame takes, at the speed the frame ends at, so while it gathers speed a frame of three steps runs about half a frame ahead -- a standing-start 1.1 km sprint is 9.089 s at 60 and 120 fps, 9.092 at 144 and 9.073 at 20 -- and the design's hundredth across all four rates is met at the three that take a step or less a frame; mending it means the drive's speed worked out a step at a time, which would move the held traces of its earlier waves. **The start boost is earned in a window round go and put on the drive only once the clock has reached go**: handed over earlier, the countdown's own mask (throttle nought) ended it on the drive's next step. A crash waits `putBack` seconds of the run's clock and stands a fresh spawn of the same garage entry as the kind the run was raced as at the last gate crossed, facing the next, and **the wreck's own materials are held until that spawn is prepared** (`World.disposeVehicle`'s `keep`, `App.runKept`): disposed at the wreck, its glow sprites released two programs nothing else used and the put-back built them again on a live frame. **A hull stood somewhere on purpose has the race view's target turned with it** (`aimRaceViewAt`, at the grid and at a put-back): the target is a heading of the world's held within the lead of the nose, so one left from before the hull was turned had the first smoke run's pod veer up to the lead's width off gate 0 at go and circle it for minutes; the bests are kept on the character by `<world>/<track>` and then the kind raced as, by minimum, with the levels and the best lap's splits, and the mark carries them under `r:` only while there are any, so a record with none keeps the mark it had. A track's corridor (`clear` either side of every leg) keeps the procedural flora off at the next arrival through the same `keepOff` circles the dressing uses, and `track({ check })` reads trees only on the ground chunks built now, which it says. The editor's list is the debug menu's third view (`DebugListSource`, `TrackList`), drawn by the game and acting through `__debug.track`'s own shapes, so a button and a console line can never do two things. The TEST course was chosen by a one-off script over Tatooine's own pack and is held by `raceTrack.test.ts` to the same two rules whenever the packs are there (`tools/swg/tests/courseThings.ts` reads a pack's placed things, plantings, lairs and posts in node). Two tests read neighbouring lines of `main.ts` by adjacency (`engineFx.clear()` straight before the world's own call; `drive.repair =` written once), so a line of a new feature goes beside such a pair, never between it. **The game's half of a run is rules the node test runs, never a copy of them** (`runKindFor`, `runInput`, `runStep`, `putBackSpawn` and `runBestOf` in `raceRun.ts`; `App.startRun`, `stepRun`, `putBack` and `keepRaceBest` write the world into one kept record and do the side effects, nothing more): the first cut tested the start on the test's own copy of the game's input code, and its clock against a truth stamped with the very clock it checked, and a mutation run found some two dozen ways to break it -- the start window, the boost's end, the countdown's hold, the kind raced as, the put-back, the splits, the reader's limits -- that every test passed. The truth is now the physics' own steps counted from the run's start (step k at `k × FIXED_DT − countdown`), go must fire on the countdown's own step, and `standAt` is run at speed on the real Vehicle. Three faults it had hidden: a grid is a body's place and a gate a hull's middle, so a put-back before the first gate took the middle off the grid's height and stood the hull that much under where the countdown had; a hull taken away without a wreck (the garage's clear) was read as a crash and put back; and the living were held only past `livingAbove`, which a bike's plain top sits under, so its lap built seventeen programs of the bodies stood beside the course (a DLT-20A rifle one of them carried among them, whose shader's name read like a rock's): a run now holds them whatever the speed (`holdsLiving(v, inRun)`). The curtain gives the keys back as it found them and asks the lock only through `handBackMouse`, because a run is started from the debug menu's Gates view, which holds the mouse; and with the race pass off no track file is read and no corridor kept clear. A headless tab driving a run must stand in for `requestPointerLock` (a promise that never settles): refused, the game asks again every 1.2 s for good, and now and then headless Chrome sends a lock change the game reads as the lock lost, which opens the Escape menu -- after the curtain had taken the keys back regardless, that menu was invisible to the first smoke, and once the curtain gave them back as it found them it held a pod on its grid for four minutes.
- **Another player's vehicle is drawn by the motion their own game sends, and whatever rides that picture takes the very same step, from the very same record, at the very same moment** (Racer wave 4a: `src/net/peerReckon.ts`, `cleanMotion` in `server/vehicleWire.mjs`, `RemotePlayers.update`, the reckoned branch of `HullFrames.stepPeer`). A `veh` carries `vl` (the sender's velocity), `yr` (its turn about straight up) and `at` (when its place was true on the shared clock, `placeMoment`), all three or none, and the hail says `veh: 2`; glided as before, a full pod was drawn 22 to 40 m behind itself. **A state goes out before its frame's own physics step** (`stepNet`, then `Physics.step`), so the place it carries is the one the frame before's step left: its moment is the frame's start on the shared clock less the frame's own `dt`, which the bodies have not taken yet, and less `Physics.behind`. The first cut took off only the remainder, and every picture stood the sender's speed over its frame rate behind (2.8 m for a full pod at 60 fps, 5.6 at 30), which no test could see because both modelled the sender the other way round (step, then send) and the smoke measured against the sender's own log: a stand-in of a sender sends before it steps, as `App` does (peerReckon's `simulate`, grappleVehicles' `PeerStandIn`, each with a `late` stamp the tests show failing). A turn the race drive or the flight writes this frame is a frame ahead of that place too, and is carried back over the frame's step (`turnBack`) to the turn the body stood at beside it; their velocity is not, being the one the coming step moves the body by. A race drive paused while play stands still sends no turn rate, or every other screen drew it turned up to fourteen degrees off its still hull. The picture moves along the arc the sent velocity and turn carry it (carried straight it stood 3.8 m outside a pod's hardest turn) and eases (`RECKON_TUNE.ease`) toward the state's place carried by the state's age, which is read on the shared clock and never from when the state was heard, so the wire's jitter and a busy frame move nothing; no motion, no clock shared, or an age when heard past `early`/`trust` glides as before, and a state is carried no further than `most`, so a peer gone quiet stands rather than sliding back. **The moment is read once, at the frame's top** (`reckonAt`, before the player's update), and handed to the rope (`RemoteBodies.stampReckon`) and to the pictures (`RemotePlayers.reckonAt`) alike, and **the record the rope reads is the ride's own, by reference** (`PeerView.shipReck`, `PeerHullBox.reck`): a state is heard between frames and the picture is reckoned from it in the next, so a copy taken as the list was last refilled handed the rope the state before on every frame one came. grappleVehicles' test 13 holds the pose the rope ends a frame on to the picture reckoned after it to the bit, under a vehicle whose speed surges so two states are two predictions, and parts them by 0.01 to 0.5 m with either fault (the frame before's moment, the copy); every tow on the smooth pose there is held to the trace the code at the wave's start made of it (`PRE_WAVE_TOWS`, c862036), since its check that the smooth path had not moved compared two runs of the new code; and the game's own hand-overs (the moment, the record, the stamp, the turn carried back, the nudge's list, a group run's shared start and its words to the group) are pinned as text in grappleWiring, raceTrack and raceDrive's section 25, because every behaviour test makes them for itself. The figure rides the reckoned picture in the ride's own frame (`rideOn`) instead of gliding after it, and the motion blur, a pod's gait and `vehiclePose` read the velocity sent while a picture is reckoned. Numbers go to the reckoning in kept records (`ARC_IN`, `TURN_B`, the rope's `rn`): handed `carriedIn` and `slerpInto` as arguments they made 2.6 B a step. Measuring the rope on a reckoned pose found an old hole of the rope's own: hung at a peer's rear face the fan met the box on every frame, and the search after it handed `fanOf`, `clearAt` and `cast` its numbers as arguments, about a kilobyte a frame in two processes of three whenever the compiler left them out of `sweep` (the young generation's count read 13 B a frame only because 28 scavenges had emptied it, which is why a window with any collection measures nothing); they go in `fq` and `cs` now. **A group race is words and every browser's own run** (`src/net/raceNet.ts`, `src/race/raceGroup.ts`, `server/raceWire.mjs`, hail `race: 1`): the leader's `call` lists the members by relay id in grid order and the start on the shared clock, the relay carries `race` to the sender's group alone as it carries `cross`, a member's slot is its place in that list behind the grid, and its countdown holds to the shared start until go (`RaceRun.sharedStart`, the remaining time rounded up to whole physics steps) and runs on its own steps from there, so a group's go is one instant and each member is timed as a run alone is; laps and finishes go out as words and every browser works the same standings out of them. The nudge between pods (`RaceDrive.near`, filled from `RemotePlayers.nearPictures` before the hulls step, `nudgeReach`, `nudgeStick`) costs nothing with nobody near and allocates nothing with somebody near (`raceAllocOwn.ts` runs a mirrored pair in and out of reach so the weave still crosses its gates).
- **A pod put together from parts is built at its spawn out of the two pods the garage loaded once, writes neither, and is framed in its cockpit pod's idle** (Racer wave 4b: `src/vehicles/podHybrid.ts`, `Garage.hybrid`, `instance` and `frameModel`, the panel's engines choice in `src/ui/vehiclesUi.ts`). Every skinned pod is one skeleton to the bit, so a hybrid is the cockpit pod cloned with a skeleton of its own keeping only the triangles whose every corner is weighted heaviest to `body`, and the engine pod's skinned meshes keeping the rest (the 16 to 32 a pod has reaching into its own cockpit, its control cables, go with the engines) bound to the clone's skeleton; the engine pod's points whose nearest joint is not `body` are hung on the clone's joints of the same names and the cockpit pod's own such points taken off, so the seat, clips and rider pose are the cockpit's and the glows, parts, smoke and fire are the engine pod's. Five things were learned on the way. **A kept geometry holds only the vertices it uses**: a skinned mesh's box (`computeBoundingBox`, and `setFromObject` asked to be precise) walks every vertex of the position attribute, not the index, so a triangle filter by index alone framed the hybrid and sized it over the very triangles it had dropped. **A re-pointing writes into the hybrid's own copy, never a mesh's geometry**: the wardrobe's `remapSkin` writes `setAttribute` on the mesh's geometry, which on a clone is the cached one every later spawn of that pod reads; and a joint the cockpit's skeleton lacks refuses the half in words (`PodHalfRefused`) rather than riding the root. **A geometry worn whole is said shared by the hybrid itself** (`userData.shared`, whatever the clone copied): the test that disposed a hybrid as a vehicle disposes itself caught a cached geometry going with it once the cache's own mark was absent. **A hybrid with clips is framed in its cockpit pod's idle** (`framingClip`, `posedExtents`, the skeleton put back at rest after): the engines ride clips made for another pod's engines, and framed at rest the light-bending cockpit's idle stood 2.08 m under the hull's underside on Anakin's engines, under the ground; framed in the idle neither the idle's first frame nor the run's stands under it on any pair. And **a seat row is in its own pod's frame**: the owner's `POD_SEAT` row for the cockpit pod is moved by how far the hybrid's framing stands the cockpit from where that pod framed alone stood it (`cockpitAlone`, `hybridSeatCockpit`, `podSeatRule`'s `cockpit`), a row for the hybrid's own id winning over it. The framing and the seat's cockpit are pure functions the garage calls (`framedExtents`, `hybridSeatCockpit`), the two pods a hybrid is asked to be made of are looked up again among the skinned pods by id (`pickHalves`: a hybrid given new engines is its cockpit pod on them, never a hybrid of a hybrid, whose cockpit no garage could find when it came to be built), and the hand-overs only the game makes (`find`, `instance`, the framing's and the seat's calls, the failure and picture disposes, the wire's id, the panel) are pinned as text in `podHybrid.test.ts`, since the garage, the peers and the app do not load under node and a review found every one of them could be broken with every test passing. The def is its own (`hybridDefOf`: made once a pair and kept, never in the list, so `reclassify` and `clearRaceThrusters` walk it apart), copies none of the cockpit def's race thrusters (they are worked out from its own model's engine points at its first spawn, `noteRaceThrusters`), and sounds by `soundFrom`, the engine pod's id and template, never by its own id, which joins nothing, nor its template, which is the cockpit's. **It goes on the wire as one id naming both halves** (`pod_racer_one.podracer_anakin`, `HYBRID_SEP`): every relay's `VEHICLE_ID` pattern already passes the dot and the longest pair of the converted pods is exactly the relay's 48 (`wireVehicleId` sends the cockpit alone for a pair that ever were longer), so no relay changed or needs restarting, a browser built before finds no such vehicle and draws no picture without throwing, and a browser built for it builds the same hybrid from the id (`Garage.find`), which 4a's reckoning and the grapple's peer box take as any picture; a picture lets go of the geometries the hybrid made of its own when it is dropped (`disposePodHybrid`), which no picture had before.
- **A loading screen stands what it brings you to only once the pack is in, waits a frame only where the frame shows something, and a travel to a place is one screen placed from the far world's own places file** (the performance pass's first wave: `src/world/loadingTune.ts`, `App.settle`, `App.teleport`, `App.standOnCourse`, `World.courseNeed`). `World.load` empties the people's rows (`standingPeople.unload`) and only `loadPack` hands them the new world's, so the two forced passes the arrival's warm-up made in between stood nobody on every path, and every town's people were stood on live frames after the screen lifted (27 to 29 colour programs in the first 30 s, 360 to 520 ms frames on Direct3D 11; `standOrder.test.ts` drives the real `StandingPeople` in that order). The settle stands them instead: the people after its first settled loop (the catalogue awaited, bounded, since a first world finds it still in flight), the ticket collectors there too (`standCollectorsNow`, which `standTravelRest` must not push twice), the people of ours and the creatures after the shuttles (`standOursNow`, `standWildNow`, a wild pass forced past its hold and its few sites a pass, a site still waiting for its ground stepped over and tried again inside the bound, which the wait holds for even with every body up, since a site that waited has stood nobody to keep it), the people again just before the sweep, and then their bodies waited for up to `standWaitMs` from the first stand; a body still loading at the bound is left to its load and counted (`afterBound`). The sweep waited a whole frame per batch of eight whatever it built -- 28 s of a 44 s screen at Mos Eisley, its own work 0.45 s -- so behind a screen it waits only after a batch that built a program or after `sliceMs` of work (`sweepYield`, `World.sweepBatches`); in play, the Effects switch's sweep, every batch still waits, and a hidden tab's message-channel frame is untouched. Each batch that built is timed into the session's milliseconds a program (`programMs`), which is what the screen's words and the pacing's `hard` read: the trivial probe timed straight after the game's own draws timed those draws (267 ms for a link that costs half a millisecond), so it decides only whether the Interface page blames the driver, three links on frames of their own after the sweep, each after a drain (`checkDriver`), the least kept. The world is drawn at most every `drawEveryMs` while the screen is fully opaque (`LoadingScreen.opaque`, past its half-second fade and before `hide`), every frame from the reveal on. A place on another world is worked out in the game's frame from that world's `pois.json` centre before anybody travels (`placeFromPois`, teleport's own two lines, which `loadingRules.test.ts` runs as text), and the travel comes out there with `ground` and `port`: stood on the real terrain, kept as the spawn and stepped into a port's building **before** `cameOut` and the saved place, or the group's word and the saved place are the world's spawn (the followers came out at Bestine); a follower's `besideAt` still wins, and no centre, space, or a layout centre that disagrees (`centreAgrees`, the safety net) runs the two old phases exactly as they were. A race start asks a strict need test before anything is shown (`raceCurtainNeed` over `World.courseNeed`: the hull within `courseNear`, the grid settled, every model `LayoutStreamer.modelsAlong` finds along the legs sampled every `courseStep` loaded and warmed, nothing queued), because a start with no curtain can load and warm nothing (no screen is up for the warm to load under); otherwise a course curtain that never calls `settle()`: the grid's ground waited for only when the hull was far, the course's models loaded for at most `TRACK_TUNE.warmWaitMs` (`loadBounded`, which races the loads against a timer, so a load that never settles cannot hold the curtain) and compiled through stand-ins (`compileCourse`, which must never call `placedStandIns`, since that consumes the placed warm's list), the queue flushed, the screen hidden at once.
