A model known not to load (`LayoutStreamer.hasFailed`, noted by `loadModel` as by a tier) is counted apart (`courseCounts`' `failed`) and never asked for again, or one broken file along a course took a curtain on every start; and the session's memo of what a course warmed is let go with the streamer at `unload`, or it held the world left behind's whole layout. It keeps `traveling` for the seventy-odd places that read it and sets `curtainOnly` beside it, which only the story's leave and a call's end read: a start is a moment at the grid, not a journey, and must not end a call or say the player left every area. And the queue's idle rule judges frames counted, never the wall time since the last tick: a frame that built a program for 1.6 s read as a stopped loop to the first timer after it, which flushed the rest of the queue in that timer task, outside any frame (37 of 170 programs of a Bestine session); a waiter now needs two of its own polls spanning more than `idleMs` with no new tick (`QueueWatch`), the single-instant entry test is a 2 s backstop, **both take `idleShownMs` (10 s) on a page on screen** -- there the browser holds the frames back while its GPU process catches up, with the main thread free and the waiters' timers running (486 ms with no frame after a cold Direct3D 11 Mos Eisley screen lifted, read as a stop at 250 ms, and the flush that followed blocked the page 3.9 s), so only a hidden tab, whose frames really stop, is answered quickly -- the frame loop ticks the queue again after its draw, and a flush in play takes only the jobs there when it began and stops the moment frames resume. `flushedInPlay` counts every program the idle rule builds on a page on screen with no loading screen up, a waiter's flush (`flushPlay.shown`) or the entry test's own outright build (`noteIdleBuilt`), which must stay nought: counted only when it built within `idleMs` of a frame's tick, it could never rise, since the flush begins only after its waiter saw no tick for longer than that and stops at the first one. `programPace.test.ts`'s day-one half loads the old queue out of git at `5f3cff4` and shows it calling that 1.6 s frame a stop. A queued compile that ends on a room's pass puts the world pass's lights back with a compile of nothing under layer 0 (`compileOne`), or the next shadow pass keyed three's depth material on the room set; outdoors beside buildings a frame's last real pass is still a room's, so skinned depth programs are still built in play there until per-pass render states (wave 2). Four things the smoke taught. **The driver check times a link only while the world builds nothing** (`World.compileBusy`: no outright build, nothing queued, no flush; never "every body up", which a town whose wildlife was still landing never was), and one screen a session, the first whose check is not done, waits up to `driverWaitMs` for its tries ("checking the graphics driver"), every later one letting the check run on over its frames: a body landing behind a screen builds outright with a frame's breath per object, so the hundred milliseconds a screen used to have after its sweep were all busy, and on Vulkan the check never ran over two arrivals (`shaders().driver.waits` says why it is still waiting); held for it on every screen until done, a cold Direct3D 11 town whose bodies were still landing as each screen lifted paid a second a screen for a sentence on the Interface page. On headless Direct3D 11 a trivial link really costs about 235 ms with its drain at 0.3 ms, measured on a page of its own, so the check calling that machine slow is the check working; Vulkan's is 0.5 ms. **A course curtain warms shadow depth only when something on the course sways** and draws no frame of its own: both together were most of a 3.2 to 3.8 s warm curtain, which is 2.1 s without them. **What was built for a body up as the screen lifted is read off three's own records of that body's materials** (`after.forUp`: `World.readyBodyMaterials` kept as the screen lifts, then `programsForUp` over `renderer.properties.get(material).programs` for the ids past the renderer's highest at the lift and inside the window), never by whether a program is skinned, since a body stood after the screen builds skinned programs too, and **never by the queue label of the body's prepare**: a body is up only once its prepare has built, so a label-keyed count could see nothing but a prepare made afresh, while the programs the target is about (a room set, a recolour, three's own first draw) are built under other labels or outside the queue -- it read nought in every session whatever happened. The queue's labels (`model:`, `look:`, `weapon:`, `hologram:`) still name what `lastBuilt` shows. **A counter that proves a target must be able to rise in the game**: before trusting a nought, find the one case the game can reach that makes it one. And **two sessions' program keys are compared by key, never by name or a hash of three's key text for a shader material**: `shaders({ full: true })` names a key after the first material that ever built it, and a `ShaderMaterial`'s key carries three's shader-cache ids, numbered in the order a session first met each source, so the same effect pass hashes differently in two sessions; what remained after setting those apart, between a run with `sliceMs` 0 and one with the default, was the crowd's own random draw of who stands where.
- **Three keys a program on the light state of the scene object it is drawn under, so every pass draws through a scene of its own, and a program built in play is issued and polled, never waited for** (the performance pass's second wave: `src/world/passScenes.ts`, `src/core/linkMode.ts`, `src/world/roomDefer.ts`, `src/core/skinnedLoad.ts`, `src/world/personKeys.ts`, the motion blur's own jobs in `src/core/fx/velocity.ts`, `src/player/bytesHash.ts`, `src/player/partsFile.ts`). Three keeps one render state per scene object and depth (`renderStates.get(scene, depth)`), and its light state's version moves whenever the counts change (a module-wide counter in `WebGLLights`): drawn by one scene, the world pass, a building's rooms pass and the shadow pass handed one state back and forth three times a frame outdoors beside buildings, every lit material chose its program again at each, and a depth program was keyed on whichever pass had drawn last. The shadow pass and the rooms pass now draw and compile through proxy scenes that share the real scene's `children` array by reference (never a copy, never a reparent) and copy its fog, environment, background and override material at each pass (`SCENE_FIELDS`), each keeping a light state of its own; they copy `matrixWorldAutoUpdate` too, so the portal renderer's one matrix walk a frame still holds. The light churn is nought (it was three a frame, `links().lightChurn`), a depth program has one set (every light, the shadow pass's), `PortalRenderer.primePasses` sets each proxy's lights up once a world, and a compile goes against the pass's own scene (`passScene`); `combinedPrograms` leaves depth programs under that every-light set out (`isDepthProgram`), since it is a real pass's now. `perf({ ab: { key: 'renderStates' } })`'s `shared` is the old way, warm-up and put-backs included. **`KHR_parallel_shader_compile` answers on the owner's machine** (Chrome 155, Direct3D 11): a boot check links a salted probe and polls it (`LinkCheck`), and in `parallel` mode the queue issues `issuePerFrame` programs a frame and polls `COMPLETION_STATUS_KHR` (`LinkFlight`, kept arrays, nothing made a frame), resolving an object only once every program it was issued with reports ready, a program past `boundMs` the old way (said once), and everything at once while the tab is hidden, whose polls never answer; the sweep behind a screen and the outright builds poll their own flights the same way. Two things were learned getting T9 to nought. **An object a flight saw ready finishes only what reports its link done** (`resolveLinks`' `onlyReady`): its materials may be shared, and another job may have issued a program for them in another pass since (a second person of one look whose first person's rooms pass is in flight), and finished there that link was waited for on the main thread, 386 ms on Direct3D 11. And **a program `compileAsync` warmed is not finished**: it waits for the link and never asks for the uniforms, so its first draw asks, synchronously, and in play that question waits behind whatever the GPU process is linking for somebody else (two water-mask programs the effects warmed behind the first screen, first drawn 84 s later, waited 111 ms each); `PostFX.warmUp` resolves what it warmed before it returns. The link-wait watch (`watchLinkWaits`) wraps every GL call on the game's context that can wait on a link, timing it whoever calls (three's own first draw included) and leaving the completion polls untimed, and T9 is read there, never off the queue's own clock. **ANGLE on Direct3D 11 builds a second vertex shader on the GPU process the first time a skinned program draws when the joints are unsigned shorts** (25 to 57 ms of the whole browser stalled), so every skinned mesh's joints are stored as floats at load (`skinnedPlugin`, registered by `surfaces.withPlugin` and beside it at every other loader of a skinned model, a text test holding every `new GLTFLoader(` to it, the rig's animation-only loader excepted); and every skinned primitive's morph count is padded with zero targets to the next rung of `PERSON_KEYS_TUNE.morphLadder` (0, 7, 33), so the motion blur's warm variants (8) and the skinned depth combinations (27) are closed lists of ours, which `warmLists.test.ts` holds every converted primitive inside. Three has built each mesh's `morphTargetInfluences` by `afterRoot`, so the padding extends every built mesh's influences in the same step, never through `updateMorphTargets` (which renames the targets the body sliders read): padded in the geometry alone, `getVertexPosition` and a skinned mesh's bounding sphere read an undefined influence and come out NaN. The two cost 3.1% of the same people's model memory (264.0 against 272.3 MB at Bestine with the budget out of the way), so `MOBILE_CACHE.budget` went from 180 to 190 MB; `swg.floatJoints` and `swg.morphLadder` at '0' are the hatches. **A motion-blur variant is keyed on the empty light state of its own draws**, so one met in play goes on the world's queue as a job with its own compile (its dummy as its own scene under the effects' target) and finish (`OwnJob`), never through `compileOne`, which would key it on a pass's lights and leave its first real draw to build it again; a job let go unbuilt says so (`dropped`) and the next draw asks again. **A body stood outdoors is shown once its world pass is built, and its rooms pass follows as work of its own** (`compileReady`'s `roomsLater`): it is never shown in a building pass until that answers (`ActorRoutes.add`'s `roomReady`, a wide record flagged `F_NOROOM` rather than skipped, a lit one left drawn and counted), with the routes off its root is hidden round each rooms pass (`roomPending`), a body in a room while not ready has its job made urgent (`roomsNow`), and a body with a light under it is never deferred. **A shared look's renders are made in the characters' worker with the pixels' hash made there** (`Customizer.shareRender`, `hash: true`): over a two-minute Vulkan walk the main thread's look renders were 62 stretches of 40 ms or more between frames, 5.2 s, and are none; a customizer that renders elsewhere refuses a share outright, since `run` takes its private branch first. And a parts pack's `parts.json` is fetched and parsed once a folder for the session (`partsFile`), out of `character.ts` because a parameter property keeps that file from node. Four things the review found, each of which every behaviour test passed. **Work deferred off the frame is tied to the life of what it compiles, both ways round** (`deferRooms`): a cached look trimmed while its rooms batch waited had that batch compile its disposed materials (programs nothing releases, and the cascades' strong map taking them back), so the owner going marks the handle `gone` and the work compiles nothing; and a world's unload clears the queue, answering every batch unbuilt: a handle marked ready on that answer put a body of the cached look in a rooms pass with no program in the next world, so work let go (`OwnJob.dropped`) leaves the handle not ready and asks again (`promote` urgent, `resume` below it). **A check made while the tab is hidden decides nothing** (`LinkCheck` waits a hidden spell out and starts its window again when the tab shows, polling the program it already compiled): decided on, a page that entered play hidden was `blocking` for its whole session. **A program three destroyed in flight has no handle** (`program` undefined; `isReady` then answers null for ever), so the flight strikes it off (`programGone`) rather than waiting the bound and blaming the driver. And **what a program built in play was is read off its cache key, never its label** (`playKindOf`): a label is the first material's name, and the sway's depth twins carry names of their own. The hand-overs only the game makes (the queue's link mode, the flights' `onlyReady`, the effects' resolve, the watch, the check, the variants' queue, the deferral's every hand-over) are pinned as text in `programWiring.test.ts`, since a review broke all of them at once in a copy and `test:all` stayed green; and the ladder's hatch gives the first wave's warm lists back (`warmLadder`), or a "before" measured with it off is a mix of the two.
- **The scene's one matrix walk a frame steps over a body nothing drew and a body nothing posed, so every write under a creature's or person's root says so** (the performance pass's third wave: `src/world/mobiles/bodyGroup.ts`, `crowd.ts`, the tiers' last part in `lod.ts`, `Mobile.posed`). A mobile's root is a `BodyGroup` whose `updateMatrixWorld` composes its own matrix and stops there while the manager's whole cull hides it (`culled`, set beside `group.visible`, never the portal renderer's per-pass routing, which hides before the first pass walks and would leave a body indoors a frame stale), or while nothing has posed it since its last whole walk and its world matrix is that walk's to the bit (`still`). **Still is "nothing posed since the last whole walk", never "the mixer did not run this frame"**: the world steps every frame while it is drawn less often (ten times a second behind an opaque loading screen, never in a hidden tab), so a frame's own flag would keep a walk's matrices from before a mixer run nobody walked. So `posed()` is called by the mixer's every run, the ragdoll's every step, every write of a position, a turn or a scale of `inner`, the weapon holder, the muzzle or a spine bone, every add of a node under the body (a node hung since the last walk has never been walked: the walk test's own setup showed it), a hologram's fade and a re-bind of the skeletons; `walkSkip.test.ts` holds the writers in `src/world/mobiles/` to a list, each in a method that marks the body, and a new writer fails it until it is listed and marked. A fighter is posed from `npcs.ts`, outside that folder, so its root never skips as still, and it has no whole cull, so it never skips at all yet. **The skip is only the walk reaching the body through its parent**: anything that needs the whole subtree now calls `walkWhole()` (the weapon hung in a hand, the ragdoll's start), and a reader of one node works its own matrix out with `updateWorldMatrix(true, false)`, which the skip never touches. A body the walk stepped over as culled is never listed for the motion blur (its meshes' matrices are stale history), whatever the list's own switch; one it stepped over as standing still is, its matrices being the very ones a whole walk would have made, and dropping those too would churn every far body in and out of the list as its mixer runs, each return a record with no history. **The two skips cover for each other** (a culled body standing still is skipped by either), so each alone measures only what the other leaves it: `perf({ ab: { key: 'crowdExact' } })` flips the four exact fixes together. **An exact fix is proved on the scene the portal renderer drew, never the canvas** (`crowd({ compare })`, `readRenderTargetPixels` of the effects' scene target): two draws of the same state differ in a hundred canvas pixels by the effects' own eases and dither; and a switch the manager applies on its pass must also apply when set (`applySmallParts`, `applyEmpties`), or a compare drawn with nothing stepped compares one picture with itself. And two ways a frame boxed a number came out of this wave's own allocation check: a conditional write of a tune's number into a local the function then stores (`if (shared && think > floor) think = floor`) boxed it on every call, 16 bytes a body a frame, where `Math.min` keeps it a double; and a function's number returned once a frame into a field (`shareScale`) is worked out again only when the camera's field or zoom moves. Two more came out of its review. **Whether a body's root stands still is judged over time, never from one frame to the next** (`crowdInput`: within `holdStill` of where it came to rest for `holdAfter` seconds): the physics moves a body only on whole sixtieths of a second, so at 144 frames a second a walker shows no movement on most frames, and the first cut's frame-to-frame measure would have held a far walker's pose on those frames and slid its feet at high frame rates only; the test steps a walker in sixtieths under 144, 60 and 30 frames a second. And **a test that reads source as lines splits on `/\r?\n/`**: this checkout's working copies of `mobile.ts`, `manager.ts`, `main.ts` and `spineFold.ts` are CRLF (git's `autocrlf`), so the writer audit, split on `\n` alone, never found a method's closing `  }`, read every method as running to the end of the file and passed with `posed()` taken out of any writer above the file's last one. And the numbers a frame writes for every body live in a class whose fields take their first values where they are declared (`CrowdRecord`, `CrowdView`), not in the manager's object literal: written there, the share made a new heap number every write in node's measure of the crowd's frame (16 bytes a body a frame), where the class makes none.
- **The sun's middle and far cascades are drawn by age and held where their maps were drawn, and a held light is put back between `csm.update()` and `refreshCascadeBoxes`, never after** (the performance pass's sixth wave, built fourth: `src/world/shadowCadence.ts`, `shadowBatches.ts`, `shadowDials.ts`, the hooks in `portalRender.ts`). Three skips a light whose shadow's `autoUpdate` and `needsUpdate` are both false, walk and all, and leaves its map as it was; that is the whole mechanism. Its trap is that three's cascades move every light every frame and `refreshCascadeBoxes` works every matrix out again from where they were moved, so an old map read through this frame's matrix is wrong over nearly half a picture: `ShadowHold.decide` runs in `World.updateShadows` between the two and puts a held cascade's light and target back where its map was drawn, so every reader (the receivers, the ambient occlusion's cascades, the grass's matrices by reference, the leaf cards' push toward the light, the bodies' cascade test) sees one cascade whose matrix is its map's. `autoUpdate` is written by `decide` and `dirtyAll` and nowhere else, so a draw no `decide` preceded draws what the last frame left due at the pose it left (which is why `bench` decides before each of its draws, or it timed whatever the last frame of play left due rather than the pass play draws); `drawn`, after every shadow pass through the hooks, records the pose of each cascade three really drew (three clears `needsUpdate` as it draws) and makes one asked and not drawn dirty; a frame with no shadow pass, a whole draw outside a frame (`drawShadowsOnly`, which asks every map itself) and the world's own events (a shadow setting, shadows switched on, a jump, an unload, the depth warm-up) make every cascade dirty. What decides a hold, in order: no record or no map, dirty, the age (`maxAgeMs` [0, 13, 41], counted in time, so at 144 Hz the middle is drawn every 2nd frame and the far every 6th), then the guards over three's own fitting -- the sun moved past `sunStepDeg`; across the light every corner of the band the shader samples the cascade over (CSMShader's `csmx` to `csmy`) inside the held box with `coverMargin` to spare, or what the fresh box spares it; along the sun the held map's deep end no more than `coverDeep` short of the fresh map's, or of the band's deepest corner where that is nearer (one-sided: a held light nearer the slice only deepens its map), and the held light no more than `coverHigh` nearer the slice; and any listed mover faster than `fastMover` (the world's vehicles by their own speed or by the distance their places went **timed in the physics' own steps**, a stood shuttle in flight, a peer's figure or ride) whose shadow could reach the band -- and the stagger keeps the middle and far from both being due by age on one frame. A body moves only on whole sixtieths of a second and a hull's group copies its body before the frame's physics step, so `stepVehicles` writes the step count the hulls stand at (`ShadowMovers.steps`) straight before they copy their bodies, and a hull's distance is divided by the steps between two places, never the frame's step: timed in frames, a 10 m/s hull read 24 m/s on the 144 Hz frames that took a step and was listed on 4 frames in 10, so the 20 m/s line was really an 8 m/s one at the owner's rate (a hull whose pose is written every frame, a hold, is still timed in frames). **Each shadow map's depth reaches its own band** since the owner's answer to Q8 (`shadowCameraFar`, `farDepth` 'whole'): LIGHT_MARGIN plus the larger of the shadow distance and its box's width, the far one about 908 m, where LIGHT_MARGIN plus the shadow distance alone (470 m, from a light 150 m over the slice's most sunward corner, while the far slice runs up to 750 m along the sun) left the far band's ground across the sun unshadowed on most headings, every frame and with no hold at all. So `coverDeep` ships 0 and the deep guard is exact for little: over 15,552 poses against three's real `CSM` in node 12,328 holds pass and not one leaves ground of the band past its deep end or outside its box; turning at 90 degrees a second at 144 Hz the middle is drawn on half the frames and the far on 17%, where the old depth with the same exact guard drew the far on half (`farDepth: 'slice'` puts the old depth back to compare). **Anything that turns metres into a shadow map's depth with one range must take each cascade's own**: the leaf cards' crown skip divided by the near cascade's range for all three, so with the far range twice as long the far band's leaves skipped twice the metres they should; `uSwgLeafRange` is now a vec4 by cascade, picked inside `swgLeafBias` by the lookup's own unrolled index. **The guards' frame copies are tested through the real `decide`**: `ShadowHold.decide` works its light frames out by hand (`lookInverse`), its band and corners off the view it reads and the sun's step, none of which the positional `holdCovers` the coverage cases run ever touches, so the coverage test asks the real `decide` every one of the same 15,552 holds and holds its answer to `holdCovers`' over three's own matrices; an exact tie on the cover line (three snaps every box to whole texels, so a held box very often stands exactly where the fresh one does at the decisive corner) breaks either way between three's matrices and the hand-written ones, 1e-13 apart, and is the one difference allowed. **A region tier's still casters reach the shadow maps as one draw** (`ShadowBatches`): a tier's bulk instanced meshes and its level groups' copies, once the tier is shown, go into one `BatchedMesh` on layer 28 (`FX_LAYERS.shadowCast`), which every view pass leaves out and the shadow camera, enabling every layer, draws; `passesOf` answers it no pass, so no warm-up builds a lit program for it, and its one depth combination is warmed with the rest (`batchedDepthCombo`). Its sources keep their `castShadow` and are turned off only between the hooks' `before` and `after`, and put back in a `finally`: the casters' rules (the page's switches, `applyCasters`, a level group's cast rule on every repack) write `castShadow` on the sources at any moment, the census and the cascades' box test read it, and a source turned off for good would be turned back on by the next sweep and drawn twice. So the batch reads each source's own `castShadow && visible` every frame as its answer, follows an instanced mesh by its matrix store's version and a level group by its repack count, and leaves out whatever would draw differently in one draw (a material array, the sway's own depth material, a side, a cut, a blend, a displacement, a layer of its own, morphs, a draw range, a source not at the world's origin). Two things about a batch the review found, each of which every test passed. **Three culls the whole batch against each cascade by its own sphere before it looks at any instance**, so the sphere grows (`Sphere.union`) to hold a copy whose matrix is rewritten past it, or that copy cast nothing while its source cast nothing either; and **a batch the portal renderer quarantined** (its draw threw, so it was hidden for good) is never shown again by `around` and swaps nothing, its sources casting on their own, or shown again every pass it threw every pass, cut the shadow pass short and left its sources casting nothing. A test of the batches' repacks must keep both levels holding copies: a sweep that empties a level flips its pieces' casting, which marks the group changed whether or not the repack count is followed. The scene's matrix walk is made outright before the shadow pass and timed on a line of its own (`sceneWalk`), and the hooks round the pass on another (`shadowHooks`), so the report's `shadows` is the pass and nothing else. And `Matrix4.lookAt` boxes a number on every call (its `normalize` hands the length across a call the compiler does not build in), so the guards' light frames are written out by hand (`lookInverse`); a test that measures a frame of it holds three's own `updateMatrices` and the level group's own sweep apart, because both make numbers in node whatever the wave does. **A far grid of the terrain is found by number** (`farGridIndex.ts`, filed beside `SwgTerrain.farGrids` and taken out with it): the cached ground a frame asks off the built blocks (the movers' ground, the weather's) built the grid's string key, four numbers turned into text, on every call; a corner the index cannot file, or two corners met in one cell, sends the lookup to the string key, never to another corner's grid. `__debug.shadowPass()` reads each cascade's age, limit, draws a second and why, `shadowPass({ compare: 'hold' })` and `({ compare: 'batches' })` hold the two to pictures of the effects' scene target, and the perf switches `shadowCadence`, `shadowBatches`, `shadowBoth`, `groundShadows` and `farDepth` put each part back to compare.

## Where things stand

Working: character creation and selection, a backpack of owned items with the game's names, pictures and body slots (double-click to equip, a starting kit, saved worn and held pieces), and the two screens that hand a character clothes and weapons drawn the same way — a folding group per body slot holding a grid of the pictures the converter bakes, the game's own name under each, what is worn tinted, a find box and a strip naming the piece in full, where both were lists of up to two hundred lines of text with everything that mattered in a tooltip, planets with buildings and interiors, Jedi and Bounty Hunter combat, vehicles, ships that fly, steered by a mouse cursor that stays where it is left (a small circle in the middle aims the guns), with wings that open in flight or at the pilot's key (U), that hold the height they stop at and come down to rest on the ground, or on a hangar's floor, when the pilot flies them down or cuts the engines, and out in space set down on any face of whatever they have come to a stop over, engine glows and trails, guns from the weapon hardpoints firing the game's own bolts (the projectile and weapon tables, the bolt and hit effects, the ship's velocity carried) that hurt ships, a target with a lead reticle (Tab cycles), riders seated in each vehicle's own pose, every mount the game sold and the walkers with their own clips, pod racers with Racer-style air brakes, the game's emotes, dances and flourishes on the wheel and the number keys, the effects chain (ambient occlusion, god rays, depth of field while aiming, motion blur for the camera and for moving things, bloom, a lens flare, edge smoothing, lit lightsabers lighting the ground, walls and bodies around them, water mirroring the shore and the sky through the picture itself, heat haze over lava, behind running engines and in front of a held flame thrower, daylight beams through the doorways of the room you are in and a glow around its lamps, a colour grade per planet and hour, a vignette and a film grain) behind the Effects setting, a flashlight (F) that lights the inside of buildings, dungeons and a ship's rooms as well as the street, a room seen through a doorway and the street seen out of one, dust motes in rooms and ship interiors, weather from each area's own environment rows (rain, dust storms, snow), on a schedule every player shares, with the sky following the area under the player, wet ground with puddles and rain rings that dries after and snow that settles, effects that particles carry (light dust storms, falling leaves, Mustafar's lightning), lava drawn from the client's own lava shaders (flow, colour ramp, crust, glow), cockpit frames with first-person view and the seated pilot's eyes on it, ship interiors as a still room with its own physics (the "gravity hull" approach), boarding, walking the rooms with their own lights and sunlight, flying a multi-crew ship from its bridge, gravity boots that hold a walker to any surface out in space, so an asteroid, a station or another hull is walked on with the face under the feet as up and the view turning with the curve, hyperspace from the ship menu's System Map to a system's points, stations and launch point, within a system and between systems (flown inside a tunnel of light behind the ship; a ship with rooms is carried to another system with its crew free to walk them), with Kessel, Ord Mantell and Deep Space to fly in, a made-up system of our own to fly across at ten kilometres a second, docking at a station along the hull's own approach and exit lanes, which repairs the ship free, keeps its wings shut from the approach until the launch has flown it clear and refuses a jump or a crossing until it launches, a small ship clamped onto a bigger one and carried on its back while the carrier's pilot flies both, with the crew walking between the two ships' rooms at their own doors, each orbit lit by a star that is really in its sky (the ground sky's stray pictures gone, the stars fixed while you roll, the sun's rays and the flare reaching space), nebulae hanging in every orbit as clouds of glowing and misty sheets with a haze inside them, a shake where the table asks for one and lightning on a clock everyone shares, by the game's own rule, that drains one shield of any ship it passes (which under that rule it very seldom does) and that a switch in the settings stops hurting anything, and the engines, reactor and shield recharge of a ship inside one weakened by how thick it is, a galaxy map with every system at its canon square and the game's own shuttle routes drawn between them, from which a pilot picks a world and jumps straight to its orbit, a small server for playing together, grown in place from the relay that came before it, which holds one clock so that everyone's time of day and weather agree and knows each player by a key their browser makes for itself and never sends, writing both to a file beside it that survives a restart or a power cut, holds your group — up to eight, asked in at the game's own ninety metres, with a strip down the side of the screen and the map's Group tab (the full stop) carrying each member's world or how far off they are, a marker for each of them on the planet's map and on the space map and a chevron over one who has wandered off — and carries a line you can type to everyone on your world or to your group, and carries the fight between you as well — the bolts two people fire cross, each browser flying its own copy of a shot as a picture that hurts nothing and cutting it short where the one who fired says it landed, so the mark and the burst are in the same place on both screens; whether it hit is the shooter's screen to decide and whoever was hit is the only place health is ever taken off, which is what fills the group roster's bars; a lit blade turning one of those bolts away sends exactly one bolt back rather than two; a death is announced by the one who died and plays as a clip rather than a heap; another player's lightsaber is drawn with the same renderer the player's own uses, in the colour they chose, lighting the ground and the walls round them through the one pass that already does that work, showing both ends of a staff and one in each hand, and spinning through the air when they throw it; and damage between players is off unless the server was started with `--friendly-fire`, with the game's own duel inside it at the game's own 128 metres; and it carries **going where your friends go** — a hull another player flies can be boarded, so walking up to a friend's parked ship and pressing the same key that boards your own builds their rooms here, once, and puts you in them, their lights on and their floor under you while they fly, and the step across between two ships clamped together now works between your hull and theirs as it always did between two of your own; and whoever leads a group takes it with them when they cross to another world, up into space, down to a planet, across the galaxy map or through a jump, each member who says yes making the crossing their own way and coming out a step to one side of the leader rather than at the world's own spawn — and a mixer behind the game's own sound bank, which plays the interface's own clicks and the sound of the place — each area's day and night beds blended at the very weights the sky blends its own rows at, the interior table's bed for the room you are in, the sound objects the game's own worlds place, and the loops its particle effects name — with a Sound page in the menu: a master and a slider for each layer, and switches for headphones, the room echo, keeping the sound when the tab goes and the sabers. Every body has feet and a voice: a step lands on the frame its own animation marks it on and sounds like whatever is under it -- the water, the room's own floor, the thing stood on or the ground itself -- and creatures, droids and people call out when they turn on you, grunt as they strike, breathe while they stand and hit the ground when they fall, from their own client data. Lightsabers hum where they are drawn, light and go out, whoosh through a move on the frames Jedi Academy's own animations mark it on, ring off a bolt turned away and sound when they meet a wall, a body or the water, with Jedi Academy's files the default set and the game's own a choice in the Sound menu, and every Force power has one of the game's own sounds. Every gun fires with its own sound and every bolt lands with the sound of what it struck, a bolt passing close whines, fists and blades swing and land, grenades arm, knock and blow up, and a held flame or beam loops while the trigger is down. A sound from another room is heard through the wall and one in the room with you carries that room's echo, longer in the six rooms the game's interior table marks apart from the other 259, with a switch of its own; headphones place sounds around your head. Every machine is heard as well: a speeder idles and revs from its own client data, a ship's engine is the loop its fitted engine part carries and a hull that wears no part falls back on its own looks table, wings sound as they start to move, a ship going past whooshes, water runs under a speeder crossing it, a blow on a hull sounds for the layer it reached and a lost system powers down, a hull blowing up is heard, a target picked and a line got on it have the game's own tones, all three stages of a hyperspace jump sound, a lift sounds at the stop it puts you at, and a ship's rooms hum while you are aboard. No music plays at all, and another player's ship is silent. Ships take the game's own components per chassis slot, an astromech (sunk in its socket so the dome shows) or a flight computer, and paint where their shaders allow it -- the hull's own palette or any colour a garment takes -- edited on a page beside Spawn and kept per character; a speeder whose shaders take colours has the same page with its paint alone once the gallery is converted again. The interface is our own from end to end: none of the client's own interface art is used anywhere, and every shape, mark and colour on the screen is drawn by this game out of one table of eighteen named colours and one sheet of glyphs we drew by hand. On foot that is two bars with a grey ghost behind each that walks down after a blow so the size of it shows, a row of square cells each with its own mark, the key you have bound and what it costs, a cell for each hand with what is in it and the game's own name for it, and a crosshair that draws tighter and brighter while you aim and fills a ring round itself while a shot charges; over whatever the crosshair rests on, that thing's name and health, in place of the corner's old "nearby"; and in the top-left corner a minimap that is a plain circle, with no plate behind it, the planet's name laid over its top and, over its foot, the very two coordinates the map window shows, and a north mark on its rim. Along the bottom, at most four things you can press where you stand, each with your own key on it, gathered eight times a second rather than asked of the world every frame; the long line of every key is still there behind a setting, and now starts off. A blow on you draws a wide arc on the side it came from, one of yours that tells ticks the crosshair and ticks it red with a ring on a kill and says what it hurt in words, and small numbers over heads are behind a setting that starts off, since the game said its results in words. At a ship's controls the reticle carries three arcs -- the speed up the left with a tick for the top speed with the wings open and another for the plain top, scaled to the hull whole so a nebula or a part knocked down moves the ticks down, the guns up the right on their own refire clock with a notch where one is down, and the booster across the bottom, absent on a hull that has none -- the ship's own shields, armour, hull and one pip per fitted part stand under the middle where the body's health bar was (which stands down while they are up, since a body cannot be hurt at the controls), and a target is four corners closing onto the hull itself with its name, range, side and the three layers of the face turned toward you, or an arrow at the edge of the screen pointing the way to turn when it is behind you. Everything the game says once -- the damage from a crash, the relay connecting, a refused dock, every notice a cruise or a jump sends -- goes to a message line at the bottom left that holds it long enough to read and fades it away, repeats bumping a count rather than filling the screen; before this it went to the prompt line, which is rewritten every frame and swallowed all of it. The shapes are drawn on a plain 2D canvas over the picture that touches no WebGL context and so compiles nothing, and every switch and number the display has is an **Interface** page in the Escape menu beside Graphics and Sound -- the scale, the sharper canvas, what is shown while flying, the message line and how many lines it keeps, that long line, and four for a fight -- with a button that puts the lot back; `__debug.hud()` reports what the overlay costs and how many DOM writes a steady second took, which should be none. Every panel in the game now takes its colours from the same eighteen names.

Every creature, droid and NPC the game shipped (`mobiles`) can be stood from the spawner's NPC tab, searchable and grouped: creatures on their own models, people on parts bodies, and dressed NPCs in their own clothes and colours, armed off the rack, their lightsabers lighting what is around them. What a world has living in it on its own is what its data stands (pass 11, below). They fight, flee and follow you through doors, and indoors they walk the floors the client itself authored for each room: the converter reads every cell's own walkable mesh and the pack carries it, so a body in a building searches its way to the next corner and round the furniture instead of pressing into a wall until a stuck check gives up. The fighters (`npcs.ts`) think with the mobiles' own brain now, so a fighter and a creature reason alike, and a fighter at last has a body: the player's own character controller, so walls, steps and slopes stop it where it used to walk through them, and the player's own three-way carry, so it stands relaxed, stands ready or raises what it is holding and aims down it at you. Since then dressed people share the bodies, clothes and colour renders they have in common (`lookShare.ts`), so a full cantina fits the model memory budget; a body chasing you through a door walks the outdoor grid to the right door and through it (`doorway.ts`); and a fighter has a spawn record and is shared through the server as a creature is.

Pass 7 gave them the rest of it. **Outdoors** the converter bakes a walkability grid per world at the terrain's own two metres with its separate regions labelled (`tools/swg/navgrid.mjs`, `nav.json`/`nav.bin`, about 24 MB over the eighteen ground worlds), and a body is handed a corner it can see exactly as it already is indoors -- the steering itself did not change; the fighters then, and since pass 11 every creature and person the world stands too, on a grid baked again at 45 degrees, the slope a dynamic body can really climb. That decision was made twice: once on the old broken terrain and again on the mended ground, where the blocked-cell census barely moved and the town-to-town routes came out identical to the metre. **Aiming and swinging** came back with the `DIR ` tag fix; every body now has a per-weapon carry row and folds at the spine, on the fighters' own numbers. **Postures** are a field on the brain's answer, four of them, with what each means read out of the client's own state table rather than invented. **Facing and travel are two numbers**, so a body keeps its gun on you while its feet go elsewhere; the slide is capped at 60 degrees because no strafing clips exist in either game's set, and it is a duty cycle rather than a state or it would have deleted the kneeling. **Cover** is ours entirely -- the client has the word, the state number and 139 commands gated on it, and nothing anywhere that computes a position -- and is two rays a candidate spot at the heights the game really aims bolts at, so blocked low and clear high is a place to duck and rise from. **Five skill tiers** (`GROUND_SKILL`, the ground's `PILOT_SKILL`) govern whether a body slides, how it uses cover, its reaction, its accuracy and its firing discipline, and **tier 0 is deliberately the fighter this game had before any of it**, so the two can be put side by side with `__debug.fighters({ tier: 0 })`. And `src/world/errand.ts` will send a body to a named place four kilometres off and report honestly, refusing to call a run a result when the body spent it outside the player's collision bubble, where nothing can stop it and arriving proves nothing.

Pass 11 put the game's own people and creatures where its data puts them, at their own levels, fighting like the player and shared through the server: waves W1 to W11 and a fix round, with a performance wave, the Theed hangar landing and the debug menu between them, then a wave of doors and the dungeons (W7). **The people**: the 10,358 the emulator's screenplays place (every town read at last, with Death Watch, the squill cave, Jabba's palace and the givers) stand in the world's frame, nearest first under two caps of forty -- those who may be fought, and the furniture -- in their own rooms, moods, levels, weapons and sides, on whatever the data stands them on (Endor's tree villages, the lake huts, a bridge, a balcony, a step), held there until it is solid, a town's crowd drawn from its own lists and dress groups, its guards the side that holds the world (Imperial unless changed), its patrols walking their rounds on the server's own timing, and its seated people on chairs and at tables of ours where the data has none (`seatProps.ts`). Dressed people share what their looks have in common, so a full cantina fits the memory budget. **The wilderness** is laid with lairs, herds and camps on a seeded grid that reaches 61 to 79% of every world against a quarter before, a camp is its own tents and props and a nest its own condition and its fog or insects, and every body fights at its own creature's numbers, scaled by one Difficulty slider (the Gameplay page, `__debug.difficulty`). Bodies climb steps, walk the outdoor grid and find their way through a building's doors; people of ours fill the buildings the data leaves empty, sitting in their seats first, and travellers of ours buy tickets and fly off from every starport on the shared shuttle clock. **Fighting**: every humanoid person takes a tier from its level and kneels, goes prone, takes cover, slides with its gun kept on you, rolls (Jedi Academy's roll) and hops or Force-jumps by type, and a lightsaber carrier fights with the player's own move machine, chaining, leaping and turning bolts away by tier; anybody not fighting can be spoken to with E, and up to three asked to follow you and fight at your side. **With a server** every seeded body that may be fought is shared by saying it has been seen, kept by one browser and driven on the rest with its postures, rolls, cover, blocks and shots, a death naming who struck and a creature's blow on another player checked by the server; an admin stands what everybody sees, puts a weapon in any hand already standing and sets the day's length. The old automatic path -- the planet's one species stood in a ring round the arrival point -- is gone, switch and all. **Doors** stand in the buildings' doorways wherever the client's own portal sides name one, 2,267 over 18 packs: they slide open as anybody walks up, sound as the game's did, block the way while shut and close behind you. **The dungeons** are the archives' own world of copies, a copy per group: the Corellian corvette through its three ticket takers' own answer, crewed by the emulator's rows room by room, its seven locked rooms opened at their keypads and panels, its lifts on the lift menu and its airlock and escape pods the way off; the heroic Star Destroyer by **Go aboard** in the ship menu while docked at Deep Space's, with an Imperial crew of ours; and the Tomb of Exar Kun, Axkva Min's prison, IG-88's arena, the meatlump hideout, the Myyydril caverns, the Avatar platform and Mustafar's six dungeons at their ways in out in the worlds (ours where the archives place nothing to use, marked so), every one of them going back where the player came from. Kashyyyk's two zones named for their dungeons are named for what they hold. The performance wave measured what made the towns slow and drew less of it (only what a building's doorways show from inside it, each body only in its own rooms' passes, one skeleton a body, the client's own levels of detail, small plants stopped at the game's own distance; `__debug.perf()` measures), and the debug menu runs every helper from a window without pausing the game, its knobs in a group of their own and any pin able to run again at start.

Not done, or not yet seen by anybody: none of it has been looked at by eye in a busy town at the owner's frame rate; whether the seated people line up with the chairs of ours (their sitting idle is lent from a species rig), whether Tuskens look right with staffs and how hard a fight at the game's own levels is are the owner's to judge, the last with the slider; the travellers at Mos Eisley's starport still stop at a knot of junk props the physics closes and the grid calls open; a rifleman shoots through a door rather than walking in; a body on a threshold can flicker between rooms; a body on a platform can still be drawn off it by a fight and fall, and nobody standing up there has been seen by eye; Fort Tusken's gate is still one closed cell of the grid; some look materials (belts, hair, a Jawa's eyes) still compile in play under room light; a follower is this browser's alone and is left behind on a world change; a fighter cannot be re-armed in place (`arm` refuses it in words); and Kashyyyk and Mustafar have no wildlife or people this pass (the owner's D13). Of the doors and the dungeons, nothing has been seen by eye: not a door sliding, not a copy, its crew in their rooms or a keypad's door opening, which a driven tab walked through only in numbers (into a corvette from Tatooine's taker and out at the airlock, a keypad's room unlocked and its door open, aboard the Star Destroyer from Deep Space and back to the docked ship, into the Tomb of Exar Kun and out). The heroic dungeons stand empty, since the reference holds nobody for them; the corvette has no time limit, no reward and none of its givers' quests (the owner's D6), and its escape pods simply leave; with a server a cleared copy stays cleared for an hour for the group sent back to it; the way back is kept in one browser, so the same character elsewhere leaves by the dungeon's own way in; a group follows the leader into a copy on foot with each member's own ship kept to come back to, but a keypad's room opened by one member reaches only those already in the copy (and only through a relay restarted for the word), and a member who comes in later finds it locked; a character reloaded inside Mustafar's, Myyydril's or the Avatar platform's copies is not given its way out back, since those copies stand in ordinary worlds that no reload looks for a copy in; Mustafar's, Myyydril's, the meatlump hideout's and the Avatar platform's ways in have not been walked through in a browser; and the Morag cave and the small lair cave are interiors no world places and are not stood. The one program a corvette built in play -- two, really, a blaster bolt's core and glow under the room's lights when its crew fired a colour of their own -- is gone with the bolts' keys fixed, and a reload straight into a crewed room with the crew shooting built nothing.

**Story foundations** (pass 12, waves 1 to 9) built everything a story of the owner's own needs except the story, which lives in `story-private/` and is the owner's to write; every committed piece of content is the test set, labelled TEST. Each character keeps a **story book** that survives a reload and settles with a server the way the character does, alone in the browser and on the server with one, archived when a copy replaces it. **Waypoints**: up to a hundred per character, set from the map or the console, standing in the world as marks of our own with the map's Waypoints tab (Y) and a minimap that fades in each city's name. **Quests** are data (`src/story/`, read as JSONC, with a checker, `npm run story:check`, that names every broken link, every signal nothing raises and every step that can be reached and never end): they run alone in the browser and on the server with one, which checks arrivals, rooms, uses and kills against what it knows and is the only one that pays, every reward exactly once; a tracker on the right, the message line and quest waypoints on screen and both maps say each step. **Conversations** are data trees played in a window of ours with the camera cutting between speaker and listener and a gesture on every line from the game's own emotes, and the story's named people are stood where it places them; the game's own people greet in the client's reaction lines, kindly, plainly or coldly by standing, and the town heralds speak the emulator's trees folded into ours, in the client's own words, and mark the places they name. **Documents** are paper drawn by us (fields, tables, stamps, signatures, redactions, versions by the branch taken), each frozen the first time it is read; **the journal** (J) keeps only what the character witnessed, word for word, and only grows; **the ISB's file** grows behind the player's back and cools people toward them before they can read a word of it. And the last wave gave the story its weight: **Standing and Trust** on the Rebellion, the Empire and freelance work, with ranks on ladders the owner writes that are held, not worked out, and so can be lost (demoted, suspended, burned), Standing shown as a bar with no numbers and Trust only in words on the journal's Standing tab, rank changes said in words on the message line and Trust never; **named people** with their own Standing and Trust toward the character, who can refuse to speak, be vouched for and be killed for good, the People tab showing only what the character knows of them; **consequences** -- a fine taken from the purse with the rest owed as a debt, a job closed, a contact refused, a track burned; and **one companion**, who follows, fights at the player's side, goes down rather than dying, gets up when E is held beside them or by themselves when the fight is over, waits where told, and dies only when the story kills them.

Not done of the story, or not yet seen by anybody: none of waves 6 to 9 has been looked at by eye -- the Standing and People tabs, a rank changing on the message line, the companion following, lying down and getting up, the conversation window's cuts and gestures -- since the tab they were written in draws no frames. There is no story yet, only the test set, and no ladder but the TEST rungs; a rung's `pays` and `brief` are read and do nothing, so a rank pays no more and a conversation briefs no deeper for it. A quest's experience is recorded in the book and paid as a bonus into the skills used while it ran, by whoever holds the character's progress (this browser, until wave 3 of Progression I; a server that holds the story pays none yet). A debt is recorded and nothing collects it but the story's own `debt()`. The companion is this browser's alone (nobody else sees them, the server takes the browser's word for down and up, and their kills are credited to nobody), rides undrawn in anything the player rides, and is stood beside the player rather than walking in through the door after them. Holding E beside them to get them up has been driven in node and never pressed in a browser. A mortal companion waiting where they were left goes down rather than dying, as beside the player, but nobody gets them up there until they are spoken to, which a body lying in a fight's state refuses, so they lie until the player walks out of the cast's reach and back. The vocabulary's later pass is refused rather than built: the step types `collect`, `give`, `carry`, `killLoot`, `pay`, `encounter`, `spawn`, `waves`, `escort`, `perform`, `craft` and `space`, and the actions `impound`, `licence`, `district`, `take` and `misfile`. A conversation's `cues` are kept and not played, a job's `client` and `verb` are stored for job boards that do not exist, and there is no recruiter but the story. Of the emulator's conversation trees only the heralds' are adopted (eight heralds stood on four worlds, seven of whom speak); every other tree is kept, unplayed, until it is. The two-browser path -- a server running a book, paying, freezing documents and keeping everybody's words -- has been driven in node against a real relay and never by two people.

NPC starships fly the garage's own hulls with their tier chassis's fits, in the game's formations (arrow, claw, wall): patrols keep to a space zone's stations, hyperspace points and arrival lane (woken within 3 km, put away past 6 km unless fighting, back 150 s after one is wiped out), and the NPC tab's starship groups stand one of a family at a tier, or a patrol of three, 700 m ahead. They dogfight by side (Imperial, Rebel, Black Sun, pirates: `factions.ts`), attack the player's ship on sight or leave it alone by side, turn on whoever hurt them (the player on foot too, with a quarter of the damage), taunt in the game's own lines, hold still while a panel is open, fly with their wings shut and open them the moment they take a target (shutting them three seconds after the last one is lost, which is ours: the game shut them at once), and fight through shields, armour, components and chassis with the game's own hit, target and explosion effects; the flight display colours the target's bracket by side and shows the flown ship's shields, armour, hull and a pip per part, and a part knocked down says so in words on the message line. Every stat, the tier pick, the pilots (a hand on the stick that caps and slows the turn by tier, and their aim) and the rosters are invented and kept in `shipStats.ts`, `loadout.ts`, `npcBrain.ts` and `roster.ts`. Not done: missiles, countermeasures and turrets (guns only), the hit effects' light and camera shake, the 105 tiered types with no garage hull, NPC ships across the relay (each player sees their own).

**Materials and ground** (pass 14; its converter half and its one conversion are on main, its runtime comes wave by wave). Every converted model's normal map is decoded the client's way, by the slot it sits in, and its shine read off its own pixel program and MATL (material format 6); the ground's families lie on the client's 8 m pattern with each pole's alternate and colour worked out, and the terrain packs carry every alternate, the client's border masks, its cloud tile and its colour ramps (terrain shaders version 4). **The ground is drawn the client's way** from them: its mesh, heightfield and far tiles on the client's own height points (the twelve odd-centred worlds' ground stood a metre off them), every 4 m tile its corners' families through the client's painted masks, each in the alternate its pole chose, the pictures along the terrain's own x, and the colour map's tint over all of it, normalised to each world's own brightness with its hue kept -- one program for every planet, so a travel builds none, at about two and a half times the ground's old GPU memory (Naboo 20 to 48.6 MB, Mustafar 53.9). The client's cloud shadows are built into it and switched off until the owner has looked (`__debug.ground({ clouds: true })`). Seen only in a driven browser: whether the tint is the dirt pass the owner remembers (Dantooine's flower fields come out much pinker under it), which family should be on top at a border (`order`), which way round the pictures belong (`mirror`), whether the client's ground shone `flat`, and whether the clouds stay are the owner's to judge (`localStorage['swg.ground.tiles'] = '0'` and `__debug.ground({ colour: 'off' })` are the old look). The client's own highlight is drawn in place of three's GGX on every model converted at format 6: broad and soft at each shader's own colour and power, hair, fur and the two-tone cloth in the client's band, glass held under a white surface by a cap of ours, leaves and every shader whose program has no specular with none, and a wet surface keeping three's glint and a snowed one three's matt; and a surface whose shader has no reflection cube takes no reflection and no sky-coloured fill. None of it has been seen by eye (`__debug.spec({ use: 'three' })` and `__debug.reflections({ plain: 1 })` are the old look, live). The leaf cards are drawn the client's way round -- from their front, casting from both sides, never glass (Naboo's and Rori's canopies cast shadows at last), each face of a two-sided card lit on its own side, a far blended crown kept full -- and lit by the client's own ramp, fills and emissive, carried through three's tone curve to the client's bytes, with no tree shading its own leaves; every tree and shrub sways in the weather's wind from its own canopy base, trunks still below the crown, its shadow swaying with it, at no measured frame cost and with nothing compiled in play. They run on today's packs (each card's class guessed from its shader's name), and the flora wants converting once more (`npm run swg -- flora '@SWG' all assets-private --retail-only`, which `status` asks for) for the client's own classes, the leaf pictures bled so their edges do not darken with distance, and the table of every world's card shaders that rules a placed tree whatever world was visited first. Seen only in a driven browser, in numbers and in pictures, never by the owner: whether the leaves read brighter and less flat than before, whether the sway is too much or too little in a storm, and whether any leaf can be seen off its branch up close are the owner's to judge (`__debug.cards({ rule: 'three' })`, `__debug.cardLight({ on: false })` and `__debug.sway({ on: false })` are the old look, live). **The ground has its own grass, flowers and small plants now, and the far hills their tree cards**: the terrain's radial flora, planted where the client's own affectors plant it (roads and rivers now clear every kind of flora, as the client's did), one tuft a near tile (a metre across on most worlds) out to twenty to thirty-two metres, each in its own picture, size and turn, tinted by the colour map, lit by the leaf cards' client sum and swaying in the trees' wind, and beyond where our own trees stop the file's far cards to the far tiling's end -- one program for every world, built once behind the first loading screen, at about a twentieth of a millisecond a frame. They want the light `radial` pass (seconds; `status` asks for it), which has been run here. Seen only in a driven browser, in numbers and in pictures: whether the tufts read as a grid (six worlds' tiling has no wander of its own, Dantooine, Endor, Rori and Yavin 4 among them, and there ours is three tenths of a tile), whether Dantooine's dry grass is as dark as it comes out, whether the far cards are too dark or too few, and whether grass under a jungle canopy stands too bright against the shaded floor are the owner's to judge (`__debug.grass({ on: false })`, `__debug.farCards({ on: false })`). **The grass now takes the sun's shadow and can cast its own, and every flora distance and shadow is a setting** (wave 8): grass in a tree's shadow is darkened as a leaf in shadow is (on), the grass near you casts (off, as the original's never did), and the Graphics page's Flora section holds the grass's and the far cards' distances, the grass's density, the small plants' and the trees' distances (a shorter Tree distance hands the ground past it to the far cards, lifted onto it, with no bare ring between), and which of trees, small plants, grass and far cards cast, all live and none building a program in play, each also a console knob that moves the same setting and never saves it; the grass takes the ground's own tint by default, as the owner asked; and the Shadows section's Smallest shadow caster, which reached almost nothing before, now governs placed buildings and props, what players put down included. Seen only in a driven browser: whether casting grass is worth turning on (its shadows swivel with the eye, and a dense field may read as a dark carpet), whether shaded grass matches the shaded ground beside it at Naboo's noon, and how dear small plants casting is on the owner's machine are the owner's to judge (`__debug.floraShadows()`). Tatooine's manifest named the old `pack` command's categories, five of whose models were from before format 6 and drawn by nothing; the owner's install has had its snapshot again and is clean, and on any install converted before, `status` asks for the snapshot that drops them (`npm run swg -- snapshot '@SWG' tatooine assets-private/tatooine --center=auto --radius=all --retail-only`, with the dev server stopped). **And what is worn, held, flown and ridden, and the creatures, reflect the same sky the world does**, where their shader has a cube (their chrome drew dark before: no model but a planet's own had a reflection at all), built behind whatever prepares them with nothing compiled in play, while the wardrobe's doll, the select screen, the creator and the ship edit page give those same materials a studio of their own around their own draws, where a hull's plain parts now take none of it, as they take none of the sky. Seen only in a driven browser, in numbers and in pictures: whether the studio's half strength is too bright on the select screen's armour, and whether chrome in the world's shade now reads too light, are the owner's to judge (`__debug.reflections({ models: false })` is the old look, live, and `localStorage['swg.reflections.models'] = '0'` keeps them out at load).

**Progression** (Progression I, on `claude/progression1`, waves 1 to 3 of 4). Built: the rules in `src/progress/` -- one record a character, kept in this browser beside the story book and counted in the character's mark as the story is, changed only by a closed list of whole, clamped changes; one table of every stat the owner's document names (Table A); the four core skills of the seven, with a curve and a milestone every 25; Demolitions as data; and the challenge level against the game's own 90-level table (converted by `customization`) -- and every read site those stats touch reads its kept stat, today's number until something is earned, to the bit. **The character now learns by doing, in this browser alone**: fights are noticed (opened by the first blow given or taken, a bolt's included, closed won, lost or broken, every member looked at four times a second so a burn's or a follower's kill closes it too) and a won one pays every skill the player's own blows used, against the challenge table, less for the same creature again and more for a new one or a new planet, a tenth for a close loss, Fortitude for a fight that took half the health in blows (never a fall, the lava, drowning, a crash or the player's own blast), doubled after four hours away (rested); a landing roll that saves some of a fall pays Acrobatics on its own, capped a window; a quest's experience is a bonus into the skills used while it ran. The core skills steady things as they rise -- Marksmanship 25's steady aim, Acrobatics' roll dodge and its 8 m free roll, Fortitude 75's last stand -- and Demolitions is learnt (and given back for credits) at the emulator's own commando trainers through E and Train, which opens the Skills tab's new Progress section: the skills and milestones, Skill Points and challenge level, the tree and its costs, and where the nearest trainer stands; every payout and milestone is a line on the message line, and C is the developer's class switch. `__debug.progress` grants, gives XP, stands a trainer in and learns through the real rule; `__debug.encounter` shows the fight open and the last eight closed with what each paid. **With a server (wave 3) the server holds it**: a relay restarted for this wave hails `progress: 1`, settles each character's record the way the story book settles (what was made alone carried up whole on first contact), works every payout out itself from the browser's claims by the same rules, takes a shared body's death only on its keeper's own word and parks a claim that comes ahead of it, keeps the XP buckets by character for its whole life, takes a node's fee and gives it back in one step, and pays a quest's bonus from its own story host (owed, and paid once, when the character's progress has not settled yet); the browser only claims and shows, and against an old relay or a server whose hail says nothing of progress it holds the record itself exactly as before. `__debug.progress()` says the line's state and `{ sync: true }` asks again; the admin's grant, XP and reset go to the server. Not built: Demolitions' own mechanics -- the cook, the arc, sticky grenades, two at a time, a launcher lock, swapped warheads, more than one det pack (wave 4). Nothing of it has been seen by eye: a driven tab and a node relay answered in numbers only, and how much a fight pays, how fast the skills rise and whether a trainer's Train reads right are the owner's to judge.

**The grappling hook** (Grapple, waves 1 to 4; the fourth waits for the relay to be restarted). Built: a hook every character carries for now (`GRAPPLE_TUNE.everyone`), fired with Mouse 4 at whatever a small diamond by the crosshair marks -- anything that stands still between 1.5 and 100 m, and from wave 3 a loose crate or ball and a vehicle's hull, still or moving, never a window, the water, a flow, a door, a rideable creature or anything else that moves -- and aimed so the dart lands on the mark whatever the shooter's own speed. The rope bends round ledges, corners, trunks and thin posts as the body swings past them and comes off them in order as it swings back, fouls past 32 bends (with 96 more for the ground's own creases), and comes loose if what it holds is taken away. On the ground the body walks as ever and the rope stops it only at its length; in the air it swings at the game's full gravity, the move keys sway it, Shift and X reel in and out, Space boosts off the line and F hauls straight in, over a roof's lip and up onto it, or onto a walkable anchor to stand there; and every way the game moves a player (boarding, a lift, the doorless way in, a travel, the select screen, a hand filling, dying, being stood somewhere new, walking into or out of a building, noclip, mounting, drifting on foot in space) lets go first. Under a high anchor, walking out past the rope's length lifts the body off the ground into a hang, and with the line in a Jedi's Force jump is cut to a tap's height the frame the feet leave the ground (the design's own rule, §3.1, held by test 18; a question for the owner). In water the rope holds: it fires at the surface and under it, a reel-in past 45 degrees lifts a swimmer out up the rope rather than flinging them at the bank, the haul gets a swimmer out in a second or two, and a swing low through shallow water dies away. Its five keys are on the Controls page; with the pointer locked, Ctrl and a move key no longer reach the browser, and the side buttons' own back and forward are guarded against, which nobody has yet pressed on a real tab. **Wave 2 gave it its danger, its look and its sound.** A swing into a wall faster than 16.8 m/s hurts by the design's table at full gravity (20 m from level 35, from 60 degrees 9, at most 60, the whole health past 50 m/s, which last stand may still leave a point of), measured against what was struck and only off a face steeper than the climb, and so does flying into one in the fast flight after letting go at speed; hanging against a wall, the haul's own push, a catch and a landing cost nothing. Past 23.2 m/s a slam staggers for half a second (no sway, reel, haul or boost), raises the red flash and the damage arc toward the wall, shakes the view through the camera's own register and darkens the screen's edges through the player's own vignette (never drawn with Effects or the vignette off); the rope holds through it. A slack rope's catch jolts the view a moment, the left arm points up the rope at its first point (held to 45 degrees behind straight up), the drawn body leans into the swing by 0.7 of the rope's angle and eases upright over 0.3 s when let go or afloat (the turn and its origin go to other players while it leans and for half a second after it is level, which needs no relay restart; a friend sees it ease back with no snap, and facing past a quarter turn sees it unmirrored only on this build), the heading follows the view on the rope, the camera pulls back with speed to 1.3 and comes back to exactly 1 within 1.5 s of the feet finding ground, and a thin arc round the crosshair says how much of the 100 m of rope is out, amber past 90 per cent. It fires with the bank's launcher clank, bites with three clicks played at the hand, ratchets only while the reel is held (at most twelve a second), rumbles while F hauls, whooshes as it boosts off (Jedi Academy's melee whoosh, or the game's own unarmed swing without that pack) and strikes a wall with a body's fall; the speed wind is built and off. **One change reaches a player who never fires**: the swim holds the fall's apex on every swimming frame (`jka.holdApex`), so a fall into deep water no longer hurts on the shore. **Wave 3's first part gave it the props and the progression rows.** A dart that strikes one of the loose crates or balls round each world's arrival (the developer's own, stood only with `localStorage['swg.scratch'] = '1'`, so a player meets none) hooks it when the mass rule says the rope moves the thing (20 kg against the tether's 40); F then yanks it in at the grip's own 14 m/s until it is within 1.2 m (about a second from 15 m) and it drops at the feet with the line let go (from past 21 m the yank's 1.5 s runs out first and it drops 2 to 3 m short, where it is, never thrown on), and with no F the line is a tether that drags it along the ground once walked out past its length, the body walking exactly as it would with nothing on the line, Shift walking, X crouching and a crouch at a landing rolling as they do with no hook out; a crate the pool puts back on its stand, one dragged within 5 m of where the pool would, and one whose straight line stays blocked a quarter of a second each let go, and one made too heavy through the knob is refused with "Too heavy to pull.". The bar says let go and yank it. The hook's range, reel speed, haul speed and tether strength now go through the progression's stat table at the tune's own numbers (Athletics will raise them when it is built), and Acrobatics multiplies the sway, so at 100 a swing builds a quarter faster (6.4 m/s in 2 s against 5.1). **Wave 3's second part put it on the vehicles this browser simulates**: the rope no longer passes through a hull, the diamond turns amber on one, the dart meets a moving bike where the diamond said (aimed against its speed), and the rope stays on whatever it bit while it moves, bending round its edges as round a pillar's. A hull pulls the player along, and the player pulls on it by the two masses (a bike takes a fifth of a catch, a pod a twelfth) wherever its drive lets it be pulled -- never a ship in flight, a landed or held hull or one in a jump -- and its own hit tests are told, so no rope ever reads as a crash; a speeder parked at rest with nobody on it holds against a hanging player as though braked (it slides 3 to 4.5 m as the rope first catches and then holds), and with nobody driving it, it can be set going from the console (`grapple({ drive })`) to try a tow alone. Hooked on from beside a passing pod the rope slips rather than snapping the player about (2 to 5 m paid out at 40 m/s, about 60 at 100, and past 150 "The line ran out."), the body is dragged behind skidding on its own friction or skimming over water, never below the terrain, and Shift or F draws it up to hang just off the hull's tail, which neither stops nor hurts the hull; a hull flown across a hanging rope slips its turn instead of slinging the player at hundreds of metres a second; a body that comes to rest on a moving hull's top rides it hanging, its fall held off by the hull rather than piling up under it; hauled to the top of a hull standing still the body goes over its edge and stands on it -- shown only on box hulls in node: on the real open-topped landspeeder in a driven tab the haul from the ground pressed the body into its side and stalled 2.3 m short, and from above the dart sat in a recess whose bend 5 cm from the anchor kept the arrival from ever letting go. The anchor's hull going (gone, destroyed, a jump, teleported, refitted, its wing starting to swing) lets go in words, and a bend on such a hull stays where it was. A rideable creature is refused by the dart and passed by the rope. **Wave 3's third part put it on another player's vehicle**: the dart bites the box their game keeps round the vehicle a friend rides (never a mount, nor a hull clamped onto another), the rope bends round it and holds while they ride, and it tows you behind them, riding a smooth pose of its own dead-reckoned from their states rather than their picture, which moves in jerks ten times a second, so your end of the rope breathes against their drawn vehicle by a metre or two at speed. Nothing is put on their vehicle, so they feel nothing (and from wave 4 see the rope on it). Their getting off, leaving or being hidden lets go in words, a state landing 10 m from where the pose expected it lets go as a teleport, and a slam that came of their vehicle hurts only where they could hurt you (the server's friendly fire, or a duel). While the rope rides their vehicle the body is held off it as off a hull of this browser's, so reeling or hauling up to it, or its stopping dead, never wraps the rope round it from inside. In two driven tabs on one machine against a relay of this build, a rope on a friend's speeder towed the body 205 m at about 17 m/s and let go in words when they got off; that smoke ran before the pose stopped riding the relay's velocity raw (it stood off their picture by up to 0.10 m per m/s, where the design asks under 0.06), and has not been run again since: in node, with states timed as the game times them (sent from the sender's frames, heard 50 ms on with 5 ms of jitter), a tow at 40 m/s now holds within 1 m/s of the vehicle at 144, 60 and 30 fps and the pose stands within 0.02 m per m/s of the picture, and a vehicle that stops dead leaves the rope's pose running on about 9 m past it before it eases back. **Wave 4 put it on the wire and in the backpack.** On a relay restarted for it, every other player sees your dart fly out and stop where it strikes and your rope come from your left hand, bend where yours bends and sag as yours sags, with your arm up the line and your fire, bite and let-go heard at your hand; a rope on a third player's vehicle sits on that vehicle on every screen, the rider's own included, because its points there go over in the vehicle's own frame; and other players' thrown sabers are seen in flight at last, which the browser had always dropped as it read their states. A relay that has not been restarted passes none of it, and the game is as it was. The rig is an owned thing, **Grapple rig**, carried (in no slot and no hand) under Worn and held, with a mark of our own drawn for it, given to every character once -- by the record's own mark with no server or one whose ledger keeps no gear, which then neither takes it nor is handed it (with a server saved, only once that server has said so or failed to answer), and by the server's first list of a session where the hail says `gear: 1` -- and traded and destroyed like anything owned; owning it changes nothing until `__debug.grapple({ everyone: false })` makes firing ask for it ("You carry no grapple rig."). Not built yet: nothing sells or makes a rig, and a crate on another player's line is drawn as their dart coming home rather than as the crate's rope.
