It has been driven in node at 144, 60 and 30 fps against real Rapier, the game's own movement and the game's own `Vehicle` class, and in a driven tab in numbers; none of it has been seen, felt or heard: how hard a slam should hurt, whether the shake and the daze are too much or too little, whether the lean and the arm look right, whether the camera's pull helps, and whether each of the bank's sounds is the right one are the owner's to judge, as are how long a swing lasts, whether full gravity is too quick and whether the diamond is too much on screen, of wave 3's first part whether a yank's pace, a crate dropping at the feet and one dragged behind read right, and whether Acrobatics' quarter is felt, and of its second whether a tow's snatch, its skid and its skim feel right, whether a parked speeder's brake is too strong or too weak, and whether the stutter of a fast vehicle against a towed player at 144 fps (the game's own, every fast vehicle's) is acceptable, and of its third whether a tow behind a friend's speeder is smooth between two real machines and whether the rope's end breathing against their drawn speeder reads right, and of the fourth whether another player's rope and dart read right from across a street, whether their let-go's lowered clicks are the right sound, and whether the rig's mark reads in the backpack.

**Racer handling** (on `claude/racer`, waves 1, 2, 2b, 3, 4a and 4b of its waves). Built: every pod, and every bike once U puts it in racing mode, is driven by a race drive of ours instead of the springs -- a speed curve the throttle climbs to the owner's ceiling (600 km/h for a pod, 350 for a bike, both halved by the owner after his first ride; a ceiling moved up is held under 1,368 by the physics engine's own cut), a boost on Shift, charged at speed, that heats the engines, an air brake on S, a slide on Space, a lean on Z and V, a turn that eases toward the stick and never past the hull's maximum turn rate, travel that lags the nose so a pod drifts wide at speed and grips as it slows, a slope's push downhill, a height set over the ground and the ground half a second ahead, held loosely since the owner found the first ride's hold a magnet (wave 2b: the ground never pulls the hull down harder than gravity, so a crest is a jump that lands where a thrown thing would, a dip is carried over on momentum, rolling ground is ridden without bobbing and a long descent close, and `race({ hold: 'firm' })` puts the magnet back), glided over pits narrower than half a second of travel (the metres a quarter second covered before the halving), walls and steep terrain faces scored as a scrape or a crash by how hard and how square they are met, and a hull that wears to a quarter and is destroyed only by a crash. The gallery's vehicles are sorted by a table of ours (every landspeeder, skiff and transport races as a bike; the Koro-2 and the Organa stay hover cars, and race only spawned as a bike), a racing vehicle collides as a box while it races, the camera follows a pod or a racing bike in a race view of its own steered by a heading target the mouse moves, with the view widening and the camera pulled out with speed, and the flight display shows the speed, the ceiling, the boost and the heat, the mode and the slide. **Wave 2 gave the engines parts**: a raced pod has two engines of three parts and a raced bike one; a scrape damages a part on the struck side (and costs the hull a third of what it did), a hard strike from below any part, and running the boost dry sets one part on fire, which burns it out in about eight seconds; badly damaged parts on one side pull the pod toward it, a part out lowers the top (the speed easing down to it) and, like a fire, refuses the boost, and holding R while racing with nothing in hand mends the worst part, fire first, with the top held to six tenths of itself meanwhile. The display's condition block names each part, red while it burns, and the heat arc carries a notch for each part out. Each pod's and bike's glows stand on its own engine points and dim with their side's thrust, the ships pack's own smoke and fire stand at a side as its worst part gets worse, and the game's own damaged-engine sounds play -- the damaged thruster set for the parts' mean damage, its fire over the engine, a spark as a part crosses one of the client data's bands, and at the last only the fire. The upgrade levels are kept with the character in each vehicle's fit (`race({ upgrades })` is still the only way to set them) and never sent to anybody. **Sebulba's pod**, the one pod the archives hold whole that no template names, is a gallery exhibit of ours on Gasgano's clips, seat and sounds, with engine points of ours; converted into a scratch folder it is the one new model of the run and its 51 others are byte for byte the install's, but the install's own run is owed (`npm run swg -- gallery '@SWG' assets-private --only=vehicles --retail-only`, with the dev server stopped; `status` asks for it). **Wave 3 gave it tracks**: a world's tracks are a file that ships with the build (`public/tracks/<world>.json`, one committed, Tatooine's `TEST: Dune Loop`, a three-lap circuit of 24 gates and about 6.3 km on open desert), and `track({ start })` stands what you ride at a track's grid behind a short loading screen that also compiles what stands along it, counts down three seconds (W pressed within three tenths of go, and not held before, earns a start boost), times each gate as the hull's middle passes through its window on the physics' own steps, so a run stands still behind a panel and a gate is timed alike at every frame rate, outlines the next gate and the one after (an arrow at the screen's edge when the next is out of sight), says a gate flown the wrong way, shows a race block of the lap, the time, each split against the best lap there, the last lap and the best, puts a crashed pod back after two and a half seconds at the last gate crossed as a fresh spawn of the kind it was raced as, and keeps the best lap and total with the character by world, track and kind, with the levels and splits they were set at. A saved track's corridor keeps the procedural plants off at the next arrival, and `track({ check })` names whatever stands in it. On the dev server a track is laid by riding it and pressing Numpad0 at each gate, edited from the debug menu's Gates view with `track` picked (go to a gate, move it to where you stand, put one in after it, nudge it, resize it, take it out; the track's name, kind, laps, who races it and its corridor as fields) and saved into the world's file; a track reaches players once its file is committed and merged. The countdown, the start window and boost, the put-back's wait, the default gate sizes and the editor's nudge are `track`'s sliders. Not built of it: no ghost of a best lap and no rivals, and the lap a standing start runs at 20 fps comes out 16 ms quicker than at 60 because the drive holds a frame's end speed over its three steps (the drive's to mend). **Wave 4a put it on the wire.** Through a relay restarted for it (its greeting says `veh: 2` and `race: 1`), another player's vehicle -- a pod, a speeder, a ship, a mount -- is drawn where their own game says it is: each state carries its velocity, its turn and the moment its place was true on the shared clock, and the picture is carried along by them and eased onto that place, so a full pod is no longer drawn 20 to 40 m behind itself; the rider sits on it there, a grappling rope on it rides the very same step, and with no clock shared or against an older relay it glides as before (`reckon({ tune: { on: false } })` does the same live). Whoever leads a group can call a race on a track of the world (`/race test_dune_loop` in the chat, the Gates view's Race the group, or `raceTogether({ start })`): every member is offered it on the message line, joins with `/race`, is stood at a slot of their own behind the grid, two abreast, and counts down to one moment on the shared clock; each times its own run, says its laps and finish to the group, every member's race block shows the standings and the message line each lap, finish and drop-out, and a best set there is kept as any run's. Another player's ride drawn within 20 m of a raced hull nudges its turn away from it rather than colliding (the Racing together dials). Measuring the rope on a reckoned picture found the rope's own fan search boxing about a kilobyte a frame when hung at a peer's face; it allocates nothing now. **Wave 4b put pods together from parts.** Any of the seven skinned pods (Anakin's, Gasgano's, the light-bending one, Mawhonical's, Balta's, the IPG and Sebulba's) can be spawned on any other's engines, 42 pairs, none refused: the garage panel has an engines choice beside each skinned pod's spawn button (its own, or another's by the garage's name for it, kept in this browser), and `__debug.spawn('pod_racer_one', undefined, { engines: 'podracer_anakin' })` does the same. It is built when it is spawned, from the two converted pods, with no conversion and no owned parts: the first pod's cockpit, seat, clips (a Gasgano, light-bending, Mawhonical or Sebulba cockpit plays its idle and run, and the other pod's engines ride them) and rider pose, the second's engines, glows, engine parts, smoke and fire and engine sound, on the shared pod box, and spawned as a bike it races as one; the materials are the two pods' own, so one of two pods already drawn this session builds no program. Other players see it built the same way from its id, which names both pods and goes through any relay as it stands (no restart); a browser built before draws nothing for it. `__debug.pods()` is the console. Every number is ours and lives through `__debug.race` and `__debug.engines`, and the numbers of feel among them are sliders in the debug menu when `race` or `engines` is picked (`src/vehicles/raceDials.ts`), with the console line that keeps them; `localStorage['swg.race'] = '0'` and a reload put every vehicle back exactly as it was, and `__debug.race({ on: false })` does it live but for a vehicle already standing, which keeps the kind it was spawned as until it is spawned again. Not built: nothing buys an upgrade, no hybrid is a thing anybody owns (the parts economy is undecided) and none of the pods takes a colour, nothing races beside the player but a group (no rivals, no ghost of a best lap), the ground's own grip and speed are not read, the nose trim from the view is built and off, damage is gone when a vehicle is spawned again, no other player hears a pod's fire or sees its engines' smoke, since the wire carries its pose and motion and nothing of its parts, and a group race is never judged by anybody but each member's own browser (a member whose copy of the track file differs is told and sits it out; one on an older build never hears the call). None of it has been seen by eye at the owner's frame rate: how a pod feels, whether the slide is too much, whether the race view pumps in a corridor of trunks, whether the ceiling is too fast for the ground, how fast parts wear and whether the pull fights the hand, whether the smoke and fire read at their sides, and where Sebulba's pilot sits (borrowed from Gasgano's pod, perhaps half a metre low; `__debug.seat`) are the owner's to judge, and of wave 2b whether the halved speeds feel right and which crests now throw a pod too high (`race({ hold: 'firm' })` and `race({ hold: 'loose' })` flip between the two holds live, and `race({ tune: { looseFall, looseGrip } })` grips harder), and of wave 3 whether the gates' outlines read at speed, how long the countdown and the put-back's wait should be, whether the start boost is worth chasing, and the TEST course itself, and of wave 4a whether a friend's pod drawn where it is reads smooth between two real machines (and how quickly `ease` should take a turn up), whether the nudge is too strong or too weak, and a group race with real people, and of wave 4b how each hybrid looks (where one pod's cables end against another's cockpit, the cockpit on its idle hanging well under engines that clips made for other engines leave high, and the light-bending pod's own colours as either half) and whether the seats sit right (by the cockpit pod's own player point, as alone). Measured in node, the loose hold flies a full pod over hilly ground for about a quarter of the way and up to 43 m over its line, against the firm hold's sixth and 22 m; a steep 20 m face it crashes against only from 80 degrees where the firm hold crashed from 60 (at 60 it may fly the face's top instead), and a deep crevasse it carries further (100 m) before it crashes (160 m) than the firm hold (crashed at 120). A driven tab rode both holds on Tatooine's dunes and a crevasse, in numbers only. A headless tab of our own ran the TEST course in numbers only: a full pod through gate 0 in 0.77 s off the grid with the start boost, laps of 48.9, 51.5 (a crash and its put-back at gate 9) and 48.0 s, 2:28.4 in all, the clock and the hull held still with the map open, no program built after the start's curtain; a Koro-2 raced as a bike lapped in 1:51.8, and both bests read back after a reload. That bike's first lap built 17 programs in play, of the people and creatures stood beside the course as it passed (its plain top, 57.8 m/s, sits under the world's `livingAbove`, 60): a run now holds the living whatever its speed, and the same lap ridden again built none. Wave 4a's smoke ran two headless tabs of our own against a relay of this build, in numbers only: one pod driven straight at about 91 m/s, the other tab's picture of it stood within 0.94 m of where it was on average (0.010 m per m/s; worst 2.3 m) where glided it stood 14.4 m behind (0.151 per m/s), measured frame by frame against the driver's own log on the shared clock -- with the first cut's stamp, which a review then found left out the sender's own frame step (every picture its speed over the sender's frame rate behind, which a log of the sender's own places stamped the same way cannot show); mended in node and not smoked again since; when the driving tab's own game froze for a second the picture ran on half a second past it and came back (up to 43 m at that speed, `most`'s price); the two formed a group, the leader called the TEST course, the other joined (the console's `join`, the one call `/race` makes), both stood 8.9 m apart on the grid and went at one instant, a page-side hand of ours steered each through all 72 gates, and both race blocks ended with the same standings, `1 Smoke 5:13.3, 2 Bo 5:18.6`, each best kept; the leader's own clock read 5 s behind the other's at the end because its game froze for a second several times and a run's clock is its own physics' steps. Wave 4b's smoke ran headless tabs of our own, in numbers and three screenshots for the owner (`A:\swg3js\racer\proto\w4b\`, never committed): Gasgano's cockpit on Anakin's engines, Sebulba's on the IPG's and Anakin's on Mawhonical's each spawned, mounted and ridden, seated by the cockpit pod's own player point on its `body` joint, glowing on the engine pod's own rear points and sounding the engine pod's own client data (the IPG's for the IPG's engines, Mawhonical's for Mawhonical's, Gasgano's for Anakin's, as the mount tables lend it); a hybrid of two pods spawned and drawn before built no program (`shaders({ since: true })` nought); the panel's choice was saved and spawned the hybrid it named; and through a relay of this build the other tab drew the rider's hybrid from its id, reckoned, within 0.05 m of the place carried at 80 m/s, and let its picture go when the rider got off.

**Real-world ground** (the owner's, for the map maker to come). A planet may lay its ground from a real elevation raster rather than a converted terrain (`PlanetDef.dem`, `src/world/demTerrain.ts`; the raster converted by `tools/demconverter/dem2swg.py` into `public/dem/<name>/base16.raw` and `base.json`), borrow a converted pack's sky (`borrowSky`) and paint itself with a converted pack's ground families by rules of elevation, slope and hollow (`ground`, `src/world/demShader.ts`, worked out live with `__debug.groundRules`, `slopes` and `families`). Four such worlds stand in the planet list, kept off the galaxy map: Monument and Helensar with their rasters committed (about 20 MB, in every release), Granyon (the Grand Canyon, painted from Lok's families) and Rainier (Seattle) without theirs, which stayed on the owner's other machine and will be made again smaller, so those two stand on the planet's own noise until then. Every house deed may stand on such a world, since none of the server's zones names it (`wrongWorld`). Not built: placing props and buildings by hand on them beyond the deeds, painting flora and textures, and editing the ground live, which are the map maker's.

**Performance** (on `claude/perf`, waves 1, 2, 3 and 6 of its waves, the sixth built fourth). Built: a trip to a place on another world (a Galaxy-tab pick, an unflown ticket, a padless far port) is one loading screen, placed from that world's own places file before anybody travels, where it was two; the people, the ticket collectors, the people of ours and the creatures are stood behind the screen once the world's rows are in, and the screen waits up to eight seconds for their bodies, saying "the people here, n of m"; the sweep that compiles the scene waits a frame only after a batch that built something or after ten milliseconds of work, so a same-world teleport to Mos Eisley spends a second and a half compiling where it spent twenty-eight; the world is drawn behind a fully opaque screen at most ten times a second; a race start beside its grid has no curtain at all and a start anywhere else a short course curtain with no arrival's settle; the program queue no longer reads one long frame, nor frames the browser holds back on a page on screen while its GPU process catches up, as the game stopped, and builds nothing in a timer task; and the loading words come from real programs timed in the sweep, with the trivial link kept only to decide whether the graphics driver is blamed, which one screen a session waits up to a second for. `__debug.loading()` reads the last trip's screens and the 30 s after, `__debug.perf({ ab: { key: 'people' } })` compares a frame with and without the people, and `__debug.shaders()` says the backend, the milliseconds a program and the driver check. Measured only in headless Chrome of our own on cold profiles, never on the owner's machine: Naboo to Mos Eisley one screen of about 39 to 57 s on Direct3D 11 over seven runs (82 s in two before) and 17 to 24 s on Vulkan; a warm course curtain 2.1 s, a cold far one 5.8 s; of the 27 bodies the people pass would stand at Mos Eisley (its want of 107 less 80 budget refusals) 15 to all 27 were up as the screen lifted on Vulkan and only 4 to 9 on Direct3D 11's cold arrival, where each program takes about 400 ms, the rest building theirs in play after it; counted off three's own records of their materials, no program was built in play for a body that was up as the screen lifted (three sessions, Vulkan and Direct3D 11), and with the honest count of the idle rule's builds in a timer task, which found two and four programs on cold Direct3D 11 before the on-screen bound, none, five forced 1.6 s frames included. **Wave 2 took the programs built in play off the frame.** The shadow pass and a building's rooms pass draw through scenes of their own, so the lights no longer churn three times a frame outdoors beside buildings and a depth program is keyed on one set; on a machine whose browser compiles in the background (the owner's, Direct3D 11) every program built in play is issued and polled and never waited for on the main thread, and on Vulkan, which has no such thing, one is built a frame as before; every skinned mesh's joints are floats and its morph targets padded to a closed ladder at load, so the motion blur's eight variants and the bodies' 27 shadow combinations are all built behind the first screen; a person stood outdoors is shown once its world pass is built and its rooms pass follows; the dressed people's colour renders are made in the characters' worker; and the model budget is 190 MB, so the same towns stand the same people. `__debug.links()` says the mode, the waits and the deferral, `__debug.skinnedLoad()` what the load did, and `shaders()` carries `inPlay` and `warm`. Measured only in headless Chrome of our own on cold profiles: a two-minute walk round Bestine on Direct3D 11 waited more than 5 ms on links in 0 frames (worst 4.2 ms, 36 ms in all), and in 1 (6.9 ms, a plain caster's depth program built on the frame, below) on a second walk after the review, where the old way (every switch at its first value and both hatches off, which since the review gives the first wave's own warm lists back) waited in 61 and 85 (worst 402 and 508 ms, 7.8 and 19.3 s in all), with no skinned depth or motion-blur program built in play where the old way, measured again with its own warm lists, built 16 skinned depth programs and 4 motion-blur variants over the walk; the light churn read 3 the old way and 0; the render states' A/B at Bestine took the frame's median from 20.3 to 17.0 ms (p95 26.0 to 20.2); on Vulkan the walk's main-thread look renders, 62 stretches of 40 ms or more between frames (5.2 s) with them on this thread, are none; and the first arrival's screen took 21 to 26 s where the old way's took 60. **Wave 3 took down what the people cost every frame.** The scene's one matrix walk steps over a body the manager's cull hides and over one nothing has posed since it was last walked; a mesh that draws nothing is never drawn (49 such pieces over Bestine's 64 bodies); a body the walk stepped over as culled is not listed for the motion blur; and the smallest pieces a body wears (under 96 triangles and 0.12 m from their middle: a buckle, a ring, an eye) are hidden while the body stands under 3% of the screen's height, kept hidden a fifth past that so they never blink. Each is a switch whose first value is the old way (`walkSkip`, `walkStill`, `emptyMeshes`, `moverList`, `smallParts`; `crowdExact` flips the four exact ones together, since two of them cover for each other), and the four exact ones each drew the very same scene on and off with people on the screen. The client's own far tiers by share of the screen are built and off (`holdShare` stops a small body's animation only while it stands still, `goneShare` takes a smaller one off the screen; both nought), a body in a fight is never drawn at a far tier, and a body this browser keeps for a server thinks at least every 0.4 s. `__debug.crowd()` lists every body's tier, share, walk and parts, `crowd({ compare })` holds a switch to the same picture, and the debug menu's `crowd` carries the feel numbers as sliders. Measured only in headless Chrome of our own on Direct3D 11 at 1600x900, before and after in turn in one session: on Bestine's square (the design's T12 and T13, already met as the wave began: its 23.4 ms and 4 to 6 were measured before wave 2) the frame's median with the people went from 6.5 and 6.6 ms to 5.9 and 5.8, and the people's own share of it from 1.4 and 1.5 ms to 1.0 and 0.8; on Mos Eisley's street from 9.3 to 8.1 and 8.3 ms, the people's share from 1.7 and 1.9 to 0.9 and 1.1; in the profile the walk went from 1.34 to 0.77 ms a frame and the motion blur's bookkeeping from 0.16 to 0.08. **Wave 6 took down the town's shadow pass.** The sun's near map is drawn every frame and its middle and far maps only once their pictures would be shown 13 and 41 ms old (at 144 Hz every 2nd and every 6th frame), each held exactly where it was drawn while it still covers what the camera sees, and drawn at once when it no longer does, when the sun moves, or when anything listed as fast (a vehicle, timed in the physics' own steps, a starport's shuttle in flight, another player's ride or figure) could cast into it; each loaded region tier's still placed casters reach the shadow maps as one draw; every plain caster's depth program, instanced or not, is built behind the first screen with the batches' own; and, the owner's answer to Q8, each shadow map's depth now reaches its own band (`farDepth` 'whole', the far one about 908 to 966 m by the window's shape), so the far shadows reach the ground across the sun that the old depth left bare, and the far map is held as often turning as standing. The leaf cards' crown skip takes each cascade's own depth range. `__debug.shadowPass()` says each cascade's age, limit, draws a second and why, the batches and the movers, its four feel numbers are sliders in the debug menu, and the perf switches `shadowCadence`, `shadowBatches`, `shadowBoth` and `farDepth` put each part back. Measured only in headless Chrome of our own on Direct3D 11 at 1600x900, people up, held noon (the sun 46 degrees up), heading 270, the ages set to give the 144 Hz schedule at the tab's own rate, no shuttle listed, the old way (every cascade every frame, no batches, the old depth) and what ships in turn in one session: the shadow pass's own time (its `shadows` and `shadowHooks` lines) on Mos Eisley's street went from 2.97 to 1.52 ms (0.51 of the old way, where the design asked 0.47) and its draws from 1,328 to 185, on Bestine's square from 2.68 to 1.46 ms (0.55, asked 0.62); turning at 30 and 90 degrees a second scaled to 144 Hz, Mos Eisley 2.25 to 1.43 ms (0.63) and 2.52 to 1.50 ms (0.60), Bestine 2.17 to 1.49 (0.69) and 2.20 to 1.51 (0.69), against the design's allowance of T16's share plus 0.08 (0.59 and 0.63), so turning is not met at either town, the far map drawn on one frame in six or seven throughout; on the graphics card at Mos Eisley 2.81 to 1.60 ms (0.57, asked 0.60), turning 2.38 to 1.60; the frame's median from 9.2 to 7.7 ms at Mos Eisley and 9.0 to 7.3 at Bestine; the far map's longer depth cost nothing measurable on the main thread standing and 0.05 ms on the graphics card, and turning at 90 degrees saved about 0.25 ms against the old depth with the same exact guard, whose far map was drawn about 100 times a second; Bestine's premium over the open desert in the shadow pass from 0.51 to 0.28 ms (the scene's walk beside it, 0.9 against 0.55 ms, is not the wave's); a two-minute run through Mos Eisley with its doors casting and on to the nearest camp built no depth program in play (it built none of any kind, though it covered only 270 m between the walls); every picture pair held to the fresh draw, the view still or stepped 4 degrees, with the old depth and the new, came out with no pixel over 8 levels at any view tried, the batches too, and from a hill 200 m south of Bestine looking across the sun the new depth shades 0.7% of the picture at noon and 1.6% at a 26-degree sun that the old depth left bare; a riderless speeder at 40 m/s 150 m across the view drew the far map as a mover every frame it was over 20 m/s and was held again under it, and one ridden at up to 36 m/s never drew the middle or far map as a mover. Not built of it (wave 4 and after): the dressed people's own colour programs (about 25 to 30 over that walk, built off the frame now but built in play); a fighter's root never skips the walk, since it has no whole cull and is posed from outside the mobiles' folder; a caster drawn from both sides or swaying (254 and 51 of Mos Eisley's) still casts on its own; and the outer part of the far cascade's fade band, 320 to 360 m, which its box only partly reaches, is the Light and shadow pass's. Nothing of it has been seen by eye: whether the screen's words read right, whether the world drawn behind the screen at ten a second shows anything amiss on the reveal, whether a person first seen through a doorway pops into a room a moment late, whether a small part leaving a far body can be seen go, whether the client's far tiers are worth turning on, whether a moving thing's shadow 54 to 320 m off can be seen trailing it while the view turns, whether the far shadows now reaching the ground across the sun look right, and how the arrival, a walk and a crowded square feel at the owner's frame rate are his to judge.

**SOE complete** (on `claude/soe-complete`, waves 1 to 4 of 4 and a fifth the owner asked for on 2026-10-19; the real conversion of the owner's packs for waves 1 to 3 ran on 2026-10-19, 12 minutes 28 seconds, and wave 4 asks for Rori's again, which `status` names). Built: every command reads SOE's last publishes out of an NGE install through SOE's final names list (`docs/ASSETS.md`), the one file of the game's the repository carries, by the owner's decision of 2026-10-18; every pack says which mount it came from and `status` asks once for whatever came from the other; and the converter half of the menus: 63 vehicles in the gallery (the airspeeders and two more walker templates sorted as kinds), 182 houses, 28 deeds of ours over the client's house deeds Core3 has no row for (their lots and upkeep not known, which the Housing tab says), fourteen more ridden mounts found by their appearance (the beast master's, the trading-card ones, the whisper bird, the reptilian flier and the snorbal among them, every one on a saddle of its own), SOE's final creator table (424 rows, 489 hairstyles, with rows of the Sullustans' and the Wookiees' own), the trading-card decorations as props (their deeds, datapads and travel terminals left out), 295 NPC ship types in 47 families up to tier 10 which fly, fit and take damage as tier 5 above it (`npcShips({ ladder })`: the skill reaches ships in the air, the fit and the strength the next ship stood), the AT-XT seating two in the bird saddle's pose, the `undead` walk and run converted and worn by nobody, and no lair stood on the sites the late buildout tables added (circles of ours on Dathomir, Endor and Lok). A pilot conversion of a copy of the owner's packs took 12 min 25 s, left nothing a stamped command owns unwritten, and the suite is green on it and on the packs before it. Wave 2 finished the menus at run time: the four last dances named on the emote list (`breakdance 3` to `6`, the performance table's own keys), beards reading "Beard N" and the other gender's hairstyles left out where this gender's already show their meshes (so a Wookiee of either gender sees the table's twelve and a Sullustan man his thirty-nine), the clothes that read "Other" for want of a word in their ids under their arrangement's first slot (Other is two to four pieces now, where it held over a hundred), the NPC ship ladder's tiers 6 to 10 as sliders in the debug menu (`npcShips` among the Tuning helpers), and `__debug.late()`, saying for every pack already fetched whether it carries SOE's final files and for every menu whether its late thing shows, which `lateMenus.test.ts` asks of the packs on disk (33 checks of packs converted with them on the owner's). A headless tab of our own on the owner's packs found every one of the twelve packs it fetched carrying them and every menu showing its late thing, opened the seven menus they fill with no program built in play, and moved the ladder's sliders through the debug menu; a tier 10 X-wing stood with tier 5's shields, armour, hull and gun damage. Wave 3 gave the owner's install SOE's own people for Kashyyyk's seven zones and Mustafar: `spawns --nge=<his NGE copy>` reads their placed spawners, the people who stand in one place, the corpses, the dungeons' crews room by room in every copy and Mustafar's wild regions into `assets-private/nge/`, and every arrival on one of those worlds stands them after everybody else, behind its loading screen, with Mustafar's herds laid by the game's own grid; nobody else ever has it (no launcher passes the flag, nothing of the copy is committed), on a server others share it is left out unless he says `__debug.people({ nge: 'always' })` or keeps `localStorage['swg.nge'] = 'always'`, and `'0'` leaves it out everywhere. It gives no city crowds (SOE's crowd in Kachirho and Mensix came from a table his copy does not hold), no quest givers' conversations (they stand, silent beyond the game's greeting), no travel rows and no comparison with Core3 (which holds nothing on these worlds). A pilot conversion into a copy of what `spawns` reads wrote it with nothing unresolved and every zone's placed objects on the pack's own at a median of nought, and left the Core3 half's packs byte for byte what the command writes without the flag; a headless tab of our own stood its people on Kachirho and Mustafar, Mustafar's herds over its own regions, a Myyydril copy's crew in its rooms, left it out against a relay of this build and stood it there once switched to `always`. Mustafar's arrival point, the flattest spot near its layout centre, stood among SOE's own aggressive people, and arriving killed the player there and again at every coming round, so the overlay's people and herds who pick a fight on sight are kept 64 m (ours, `__debug.people({ arrivalClear })`) from where the player comes in and back, a teleport's spot included; its patrollers walk their rounds. Wave 4 gave Rori Restuss in both of its states: the living launch city, which no copy of the final client's snapshot places, read out of the late patch's copy with its raw ids and appended after everything else (696 of its 820 objects stand, with its own starport and shuttleport, their nine travel things, 17 fittings, and Core3's 37 people indoors who find their rooms only now), or its 35 ruins on the city's own places, the late camp's two dead pads its only ports, Core3's town within 700 m skipped and a First Brigade of ours standing guard (23 people at posts by a rule over the ruins, every number a slider under `restuss` in the debug menu); the camp and the faction cantinas stand either way, and nothing that stands in only one state moves anybody's index. Which it is, is a setting, the ruins unless changed (the owner's choice; Esc → **Gameplay** → The world, `__debug.restuss('living')`), taken at the next arrival on Rori and never in the middle of one; on a server it is the admin's word for everybody (`worldStateSet`, the hail's `states`; the relay must be restarted for it), which a session's first arrival reads by a look at the saved server's hail of its own. A permanent placement row may carry a state, so rubble the editor puts down stands in the ruins alone. A pilot conversion of a copy of the owner's packs took 3 min 16 s (Rori's snapshot, its walk grid, its placed sounds -- 231 emitters, 77 of them the living city's -- travel, fittings, spawns, effects, doors, the creator's Rori scene and the conversations), kept every row the pack held before in its place but the fittings' last three (the server's own, keyed by nothing that outlives a run), and left `status` asking nothing and the local overlay unasked; every suite of `test:converter` and every other suite that reads the packs passed on it but `ngeSpawns`' one check that git ignores its folder (the copy was no checkout). A headless tab of our own stood both states in numbers and screenshots with no program built in play across either arrival (counted over the whole history, `shaders({ full: true })`, since the plain list stops at twenty), played 231 placed sounds in the living city and 154 in the ruins, bought a ticket at the living shuttleport's terminal and had the living starport's collector take it and begin the ride, and on its own dev server put rubble down with the editor, turned it to ruins only with the State button (it then stood only after the arrival in ruins) and took it out again; the brigade's 23 people stand clear of every ruin; and on a relay of this build a non-admin was refused, the admin's word reached the other, the next arrival stood it, a restart kept it, and a tab whose own setting was ruins stood the server's living city and another that started on Rori stood the server's ruins on its very first arrival. Nothing of wave 4 has been seen by eye: the ruins draw very dark in those screenshots, which may be their scorched pictures or a fault; whether the brigade's posts, people and levels read as a guard, and whether the ruins want rubble placed by hand, are the owner's. Wave 5 lets every one of the garage's 70 ship hulls, the room ships among them, be stood as an NPC ship flying for the Empire, the Alliance, Black Sun, the pirates or nobody, at a tier from 1 to 10 through the ladder, alone or as a patrol of three in that side's formation (the NPC tab's "Any ship as an NPC" list, found by its find box, and `npcShip('<garage id>', { faction, tier, count })`), fitted as an NPC type it draws at the nearest tier where one exists (37 of the 70) and on its stock fit otherwise, named as the game names that type or in the garage's words; no conversion and no relay restart. A headless tab of our own in Tatooine's orbit stood a pirate YT-1300 patrol that went for the player's X-wing, an Imperial X-wing at tier 8 with tier 5's chassis, shields, armour and gun damage, a neutral corvette from the tab's own button that was held while the panel stood open and flew once it shut, three Imperial corvettes 446 to 913 m apart and a Star Destroyer 1,564 m out, every stand's programs built behind its hold but one Star Destroyer shadow program after it showed (the performance pass's known plain-caster gap). Its review found the Star Destroyer the one hull the NPC ships' fighter-sized distances could not hold: a patrol of three stood two of its members past the 4 km every NPC ship was drawn to, its wingmen's own slots past the 4 km a pilot chases, and its pilots never fired nor were fired on, since a pilot measured its 1 km guns from the middles and kept off the whole 1.3 km sphere; a big hull's group is now drawn and leashed as far as its size asks and every pilot's sight and reach count the part of a hull past a fighter's sphere, all of it nought for the game's own NPC hulls. A headless tab of our own then drew an Imperial patrol of three Star Destroyers whole (3.3, 6.3 and 4.6 km off) with its leader firing on the player's X-wing within ten seconds, and a lone one traded fire with a typed Rebel X-wing patrol (48 shots of theirs in half a minute, one hit; six of its own, none), with no program built after either stand. Fighters still reach a Star Destroyer only off its bow and stern, since the break-off keeps them off its whole sphere, and its guns take their turns round the whole hull (`takeShot`), so many of its shots leave from its far side and fall short. How any of them flies, fights or looks is the owner's to see. Nothing of it has been seen by eye at the owner's frame rate: how the new bird mounts carry their rider (their saddle stands pitched about 49 degrees at the idle on the pilot where the pose's own clip root leans about 17, which may be the game's authoring or a fault of how the two compose; the saddle test prints the pitch and pins none), how an airspeeder flies, how a tier 10 fighter fights (the sliders under `npcShips` in the debug menu are where he moves it), and whether the hair grid's beards, the Wookiees' styles above their fur pattern and the humans' trim colour read right on the appearance page are the owner's to judge.

Known gaps and the agreed order for the next work:

1. Wings stand where the game's client data places them and carry their parts, nested foils included; the axis each PSOR angle turns about and its sign (the V-wing's yaw, folding forward; the B-wing's roll, the side its closed wing lies) are the owner's to confirm, and the speed factor's reading (a top-speed cost while open) is ours. So is the wings key's rule that a wing swinging below the belly still waits for room and folds on landing. NPC fighters fly shut and open to fight, and a dock holds the player's ship's wings shut from its approach to the end of its launch, both after the game; the three seconds a fighter keeps them open after a fight are ours, NPC ships do not dock yet (when they do, `holdsWings` is where they join), nobody has yet heard a dogfight of six fighters each sounding its wings as it takes and loses a target, and none of it has been seen by eye. Ships come down and rest on a planet's ground and inside a hangar room, but a ship with rooms of its own still spawns outside a building (boarding one inside a building is its own piece of work), no hull has gear to lower, and a ship setting down out in space flies its settle in the world rather than in the frame of what it is coming down on, so something moving quickly would be landed beside rather than on. A wing set sounds once as it starts to open or close, and every machine runs on the engine loop the game's own files give it, but the boosters' own rocket loop is read and never played, a damaged ship still sounds undamaged although its damage loops are read, and two hulls and six of the gallery's vehicles have no engine sound in the archives at all. A mobile inside a building finds its room by the point it stands on rather than by the cell the manager tracks for it, and of Jedi Academy's own sound marks the saber whooshes are played from the clips themselves while the body falls and the melee swings are still read and dropped, which is the one place a blow could come to sound twice if they are ever switched on.
2. A hull's rooms are hidden until someone boards; the proper fix is drawing them through the portal renderer with the windows as exits, so they show from outside. The same holds for a hull another player flies: its rooms are built here only when somebody asks to board it, out of sight and prepared before they are shown, and are hidden again the moment the walker steps out, so from outside a friend's ship is still a picture with nothing to see through its windows. They are dropped when that hull stops being drawn (a distance rule would be one number and one call site) rather than when it flies far off, and a peer's canopy stays solid while you are inside it, because the panes a ship's glass is swapped for are recorded when a hull is spawned and a picture has none.
3. The relay carries each player's vehicle (garage id, place, turn, the rider's pose and whether they ride, pilot or are aboard, and a winged ship's wings (open or closed), and whether it is set down on the ground, which stands it still on the other side instead of gliding it toward each message) and the figure's whole turn when aboard or adrift, drawn on the other side as a picture with no physics (`Garage.visual`); and whether they are hidden just now (`j`, in a hyperspace jump from its start until its tunnel opens, or in an ultra-fast cruise for the length of the run: the others hide them and their ship meanwhile and show them where they come out, since ten places a second a kilometre apart is a teleport on their side; the relay itself is unchanged and needs no restart for either); and each player's look and the weapons in their hands in their hello (`packLook` in `player/look.ts`, `held`), resent 400 ms after any change of clothes or weapon and put on the other side compiled before it shows (`applyLookPrepared`, `applyHeld`); and the ship they fly with its fit (`hello.ship`: a component per slot, the paint values and the droid, checked by `server/shipWire.mjs`), sent once when the Edit page closes or the ship changes and applied on the other side in place (a repaint, or the changed parts restaged: `RemotePlayers.refitRemote`); it does not carry the atmosphere crossings. Another player's ship and mount are silent: a peer's hull is drawn as a picture rather than made a machine the mixer knows, so none of its engine, wings or flyby is heard, although their blade now hums, ignites and hisses as a fighter's does, since it is drawn with the game's own renderer and that renderer is also the ear. The relay can also carry one word to one player rather than to the room (`ask`: a pilot asking another for room on their hull, and the answer), which is the first directed message it has ever had, and a ship clamped onto another player's hull is passed on as the hull it rides and the place on it, so the others draw it sitting still on that hull however the hull moves and the pilot sitting in it stays in their own cockpit; the relay must be restarted before either works. Emotes, dances and flourishes cross. A swing while moving or in the air plays on the upper body over whatever the legs do (Jedi Academy's run, walk, back-pedal, the angled strafe, the jump: `rig.overrideUpperOnly`), with the legs' angle and the torso's turn to the camera kept; standing, the swing has the whole body. Testing in a hidden Chrome tab: after five minutes hidden Chrome throttles chained timers to one a minute, so a test script's awaits stall; reload the page to start the five minutes again. The relay is now a small server as well, and the two are one program: `npm run relay` still starts it, a browser built before it still plays against it unchanged, and a browser built for it that finds an older relay at the other end says so and turns everything new off. What it holds is **one clock** — the world's wall time and how long a day is, handed out in its first word and sharpened by a round trip every ten seconds, which the time of day and the weather schedule both read, so two people on one planet are no longer at noon and at dusk at once — and **who each player is**: a key the browser makes for itself with `crypto.getRandomValues` and never sends, a public name that is a hash of it, and the characters that player owns, all written to `server/data/world.json` with a log beside it so a machine that loses power loses nothing. The same character opened in a second browser goes to the newer one and the older stops asking for it back; a character played with no server and then brought to one is settled by a change counter, the higher winning, with the question put to the player on a true tie. News goes only to the world a player is standing on, and a peer is taken apart on the other side only when their line actually closes. A join word on the address (`--word=`) keeps a stranger who guesses the port out, and damage between players is a switch on the server (`--friendly-fire`) that starts off and that nothing reads yet. **With no server address set none of this runs at all and the game is exactly what it was**, which the day's own test pins to the last bit. Everything about it is invented except the day's 720 seconds, which is the game's: the protocol, how a player is named, the rates, the caps, the patiences and where each planet sits in the shared day. The server's own numbers are one `TUNING` object it prints back on `http://localhost:8787/` and moves for a run with `--set <name>=<number>`; the browser's are `__debug.session()` and `__debug.day()` (`__sharedDay()` is the same knob on the window). It holds **groups** as well: up to eight, asked in within the game's own 90 m, accepted or declined on a countdown, left, handed on, put out of and broken up, with the leader alone able to ask people in, remove them, hand the group on or offer it a trip. A member is keyed by their character rather than their line, so a reload keeps its place for a minute and a half and the group hears only that they stepped out and came back. The roster is its own light message that never reaches `RemotePlayers`, so a member on another planet costs this browser no rig, no clothes, no compile and no ship: it stands down the side of the screen and in the map's Group tab (the full stop, shown only while a server holds your group), is marked on the planet's map and on the space map, and a member past forty metres gets a chevron with their name over their head. And there is a **chat line**: Enter opens it, plain words go to everyone on your world and `/g` to your group, every line comes back from the server so that everybody reads the same words in the same order, and what anybody says floats over their head for a few seconds and stands in the message line. A player standing in a hull somebody else flies names that hull rather than sending a copy of it — the fix for two crew drawing two hulls on everybody else's screen — and that hull is now something you can really stand in: asked for one in particular, the browser builds that player's rooms for real (`src/net/remoteInterior.ts`), their own small physics world in the hull's frame with their own lights out of the pool that already exists, hung under the picture so they ride it as it flies and prepared and compiled out of sight before anybody is shown them. Nothing is built for a ship merely in view. You board one by walking up to it and pressing the same key that boards your own, or by stepping across at the way in from a hull of yours clamped to theirs, and E anywhere in their rooms is the way back out; the rooms step themselves once a frame whatever becomes of the player they belong to, because somebody standing in the ship of a pilot who has just dropped off the line is standing on a floor that has to go on being there, and a walker is always brought out before such a world is freed. Whoever leads a group can also take it with them: a crossing to another world offers the rest of the group the same trip with a countdown on it, and whoever says yes crosses their own way and comes out a step to one side of the leader, which is one word of its own (`cross`) going to the group and nowhere else. Still left of boarding and of travelling together: a passenger is not carried across by anybody's crossing (they are asked to step out first), nothing tells the other players that you boarded beyond the `in` field they already had, boarding from the gravity boots is not offered, two other players clamped to each other — neither hull yours — is not a crossing this browser offers, and the step to one side a follower comes out at is checked against nothing, so in space it can land inside something the arrival itself was chosen to be clear of. And it carries **the fight**: every player on your world has a body where they stand and a box round the hull they ride, both sensors in a collision group of their own that collides with nothing, so a bolt, a blade and a sweep stop on them while the steps you take, a ship looking for somewhere to set down, the roof over the rain and the ground under a speeder all pass over them; a shot crosses as an explicit message and is flown again on every other browser as a picture that hurts nothing and is cut short where the one who fired says it landed; the shooter's screen decides the hit, the one who was hit is the only place health is ever taken off, their health comes back at five looks a second and only when it has moved (which is what fills the roster's bars), and the one who died announces it with whoever struck the last blow; a blade turning one of those bolts away is a word rather than a second bolt; a peer's lightsaber is drawn, lit in the colour they chose, heard, thrown, and lights what is round it; and damage between players is the server's `--friendly-fire` switch, off unless it is asked for, with the game's own duel and peace inside it. Still left of the fight: a Force push, pull or grip does nothing to another player (the powers fire and the peer does not move), a creature or a fighter this browser alone holds (one stood from the console with no server's record, a follower) cannot hurt one, though one of the world's kept here can (its keeper's word to the player struck, `npcBlow`, which the server checks for reach and for whether that player's game has the creature at all), a peer standing in a hull's rooms cannot be shot (bolts fired aboard fly in that hull's own physics world and peer bodies live in the planet's), a ship's condition shares do not cross so two browsers each keep their own idea of how battered a peer's hull is, a homing rocket flies straight on every screen but the one that fired it, and a reflected bolt sounds like the plain blaster rather than the gun it came from. The world's own creatures are built: the server keeps what stands in a world and grants each one to the nearest browser within 180 m, changing hands only when a challenger has been a quarter nearer for three seconds; that browser runs its brain, its physics and its damage, and every other holds the same body `driven` (kinematic, brain off, eased toward a row four times a second, still shootable, still in the list of the living), with a blow struck there asked of the keeper, who is the only place health comes off. What the world's data stands -- the lairs, camps and nests, the people at their posts -- every browser stands for itself from the same rows, and those that may be fought go on the wire by saying they have been seen (the seen lesson above); an admin stands what everybody sees from the console, and can put a weapon in any hand already standing (`__debug.arm`). A hand-over carries the brain as far as it means anything on another browser: what it was fighting (as `p:<relay id>` or the creature's world name, never a key), its stun, slow and burn by the seconds left, and where it was walking; its grudges and a hold start fresh on the new keeper. The item ledger **is** built (`server/ledger.mjs`, wired through the relay; its browser half is `src/net/trade.ts` alone -- `src/net/owned.ts` is the creatures' keeper): the server keeps what each character owns, a row per thing under the name its browser minted, with its colours, and hands one thing from one player to another, name and colour going with it. Two of one shirt are two things to hold, show, trade and colour apart (the hail says `items: 2`; the relay must be restarted for it), and a thing's colours are its own (`tint`, by bare variable name): set on the appearance page for the copy worn, kept by the server (written lazily), carried by a trade, shown as a swatch in the backpack and the trade window, and newest-wins between this browser and the server. A job's reward still gives one of each, and nothing yet furnishes a house with *this* chair rather than *a* chair.
4. The lifts and terminals inside ship rooms are objects the server spawned, not part of the models. The shafts are cells named for them (`elevator1`, `reactorlift`, `empelevator`; `LIFT_CELL` in `lifts.ts`, which leaves out rooms named `elevator_room`) and have no floor at their upper levels, so a shaft's stops are its doorways: `liftStops` reads each portal out of the shaft (and out of any shaft it opens into, or is paired with by an `_up`/`_down` name) as a level, and E opens the lift menu (`liftMenu.ts`); a pick puts the player just through that doorway. No car moves, and stepping into a shaft from an upper doorway drops you to its bottom. A pick that changes level sounds where the player is put, one of the game's own elevator sounds going up and another coming down, which is our choice of when to play them rather than the game's. Buildings with no passable doorway from outside near the player (dungeons entered through server objects, stations with doors in the air) get an `E enter` that puts the player in the entry room (`doorlessNear`/`entryOf` in `layoutStream.ts`). The gallery's Interiors section labels the layouts with shafts.
5. Space: the zones convert and fly (`space` command; stations, asteroid fields, planets in the sky, the nebula skybox, hyperspace points and the jump scene; Kessel, Ord Mantell and Deep Space are systems of their own with nothing below, and what is made up in them is marked in the pack), reached from the top of a planet's sky and left through the ship menu (P; E aboard a multi-crew ship is for the controls and the door). Nothing but a station's own hull stops a ship flying at it (the zone's unbuilt ground at y -3000 did, with half the stations under it), and a boost in space asks the physics for no more than the 400 m/s it gives a body. A crossing carries the local player's place in the rooms and the ship's damage. A whole group crosses together now — the leader's trip is offered to the rest with a countdown, each member who takes it makes their own crossing and comes out a step to one side of the leader — but the relay still carries no crossing for a **crew**: a passenger standing in somebody else's hull is not carried with it and is asked to step out first, so a ship's crew still does not cross together. A jump is flown inside a tunnel of our own (the camera behind the ship; within a system the hull is moved in it, to another system the world is swapped under the hull, its rooms and crew carried across and walkable meanwhile); the others are told to hide the ship from the jump's start until its tunnel opens (the relay's `j`), a group jumps together (each member their own jump, arriving a step to one side of the leader), and a crew on the relay is still left behind — whoever is standing in somebody else's hull is asked to step out before they can take the trip. A small ship can be clamped onto a bigger one and carried on its back, the carrier's pilot flying both, with the crew crossing between the two ships' rooms at their own doors; a clamp onto another player's ship asks their pilot first, over the relay's one directed message, and the relay must be restarted for it; the step across at the doors works between your hull and theirs as well now, the first one building their rooms here. Flying into a carrier's hangar room, the other form, is not built. The System Map is a sub-panel of the ship menu (`hyperspaceUi.ts`), not a tab; the map's hyperspace-point rings are not built. Not done: landing at a station, flying the Star Destroyer from its own bridge (wanted later, the owner said), the stations' interiors (wanted as rooms in the same zone with the ship-interior technique, a still room in the station's frame, so ships show outside and players inside), the zones' nebulae now hang in the sky, strike by the game's own rule and weaken a ship inside them (neither seen by eye: whether the game's rare strikes or `bendWithin`'s old aim is wanted, and how strongly a nebula should weaken a ship, are the owner's to say), but how many sheets a nebula is made of, how big they are, how far in the far ones are pulled and what the haze looks like are all ours and have not been seen by anyone yet (every one of them moves live through `__debug.nebulae`), the fill they cost has never been measured in a visible window, and the sounds each row and each strike name are in the pack and nothing plays them, the space loading pictures (`texture/loading/space/`, which the `loading` command does not pick up), a made-up system of our own (`space_sandbox`, written by the `sandbox` command from a seed, 250 km across, everything in it invented and marked so) which has never been flown in a visible window and whose layout one number changes, the zone's own POIs on the map; all three stages of a jump sound now, and the warp effects' turn about the hull (`__debug.jumpFx`) is not yet confirmed by eye; on foot in space the player drifts with momentum (`evaUpdate`) but nothing yet needs a jetpack or a helmet, and the gravity boots that hold to a surface out there cut their patch out of one body's colliders only, let go rather than re-anchor once the walker crosses the edge of it, and name a hull for the aboard path's sake, so a zone with no ship in it has none; the planets' places and sizes come from the terrain file's PLAN forms and each body's `.pln` radius (read as a radius: no two bodies intersect in any zone and the close ones sit just outside each other, Ord Mantell's moons 3 to 34 m off a radius-50 planet, Endor and Yavin 4 about 125 m off their giants; diameter is not ruled out by the client); the game hangs every body at the same 2600 m, and overlapping bodies are drawn in their PLAN order (three sorts equal render orders by material id), which in every retail zone puts the nearer body in front (Yavin 4 over Yavin, Endor over its gas giant, three of Ord Mantell's moons over the planet); in space the star field and the star sprites are drawn in the opaque list before the bodies, so a planet covers the stars behind it, and a star group inside a body's disc never flares (`SwgSky.setSpaceOccluders`); the halos are not drawn.
6. The engine client data (`clientdata/ship/component/eng_*.cdf`, `GLOW` forms) carries glow sizes per engine style; their float layout is not decoded, so glows are sized by hull height.
7. Mounts wear the game's saddle on the creature's saddle hardpoint where it has one (the 22 the game let players ride, 36 with SOE's final names list) and a guessed one on the posed back otherwise (the top of the back's own vertices, never a neck held up over it); the rider leans with the saddle as authored (the tauntaun's and cu pa's 5 degrees back, the lava flea's 16 at its idle), and a tilt damping in `syncMount` is the follow-up if the owner finds it harsh. God rays are a depth-based radial pass in `postfx.ts` (sky is depth past 2.5 km; the sun projected to the screen; off with bloom off); the effects still wanted are listed in the README's "Effects to do". Moving things blur by their root's motion only: limbs, a ragdoll's fall and a peer's roll do not blur on their own (a previous-bone texture per skeleton is the sketched fix).
8. The map window (M) has four tabs, Here, Galaxy, Waypoints (Y) and Group (the full stop, only while a server holds your group), each key opening the map on its tab and shutting it from there, and M opening it on the tab used last: a planet's map is the client's `texture/ui_map_<planet>.dds` over the terrain's width (`maps` command, which also writes `galaxy.json` beside the packs: every planet's ground width and the game's own 30 shuttle routes with their prices, directed because the fare is not the same both ways, which the galaxy tab draws as the lines between the systems; where a system hangs in the galaxy is not in the archives at all, so the squares are canon and the layout round them is ours and marked as such wherever it shows); the space map draws the zone's own pack — its stations by the game's names, its hyperspace points, its launch point, its asteroid fields and its nebulae — as layers that switch off and on with the client's own zone-map icons, over the placed rocks and every ship; everyone in your group is marked on the planet's map and on the space map too, named, the leader's mark larger and inside a ring, switched by a box of its own that is there only while there is a group; it follows your ship until you slide the view and F brings it back, and a click names a place and offers the jump. The galaxy tab draws the galaxy itself, with a panel for the system picked and a jump into its orbit. Not done: other ships needing a ping, players you are not grouped with on either map, a member on another world (the strip and the Group tab carry their world instead), the docking lanes drawn, the System Map opening with the map's pick already chosen; the galaxy's star disc is a seeded spiral of ours rather than the client's own picture of the galaxy, the shuttle fares are read and not drawn, and the globes' pictures are about 15 MB uploaded into the map's own context and kept for the session. The pod racers' seats are the owner's table (`POD_SEAT` in `src/vehicles/podSeat.ts`: fg_8t8 and the IPG so far), else each pod's own `player` point, else its cockpit joint, hung under that joint so the pilot rides the pod's clips; the owner's `__debug.seat` pastes for pod_racer_one, two, Balta's and Anakin's are what to add next, and the gallery has to be converted again before the game's own point seats any pod (until then the joint's origin does, about half a metre from it).
9. The plain models' colour variants are whole GLBs beside the base (`mobiles/variants/`), and a combination whose palette none of the appearance's shaders bake comes out identical to the base and is not written: `shaderNeedsBake` looks at the first pass only unless asked for every pass, so a hue applied in a later pass never reaches the texture (the ships command bakes every pass of a ship's paint shaders and the wardrobe asks for `allPasses`; the mobiles' variants and their own wearables still use the first-pass check). Every `--var=name=value` on the command line is lost the same way for `sat`, `parts`, `player` and `species`, because the option parser splits at every `=` (`cli.mjs`); the `mobiles` command passes its values as a Map and is not affected.
10. Weather: a storm's own bed plays from the sky's rows, but the thunder its rain sheets carry is off by default so that the two do not double (`__debug.ambience({ weatherParticles: true })` adds it), and the owner's ear has not settled that yet; the climate weights and Life Day's dates are invented; weather writes no depth, so ambient occlusion and depth of field treat rain as whatever is behind it; Mustafar's lightning (a chain of eleven carried effects) plays, but its look has not been seen yet; props inside buildings share materials with the same props outdoors, so seen through a door from beyond the roof grid (about 36 m) they can show a faint wet sheen.
11. The backpack: species cuts are recorded, not converted, for the shared human folders (a Rodian wears the human hat mesh; `fit.own` names its cut), and the species packs' own dress (shirt, trousers, shoes) overrides the appearance table for the species it blocks. NPC fighters still pick outfits by the give tab's regex groups and ignore `fit`, so a Wookiee fighter can wear a blocked shirt. Item modifiers (armour, damage variability) are not modelled; nothing limits how much is carried.
12. Sound: the game is no longer silent, and it now has music -- **only the music players make**. Hold one of the game's instruments (they come through the weapons pack, since that is what puts a model in a hand) and a row under the world offers the songs that instrument has a part in, a play button and its eight flourishes; what sounds is the game's own track for that instrument, and standing beside somebody on another instrument is the two halves the game wrote, lined up off the clock everybody shares with nothing sent between browsers. The background score is deliberately left unconverted and always will be. Still to do there: another player's performance does not cross the relay yet (your own instrument and anybody you can already hear in the same browser are what work), no animation plays while you play, and the samples want the `music` command run with them (it is about 100 MB). Working: the mixer and the game's own bank behind a Sound page (a master, a slider a layer, and switches for headphones, the room echo, keeping the sound when the tab goes and which saber set), the world's own beds, rooms, placed sound objects and particle loops, every body's feet and voice, the guns, fists, blades and grenades, the blades' own hum and whoosh, the wall a sound is heard through and the echo of the room it stands in, and every machine's engine, wings, flyby, water, hits, jump, lift and room hum. Still silent: no music plays at all (the score and the instrument parts are left out of the bank on purpose, and the eighteen music templates it does keep are the ones a world places in a room); another player's ship or mount over the relay, since a peer's hull is a picture rather than a machine the mixer knows — their lightsaber is heard now, because it is drawn with the game's own blade renderer and that renderer is also the ear, which nothing asked for and nothing forbade, so say the word if several peers with blades out are too much; the boosters' own rocket loop, which is read and never played; a damaged ship, whose damage loops are read and never chosen between; two hulls and six of the gallery's vehicles, which have no engine sound in the archives at all; the nebulae's rows and each strike's sound; and the thousands of spoken lines in the bank, since nothing in the game talks. Ours rather than the game's, and all of it tunable live: how much of a room's echo a sound takes and how long the two echoes are, how much a wall takes off a sound, which of the game's four elevator sounds a pick plays, which of the three power chimes a hull answers to, the idle, speed-up and slow-down loop beside a run loop, and every number in `MIXER_TUNE`, `DISTANCE_TUNE`, `VEHICLE_TUNE`, `FOOT_TUNE`, `GUN_TUNE` and `SABER_TUNE`, through `__debug.audio`, `vehicleSounds`, `footsteps`, `surface`, `gunSounds` and `sabers`, none of which can be heard from a driven tab and all of which answer in numbers.
13. The interface: the whole screen is ours now and none of the client's own interface art is used anywhere. Built: the palette, the overlay canvas and the geometry table under everything; the flight display (the reticle, the three arcs, the ship's condition with its pips, the target's bracket and its edge arrow); the message line; the sheet of marks we drew by hand and the on-foot bars, ability cells, hands and crosshair that wear them; the four-slot action bar, whose state is gathered eight times a second into one struct that the long prompt line reads too; the name and health over whatever the crosshair rests on; the damage arc, the hit tick and the numbers over heads; the Interface page in the Escape menu; the minimap as a bare circle carrying the planet's name, the map window's own coordinates and a north mark, written only when the rounded pair moves; and every panel's colours moved onto the same eighteen names. The journal is on J and the pick-up on O, and a key a player had already chosen for something else stays theirs. Not done: the radar, which was the optional third wave and is not started. Loose ends worth knowing. The wing states and the message line's kinds have marks drawn for them that nothing shows, because `src/ui/shipHud.ts` and `src/ui/messages.ts` were not opened in that wave; two lines each when they next are. `hudMath`’s layout struct is still informational -- the flight display places its own blocks and the message line is placed by `hud.css` -- and `--hud-msg-w` is not written, so a long message line is clamped by the stylesheet’s own fallback. The bar holds four actions, so on a winged fighter with guns `next target` falls off the end, a ship's burn is deliberately not offered (it is a key held everywhere, not an action taken at a place), and standing in a lift shaft at a bridge's controls the bar offers the ship while the long line offers the lift. With the message line switched off the NPC pilots’ taunts go with it. A blow a body refuses for a reason other than being dead (an ally’s, which nothing outside the body can see) still ticks the crosshair, and a blow on a ship or a turret does not tick it at all, since the hook watches the living. The hit tick is four marks of its own just outside the crosshair rather than the crosshair’s own ticks jumping outward, because the two crosshairs belong to files that wave did not open. Aboard a ship's rooms a bolt's arc can point the wrong way, since the bolt's place is the hull's frame and the camera’s axes are the world’s. A number rises from `pos.y + halfHeight`, which is the head on anything measured from the feet and the middle on anything measured from its centre. The map's own canvas colours (`mapUi.ts`) are the last place in the game where a palette value has a second home: a 2D context takes strings, so they want `colourOf(COL.…)` from `src/core/palette.ts` rather than a `var()`. `App` still keeps a wrapper round the player's damage record as a second source for the direction of a blow, which the record itself now carries; it costs nothing and can go whenever that line and its check are changed together. And nothing of any of it has been seen from a driven tab: how it looks, whether each of the marks can be read at a glance, whether a hairline reads over a noon dune and over a starfield alike, whether the wings' amber span and the boost's cyan one read the right way round, whether the short bar has hidden something the owner uses, and what the overlay really costs are all the owner’s to judge.

14. Homes, props and what a world stands for good. A building can be put down in the world you are standing in and walked into, out of the gallery pack, on the ground as it finds it, and so can any of the game's props (`__debug.house`, `__debug.homes`, `__debug.prop`). **Yours are kept by a server or by this machine**: with a server they are real -- the relay writes your houses, and once restarted for it your props, down beside the characters and the items, everybody on that world sees everybody's, everybody arriving later is handed them, and they outlast a restart or a power cut -- and with none both are kept on this machine per world, shared by every character on this browser, and stand again the next time anybody comes back with no server. A line that drops keeps the server's things standing and refuses changes until it is back. **Everything in hand is placed the same way**: it appears out where the camera looks; R held with the mouse moving up and down turns it and F held raises or lowers it (a slow hand a little, a quick one a long way), Shift with R tips and rolls a prop, the wheel with a key held steps to the grid and the wheel alone pushes it out and pulls it in; a click puts it down, Escape gives it up without opening the menu, Delete gives up a deed or a prop taken fresh and throws one picked back up away; and nothing fires, swings or throws while something is in hand. O picks your nearest prop up exactly where it stood, and at your own door picks the house itself up the same way, so a house is moved rather than built again (a server's moves when the server answers, and only on a server restarted for it). The ground is refused in words when it stands too far over the doorstep, falls too far away or tilts too much, judged at the house's floor and on the ground it really stands on whichever way it is turned; a door left high is a note and never a refusal. A spot is refused when the world already has something standing there or when it is under water, and a prop of yours within a story placement's guard. **A building is put down from a deed, by hand**: the Housing tab lists the 47 buildings the archives and the emulator between them let a player buy, with the game's own names, sizes, lot counts and daily upkeep, and a double-click takes one in hand with the client's own footprint grid under it, green or red. **Permanent placements**: every world stands, for every player, the rows of two files that ship with the build, `public/placements/story/<world>.json` (the story's dressing) and `public/placements/world/<world>.json` (map edits); a row reaches players once its file is committed and merged to main, stands solid and guarded as part of the world (O says so and never picks one up), and a quest names one as `"template": "place/<id>"`, which `npm run story:check` checks against both files. None is committed yet. On the dev server alone they are written from inside the game: the Props and Housing tabs' Yours or Permanent switch, L in hand for which file, a label at the top saying where things go, the first save to a file each session on a second click, O on a permanent row to move it, and a Standing here list with Go to, Pick up, Delete, Rename, a name and a note, Solid, and a story object or an area to paste; `__debug.storyHere()` hands over the same two beside the nearest row. **None of it has been seen by eye**, here or on the relay, because the tabs it was written in draw no frames: not the hold to turn and raise, the label, the editor's list, a house moved, a server's props, or a permanent row standing in a town. What is not built: nothing costs anything or counts against a lot (the numbers are read and shown and nothing enforces them), no maintenance is taken, there is no city zoning and no permission list, and nobody but its owner and the admin may change a thing of yours. Known to be missing rather than undone: a house put down from the console or by its model alone is judged on its model's box (a deed's footprint judges the rest); the procedural flora is kept off a patch only on chunks built after it was placed, so a house put down in a wood stands in the wood, and so does a permanent row added while its chunks already stand; a moved house leaves what was put down inside it where it stood; nobody stands in a permanent building (a row's `fill` is read and set aside), its doors are never locked (`locked`, the same), and the outdoor walk grid does not know it is there; every permanent row is a placement with meshes of its own, which suits a yard and not a town (2,000 rows a file is the cap, and folding rows into the streamer's own tiers is the map-maker's step); rows out of a world's own snapshot pack are read and stood but the editor does not write them; this machine's props are never handed up to a server, and its houses are this browser's rather than a character's; the story workspace's reference builder does not know `place/` ids yet, which is a story session's to teach it; and **story dressing does not come from a server**: a story played on somebody's server is dressed by what each player's own build carries, so the owner's dressing must be committed here to be seen by anybody.

15. Travel: **the shuttles run, from the terminals the game put them behind**. Walk into a starport, use the travel terminal standing in it, and a window shows this world's ports on this world's own map with the fare from where you stand; its galaxy side picks another world first and then a port on it. Purchase takes the fare out of your credits and puts a ticket in hand, and the ticket is handed to the ticket collector outside -- which will only let you board while a shuttle is really there, on a clock every browser shares. The terminals, the collectors and the shuttles are where the game's own building templates put them, 189 of them over 11 worlds. The shuttles now land, wait and lift off on that clock, each on the game's own rig with its own clips (the transports fold their struts and open their doors), are solid while they wait, and hum, fire their engines, raise smoke on the pad and sound their landings and take-offs at the moments their own clips mark; how the effects look has not been seen by eye, nor whether the pieces sit right on their joints, whether each pad's approach clears the town round it, or whether the shuttle's lift-off, which ends 67 m up and is then simply gone, wants a fade. **A ticket is flown now.** Handed to the collector it seats you, out of sight, in the shuttle on the pad, which lifts off on that clock and on its own clip; a ticket about this world is flown there by the shuttle's own pilot and landed on the far pad's own clip, and a ticket to another world, with the terminal's box left unticked, flies the whole way -- out of the sky past the space gate, up into the orbit, the jump every ship makes (or, between Corellia and Talus or Naboo and Rori, a flight across the system), a turn toward the far world's disc, down to a point high over the far pad's approach, and in to land -- while with the box ticked (always, for Mustafar, which has no orbit) it is carried across under a loading screen straight onto the far pad's landing. A port with no rigged pad (six of the game's, Kachirho among them) sets you down on foot, a trip that cannot go on is skipped or sets you down at the port, and nothing leaves anybody in space. One whole trip through space, Corellia to Tatooine, has been flown in a driven tab with no errors; none of it has been seen by eye -- the swap on the pad, the blends at the cut and the join, the chase view of a 43 m hull, the jump from a passenger's seat, a transport landing on a shuttleport's pad. Still not built of it: nobody else sees you ride (you are hidden from the others for the whole trip), a trip has no stops on the way, and none can start from Kashyyyk or Mustafar, which have no terminal. One pad is known to be flown badly: onto Naboo's Lake Retreat Shuttleport, from another port or down from space, a ridge up to 375 m over the pad stands on the landing's own line behind its join, so the pilot dives at up to 35 degrees, goes round once and still reaches the landing 34 m (a transport) to 164 m (a shuttle) too high; the real-ground script names it on every run (`KNOWN_HIGH` in `tools/swg/tests/shuttleCourseTerrain.ts`) and fails on any other crossing down that does the same. What is not built: a ticket does not survive a logout, nothing is refused for being the wrong faction or over a lot limit, and the developer's own `__debug.shuttle({ list: true })` still travels with no ticket at all. Nothing of it has been seen by eye.

## How to work with the owner

Give them exact commands to run and exactly what to look for, one list, every item independent of the others so they can do it all in one session. When something cannot be judged from here, ask for the one observation that separates the causes rather than guessing twice. Converter changes need a reconversion (`npm run swg -- ships @SWG assets-private --retail-only` for ships; the README's table for the rest); say so every time, and say when a change needs none.

**Feel is the owner's to decide, by hand.** How a thing feels -- how high a jump goes, how hard a pull, how fast, how sudden is too sudden, how much a camera eases -- is his call, made with live knobs (his words, 2026-10-17: "the 'feel' of driving over things, or what is 'too sudden' of a hill steepness change should be something I decide"). Build the mechanism, give each such number a live knob with a sensible start and its reason in a comment, and stop: never pick one by measuring and re-measuring, and never run a measuring campaign over worlds or frame rates to choose one. Where a helper's numbers are mostly feel, hand the debug menu a set of dials for it (`src/ui/dials.ts`; the race's and the engines' are `src/vehicles/raceDials.ts`), each set through the helper's own setter, so he can move them as sliders and paste back the line that keeps them. Measure and fix only what is not taste: the code contradicting itself, something under the ground or through a wall, a difference by frame rate, an allocation in a frame, a crash or a wreck nothing caused, a hard rule broken.
